taming the firebirds

TAMING THE FIREBIRDS

Book One

Lech Mirsky

Every word here comes from my memory, I'm doing my best to tell you everything about the events I was so lucky to have witnessed there.

Copyright © 2026 by Lech Mirsky. All rights reserved. HUMAN AUTHORED.

WORLD ONE - EARTH
01 - THE RING
part one

In a gorgeous, modern city, two sisters, Rina and Nina, lived on the sixth floor of a white, glassy tower. The girls were twins, so strikingly alike that sometimes even their mother guessed wrong. That spring, Rina and Nina turned thirteen - "thirTEEN at last!" - was written on their birthday cake. Now it was summer, July rolling on, seventh grade behind them and endless vacation days ahead.

It was the third morning that a vague, round glow kept appearing in the sky along with the sun, a foggy scarlet circle flaring into life at a great height – an unblinking eye staring down at everyone from the clouds. People felt unsettled watching that “bleary gaze”, but so far, no one could explain what it actually was.

"Everybody on Instagram is arguing about whether it's aliens or a missile test gone wrong", Rina said looking up at the sky.

Nina laughed. "No, what missile? It's just angels getting married. But they messed up – they dropped the bride's ring and it rolled straight down here".

Since the sisters overslept (it was summer, after all), they scrapped their plans and went for a bike ride – first, all the way around the Field, then to the River, through Darnitsa, and back. Six miles total. Tired, the girls were resting on a bench near the daycare in the shade of a poplar tree.

“Look!” said Nina. “This silly Bik's in love with Lizka”.

In the corner of the bench, written in a white marker, was:

Liza + Bik

“Bik? That's Polensky”, Rina said confidently.

“No, Polensky is Brik, and Bik is probably the new guy”.

Nina picked up a tiny shard of glass from the ground, and with its sharp tip, scratched a heart right above the plus sign. It actually came out nice.

“There”. She grinned. “Now it's true love forever”.

“Forever. Until the staff repaints the bench”, experienced Rina corrected her little sister (twelve minutes younger).

At that moment, Igor, San, and Dan came over from the tennis court. Igor invited the girls to his birthday party, which would be on Saturday.

“We're not sure yet”, said Rina. “If we're still around and don't leave town, then we'll come”.

“Fine, I'll call you later”. Igor winked. Rina didn't catch the hint and shrugged.

“Totally. Just call us in a couple of days. In the morning”.

“Okay, I will”, Igor agreed.

As the boys left, Nina watched them go and said, “Honestly, I don't really feel like partying. It'll be a bunch of random people. And besides... you know”.

Rina said nothing, just raised her eyebrows...

They decided to play “Around Europe” – the famous race through four neighborhood blocks with the final sprint along the Waterfront. The girls jumped on their bikes.

“Two! One! Go!” Rina shouted, and they both shot forward – the pumped-up tires humming.

Soon Rina started to fall behind, but she had her secret weapon – the “Nitro-Pedal”, her own invention. Here's how it works: you pull yourself up, tense your body – then boom! – hit the pedals like mad. The front wheel lifts off, and the wind whistles in your ears.

The girls flew into the final courtyard. Nina was thrilled – beating her sister at “Around Europe” was a joy.

“Come on!” she cheered and turned to the left, almost tipping her bike over.

But at that instant their mother's voice rang out. They stopped and looked up – Mom was standing on the balcony, waving.

“Nee-na! Ree-na!”

Breathing heavily, the sisters glanced at each other. What happened? Why so soon? However, there was no choice – the race was terminated. Rolling toward the building, Rina called out:

“Right now?”

“Aunt Vera's here!” Mom replied, beaming.

They didn't expect it. Aunt Vera? From America? No way! As if Mom had heard their thoughts, she added:

“Yes! She's already here! Come on, we're waiting for you!”

The girls grabbed their bikes and dragged them up the stairs to the entrance, jostling past one another...

“Rina! Nina! Wow!” Aunt Vera gushed, hugging the girls – their cheeks burning from the race. “I can't believe it – how big you've gotten! If I saw you on the street, I would never recognize you”. She pulled back to spin them around. “Oh, wow, look at you two – so grown up, practically adults!”

Aunt Vera was Mom's cousin – six years younger, blue-eyed, always radiant – she was the heart of the family, the life of the party, and her party was every day. But three years ago she landed a job in Sweden, where, as her friend Valia put it, she “had a whirlwind marriage” with an American engineer. Soon after, they flew off to his beloved Alaska to work there for a gold mining company. Now she calls herself a ‘Cassedanian'. The girls tracked her down on Google Maps, imagining Cassedan as an icy town pierced here and there with golden shafts. In truth, it was...

“Girls, girls! I'm so happy to see you! But I'm only here for three days – I have to fly out on Thursday. I already got my tickets.”

“You always stay for three days. That's so short!”

“Business, girls. I'm flying to India with some papers to sign there, that sort of thing,” Aunt Vera smiled. “And then straight home, to the mine. You can't imagine how much work we've got there at the mine!”

“And it's still winter in Alaska?”

“What? Winter?” Aunt Vera laughed. “Of course not! It's been summer for weeks now, and it's going to get hot soon – just like in Odesa!”

The girls didn't believe her. What kind of Odesa could there possibly be at the edge of the world, three inches from the North Pole? At least on the map…

As always, Aunt Vera brought all sorts of stuff, including Alaskan gifts. “The northernmost souvenirs”, she called them. Rina got a cute belt woven from colorful leather strips and a scarf of impossible softness. Nina got a scarf just like Rina's, only with a different border pattern, and a pair of magically beautiful reindeer-fur mittens with red leather cords and scarlet embroidery on the backs – big snowflakes.

Mom was setting the table. Her favorite cousin's visit was a big event – they hadn't seen each other in more than nine months. The girls barely had time to stow their bikes when Mom came over and said:

“Nina, we're out of bread. Go to the store, please”. And she took some cash out of the cupboard. “Get that barley loaf, or if they have the rye baguette, get that too. And lavash. Oh, and four pastries, of course, any kind you like”.

“Should I ride my bike?”

“No, what bike? How will you carry the pastries?”

Nina sighed – she was stuck walking it.

“And don't linger, try to be quick – lunch is ready”, Mom added as Nina stepped toward the corridor.

“Linger? Where would I even?” Nina thought, shrugging.

And really, where? It was five, well, maybe six minutes to the store across the Field. Nowhere to stop.

She paused for a second and grabbed her new Alaskan mittens – just wanted to take a closer look. The fur shone platinum, like something truly precious. And the embroidery was insane – the finest threads were packed so densely, so artfully. Nina imagined an Inuit grandmother bent over her needlework, patiently stitching carmine silk, while the long polar night was stretching beyond the tiny window.

Dashing out of the tower, Nina ran straight into her classmate Lida Babich – a dark-haired, small-framed girl who had recently moved from the Right Bank of the River and whom her teacher, Zinaida Abramovna, had once dubbed a “hopelessly good student”. That weird label stuck to poor Lida, and now everyone called her ‘Hopeless'. Lida was offended, especially since no one in class ever figured out what Zinaida Abramovna actually meant.

“Hi!” Lida greeted, waving to Nina.

“Hi!”

“Where are you going? Out for a walk?”

“No, I already went out. They sent me to the store. For pastries”.

“Pastries?”

“Yeah. My aunt came to visit. From America”.

“America?” Lida repeated.

She always repeated – a little trick to buy some thinking time.

“Yes, America. Alaska. Can you believe – it's still winter there!” Nina lied on impulse.

“Winter?” Lida repeated with surprise and looked around.

“She gave me this!” Nina took out the mittens.

Lida raised her eyebrows even higher.

“Let me see!”

Nina handed over the mittens with obvious pride.

“Handmade! Arctic craft! National art!” she commented in a TV voice as Lida quick-stepped along beside her, staring with admiration. In the summer sun, the reindeer fur sparkled, shimmering like fresh frost.

“This is as far as I'm going”, Lida declared abruptly, handing back the mittens. “Just so you know, I'm waiting for my dad, we're going to Maidan for something”.

“All right then, see you”, said Nina casually.

“Bye. Do you guys have a summer house?”

“Of course we do”.

“And you're going too?”

Nina shrugged.

“Maybe. It all depends on Mom, on her work”.

“Same here – depends on my mom too”, Lida sighed. “Well, I'm off. Bye. See you tomorrow”.

“See you”.

Nina stuffed the mittens into her pocket, looked up at the glowing red circle still hanging in the sky – the ‘bride's ring', she thought with a smirk – and strode along the wide asphalt path in the direction of the Field.

The ‘Field' – once an overgrown mess of briars – was now a flat, empty lot. The city wanted to build something there, but later they changed their minds – the project froze, workers left, trenches were filled in, and soon the place became a hangout for everyone – first kids, then paid dog walkers and their countless pets.

A dirt track, beaten down by thousands of footsteps, led from Nina's house through the Field directly to the grocery store. In dry weather, it was the shortest way. But after a good rain, the clay trail was hard to get through.

Nina entered the store – not many people, no wait at the checkout. She put bread and lavash in the basket, picked out pastries – two big jelly rolls and a couple of plump cherry turnovers – then with the change snagged a piece of “Love is...” gum. And headed back.

While she was in the store, a bunch of boys had run out onto the Field. Most of them were unfamiliar. Except for Jack Edwards, known as Sir-Jock. He really was a “Jock” – he was in a horseback riding club, and a “Sir” – his dad was English. Jack grinned happily and waved, Nina waved back.

Almost in the middle of the Field ran a long mound carpeted in thick weeds. There was probably a large pipe underneath – a leftover from the construction, but no one knew for sure – everyone was used to it, a pipe is a pipe. Nina scrambled to the top and looked around. The field and surroundings opened up before her: people, cars, dogs, boys, the store, the bus stop.

Out of nowhere, a strong wind struck, sharp gusts battering her. She was about to run down, but at that moment, a sudden swirl kicked up a wave of stinging sand right into her face. Nina shielded her eyes. The wind raged, whipping her hair. Then something huge exploded – a bone-shaking blast jolted the ground, a clattering echo rang off the buildings.

Nina glanced skyward, and her blood turned to ice – hanging right above, huge hellish flares rolled out in heavy, red billows, as if someone had unleashed an immense eruption of fire in the air. But it didn't rise – it reached greedily toward her. The blaze roared in the frenzied gale, scorching her face. Nina was barely breathing, staring into the infernal flames.

Suddenly, three enormous black birds burst from the fire. Nina screamed. The nightmarish, squalling harpies dove at her. The two most hideous ones seized her with long claws, yanked her upward with savage hatred, tearing her from the ground.

She let out a shrill, desperate screech, flailing her arms, but it was too late – the birds were dragging her into the flames. Nina blacked out, came to the next instant, opened her eyes: terror and pain, heat and thunder! No air! Claws dug into her shoulders. The birds hauled her higher and higher, surrounded by a dazzling, burning hurricane.

A moment later, the fire dissolved in the air and vanished. Nothing was in the sky, just a lazy herd of shaggy clouds – not a trace of the explosion, nor a hint of the red ring. The wind died down, a ginger dust settled gently onto the grass. Two speckled dogs ran right over the spot where Nina had just stood. People walked, rosy-cheeked toddlers chased one another. Nobody noticed anything – no wind, no fire, no monstrous birds. Nothing.

Meanwhile, the table had long been set. Vera and Mama were in the kitchen, discussing the news, gossiping and giggling. Rina rummaged in her wardrobe. Minutes ticked by.

Finally, glancing at the clock yet again, Mama called out:

“Rina! Please go to the yard and check. Maybe Nina's chatting with her friends somewhere – call her”.

“All right”, Rina replied. “I'll call. In a sec”.

Hastily tossing things back into the wardrobe, she grabbed her sneakers, slipped them on, and darted from home. On the ground floor, jumping out of the elevator, she stepped outside. The yard was empty: no girls, no familiar faces, hardly any people at all – just some little kids with their parents, and two unknown guys shouting across the lawn, clumsily trying to play tennis on the court.

“Sketchy. Where could she be?”

Even though everything around looked normal – just an ordinary sunny day – Rina suddenly felt a strong, undefined unease pressing down on her, as if something harsh was about to happen.

She walked along the black asphalt path, wet from the caretaker's hose. As she turned the corner, she noticed her classmate Lida sitting on a bench.

“Hey!” Rina said. “How's it going? Just relaxing?”

“Hi! Yeah, just sitting, as you can see. Waiting for my dad – we're going to Maidan”. Lida shielded her eyes from the sun. “I'm alone. Everyone's gone somewhere, and there's no one to hang out with. And you?”

“I'm looking for my sister. She ran to the store and hasn't been back for half an hour. Maybe she stopped by a friend's”.

“No”, Lida said. “I saw her”.

“When?”

“Not long ago, I don't really remember”.

“Where did you see her?”

“Over there”. Lida nodded toward the Field. “She was going to the store, for pastries, and showed me the mittens, you know, made of reindeer”.

Saying a quick goodbye to Lida, Rina hurried toward the Field.

“Well, maybe there's a line in the grocery store”, she thought.

In truth, she already knew it wasn't about lines. Paying no attention to anyone, she ran across the Field, ignoring the trail.

She flew up the steps and stopped in the middle of the store: a few customers along the shelves, two chatting, three at the register, and the janitor mopping the tile floor.

Rina stepped outside onto the porch. Now what? She tried to recall everything Nina had said. Could she have decided to go off somewhere? But why? Or perhaps she really had gone to someone's place for a minute and lost track of time?

No, Nina never behaved like that – she was always disciplined, and, unlike Rina, extremely cautious. If anything ever went wrong at home – any mishap or trouble – it usually happened to Rina. Since preschool, Nina had been known as the quiet one, a typical “rule-follower”. Somehow, Rina was the one who attracted problems. Mom would say Nina took after Dad, and Rina after Grandma.

But those thoughts didn't help. Rina stood there for a second, then hurried back toward home.

Passing a group of sweaty boys playing volleyball, she spotted Sir-Jock. He said something, but she didn't catch a word – just gave him a distracted nod in return.

The path led over the long mound. Rina climbed up and gasped – there, on the dusty trail, lay a scarlet cord from one of the reindeer mittens, glossy like a little snake. Rina grabbed it. In the sunlight, the string looked different – too bright, maybe – but no, it was the very same one.

Fear struck her. Nina! Something had happened. Something evil. A freezing terror washed over Rina's heart. To keep from bursting into tears, she ran down the path, leaping over drying puddles. She flew into the tower, reached her floor, sprang from the elevator, and rushed along the corridor. She stopped near the entrance and stayed there for a minute or two, trying to steady herself somehow, not wanting to look panicked. Then, exhaling deeply, she opened the door and stepped into the apartment.

Seeing the cord, Aunt Vera turned pale.

“Where's Nina?”

Rina didn't answer. Of course, she had some explanations ready – like maybe Nina had gone to the Oscar Café for her favorite pineapple pastries, or even run to ChaiCof or something like that – but looking at her aunt's rattled face, she felt just as scared and now stood there silently, holding the cord in her hand.

Mother came out, glanced, and understood everything. She tried to speak, but her throat closed up. Without knowing why, Rina felt kind of guilty.

“I didn't see her”, she said. “I found this in the Field”.

It was hard to recognize Mom – in half a minute, she became eight years older. Silently, she turned, went back to her room, and started changing her clothes, tugging at her stubborn blouse, getting tangled in the sleeves.

“Lida talked to her”, Rina added loudly. Mom rushed back in – eyes huge, face drained of color.

“Half an hour ago”, Rina continued, “when Nina was heading to the store. She showed her the mittens”.

“And the store?” Mom nearly yelled. “Did you go to the store?”

“Of course I did!” Rina shouted back. “I went everywhere! I searched around the whole building – twice! She's just gone!”

“Okay. Got it. It's been an hour”, Mom muttered, struggling into her shoes. “An hour. Almost an hour”.

“I'm coming with you”, Aunt Vera said and also began putting on her shoes.

“Me too”. Rina reached for her purse.

“No!” Mom's shout made them all flinch. “You stay home! In case she comes back! Call my phone”.

Mom and Aunt Vera left.

Rina ran to the balcony, stood there for some time, then hurried into Mom's bedroom, and after digging through the drawers, pulled out Dad's marine binoculars. Through the optics, she scanned the entire yard – every corner, every tree. She scrutinized the faces of passengers getting off the bus, looked for her sister among the smiling moms at the daycare, inspected the roofs and entrances of the neighboring buildings, and studied everything that moved. Even the clouds.

But then, a bit dizzy from peering through thick lenses for too long, she went to the kitchen, put a full kettle of water on the stove, and turned on the burner. Never even knowing why...

01 – THE RING
part two

Two hours passed. Nobody called, nobody sent a message. Rina was drifting from room to room, checking her phone for the twentieth time, stepping out onto the stairwell landing, peeking through all the windows and from the balcony, then drifting again. The clock was cruel – the seconds felt like sticky gum, the sluggish minutes dragged on: three-oh-five... three-oh-six... three-oh-seven.

Eventually, she couldn't take it anymore and decided to go down into the open. Not even the open exactly – just to stand by the entrance, out in the air, among people. That would feel better.

She went to her room, pulled out her jacket, but at that moment, the door lock clicked. Rina lunged into the hallway, nearly knocking over Aunt Vera. From the look on her aunt's face, it was clear – Nina was nowhere.

Mom and Vera, breathing heavily, both shaken, defeated, with ashen faces.

“We searched everywhere”, Aunt Vera announced as soon as she stepped inside. “First, these four yards and everything around. Asked everyone. The clerks at the store don't remember anything. Then we went all along the Boulevard to the metro and down toward the River, both sides. We checked every shop and all the cafés, even the bakeries and the candy stores – everything”.

“All right, that's it! I'm calling the police”, Mom declared, and without even taking off her shoes, she went into the living room, pulled out her phone, and dialed. She stood there waiting for about a minute that felt like an hour before someone replied and started listening. Mom spoke nervously, mumbling, coughing, and stuttering, biting her dry lips. Rina and Aunt Vera stood silently nearby.

“They say they are coming, told me to wait”, Mom muttered. She tapped the hang-up button and collapsed into a chair.

The police arrived quickly, in less than twenty minutes: a tall, young man about twenty-five with a black briefcase in his hand, and two uniformed officers – one thin with glasses, the other broad-shouldered, heavy, and smiling.

“Detective Torensky”. The young man showed his police ID. “Missing Persons Division”.

And right then, Mother, who'd been holding herself together all day, couldn't stand it any longer and broke down in tears...

Torensky asked protocol questions, took notes, examined the leather cord, studied Nina's photos. Then he pulled a long green paper form out of the briefcase and carefully filled it out in neat handwriting. The two police officers took breaks in the hallway to smoke, came back in, and left again. Finally, after packing the form, the cord, and the photos, the inspector turned to the broad-shouldered one.

“Too late to survey the yard? Your opinion.”

The officer shrugged. “Well, why not? We can take a look”.

Two minutes later, they all walked toward the Field.

“I've already checked every bush around – there's nothing”, Mom said, barely keeping up with Torensky.

“Maybe we should use a dog?” Aunt Vera suggested. The detective gave her a disdainful look. After that, Vera walked behind him in silence.

They reached the Field and climbed the mound. Torensky crouched, touched the dry, yellowed grass, then nodded for the second policeman to come over. The officer leaned down. Torensky whispered something to him, the cop turned around and walked back toward the buildings.

“All right,” said the inspector, brushing flower pollen from his trousers. “We'll now proceed to bother your friend Lidiya. She's a witness, her statement is needed for the search and record.”

Rina sighed. “You'll see she won't say anything new. It'll be the same as before”.

“Well, that is your perspective”. He straightened his black tie. “I view the matter differently”. He suddenly asked, “When did it rain here last – Saturday?”

“Yesterday evening, Saturday, yes”, Mom confirmed.

“Precisely”, he said and stepped down from the mound, walking along the trail. Everyone followed.

As expected, Lida could not remember anything special, she just repeated what she had told Rina. Her parents didn't understand what was going on and asked Mom worried questions, but from her answers, they gathered only one thing – Nina went to the store and never came back.

“Oh God, oh God”, Lida's mom murmured. “They kidnapped the girl!”

“On what grounds do you believe she was kidnapped?” the inspector asked instantly.

Lida's mom was taken aback. “No. I don't know. I just thought... well, if she disappeared, then... maybe... it means she was... abducted”.

“There are creeps like that”, another woman added. “They lure people, trick them, and take them away”.

The detective grunted something and stepped aside. Soon the second cop returned, looked at his boss, and shook his head.

Ten minutes later, the police left. Torensky promised to call the moment there was “any significant new information” – his words – and asked Mom to come to Room 26 at nine in the morning. After seeing the police off, Rina, Mom, and Vera trudged back home. Without a word, they took the elevator, entered the seemingly cold, hollow apartment.

All three sat in the living room, avoiding each other's eyes – deep down they felt somehow responsible for what had happened. Several times Rina attempted to offer the soothing words she had prepared, but looking at her mom's slumped shoulders, she only stared at the floor with a mournful cold in her heart.

“This just doesn't happen!” Aunt Vera suddenly said. “People don't vanish like this!”

“Like what?” Mom asked wearily.

“Just like that, without the slightest trace!” Aunt Vera exclaimed. “People run away from home, true. Teenagers run. Sure, sometimes they do. Fine! But before that, they pack some things, right? They take money, documents – I don't know – they buy tickets, at least”.

“Nina didn't run away!” Rina objected. “Why would she want to run from us?”

“I'm not saying that, I'm just trying to figure things out”.

“Nina was kidnapped”, Mother said quietly.

“By whom? Come on, tell me, cuz, who would take her? Were you being followed? Do you have enemies here? Or maybe you suspect someone?”

Mother shook her head.

“I don't suspect anyone. But it's obvious. Can't you see?”

“Exactly, I don't see, I don't understand a damn thing!” Aunt Vera threw up her hands. “Who kidnaps kids anymore?”

Mom began to reply, but Aunt Vera cut her off.

“All right, all right! Yes, kidnapping happens, I know! For a fat ransom, mostly. But why take Nina? For what? A ransom? Ha! Are you rich or something? A millionaire?”

“A millionaire...” Mother whispered.

“Then explain to me, please, why would anyone kidnap her?”

“What if they mistook her for someone?” Rina suggested. “Maybe they were after some banker's niece, and Nina just looks like her”.

“Nina looks like you, not anyone else”, said Mom.

Everyone fell silent.

“I don't like that inspector”, Aunt Vera stated after a long pause. “No, seriously, is this what they call an investigation?”

“He's a professional”, Mother said barely audibly. “Anyway, we have nobody else to count on”.

“What do you mean nobody else? We have ourselves to count on! I just haven't come up with what we can do yet...”

The next three hours plodded past like a coma. Mom felt sick, took a heart pill and two sedatives. Vera had a hard time getting her into bed.

“Lie down, please rest, it'll help soon, you'll feel better”, she insisted.

Vera and Rina started calling around – they called everyone they knew, many classmates, all the people who could have seen Nina somewhere today. But for now they decided not to disturb the relatives. Too early, better to wait, not everything was lost, maybe Nina would be found – hope had not completely gone out yet, hope remained, but it was fading by the hour. Finally, the list of contacts ended, there was no one left to call.

Mom fell asleep. Sunset was quietly approaching the city.

Vera and Rina sat in the kitchen for a long time, talking about the investigation, the phone calls, Nina. But mostly they just sat there saying nothing. Vera got hungry and said the last time she'd eaten anything was in the morning, on the plane. She rummaged through the fridge and piled a small mound of tomato salad and something baked – a carrot cake – onto her plate. Rina didn't feel like eating. But neither the calls, nor the salad, nor even the talk – nothing helped them relax. The tension was draining the soul, sapping strength from the body. Another hour passed in waiting and silent panic...

Suddenly Mom came out of the bedroom and declared:

“I'll call the inspector”, but she could barely stand – one hand against the wall. Rina brought her the phone.

Mother tried to dial, her fingers wouldn't obey. Aunt Vera took the phone and, looking at the detective's business card, tapped the number herself. Fortunately, he answered right away. He said he was still at the station despite the late hour, but the call didn't help much. The inspector switched to a formal tone and kept repeating the same lines: we're looking, we'll be searching, we will, we will, and offered clumsy reassurance with standard, meaningless words.

However, he also had some really encouraging news. “Currently, no fatalities have been reported”, he said, adding that no one matching Nina's photo was registered in the city's hospitals. Also, no thirteen-year-old girl had been brought to any of the police stations or the morgue. Still, somehow Mother, Rina, and her aunt knew Nina was safe without the inspector's note. Of course they were relieved to hear the detective say it, but they had no doubt that Nina was alive and well. She had just been kidnapped. That was the horror of it.

Rina was exhausted. Worry, anxiety, the unbearable wait for the worst news, the tension and grim thoughts – this long, wicked day had mercilessly worn her, dried up her spirit, and left her utterly spent. Time crawled heavily around the house, like an old bear lumbering around its den. Finally, Rina's desk clock beeped midnight.

“Rina, you should get some rest. It's late”, her mother said.

“I don't want to. What if the police call?”

“If they do, we'll wake you up”, the aunt assured her.

Rina was truly fading, her head buzzed, her temples throbbed.

“You'll really wake me if anything happens?” she asked, looking into her aunt's deep blue eyes.

“I promise!” Aunt Vera said firmly. “Have I ever lied to you?”

Rina did not say “good night”, she just couldn't voice it – nothing “good” was around. And she was thirsty. An ice-cold Sprite from the fridge was exactly the thing – she drank until she couldn't bear the pain in her teeth. Turning off the kitchen light, she walked over to the window, stood there, thinking and staring into the nighttime playground. Eight big yellow lamps cast harsh, solid-looking circles at the edge of the sports zone. A tall stranger in a white hat cut through one of the cones of light, leading a large brown dog on a leash.

She walked into her room and sank wearily onto the armchair. Beside her was her sister's half of the place – neat, cold, and empty. From Nina's pillow, a cheerful blue parrot – embroidered by their grandmother last Christmas – looked out mischievously. For some reason, Nina had named him Zeerik – a little zero. It seemed like the parrot was winking, as if to say, “Chill out, sis, we'll make it!” Rina sighed. Her throat tightened – blurred vision, tears welling in her eyes – she sat there quietly, weeping.

Rina didn't sleep in her bed. She decided to spend the night in that same armchair – fully dressed, shoes on, ready to jump up and rush off wherever it was needed.

But at some point, the ocean waves grew unnaturally loud, and a white seagull, pacing along the water's edge, was startled by the noise and squawked:

“Chree! Chree-ree!”

Rina opened her eyes, looked at the seagull, and thought, “What does she want? Where is she calling me?”

It was a dream – strange and unrelated to the day's events. In this vision, Rina sat on the pleasantly warm sand of a long, wide, gently sloping beach and gazed at the slowly rising tide of the endless turquoise sea. She watched the large white seagull scurrying comically on thin legs through the shallow water.

Suddenly, the bird took flight and hovered in the wind high above Rina's head in a deep, crystal-clear, yet somehow an emerald-green sky.

02 – HERX AND YUNNI
part one

Suddenly someone shouted from behind:

“Endlich! Finally! Thank heavens, you've been found!”

Startled, Rina turned and saw a stranger approaching quickly – an unusually tall elderly man with a short gray beard. The old man walked barefoot on the sand, wearing an expensive black tailcoat, like a pianist or an orchestra conductor. His trousers were fastened with a wide iron belt stained with rust. A sword, ancient-looking and possibly silver, hung from his belt.

Rina stared at the man, at the sword, but didn't react – after all, it was just a dream, although an extremely lifelike one.

“At my age, running along the beaches?” The old man shook his head. “You know... I'm telling you, it's not funny... But as you can see, I'm lucky. You haven't shifted too far. Luckily for you...” He stopped, looked around, peered up at the thin palm trees overhanging the shore, and said, “No, this place is no good. We'd better leave. We'll fly to yours”. He nodded toward the sea. “You don't mind, I hope?”

Without waiting for an answer, he closed his eyes tight, as if a bright lamp had been directed in his face. Rina woke up. Her leg ached from sleeping in an awkward position. She looked, and... screamed! Right there, by the window, leaning on the sill, stood that same elderly man, sternly frowning and watching her. Next to him, motionless like a figurine, was a strange boy of about ten with his eyes shut – his face painted light blue as if for a circus performance or, rather, a masquerade.

Through the airy, mesh curtains, moonlight filled the room with an orange glow. Silence blanketed the world – not a rustle, not a sound. The wall clock read 00:29.

Rina cautiously rose from the chair, straightened up, and shook her head, yet neither the old man nor the boy vanished.

“Hey, don't be afraid”, the old man said amiably and smiled. “Everything's normal, everything's fine”.

“But you... how did... how did you... get here?” Rina stammered, trying to articulate her shock. The man placed a finger to his lips.

“No, better say nothing. I'll explain it all myself. But not here. See, I've miscalculated again. An apartment, especially this late” – he glanced around – “it's, frankly, not a suitable setting for important talks”.

He squeezed his eyes shut.

The floor shifted sideways. Rina lost her balance and almost fell to the ground, she reached out for the chair but grasped only air. The old man and the boy stood before her in the middle of the Field. Around them – dark night, black grass, young, baby fir trees here and there, and sparse barberry bushes in the distance. She quickly looked at the surrounding buildings, the meadow, the trees, but saw no visible danger – the windows shimmered in the moonlight a hundred yards away, the embankment where the silk cord was found, glinted on the right.

“I think this clearing is sufficient, at least till dawn”, the old man said authoritatively, surveying the Field. “What do you think?”

She didn't reply. Hard to believe, but after the man's calm, regular words, her fear had dissolved almost completely. The dream receded too, mostly, though it hadn't faded altogether – Rina's mind was still clouded as if by a woolly haze.

However, this reality did not look like one of her nightly visions – it seemed that after all the strain and problems of the past day, Rina's emotions were blunted and nothing in this surreal scene could faze her – she just watched silently, waiting for the world's next move.

“My name is Herx”, the barefoot ‘pianist-conductor' finally introduced himself. “My full name is Herkir Esen Wo Terr Deuv, that is, ‘Strong Hand Holding the White Sword'. Since I'm your friend, you may call me Herx. I've long been used to this nickname”. He offered a guilty smile.

Rina remained quiet, the old man waited for a moment, coughed timidly, and added, “We need to talk about urgent and significant matters. I mean, about you and your future, basically. I hope you don't mind. You don't, right?”

She shook her head no.

“Very well”. Herx scratched his nose. “And here – meet my aneinn. In your terms, you might call him a student. His name is Yunni”.

The blue-faced boy opened his eyes. Rina had never seen anything similar – his huge cat-like eyes emitted a thin glow that somehow penetrated deep into her mind. She sensed it clearly – the boy could read everything in her memory and feelings. The air around her turned cold, she realized there was an immense, inhuman power in this little boy. A moment later his eyes drifted shut, and there on the meadow, bathed in the sharp moonlight, he stood as motionless as before, as if time-stopped – now resembling a vintage wooden doll.

“Okay. Listen to me carefully, girl, and don't get distracted”. Herx made a long pause. “I'll tell you what happened to Nina”.

“What happened to Nina?!” Rina screamed, snapping fully awake. The old man winced.

“Quiet! Why so loud?” He looked around warily. “We don't need attention, you know?” After another tense pause, he added, “Hey, let's sit down, eh? I was running so much today, you won't believe it, my legs are throbbing as if beaten by the guards”.

He made himself comfortable right on the grass.

Well, not exactly – he didn't just plop down. He was leaning either on his hand or on his sword, which he used as a staff, lowering himself slowly and unsteadily, grunting all the time, his knees trembling. But eventually he settled.

Rina thought for a moment and decided to sit down too, across from the man. The grass was cold, unpleasantly stiff, but dry – night dew hadn't yet fallen. The boy Yunni didn't move, standing still and composed, seemingly not even breathing.

“We know. Everything. About. Your. Sister”, the old man said, punctuating each word. Rina stared at his face without blinking, trying to catch every sound. “Don't worry, she's all right – you could say she's entirely fine. At least we know she's alive and absolutely unharmed”.

“Where is she?”

“Faaaar away”, the old man drawled and waved his hand. “Already far... But nothing is lost yet. We'll be able to help her”.

“Nina was kidnapped? How? Who took her? For what? For ransom? No? Then why?” Rina felt ready to yell, cry, demand, beg – anything. Dozens of panicking questions surged up through her emotions and blinked back tears, but above all, she needed to know where exactly her sister was. And how to get her back home. At least, she wanted to understand what had happened in every detail. She waited for the absolute truth.

“Here's how it is, let's go in order”, began the old man. “I sought you out for that very reason – to tell you the truth. But first, calm yourself. I see how hard this is for you. Please believe me, we know your sister really does feel – so to speak – normal. No, of course she was terribly frightened – who wouldn't be – but now, this minute, she's okay. I'm sure someone has already explained the situation to her. In any case, she will be treated accordingly and with great skradzhinda”.

“What?” Rina didn't get it.

“What ‘what'?” The old man's bushy brows bounced up.

“You said skrad... skra dzhi...”

“Ah! Sorry, I meant to say she'll be treated with high respect. Well, that is, like a foreign princess or something like that... You know, sometimes I confuse your language with Inhestian – very similar languages! At my age, ancient tongues are always hard, you see? ... So, yes, your sister was kidnapped, you are right about that, but not by gangsters, not for ransom, and no harm will come to her. Don't worry unnecessarily – no one will hurt her. As you say here, not a hair from her head will be touched... or the like... I don't recall exactly. Because he needs her”.

“Who needs her?”

“He, who else?” exclaimed Herx. “The Fireking!”

Rina felt her thoughts tangling into a knot of confusion. All day long she had imagined kidnappers as rough, cruel men in dark glasses and black shirts. Whatever the case, the criminal must look criminal – like in the movies. Well, in reality, everything is different, but reality has no kings! Especially kings of fire. Nonsense! Gibberish!

If not for that doll-like, blue-faced boy, and for the silver sword stuck point-first in the grass – she could touch it with her finger – and if not for the barefoot old man in a tailcoat, and for the dazzling moon above, and the obstinate mosquito trying to land on her forehead – in short, if not for reality, Rina would have been certain it was just an absurd joke of her exhausted imagination. Or a high fever, perhaps. But in fact, she wanted to believe it was a dream – a fancy piece of her sleep, that's it!

The old man stared into her face.

“No, this is not a dream”, he said a bit reprovingly. “And not your fever... Well, I do understand – you lack experience. But what's done is over, nothing can be changed. Too late. It's not a dream, it's life. Your life! And your sister is beyond our reach, true, she's in the distant realm of the Fireking. That is as clear as this moon, believe it or not – up to you”.

“But where is that realm? In which country? In Europe? Tell me! Please!” Rina tried to make sense of the chaos and find at least the right word – a small foothold in all this mess.

“Oh, I wouldn't call it a country yet. Yes, they've formed a kingdom somehow, and they built it without water. Amazing! But everything is incomplete there, even the planet.” Herx shook his head. “That broken world is beyond explanation, it's a pure jilinda, you see?”

Rina didn't know what “jilinda” was but decided not to ask. Herx stared intently at the moon and said:

“Their civilization is pre-industrial, much like the Earth four hundred years ago. Horses – the only power. But they have some great inventions – their food sources are much better than yours. ... Okay, we'll discuss it later. First, we need to save your sister, and I'm ready to help. I'll support you. Not personally, though. You see, the Universe is strictly bordered. Here, within the Circle, I have no right to interfere in alien life, even by talking or thinking. It's prohibited. But your case is different – you are the victim's sister – you have the privilege to do anything you wish there”.

“Who are you?” Rina suddenly asked.

“Me?!” The old man's eyes seemed to triple in size. “Is it still unclear? I'm a wizard, as you call it. Or a mage, magus, sorcerer, charmer, spellbinder, enchanter... uh... warlock. What else? Ah, that... shaman. You have so many funny words. But we prefer magasther – it's more professional”.

Rina briefly thought the old man might be a little psycho. Maybe he even escaped from a special institution, who knows? But then, how to explain the split second jump from a dream ocean shore to her apartment? And from the house to the wasteland?

“No, no, you're wrong”. Herx wagged his finger. “I'm not a loon. I'm old, that's true. You can't fix it – years don't wait, you know. I've certainly forgotten much, and I might not speak as I should, not as you're used to hearing, right? But I haven't lost my mind yet, no, don't even think that”.

“You read my thoughts”, she said confidently, but this miracle didn't surprise her.

“Yes, I do, so what?” The old man shrugged. “You don't like someone invading your mind. Sorry, who would? But I can't help it. I'd be glad not to hear everything, but how? Well, I told you, I'm a wizard, a sort of, but you don't believe me”. He made a sour face. “Okay, seriously speaking...” The old man raised his hands as if to pray, then lowered them in despondency. “No, I have no clue how to explain it. You people don't have proper words for things like that, you haven't even invented the words”.

He tapped his finger on his sword and continued:

“Listen, girl, if you were from another layer, I might be offended and would just go away, ending our business once and for all, but you're a human – a subjective being – so your distrust is natural. I know your history – humans never trust each other”. He giggled. “You are a civilization of great doubts. But your suspicion and disbelief can at some point hinder the entire project. To guarantee our success, we must work in sync, you kshar? There must be no misunderstanding between us. No doubts! Am I speaking clearly?”

Rina nodded. He looked at the moon again, rather concerned.

“All right, you've convinced me. If it's really that bad, then we have no choice – let me try to dispel your doubts some other way. I think having something definite will be useful and won't take much time. The main thing is – don't be afraid. You will be okay in any case”.

Herx took a deep breath, shut his eyes, and at that very moment dense snow began falling from the sky – literally pouring, millions of big, cold flakes, each the size of an apple. Rina watched, eyes wide, but couldn't endure it – she jumped up from the grass. In less than a minute, the snowfall covered the entire Field in a thick, sparkling carpet up to Rina's ankles.

The old man remained seated, unmoving. Now coated in growing snow layers, he looked like a thawing spring snowman, though still scrutinizing Rina closely and smiling rather slyly. Then he shut his eyes again. The snow immediately turned gray, rapidly faded, dissolved, and evaporated within a few heartbeats.

Something began thumping loudly behind her. She turned around and saw three large red kangaroos busily hopping across the grass. The kangaroos leaped past barely three yards away, and soon disappeared into the thickets.

Right after the rust-colored Australian trio, about sixty paces away from Rina, something metallic, angular, and monstrous began growing noisily from the trembling ground with creaks and scrapes – massive beams, trusses, grates, and antennae, all with thick rivets. Hundreds of tons of interwoven iron tore through the dirt, crawling out and rising higher and higher into the air.

“The Eiffel Tower is being born”, Herx remarked in a pleased tone. “Want me to pull it out now, the whole thing?”

“No, please don't!” she replied in horror.

“All right, as you say”. The tower started melting. Heavy chunks, like warm chocolate, were falling into the bottomless hole in the torn ground, and a couple of minutes later, no trace of the unfinished steel structure remained.

“Well, let's consider that enough for today. Now tell me, do you at least believe that I truly am a magasther?”

“I believe you”, Rina echoed.

“And do you also understand that you're not asleep and everything around you is real, or do you still doubt it?”

“I believe that too”.

She paused, thought for a moment, and then sat before Herx.

“Wonderful!” he said, rubbing his hands. “Then we may continue”.

And Herx told her everything. He poured out a massive flood of facts and details, from which Rina retained only scattered bits and caught some fragments here and there; besides, the old man was animated so much that occasionally he slipped words and entire phrases in different languages – too hard to understand. But twelve minutes later he paused, whispered something, and said:

“Okay, let's summarize it”. He clapped his hands. “Now you know how the Circle was formed, there are seven neighboring worlds in it, and the first one is right here, we are sitting on the ground, on the planet Earth, so we call Earth number one. The World of Sands is two, the Ocean World is three. Then quite near it – the World of Clouds. We'll mark it as number four. Did you follow the count?”

Rina nodded.

“There we are! It's a useful thing knowing the right number of the world!” The old man gestured, his beard disheveled. “Next comes the World of Butterflies – that's five, and then one step farther – the World of Snows. Number six. And from there, keselan to the World of Fire – final stop, we've arrived. Even if the stop is not final – it doesn't matter, your sister is there, in the Fire World, and we don't need to go farther. That's it! Done, as you might say”.

He looked into Rina's face attentively and added:

“You should know that living planets are extremely rare. Why? Because the sun must be similar to yours, the planet too, with the same distance from its star. And it must have a huge moon, and one or two enormous partners like Jupiter or Saturn. Otherwise, even if it has water and an atmosphere rich in oxygen – it's still useless. Life needs all those conditions and countless others. That's why living worlds are so scarce. I hope you understand. Any questions?”

After a quick thought she asked, “Yes, a question. I still don't get it – why did they kidnap my sister? Those... snatchers... from the World of Fire”.

“Oh, they're not just ‘snatchers', but the King's best aides, his most trusted and closest people... well, not exactly people... birds”.

“Birds?!”

“Right! But don't jump ahead. Very soon you will sorar all the details yourself, without my hints. Once you are in the Kingdom, you'll see for yourself why they need your sister so much. But until that moment – you're not allowed to know. That's the rule. Kshar?”

“Why not allowed? Why kshar?” Rina persisted.

Herx snorted and said, “Why, why... For your own safety, that's why! Trust the old man – I have no reason to deceive you. There are things that are better learned later”.

Rina sighed.

“It's okay, no need to despair”. He tapped her shoulder. “I will help, I promised. I'll give you a map of the planets. It will show you the shapes of the Second World, but once you traverse its whole extent, the map of the World Number Three will appear that same minute. And so forth, up to the seventh, the final one”.

02 – HERX AND YUNNI
part two

He turned and called, “Yunni! Yuuu-nniii!”

The boy slowly opened his eyes, clearly waking up from a deep sleep, and then slid silently across the grass, barely moving his feet. As he approached, he held out both hands and suddenly smiled. His smile was even weirder than his gaze – it seemed to have both a taste and a smell, the smile was... sweet. And it definitely smelled like honey.

A light appeared just above his palms, as if someone had lit a match. But there were no matches. The light grew quickly and soon became a large orange glimmer. Something dropped from it onto the grass. A folded square of paper. The boy picked it up and handed it to Herx. Yunni's eyes closed, the smile vanished, and he silently floated back to his spot. Rina noticed again – the boy had stopped breathing.

“Here it is, your precious map!” The old man winked. “Hold it and never lose it”.

Rina took the paper, carefully unfolded it, and... stared in disbelief – there on the thick, lacquered paper was an accurately rendered image of both hemispheres of Earth, much like what you'd find in old geography books, though Herx's map looked two hundred years older.

“Ha! And what did you expect to see?” Herx laughed. “We're on Earth, on your planet, in your original World!”

But the map only seemed regular – well, 17th century maybe, but still. No, in reality, it depicted no cities or countries. Europe and South America were tinted with the same red color, while Asia and Australia looked like they were made of mustard.

And there was no evidence of Antarctica. Instead, everything was covered in a web of thin black lines, tiny markings, and symbols indicating places and ways of transitions, internal connections, time flows, and the most important energy nodes.

Memorizing all this mess was impossible, despite Rina's efforts, and Herx felt it. From the outside, he looked like a grandfather absorbed in some strange game with his granddaughter. Only their serious faces revealed the truth.

Eventually, the old man fell silent, waiting for Rina's questions.

“Ok”, she said. “But do I really have to go through the worlds to reach the last one?”

“Yes, you do”. He smiled.

“But why?” She looked frustrated. “Can't you just send me straight to my sister?”

“No, I can't”. Herx shook his head. “It's not allowed. Within your Earth, you jump as much as you like, from any place to anywhere. But along the Circle – no, sorry, we cannot. You should go from one world to another, step by step. Skipping is not an option for us”.

“Not for us? Then who can do it?”

“Well, I've never met anyone who could. You see, the Circle has restrictions at every turn – you can't do this, you can't do that. It's like that little notice in your bus: ‘The Passenger's obliged, obliged, and obliged, the Administration has the right'“, Herx chuckled. “Take it or leave it. We didn't write the Principles, and it's not for us to improve them”.

“Yes, but you're a wizard, a sorcerer!” Rina insisted.

The old man glanced briefly at the stars.

“Sorcerer, you say. And what does that mean? The word is strong, I agree, but it was invented by you humans – emotional creatures. Actually, I doubt the term has any tangible meaning. The longer I live, the more I'm convinced there is no real magic in the worlds. Some just know how to do things that others are unable even to picture, or they do but with great difficulty. Is that magic? No. It's more like... like a kind of skill, perhaps. Tell me, have you ever used a lighter?”

“Yes, of course”.

“Do you know how ancient people made fire in the Stone Age?”

“Sure. They spun a stick in a little hollow on a dry log”.

“Good for you! They spun it, exactly! Well, there were other ways too, but some stubborn folks stuck to spinning sticks. And mind you, they huffed and puffed for half an hour every time! Can you visualize the blisters they got on their hands? Bloody ones! But that was normal. Now imagine this: you travel back in time to those aborigines, who have logs and sticks, and you pull out a little tricky thing from your pocket – a lighter. You click it – Snick-Flick! – and there's your flame! What do you think they'd say about you?”

Rina shrugged. “I don't know... maybe that I was... a witch... or...”

“Or a sorceress. A wizard, right?”

“Right”, she agreed.

“There you go. No magic exists. Not a grain of it! You've got your lighters in your pockets, and I've got my scripts for the Energy layer. Same kind of tricky, little things, actually. There's no magic”.

Rina paused, thinking.

“All right... then what's going to happen to my mom?”

“Your mom?!” Herx was puzzled. “What could happen to your mom?”

“What do you mean, ‘what'?” she exclaimed. “Mom will be crushed! Both of us will be missing – first Nina, and now me!”

“Oh, that's what bothers you. Ha! Undoubtedly, she'll be crushed!” The old man looked her in the eye with amazement. “She's a mother! How could she not be upset? But that's exactly why we're doing all this – so she'll be happy again. If we succeed, you'll return her Nina, her daughter! Doesn't she want that? Or do you not want that?”

“I do! But what if we fail?”

“Why think about that?” Herx mumbled quietly, looking at the star-filled sky. “You must believe in success, or there's no point in starting. And if we fail... well... we'll say we're just unfortunate, useless people. Especially me”.

Neither spoke. Then he asked, “Okay, but I need to hear your final word. Do you agree to go?”

Rina sighed again.

“Of course, I do. But I still have no idea what I should do over there, literally”.

“Well, that's not a problem”. Herx now looked much happier. “Hold on a moment, don't rush – we have plenty of time. Right now, the most important thing is that I've got your consent, you said ‘of course, I agree'. It's like signing a contract, correct? And now I can give you a key to the World Number Two”.

“A key?” Rina pictured a golden key with a carved loop.

What appeared instead was quite different: the old man groaned, muttered grumpily, slowly rose from the grass, and extended his hand. For a few moments, nothing happened. Then, from some place in the air, a small pink feather, twirling and waltzing, landed precisely on his palm.

“And here's your little key”, Herx beamed, cautiously picking up the feather by the quill, bringing it to his lips and blowing on it. The feather sparkled with a prickly glitter. “Do you like it? I hope you do. Take it, it's yours. This is a feather of the sand-bird Ajj. Now getting to the Desert will be easy – you need to burn this feather in an open flame. In a campfire, for example. That's all. And of course, nobody should touch it”.

“And then?”

“And then you'll see for yourself!” The old man replied merrily.

Somewhere high above their heads, an invisible bell struck. A heavy bronze reverberation swept over the sleeping city, causing dozens of car alarms to wail in a persistent, disturbing chorus.

“È finita!” exclaimed Herx. “Time's up, we have to go”. He began rummaging urgently through his endless pockets.

“But I still don't know anything!” Rina almost shouted.

“Just a moment, just a mo”, the old man mumbled. “Where is it?”

“What?”

“Ah, never mind. Too late. Come here, quickly!”

Rina stepped forward.

“Close your eyes!”

She obeyed. Herx gently touched her forehead with his finger. BANG! A mighty blue flash exploded in her mind – a lightning strike! Her knees weakened. She would have collapsed if the old man hadn't caught her.

“Done!” Herx announced, smiling from ear to ear. “That's it! I've given you the core, and as for the details, you'll find those on your own”.

“What details?” she asked, steadying herself.

“Minor ones”, he answered simply. “You'll remember them the moment you need them. Automatic knowledge, you see? Anyway, I've done all I could and transferred what was needed. Did you feel the transfer?”

“I did”, Rina said, rubbing her forehead.

“Wonderful! All right then, we are leaving. Goodbye. We'll meet again soon”.

“When?”

“Soon, I said! I think, very soon. Don't worry, I'll always be somewhere nearby. Or almost always. Or almost nearby. I'll try to help however I can”.

Rina looked at the old man and saw him gradually turning transparent, as if made of dark glass. The boy stood a few steps away, motionless. Then he stirred and opened his eyes, as if amused. But Rina somehow knew that he could no longer see her.

“Goodbye”, she said tentatively.

The old man remained silent. Rina looked through him as if through a crystal door. A few seconds later, Herx and the boy were gone.

She stood still for a moment, then took a step toward home. In the next instant, she found herself in her room. This time, she was scared for real and let out a cry, but quickly calmed herself and walked over to her desk. The lamp wasn't needed – the moonlight made the room bright enough.

Rina dropped into her chair and spent a long time examining the map and the feather of the bird Ajj. Then she took a big notebook from the shelf and tore out a blank page, intending to jot something down.

She looked at the paper, yawning, recalling the blue boy, Herx the wizard, Nina, kangaroos, Nina again, the Eiffel Tower – her eyes slowly closed on their own, her head quietly sank onto the open map of her native World. Rina rested her forehead on the table, then shifted slightly to the side, searching for a comfortable position. And there, warming Australia with her cheek, mumbling something from time to time, she slept a relaxed, deep, exhausted sleep until morning...

Rina was awakened by a young, lively ray of dawn that slipped past the curtains, ricocheted off the mirror, and immediately kissed her right in the eyes. The clock read 5:05. She got up, tiptoed to the bathroom, opened a thin, silent stream of cold water, and quickly washed her face. Then she returned to her bedroom, opened the closet, took her favorite backpack, patched together from multicolored triangular leather pieces, and began packing for the trip.

What would she need on the journey? And what kind of journey was this anyway? She had no idea. Seven worlds, but not even a list – Earth was the first, the World of Fire the last, and the rest were all jumbled in her mind.

She crammed a little of everything, at least the things she thought might be useful and what she could fit into this rather small pack.

“That's enough”, she whispered when the loading was done.

After sitting on the bed to relax for a minute, and letting her troubles fly free, as Dad would say, she looked around, tapped her teddy bear Bimka on his pink nose, then reached for the nightstand, and for some reason checked the box with her collection of ancient Polish postcards – they were in place. Then she stood up and snuck out into the hallway.

Everyone was asleep. Rina noiselessly crept up to her mother's door, cracked it open with her fingertips, and, fighting tears, whispered a quiet goodbye to her mother and Vera.

She stood there, thinking, then went back to her room and pulled thirty-six dollars out of the desk, a recent gift from Grandpa – three crisp tens, a five, and a heavy one-dollar coin – then carefully put them all in a white envelope, tucked it into the “hidden” inner pocket of the backpack – der Rucksack, as her best friend Svetka would say in her funny German – she closed it, securely tied all its strings, and slung it over her shoulders. Done! Now ready!

Five minutes later, Rina was walking toward the metro through almost-quiet, nearly empty streets. At the entrance, she pulled out her card and went in, stopping at the end of the platform. Right then, two trains arrived with a thunderous roar – one filled tightly with silent, sleepy early-morning shift workers, hotel staff, policemen, and bus drivers, heading downtown, and the other coming from the center, with only five or six passengers clustered in a corner. She slipped into the chilly car. At the final station, she got off and waited for the bus.

As if some morning gods heard her wish – the first bus was heading toward Knyazhychi. She dropped onto the seat and almost screamed – it felt icy. The bus moved, and the people walking their dogs melted away in seconds, replaced by huge workshops, warehouses, and heavy iron fences. Soon the view out the right-side window turned into a dense and unkempt wild forest of tall trees.

She hopped out halfway, at a bus stop under a footbridge, walked a few paces back, and turned onto a park road leading somewhere through the pine grove. She then turned right and walked along a clay footpath. Ten minutes later she stopped among rows of young, curly poplars stretching in both directions. Rina approached the trees and gathered as many dry leaves and twigs as her hands could hold.

Just beyond the trees was a large, semicircular meadow, completely covered in a ground-hugging plant that looked very much like pea sprouts. Rina forged ahead without watching her step. She walked about a hundred yards in, scanned the surroundings – nobody. And really, who would be there at this hour?

She squatted down and arranged the twigs and leaves into a neat pyramid, “a little hut”, as Nina called it. Then she pulled matches and a crumpled piece of newspaper from her pocket, unfolded it, slid a paper corner under the “hut”, and lit it. Thin blue wisps of acrid smoke rose. Rina waved them away and blew into the fire.

At last, after three or four attempts, she managed to build something that slightly resembled a small campfire. She then stood up, opened her backpack, took out a sheet of white paper, folded in four with the feather of Ajj inside, and without unfolding it, carefully placed it directly on the twig pyramid. The heat immediately forced the paper open. For a second, Rina saw the feather. The red tongue of flame greedily licked the pink fluff, the feather shrank, crackled, and... vanished – burned up in an instant.

Rina looked around. Everywhere lay the pea-covered meadow, and beyond it – pines and willows. There were no roads, no trails – just natural wilderness. Rina waited. One minute. Two. Three. The fire burned down to its last ember, went out, and began to smoke. Just in case, she stomped out the ashes with her sneakers. She stepped back and stood for a long time there, waiting. Nothing was happening.

“So? It didn't work?”

Suddenly, something clanked behind her. Rina turned sharply and was stunned to see a huge, tall, and clearly heavy door in a decorated frame, resting right on the pea vines. She stood frozen, with her eyes wide. It was scary. Still, after a minute or two, she mustered her courage somehow, crept toward the door in tiny steps, and examined it thoroughly.

Finding nothing threatening, she walked around it. Rina thought she wouldn't be surprised if someone told her the door had been taken from a museum, or more likely from the entrance of some old Italian palace. It was so richly painted – every shade of Mediterranean blue, though there was nothing magical about it. Just a door, really.

Emboldened, Rina pressed her fingers to the frosty bronze handle and gave it a gentle push. The door yielded easily, as if it had been oiled just yesterday, and opened silently. Behind it was the same green pea field.

“Miracles”, Rina said with a kind of bitter irony.

And indeed, it was a miracle. If through the half-open door she had seen a sea, mountains, stars, planets, galaxies – anything, quite anything! – that would have made sense. She had actually expected something like that. But the same dumb pea field looked like... a miracle.

“The miracle of a never-happened miracle”, she thought.

So, no longer afraid and not particularly caring what she was doing – as if playing a game – she flung the door wide open and stepped through. The field. And nothing more than the field! Or rather, the meadow. Hovering above those tiny bluish flowers, an orange bee was buzzing lazily. Rina turned around, sighed, touched the polished handle, and pushed it shut. The door closed softly and... disappeared.

As far as the eye could see, an endless ocean of dry yellow sand, scorched by the ruthless sun, stretched in every direction. A desert without water, with no trace of hope – an infinite, cruel, sandy death. Rina stood rooted to the spot, unable to move, barely breathing. After a while, drained by the searing yellowness, she said in a broken voice:

“It worked...”

And immediately her head was filled with a swarm of nervous, panicking questions:

“Where am I? Did I make it? What is this? The World Number Two? The Planet of Sands? Yes, sands! Everywhere! But what now? What should I do? Where should I go?”

No one answered.

A plump gray lizard, frightened, darted in the wrong direction, scurried underfoot, and in a snap burrowed into a small dune, furiously kicking up sand with all four legs just inches from Rina's sneaker.

WORLD TWO – DESERT
03 – MIRRORED ECHO

Rina had never seen deserts before. She hadn't even been to the Oleshky Sands – Ukraine's “Little Sahara” – a ten-mile stretch of yellow sand wilderness. No, her entire experience was limited to the beaches of Zatoka – water parks, hotels, and fancy tourist trains running along the boardwalk. But a beach, no matter how wide, doesn't come close to a real desert. Even the smartest TVs can't convey the sheer shock of standing in the heart of blazing sands, feeling lost, forgotten, helpless against this terrifying grandeur and enormous power of wild nature.

Hot air gusts whipped Rina's hair, the sand rustled, singing a thin note beneath her feet, quick whirlwinds drew long patterns on the shifting slopes, and in the sky, a blinding sun beamed, rejoicing in its absolute, cloudless freedom.

Desert beauty is unforgettable, but what is annoying there is the total lack of roads. The sands never care how many people have passed through – they leave no trace. The footprints dissipate in a few blinks of an eye under the relentless wind. Only a bush of dry thorns, bristling with long white needles, stands alone near a dune, a faint reminder that somewhere out there, life still exists – vibrant landscapes with forests in a violet haze and blue rivers winding through flourishing valleys. But all that is now far away, beyond hundreds of miles of nothingness spreading toward shimmering horizons.

Rina stared into the world, her heart pounding with confusion: how would she, a weak, fragile girl, survive here in this immeasurable wasteland? Who would save her out there? And did anybody even know she was in this dead zone?

Before she had taken a single step, she already felt a crushing exhaustion and a flaming thirst. The sun tried to incinerate her skin. She slipped off her backpack, took out a big white handkerchief with blue stripes along the edges, and made a kind of hat with four thick knots in the corners. Her father used to make one just like it when working outdoors.

Of all her water supplies, there was just that single, insignificant, twelve-ounce bottle of Sprite, still cold. Seeing the tiny, miserable, microscopic vial, Rina felt a bitter resentment toward the old man Herx. If only he had said a single word to her last night – if he had even hinted that in the morning she'd find herself alone among the endless waves of scalding dunes – then Rina would have taken a whole case of soda, bought three massive two-liter bottles of mineral water near the subway, and packed her beach umbrella, her dark sunglasses, sunscreen, and a large floppy bucket hat – everything she could possibly need in a desert.

No, Herx didn't say a thing, didn't explain. This wasn't what she'd expected at all. Yes, of course, she was flying to the World of Sands. So what?! Tourists go to Egypt, for example – also a world of sands, by the way – but nobody abandons them in the middle of the Sahara Desert with no water, no shade, and no help whatsoever! Herx didn't warn her. Rina remembered how confidently he'd promised to accompany her everywhere at the first opportunity. She looked around again – no one! So where was all that “support and protection”?

“Of course he's not here!” she muttered irritably. “Ha! What would he be doing here anyway? He's a wizard, possibly rich, he's probably kicking back at his luxurious lakeside mansion in Switzerland right now!”

But whether you're mad at the old man or not, standing still on the baking surface waiting for hell knows what is, at best, stupid. And dangerous. She had to find someone – anyone! – for advice on a way out of this nightmare World Number Two. And flee this place as quickly as possible!

Only at that moment did she finally remember the gift. She threw her backpack off again and unzipped the long side pocket. Even before fully unfolding the map, she realized there were no familiar continents on it anymore. Instead, the entire surface showed a large yellow blotch outlined in black against a glossy white background. Rina spread the map out on the sand and stepped on the corner with her sneaker so it wouldn't blow away. And began studying the map inch by inch.

There, right in the center, was a childishly drawn little figure – a girl in a ridiculously short skirt. Right below that cartoon character were two Latin letters, “R” and “I”, and a bold dash between them.

“R–I. Yep, that's def me”, said Rina. And immediately, she felt frost in her stomach – in every direction from the funny girl on the map was a huge ocean of sand, colored in acid yellow. But from the girl's feet, a thin black line twisted away, leading first to a gray rectangle, then to a small purplish circle, and after it toward a green spot with a black ant depicted inside it. And a golden key above the ant.

“All right, that's something”, she said with a sigh of relief.

OK, though what did she find? The trail and the key? Sure, makes sense. But where exactly is that trail?

In the bottom corner was a small compass icon.

“Here – south, north, east. The path leads east. So, if I'm following it, I need to go straight east”.

She looked at her watch. Assuming the time here was the same as in Ukraine, it was early morning yet – six minutes past seven. The sun rises in the east – even toddlers know that. Well, there was no other way to determine the direction. (Her dad's professional compass was resting in the outer pocket of the backpack, but Rina was too stressed to remember it.)

She shoved the map back, shouldered the backpack, and set off across the soft sand, heading straight toward the sun. But after about an hour, she felt overwhelming fatigue, as if she had just unloaded a ton of bricks from an 18-wheeler. Fine, now what? There was nowhere to sit down. Who wants to try relaxing on a frying pan under the burning rays? So she had to trudge on.

Straining hard, she climbed the hump of a dune and saw an endless flat valley below – the sand lay smooth and even, glinting like paving stones on Kyiv squares. Ancient sand, compacted by time and constant winds, seemed leveled by human hands. Farther ahead, she saw something dark and angular, though it was impossible to tell what it was from this distance.

“Great, I forgot the binoculars!” Rina said in frustration, but dashing home for a second to grab her dad's optics was out of the question.

She began descending and nearly tumbled down the slope. It turned out that going downhill was no easier than climbing – the sand slid and flowed like a river under her shoes and there was nothing to grab onto.

In the end, fighting the treacherously slithering sand, she made it down to the valley. The surface was as firm as concrete, her sneakers left no footprints. For the first time, she felt some ease there, even smiled a little – compared to the sands, walking on this substance was fast and almost effortless.

Even though the dark spot was off to the side, beyond her planned path, she still headed there. Strange guesses raced through her mind, but one thought dominated them all: water. Despite her cracked lips and the salty sting in her mouth, Rina held herself back, refusing to give in to thirst and open the precious Sprite. She knew that if she twisted off that white plastic cap, even once, she wouldn't be able to stop – she would gulp it all down in half a second.

The dark spot grew with every step and soon it resolved into many jagged fragments. As she drew closer, it became clear that it wasn't a spot, but a dozen huge dark chunks – the remains of a collapsed cliff. Then she noticed something else – amid the rocks and stones, a rounded white construction appeared, with a big gaping hole in the middle like a predator's maw. Apparently, it was an entrance to something, perhaps to a big room. Rina looked at the rocks and white dome with doubts and fear, but also with an expectation – a hope that pushed her forward, drove her ahead, and gave her energy from nobody knows where. The rule of hope is “Not a single step back!” Even if it leads nowhere or straight to death – who cares? Hope is unstoppable.

At last, Rina reached the gray crags. They were far more massive than they'd appeared from afar. She ran her finger along the hot, weathered surface – prehistoric rock felt like slightly hardened dust ready to crumble at the slightest touch. Slowly, trying not to crunch the sand under her feet, she crept closer to the white walls and cautiously peered inside the entrance. No, it wasn't a cave or anything natural, there was a chamber inside, carved into the soft limestone rock. She entered. In the center of the hall was a round well – more like a shaft – a yawning, terrifyingly deep hole of inhuman scale. “Forty feet across”, she estimated.

The walls of the vertical tunnel were roughly hewn into dark layers of basalt, and around the perimeter lay dozens of big black boulders, nearly touching each other. Rina imagined for a moment a desert giant collecting these heavy stones from everywhere in the sands and then arranging them neatly in a circle to adorn his underground refuge. Perhaps that's exactly how it had happened a thousand years ago, but at the moment, Rina was concerned with two more important questions: A. “Is there water at the bottom?” and B. “If so, how to get it?”

She stood for a while, pondering and resting, even cooling off a bit, enjoying the chill rising from the depths, and then walked up to the pit and looked down. She was lucky – on the jagged wall of the mine, right on the side of the hall's entrance, narrow steps were carved: a staircase spiraling steeply into the darkness. This strange structure didn't match the colossal scale of the well – the steps were small, suitable maybe for toddlers or dwarves. Anyway, she firmly decided to go down.

Though not immediately, of course. Two opposite worries had to fight it out first – the fright of the depth and the panic of a lack of water. The panic was stronger, it was an easy winner. For a minute, Rina imagined herself in the park – she discovers a huge well there. Would she climb down the wet ledges, plunging into the hellish underworld? Of course not! She would never even try, she's not crazy. But! That's the park. The comfortable, cozy park. And what about here, in the desert? Here she had only one thought left in her mind: water.

No, she'd better say it like this: Water! Or even this way:

W A T E R ! ! !

A pit, a cave, a cannibal's camp, a tiger's lair, a tyrannosaur's nest – what the heck difference does it make? To her it all looked the same. She would never think about it, she would crawl into anything if there was even the slightest chance of getting half a glass of water there...

Predictably, the descent was awkwardly complicated. The stairs looked like somebody had carved them as a joke – crooked, absurd, and so clumsy it was even funny. The first step was high above the second one, while the third one was so small it wasn't clear why it existed at all, and immediately after that came not even a step, just some sharp protrusion. Each stair varied wildly in width, height, and tilt. Worse, some of them were just... missing – Rina had to drop onto her knee to reach the next foothold with the tip of her sneaker.

The only good thing was that with every yard down, the air grew more humid and cool, and the stones even colder – until they were almost frosty. On one of the widest ledges, she had to stop and pull her denim jacket from her backpack (she'd shoved it in an hour earlier when the sun was too unbearable). Brrr! Cold! The climate was like that of a freezer truck.

And below, at a terrifying depth, lay a moist, eternal gloom, lulled by perfect silence. The only sound now was Rina's strained breathing, accompanied by occasional drops of water falling from one step to another. Luckily, the staircase was gradually widening, but the bottom was still nowhere in sight.

On one of the steps, Rina found a small stone, picked it up, and dropped it down. She just let it go, and it fell.

“If it splashes, the bottom isn't far”, she said aloud to boost her courage. Suddenly, someone replied:

“raaf tnzeee...”

Rina jumped back, slipped, and nearly fell into the chasm. She grabbed the wall with both hands and froze. She stood there for a long time, barely breathing, listening, scanning the well. However, she saw no people, or animals, or imaginary little dwarves she had invented – nobody at all.

But a few minutes later, having composed herself somehow, she decided to speak first and asked in a half-whisper:

“Who are you?”

An invisible stranger promptly responded from the opposite, completely black side:

“ui raa oohhh...”

Rina waited for a moment and said:

“Hello!”

And immediately received a reply:

“ollehhh...”

Only then did she realize: it was an echo!

“Who are you?” she asked again, this time loudly and clearly.

“ui raa oohhh...” came confidently from nowhere.

“Ui, ra, ooh”. Rina smirked. “ui ra ooh... hoo... ar... iu... Who are you. Who are you just backwards!” she exclaimed in her mind.

“Are you an echo?” she said.

“uoke ne...” The echo replied joyfully.

“If you're really an echo, then why do you repeat everything the wrong way around?”

“dnu... ara...” came the response.

“Even the echoes here aren't normal”. Rina waved her hand hopelessly.

“lamr... ron...” The echo whispered into her ear.

Rina flinched, glanced down, glanced up – there was light above, though on the opposite side only empty, impenetrable darkness. The heavy, ink-black void below was getting on her nerves.

“So where's the bottom?” she asked.

“raa... een... zee... tee...” the echo replied confidently.

“Wait”, she thought, “What ‘zee-tee' and so on? I didn't say anything like that”.

She repeated her question louder:

“Where. Is. The. Bottom?!”

“raaaa!.. eeeen!.. zeeee!.. teeee!” the echo proclaimed insistently right in her face.

“Ra, een, zee, tee”, Rina muttered under her breath, tracing the letters on the damp wall with her finger. Then she tried to reverse the phrase – eet eez nee ar.

“It is near!” she shouted.

“raa... een... zee... teeee!” the echo yelled happily, confirming her guess.

“You're not an echo at all!” Rina thought. “Someone lives here and talks in this weird, silly way. They're just messing with me!”

However, she was wrong. In the deep stone well, century after century, a solitary Mirrored Echo lived. It spent long years alone, pondering life and everything in the world in dark silence. Over time, it became the wisest echo in the deserts.
