The Mirrored Echo resided at the bottom of this enormous pit, relaxing and waiting, wistfully dreaming of those rare travelers with whom he could chat as much as he wanted. He would listen to what was happening in the upper worlds and share his “hot news” from three thousand years ago. But the Echo couldn't speak on his own. Over his long life, he had learned to alter the reflections of words bouncing off the walls, but he couldn't produce his own sounds.

After many attempts to find out who was living there in the emptiness, Rina had to admit that there was no one else but an echo. At first, she was slightly disappointed, but then she realized that perhaps it was even better this way – echoes are not dangerous. Moreover, he knew human languages.

Soon, she managed to converse with him, which at least distracted her from the tension of the risky descent, although she couldn't learn anything valuable. The Echo played with Rina's words like wind does with dry leaves, spinning and tossing them this way and that, but it was a game, not a real conversation. Rina even got a little tired of the pointless chatter.

In the end, after all the grueling efforts – hooray! Victory! She reached the highly anticipated, precious bottom of the shaft!

At first, no water was found. Everything was covered in sticky mud, icy and gross to walk on. It was hard to believe, but by that time, thirst no longer tormented Rina. On the contrary, the air was too damp, the well was too wet, so the burning scrapes on her knees and elbows bothered her much more than the longing for water.

However, the moment she noticed a barely discernible gleam in the darkness – a spring trickling from a little crevice in the wall – she immediately felt an irresistible, painful urge to drink her fill and collect as much water as possible.

Fortunately, back in Kyiv, she had not forgotten to take a dozen spare plastic bags with her. Like many other things, “just in case”. She rummaged in her backpack, found them all, took them out, and in less than ten minutes, she had two transparent water balloons, rather hefty ones, perfectly round, each the size of a honeydew melon – both very cold and sweaty.

A new boy named Roman had taught her how to make such balloons from ordinary plastic bags for water fights. The secret was in the triple knot – a tricky thing indeed. If tied correctly, the water would never leak out. The boy had brought this ingenious invention to Ukraine precisely for games. But now, to Rina, the two wet, heavy spheres meant much more – survival.

In a desert, a water supply is worth more than pure gold. The long trek through the sands in the searing wind under the flaming sun taught Rina a lot. She had already come to know the real value of the smallest gulp of the most ordinary water.

After drinking it until she couldn't manage another sip, she rested on a soggy, black boulder – which had evidently tumbled down long ago, perhaps dropped from the clumsy fingers of the desert giant. Thoroughly chilled by the pervasive dampness, and after talking with the Echo a little more (though, despite employing all her skills, she learned little from him, as he, while rather intelligent, had no actual knowledge of what was happening in the outside world), she finally decided to head back to the surface. There was nothing more to do down below.

Of course, she had examined every inch of the walls, but found no hint of doors, tunnels, passages, or even burrows, except for a tiny hole, no wider than three inches, where the spring water drained away. Nothing else.

And so, having sloshed through the sickening bottom muck in her hopelessly soaked sneakers – just living the dream, as Zinaida Abramovna says – Rina commanded to herself:

“That's it, I'm done. Time to go!”

And she climbed onto the lowest step.

Strangely enough – though typical for the planets of the Near Worlds – the climb back up was much faster and easier than the descent.

Upon reaching the final steps, she waved to the Echo and bade him farewell in the most ceremonial tone she could manage:

“Goodbye, dear Echo. Peace out!” she said solemnly. “It was really fun cooking up this chat with you”.

The emotional Mirrored Echo immediately replied:

“Ui... vaaal...”

Rina reversed the phrase, smiled, blew him a kiss, and with three quick hops, leaped up into the hall, and ran outside.

After the penetrating chill of the well, the desert's heat felt pleasant for a minute.

But only for a minute...

04 – GLIMID'S SHADOW

Rina glanced at the sun. Still cheerful, though slightly spent, it hung overhead, just past its peak, about to begin its unhurried journey toward the long-desired sunset. Rina checked the direction and continued on her way. The flat sandstone plateau, so deceptively inviting and easy to walk on at first, was shamelessly draining her strength. The rocks surrounding the white hill were far behind and almost out of sight.

There wasn't a single landmark around, just the endless, monotonous expanse. As gamers would say, zero points of interest. A minute, two, five, ten, half an hour. Rina and time trudged at the exact same pace – like a dying snail. The surrounding panorama didn't change a bit. Though she kept walking, she seemed to make no progress at all, as if some evil magic held her in place.

Several times, Rina took out the map, trying to find the path's twists and turns on the actual terrain, but there were no turns, not even a crack or pebble on the sandy plain. A maddeningly smooth, perfectly ironed lifelessness.

But suddenly something flashed by – huge and dark. Rina halted. Fearfully, she looked around. Nothing. What was that? A mirage? An instant later, a wide, massive pall of shadow swept across the sand and covered her completely for a moment. She looked up – not a fleck in the sky. She glanced back – the shadow lay ten steps away, menacingly flexing its powerful wings. Yes, wings indeed! Rina traced the distinct outline of a giant bird with enormous wings spread in soaring, though there were no birds above, not even the tiniest one.

“More weirdness”, she said and approached. The shadow slid timidly a couple of yards to the side.

“How's that even possible? There's a shadow, but no bird!”

“I hate them!” the shadow declared in a hoarse voice. Clearly, it was speaking! Its sharp beak opened and closed in perfect sync with the words.

“Hate who?” Rina asked guardedly, looking at the shadow of the bird's head.

“I hate birds!” the shadow rasped. Its beak snapped shut on the final word. Rina glanced up again, but of course, there was nothing above. She asked:

“Who are you?”

“I'm Glimid's shadow”, the shadow hissed.

“And who's Glimid?”

“That's me!”

“But you just said you're Glimid's shadow!” Rina was surprised. “So, Glimid is a shadow, literally?”

“No! Glimid is not a shadow!” the shadow retorted indignantly, flapping its wings in anger. “I hate Glimid!”

“Things just keep getting weirder in this desert”, Rina thought, and then said aloud:

“Okay, let's put it this way, you're the shadow of the bird Glimid, right?”

The shadow did not reply. Rina waited and then continued:

“So, it turns out this Glimid lives without a shadow, and you are here without Glimid. Did I get that right?”

The stubborn shadow remained silent.

“Listen”, Rina addressed the bird's shadow wearily, “if you don't mind, let me step on you. To rest. I mean, to take a break from the heat for a minute”.

Again, no response. Quietly, Rina stepped inside the shadow and found herself enveloped in... in what? Well, not a real chill, to be honest, but at least the sand around her was darker, not that bright or sparkling. The next moment she looked up and saw a silhouette of an immense bird hovering above – not a bird actually, but something like a flat picture of the bird.

Trying to be polite, Rina continued the conversation and asked:

“Do you live alone in this desert?”

“I don't live! I fly!” the shadow creaked.

“Oh, yes, of course! Sorry, I misspoke. You fly. You came from a bird... And where is the bird itself now, where is Glimid?”

The shadow snorted, shook its head doggedly, and said:

“He's in Nrsktskr”.

“In Nrs... nk... krs...” Rina tried to repeat it, but failed. “And why do you hate him? Did you two have a fight?”

“A fight!” the shadow confirmed loudly.

“Ah! That explains it”. She nodded. “I got into a massive fight with this girl from the second floor a week ago. I was so mad I couldn't even look at her. I probably hated her even more than you hate your Glimid. But now I think it was stupid. And besides, you can't keep holding a grudge forever – sooner or later you need to fix it somehow, right? When I'm back home, I'll try to patch things up with that girl, you know?”

Glimid's shadow listened attentively, its beak opened to catch every word. Rina asked:

“By the way, dear shadow, do you happen to know what's round and bright purple out here in this desert?”

The shadow thought for a moment, then shook its head:

“I don't know!”

“That's a pity. I've been walking toward that ‘round-and-purple' thing all day. It's marked on my map as round and purple, but I still have no idea what it actually is. Isn't it funny to search for something you know nothing about?”

“Funny”, agreed the shadow.

“Well”, Rina thought regretfully, “these desert guys are pretty clueless. You can't get anything useful out of them”.

After staying for a few more minutes, she thanked the shadow for the... for what? For the shade? But there was not a real thing above that could provide real shade. So, Rina paused for a second and yes, thanked the shadow for the shadow – just another oddity in the sands.

Then she stepped out, and when she looked up at the sky again, the picture of the bird was nowhere to be seen. But at that moment, Glimid's shadow let out a harsh, crow-like screech and suddenly lunged toward Rina's feet. Startled, Rina even jumped aside.

“What happened?!”

“That one! That one!” the shadow shrieked, flapping its wings.

“Who's ‘that one'? What are you talking about?”

She looked down and understood everything – Glimid's shadow was attacking Rina's own shadow.

“Stop it! Don't! That's my shadow!” she protested. “It's been with me for years and has never done anything to hurt you!”

However, Glimid's shadow was extremely aggressive, it hissed, rasped, opened its hooked beak, and raised a ruff of feathers on its nape. Rina was a bit frightened but remembered that a shadow couldn't cause any harm, so she waved her hands at it, just like old villagers chase away a stubborn chicken:

“Shoo! Shoo! Get out! Scat! Go away, shoo!”

Glimid's shadow, confused, paused its attack for a second.

“You're so mean! Get away from me!” Rina was a bit angry. “My shadow wasn't bothering you. It's small and fragile! Why are you bullying it?”

“I hate it!” croaked Glimid's shadow and snapped its beak loudly.

“Oh yes, you hate everyone”. Rina shook her head. “You're so annoying. You know what, I really don't want to be friends with you. Listen, there's this obnoxious guy in my neighborhood, he's exactly like you – he's rude and likes to bully. Everyone avoids him, and so do I. Please go away”.

Glimid's shadow shook its head, searching for the right word. Then it forcefully flapped its mighty wings, let out the irritated cry of a large bird, and flew off, gliding over the sand, quickly gaining speed. Soon, it disappeared into the yellow expanse.

“No wonder Glimid got rid of his shadow”, Rina said, watching it go. “Better to be alone than with someone so mean!”

She looked at her own shadow and waved to it warmly. The shadow immediately waved back joyfully.

“And my shadow... is kind!”

05 – SKRTSTN

Another two hours – perhaps more – had passed. Rina felt utterly depleted by the intolerable heat and overwhelming light. Thirst tormented her, even though all that remained of her “water reserves” in the backpack was a shrunken, half-empty plastic bag and the still unopened, nearly boiling little bottle of soda.

The terrain had changed again – dunes were back. Mostly, they came in two types, many were young, made of fluffy, light dust that the hot wind easily lifted into the air. Others were old – apparently from the age of dinosaurs – and almost as solid as the valley floor Rina had crossed earlier.

Some of these hardened dunes were massive and looked like mountains, though much smaller than the mining slag heaps back home in Donbas.

But in fact, there was also a third type – truly primeval ones. Most of them had long since crumbled, centuries of sandstorms had eroded them into bizarre rock formations that looked like scattered idols of unknown tribes – the strangest, almost artistic shapes. Here and there, the rocks cast welcoming shadows, and Rina moved in zigzags now, trying to cross from monolith to monolith, to hide from the sun if only for a couple of seconds.

At last, her strength was so spent that she was ready to collapse in the shade of any one of these stone “statues” and sleep right there on the ground. Which is what she ended up doing – she picked a massive rock shaped like a giant ice cream cone with the top licked off and dropped down next to it onto the sand.

She pulled off her backpack – unbelievably heavy now, as if filled with mercury – and took out the map, unfolded it on the ground. The drawing had changed beyond recognition. The view had zoomed in, no longer showing the whole desert as before, but only a small part of it, and the little figure – the girl in the skirt – was once again right in the center, and a bunch of new symbols and captions had appeared in tiny, almost unreadable letters, which, on closer inspection, looked vaguely like ancient Slavonic. Rina had never even tried to read such texts, though her grandfather was deeply into the subject – still, she couldn't make out a single word.

At the same time, the updated map showed clearly that Rina had been walking northeast toward the green spot now ringed by a black outline, and along with the black ant, it had moved to the top-right corner. The once-small purple circle had become much larger and had jumped to a slightly higher place than before. On the line between the circle and the girl figure, a small gray snake with yellow eyes appeared, drawn in a precise, professional manner.

“Don't tell me I'll have to deal with snakes too!” Rina said with a shiver.

After sitting for a while, she folded the map and tried to stand up. She couldn't. Her legs were swollen, thick as a baby elephant's, and refused to obey.

“No use, I need some rest”, she decided, and sprawled weakly on the valley floor.

In truth, going any further in her condition would have been madness. Rina relaxed. She wanted to reflect on everything that had happened during the day, but her thoughts kept breaking off and tangling. Soon she got hold of herself and sat up with some effort, trying to find a more or less comfortable position, then pulled the last of the water out of her backpack. Plus a little box of crackers, and a fifty-gram package of now-melted Buche de Chevre cheese – once her favorite treat.

Why “once”? Because although only ten hours had passed since fate and necessity had pushed her through the door in the pea field, to Rina it felt like an era, a whole century. No doubt – this long, endless day was so far the hardest time of her life.

She reluctantly chewed a piece of heat-boiled, oddly repulsive cheese, ate half a cracker – it had lost all its flavor completely – and took three deep gulps of very warm, but still divinely delicious water. Only water mattered now. Water, and obviously sleep.

The sun, never bothering to cool off even a little, hung a bit closer to the horizon, so the shadows grew thicker, stretching longer across the ground from dunes, rocks, and even boulders. New smells emerged, and much to the delight of the desert's inhabitants, timid though noticeable waves of pre-dusk freshness began drifting in from nowhere.

After the “snack”, Rina lay down, turned onto her back, and looked at the sky – bright but no longer angry... Well, still angry, of course, but not as cruel as it had been just an hour earlier. Gradually, the sky tried to return to its regular, nearly blue hue, though not a single cloud – no matter how tiny – graced the vastness.

Then everything turned dark red. Not because the sun had set, but because Rina closed her eyes and had neither the strength nor the will to open them again. She heard indistinct voices somewhere nearby. Someone touched her forehead, or maybe it was just a breeze, but she was indifferent to everything.

She didn't want to move, didn't attempt to think. A pleasant heaviness spread through her body, a total dissolution of tension. She felt as if she were not lying on this coarse, prickly sandstone but soaring in a vigorous stream of ocean air like a snow-white albatross – lightly, effortlessly, not even moving her wings.

Sleep kept seizing her mind more and more insistently, until in the next scene of her dream she saw the familiar foamy sea and a long beach wet from salty spray. There, on a large log washed ashore by last year's huge storm, sat Herx, smiling mischievously. As always. He winked with both eyes like a little kid and said:

“See? Didn't I tell you everything would be fine? And you – why'd you doubt me?”

Rina slept...

A sharp young voice was stubbornly calling someone by a strange name – nothing but vowels. Rina was puzzled by the new language and... woke up. She opened her eyes and saw the blueness.

Deep black-blue.

The universe was exactly as astronauts see it from their spaceships – endless, monumental, and supernaturally multi-dimensional. Rina lay on her back behind the flat-topped pyramid, and before her eyes, in the utterly cloudless violet-coal sky, bright, unknown, rainbow-colored constellations shimmered like strewn shards of precious stones, while it seemed that the desert had vanished forever – the darkness had swallowed it completely – everything turned black: sands, dunes, and even the rocks.

Rina was mesmerized by the spectacular parade of stars, she stared into the gelid cosmic void, into that infinity, and all at once felt like the planet was a massive spaceship hurtling forward through the limitless depth of the colossal beyond.

And by some coincidence, Rina was at the very front, on the prow of the giant planetary cruiser. Ahead – the unbounded deep. Black nothingness, filled only with rare, unbelievably distant specks of light. She was alone there. She sensed and measured the profundity of the nothing – but the nothing was immeasurable.

Breathless with awe, eyes wide, she peered into the abyss of space, into this mind-ripping immensity, and for the first time in her life, she saw it in its full, wordless truth. She lay still, looking into the black ether and trembling with the emotions flooding her soul...

Or maybe she was shivering because of the desert's night cold? Rina shifted her focus from the beauty above, propped herself up on one elbow, and looked around. In the distance, outlined against the soft dark velvet, were the rugged shapes of long dunes and fantastical rock creations – ruins of the ancient world. She stood up. Cold. It was too cold. Freezing! The Arctic!

“Ughhh!” she exclaimed, her body shuddering. “Well, this is something! So much for the desert – it's like a glacier now! I'm telling you, nothing here is normal. Roasted by day, ice-cold at night! Why? Why?”

And really – who the hell came up with this flame-ice-flame seesaw? Clearly, deserts are empty because they are so impossible to survive in – equally under the sun or the moon. And yet, someone had endured. Rina heard a quiet, rustling, careful movement. She scanned the place but saw only mica glinting in the sand.

The good news – her legs didn't hurt anymore. Almost. She felt what some people call “a second wind” – new energy. She was ready to continue her trek right now, though in this black-on-black darkness, it would be too easy to lose her way.

Then she remembered her favorite flashlight and pulled it out (why she hadn't tried to use it back in the well remained a mystery). The tiny, handy gadget sat snugly in its special inner backpack pocket. That winter, Grandpa-2 had brought it from Warsaw, it was a programmable Japanese flashlight shaped like a pen. On its side was a colorful little screen with mode selection: music, calendar, thermometer, step counter, Bluetooth, radio... – you name it. Instead of a bulb, it had a glass bead. When turned on, the bead shot a narrow yellowish-green beam like a real alien-movie death-light – blindingly strong. And a single good battery could last half the night.

Rina stepped a few yards from the rock, pressed the button, and swept the ray around. Dunes, old hills, another hill, sand, backpack, one more dune, a bush, a snake, a rock.

“Wha-at?!” she choked and moved the beam back.

Yes! A snake! A large glossy gray snake lay on the sandy ridge of a narrow little dune just twenty paces away, not daring to approach or flee, its round eyes shining with a cat-like glow. The snake turned its head, squinting from the bright light, much like a kitten, indeed. But then, quite unexpectedly, it opened its mouth and said, rather loudly:

“Sknn! Shknn!”

Rina couldn't believe her ears. Snakes don't talk! Everyone knows that. But facts are stubborn critters – the snake said “shknn”.

“Hello”, Rina croaked, almost coughing, and cautiously took a step back, keeping the light on the snake's face. “Who are you?” she asked, unable to suppress the question, which was slightly bad manners.

“Skrt! Stn!” the snake replied, but didn't budge.

“So, is it Skrt or Stn?” Rina asked, chuckling inwardly – no one in this desert was normal. Though she couldn't yet say exactly to what extent.

“Skrtstn! Skrtstn!” the snake rasped irritably.

“A-ah! Thanks, I got it now – Skrtstn. Nice to meet you, Skrtstn. My name is Rina... But if that's hard to pronounce, I don't know, call me Reana or Rayna. It's fine. I mean, I don't really like those names – I'm Rina”.

“Rnn!” the snake answered.

Rina shrugged.

“Okay, suit yourself. I'm fine with Rnn too”.

Suddenly, Rina advanced toward the snake, and now she saw her in full detail. The snake was really big – five feet, maybe more – but Rina wasn't afraid. Dad had taught her two things, first, not to panic, and second – how to tell the venomous ones from the harmless ones. Skrtstn was the latter.

The snake moved her big, round head left and right, the tip of her tail was dusted with sand. The snake tilted her head this way and that, blinking like... yes, like a cat. And on her glossy back was a barely visible silvery pattern that looked like a long phrase in Arabic script.

For a moment, Rina was struck by the fact that the little dune the snake sat on was one of those hardened ancient mounds whose sand had long ago turned to solid rock, but the snake's tail vanished into it. Still, she didn't pay much attention to the oddity. What worried her most was that the snake might get scared and slither off into the desert, leaving her with no one to ask about the words and symbols on the map.

Fortunately, the gray snake sat calmly and, by the look of it, wasn't going anywhere. And just behind her, in the beam of the flashlight, Rina spotted a dried-out, four-foot-high leafless desert tree and a round, white, fist-sized stone, lying in a hollow at the opposite edge of the dune. Maybe someone had marked the spot that way.

But when Rina tried to move closer by another step, the snake got nervous, twitched here and there like she was stuck, and hissed, yes, cat-like too. Only then did Rina take a good look and understand the truth: the snake's tail wasn't a tail at all, but more like a root, the same kind of gray-brown roots that the desert wind reveals on dried-out bushes.

Shining the flashlight closer, she noticed that near the rock's surface, the root-tail split into three branches – like thick rusty wires – that went straight down into the dune's body. Rina froze mid-action. Her flashlight beam stopped moving, she stood staring at the snake's tail, incapable of looking away.

Eventually, the snake had enough of that and asked:

“Hsss Thrrr?”

Rina understood it was a question, but the desert language resembled no other. Just in case, she decided to calm the snake down – she felt sorry for the poor creature, which was unable to hide from the sun or escape its enemies.

“Don't be afraid, please. I won't touch you”, Rina said softly, trying to sound as gentle as possible. “If you want, I will never take another step toward you”. The snake didn't respond, but calmed down, waiting.

Rina asked, “Do you speak human?”

“Mmm!” the snake hummed through her nose, then immediately repeated the question:

“Hsss Thrrr?”

Rina shook her head. “I don't understand!”

The snake tensed, opened her mouth wider, and carefully enunciated the phrase syllable by syllable:

“hhho eesss the rrr?”

Rina played the phrase in her head five or six times and finally managed to understand: the snake was asking “Who is there?” Not a proper question, of course, but perhaps snakes just lack words, especially in Rina's current language. Thus, logically, the question could mean something like “Why are you here?” or even closer to the situation – just “Who are you?”

“Who am I?” Rina was amused by the dialogue. “I'm a human, a girl! My name is Rina, as I said. I came to your world to...” she stopped – was it safe to tell everything to the first creature she met? She thought that Herx wouldn't be too thrilled if she did.

“I came to... explore your beautiful country. I'm just a tourist enjoying the local attractions”, she said – the stilted words felt incredibly clumsy and artificial. She winced at how much she sounded like she was in a cheesy spy show.

The snake didn't answer, lying there thinking. Then she nodded her gray head and said confidently, pronouncing each syllable one by one:

“Srchn the pr-pl trtzzz”.

Rina applied the newly discovered, successful method, replaying the phrase in her head for a minute or so, and yes, after the fifth attempt she understood everything except the last word, “search the purple...” – the purple something.

“Exactly!” she exclaimed, jumping nearly half a meter in the air. “Search – it's true! I'm looking for the purple one! The one drawn on my map”.

She ran to her backpack, pulled out the map, unfolded it, and showed it to the snake. The talking snake studied the yellow rectangle with numerous symbols on it for a long time, then tilted her head and muttered:

“Trtzzz!”

“What?” Rina asked and, completely forgetting caution, brought her ear to within three inches of the snake's nose.

The snake sighed impatiently, shook her head, and slowly repeated, trying her hardest:

“Terr tezz!”

“Ter tez... Teer tez… Tor tez... Tor tes… Tor tose… Tortoise!” Rina shouted. “This purple circle is the Purple Tortoise!”

The snake bobbed her head and seemed to smile.

“Can you imagine?” Rina continued enthusiastically, switching to a more relaxed tone. “I've been walking all day looking for a purple circle drawn on the map, with absolutely no idea what it actually was. Very funny, right? I kept thinking, if I run into someone, how do I even ask about it? ‘Excuse me, Sir, have you seen an unknown purple blob?'“ She laughed. “But now I have a real name – the Purple Tortoise. And that's it! Everyone will show me the direction!”

Rina paused for a second.

“And you, dear snake Skrtstn, could you by any chance tell me how to find this Purple Tortoise?”

The snake replied firmly and confidently:

“Blk! Sssnd! Srch blk sssnd!”

“Blk snd, black... snd... black sand. Srch black sand. Search for the black sand!” This time Rina translated into human easily, but decided to clarify it:

“And where is that black sand?”

The snake raised her head and pointed her nose toward the sky. Rina looked up too.

“Jel ou Str! Te jel ou str!”

“Jel ou... jel-ou… jel-ow... jellow. Yellow? Yes, yellow! Yellow Star! I need to go toward the Yellow Star!” Rina was excited.

The snake dipped her head affirmatively and, stretching out to her full length, lay down on the sandy rock, chin on the dune.

“Tired, probably”, Rina thought. She stepped aside, looked into the pitch-black sky.

The Star was found immediately. Although countless stars shone overhead, and some were yellow, there was only one so large, so amber. Rina glanced at the snake – she was resting on the rock and seemed to be waiting for something. So Rina stepped closer and, as best she could – in the most respectful words – she thanked the snake, addressing her as the Kind Motionless Snake Skrtstn.

After that, she remembered something and ran to her backpack again. Never hesitating, she snatched out two vanilla crackers from the crinkly box and also broke off a piece of cheese, then returned to the snake and carefully, almost with a bow, placed these gems right before the snake's eyes. Skrtstn sniffed the crackers and cheese with great interest and seemed pleased. But she didn't eat any.

Instead, she pondered for a moment, clicked her mouth, and asked:

“Wnt wtrrr?”

Rina understood the question instantly, without any translation. She was ready to shout, “Yes! I do! I want water! Very much!” But she restrained herself sharply.

“How can I take water from her?” she thought in horror. “This snake sits here all day long right in the terrible heat under the sun and, like everyone else, suffers from thirst”.

The snake understood Rina's uncertainty and said:

“Mch wtrrr! Vrrr mch wtrrr!”

Rina heard but didn't believe – how could a snake, practically chained to the rock, have much water? Even very much water? But Skrtstn was speaking the truth. Not waiting for confirmation, the snake started to extract the desert's main treasure – she tightly pressed her lips together and immediately began to inflate herself. Soon Skrtstn looked just like an inflatable rubber toy in a children's store.

And then Rina witnessed a real miracle: a small, apparently dead and completely dried-up tree standing behind the snake stirred its branches, and at its very top, a narrow, almost indistinguishable bud quickly opened into a flower, very similar to forest thin-leaved peonies – Aunt Maria had the same ones at her house. The petals of the desert flower were gray, like the tree itself and everything around, but inside it was pink with small white speckles.

The more the flower opened, the more clearly it took the shape of a bowl, similar to an Asian piyala, and suddenly, right from this marvelous living vessel, like a little fountain in an old city park, water began to pour, streaming and babbling! Much, very much water! A whole brook! Rina was awestruck, amazed, and enchanted, watching the water running down the dune, carrying tiny pieces of old thorns, grains, specks, and other debris. The water hurried along the ground, spreading into a dark, lovely glinting pool on the sandstone floor.

“Drnk frm flwr! Drnk frm flwr!” the snake said loudly.

“Drink from the flower”, Rina repeated in a dull, hypnotic voice, but she snapped out of it a second later. Drink means drink!

She drank her fill, and then some – two and a half fills over. The water was warm, exquisitely clear, and magically delicious. Yet the stream didn't diminish. Rina was so happy – she almost sang! Eventually, she got so ecstatic and playful that she scooped up water with both hands and poured it on the dune, the sand, the tree, everywhere! Of course, she accidentally splattered herself too, covering her sleeves and sundress in dark stains, her face and hands all wet, but she didn't feel cold, she laughed and wanted to waltz! She even splashed a little on the snake – just in case.

The snake also celebrated the pouring water – she drank some, and had as much fun as she could. In the end, Rina remembered (almost forgot!) the plastic bags – giggling, she bolted to her backpack and, rejoicing and humming a merry tune, she started filling them one after another, quickly turning thin, rustling bags into beautifully round, hefty balloons. The snake understood what the girl was doing and also danced on her dune coiling and weaving elegantly with much delight!

Finally, it was time to say goodbye.

“Dear Skrtstn!” Rina stroked the snake's neck with her fingertips. “You are the best, the kindest snake in the entire desert! I will never ever forget how much you did for me!” In response, the snake gently clicked her mouth, nodded, and affectionately rubbed her cheek against Rina's hand...

Soon, Rina was far from that dune, from the wonderful Motionless Snake Skrtstn and her miraculous little tree. The backpack had become noticeably heavier, but the weight was pleasant. The gorgeous Yellow Star impishly winked high in the sky and led Rina farther and farther into the vast, sparkling valley, toward the unknown but so promising Black Sands.

06 – BLACK SANDS

Walking became harder. The sandstone and fossilized crust had imperceptibly turned into a dusty cushion, Rina's sneakers were sinking deeper and deeper, and each step now felt like hard labor. The night had grown so dark that the ground and dunes were barely visible under the moonless sky. Just like before, Rina moved in zigzags from one hill to another, but now for a different purpose – the dunes were the only landmarks in this sooty darkness.

The desert kept changing. On the slopes of the ancient hills, she could barely make out some gray plants, although even in this impenetrable gloom it was clear they were nothing more than dry thorns.

Something rang above – a thin, glassy chime. Like tiny beads spilled by a clumsy hand onto marble. Rina looked up and saw a hundred pale-violet lights swirling in a tight flock, chasing one another in an eternal springtime game.

“Fireflies!” Rina exclaimed and stopped.

These were the desert Crystal Fireflies, as the great poet Albert Kessivinen, who had visited these lands twenty years before Rina's journey, called them in his famous book. Rina was incredibly lucky: such lights were rare, appearing only once a year along the borders of the Black Sands and even then only for about two weeks right after the Little Winds season. Then they would burrow into the sand along the shores of the long-dry lake Lemmi-Nenni, dig down to the moist layers deep below, and remain hidden there until the next year.

By evening, the fireflies had split into pairs and, as always, played their favorite game of tag – bumping into each other in midair. With every successful touch came a faint, barely audible crystal jingle. The charmed swarm spun in blissful circles over the dark dunes. A breeze stirred, and the fireflies, afraid of scattering, dipped lower – some fluttering now right above Rina's hair.

She reached out and tried to catch one of the tiny lights, which had lingered for a moment. She really wanted to examine it up close. Rina had heard many stories about fireflies but had never held one in her hand and didn't quite know what part of them was glowing – their chest or their belly.

But the firefly, noticing her hand, got spooked, darted into the crowd of its playing pals, and began knocking frantically into them with all its minuscule might, setting off a series of desperate cascades of glassy chimes. The fireflies stopped, listened, and then saw Rina standing right beneath them. Who knows what they mistook her for, but they panicked, rose quickly, and soared high into the air. A cool night gust caught the sparkling little guys and carried them off toward the pointed sandy hills. Rina stood for a few minutes, watching the lights fade.

Then, hearing the tearful whining of her tired legs, she decided to sit for a bit – to rest before dawn, before the chilly morning turned into another blazing day. She sank down onto the sand, leaned her backpack against a solid chunk of ancient rock, and closed her eyes.

Her sadness wasn't about the ‘fireflies little lights', no, it was the loneliness – completely alone in this enormous, unwelcoming world of emptiness. She thought of her sister Nina, her Mom, Aunt Vera, her Grandpa and Grandpa-2, both her Grandmas, all her friends – she even remembered her neighbors, then Nurse Kateryna and Doctor Bulah, and a few teachers, and one boy. She thought she really should have left a note for her mom, at least three lines.

But her imagination immediately pictured her mother shut away in her room with a crumpled notebook page in her hand, reading it for the hundredth time, understanding nothing, and crying, crying, crying... Rina felt a sharp pang for Mom, her sister, and her aunt. And for Svetka, of course – for everyone, and yes, for herself too.

“I'll be back soon”, she whispered to the Yellow Star. “I'll find Nina and come straight home with her!”

A glistening tear could no longer be held back, it jumped off Rina's eyelash onto her cheek and rolled down. Rina looked into the cold darkness.

Suddenly, a smile lit up her face – she imagined herself with her sister, both bursting into the house laughing with joy, riding up in the elevator, ringing the doorbell with their secret signal – “Bzzz-Bzzz! Bz-Bz-Bz! Bzzz-Bzzz!” Out comes their mother, suspecting nothing, wiping her hands on her apron, in a cloud of dumpling aromas, her eyes wide with amazement, her face glowing with mirth... The rest was a blur, but the feeling was incredibly pleasant…

Then everyone is in the living room, the tabletop is loaded with treats. Grandpa is popping champagne, Mom is cutting the cake into thick triangles – a real celebration! Bigger than New Year's! All around – relatives and best friends, everyone's laughing, shouting, chattering – joy and sparkle in their eyes.

Finally, the whole gathering quiets down – they want to hear the girls' stories about their incredible, fantastic adventures. Rina, with a mysterious wink, pulls out her magical backpack and shows off the unseen wonders from the six worlds. The guests gasp, leaning in, curious but cautious, touching the treasures with the tips of their fingers.

Then a boy in a white blazer with gold buttons steps forward and says:

“Don't go left”.

“Don't what?” Rina asked, confused. “Where did this boy come from?”

But the boy frowned, giving her a hard look, then he shook his blond head, and repeated in a stern voice:

“Don't. Go. Left! Go right, no matter what!”

“Wait, seriously? Is that your final warning? And what if I just don't want to?” Rina said playfully, with childish defiance in her voice. “What if I go left?”

The boy didn't answer – he nodded, spun around sharply, and walked off. Instantly, she felt cold – a creeping unease.

“Who was that boy?” Rina turned to ask her mother – but Mom was gone. A strange red-bearded man stood behind the girls and tried to touch their shoulders with his long bony hands. Rina flinched violently and... woke up.

Oh! It was just a dream! She'd been sleeping a while, sitting with her backpack still on – not the most comfortable position, actually. She had to spend some time rubbing her numb neck pretty vigorously...

In less than half an hour, the sun would rise from behind the lumpy indigo horizon, and the heat would return. But for now, it was cool. No wind. Low above the ground, long, thin ribbons of morning fog slithered and swayed. A small orange bird fluttered past with a nervous chirp. Probably a titmouse. Rina followed its flight until the birdie shrank to a barely distinguishable speck.

“All right, but why can't I go left?” she mumbled, still puzzled by the strange dream.

She took some food from her backpack and had breakfast – if only those crumbs could be called that – and drank some cold water from the plastic balloon, each gulp smelling like meadows. Fine, it was time to set off again. The sky was whitening, the guiding Yellow Star dissolved into the sunlight until the next evening, but Rina saw her own tracks in the sand and had no trouble finding the proper direction.

In about an hour, she reached a long, streaked dune stretching across the world from edge to edge – or so it seemed. The dune was abnormally tall with dangerously steep, sliding slopes. Luckily, the newborn sun hadn't yet heated the air. Surprisingly, despite the awkward sleeping position, Rina had gotten a decent rest, and though her neck still ached and her legs felt a bit off, overall her body held a good store of energy. Sleep had replenished her like a gas station refuels a car.

After some considerable effort – though without exhaustion – she climbed to the summit, and from there she saw an endless flat plain, spreading out before her eyes. The land was mostly identical to the one she'd crossed the day before. But this new plateau looked more cheerful – splattered across with dark shapeless spots, as if a naughty toddler, left alone in the house, had gotten hold of a paintbrush and sprayed black acrylic over the yellow hardwood floor.

“The Black Sands at last!” Rina congratulated herself and started heading downhill.

Up close, the sand wasn't really black – more of a dark gray with a slight brownish tint. “The color of a sun-tanned mouse”, she said with a laugh. But in fact, the color didn't matter – after yesterday's endless yellow, the “Black Sands” name fit this place perfectly. Beneath Rina's feet, the thin layer of gray granules, scattered by the wind over a hard golden canvas, crunched like late winter ice. Walking was surprisingly easy.

The sun finally woke up completely and was beginning to sting. To the four horizons – a dappled, speckled, flawlessly flat desert. No bumps, no dips. And of course, no shade, no protection from the white flame of the powerful star. Rina looked around and her shoulders drooped – another trial by cosmic fire lay ahead, the blinding light, the hot wind, the unbearable heat...

An hour passed, and the desert changed again – the sand layer got thicker with every step, and very soon her sneakers began sinking again into the hot, pliant mass.

“What if this isn't the Desert Planet at all?” A silly thought crossed her mind. “What if it's just Mars?”

But the new theory faded in no time – Rina spotted a trail.

She came closer and was shocked. At first glance, the prints looked like tracks made by giant ten-meter-long caterpillars, or more likely as if a large tractor had dragged an oil pipe or perhaps a heavy log behind it. Rina forgot everything and hurried along the trail without thinking about possible danger. Five hundred meters later, the trail unexpectedly split in two – one veered left, the other right. Rina stopped, thinking. On both sides – nothing but dark sand. Where to go? And then she remembered the serious boy in the blazer.

“And what if he's another of Herx's students?” she reasoned. “No, Yunni was as calm as stones, but this one is way too intense...”

Of course, she wasn't sure of anything, and it was unlikely that she'd dreamed of someone from Herx's entourage but hadn't seen Herx himself. However, after staring at the diverging grooves and detecting no real difference between them, she followed the right-side trail – just in case, as the boy had advised.

The track first curved, but then straightened out perfectly, as if unknown engineers had laid it with the utmost precision. Rina stepped into the middle line, the one that was deeper – supposedly made by the “log”. And yes, her pace quickened instantly – the sand was shallow there.

“If it really was a tractor or some other machine”, she thought with hope, “that means there were people in the desert. Recently”.

Although, just as easily, the tracks might have come from an unknown desert beast. Or a dragon, maybe. A dinosaur. Rina imagined a green brontosaurus dragging its thirty-foot tail through the sands.

“Well, okay, a big animal isn't so bad”, she said out loud, trying to sound as convincing as possible, as if addressing an imaginary dino, and her own voice somehow reassured her. “A big monster is fine. I'll see it from afar and hide...”

07 – OLUVILA

However, the dark valley was not perfectly flat as it had pretended to be upon first meeting. Barely two hours passed before the strange trail turned to the right again and began a long descent into a rather wide gorge, totally dry and invisible from afar but steadily deepening every minute. Rina grew alert, stopping often to listen to every sound that reached her. But she heard neither the rumble of a tractor nor the roar of an animal – nothing except the whisper of sand under occasional gusts of wind.

Another hour of that slow, cautious trekking passed, and the chasm deepened so much that the horizon disappeared entirely. On both sides, the path was flanked by light-gray stone walls. But soon the canyon widened and deepened even more, and two hundred yards farther it made a sharp turn to the left.

Still vigilant as ever, Rina crept forward, quietly peered around the corner, and... froze, totally stunned by an incredible sight – just a few dozen paces beyond the bend, in a round sandy clearing, a giant tortoise with a purple shell was sleeping. The tortoise was enormous – of phenomenal proportions – almost the size of the twelve-seater minibuses Rina and Nina usually took to the Darnitsa market.

Having had quite enough of staring, Rina suddenly felt an overwhelming joy at her first great victory. An explosion of elation! Right in front of her was that very Purple Tortoise – the mystical purple circle on the map she had traveled toward for so long, so hard, and so stubbornly! All fatigue vanished in an instant! She felt like dancing, screaming, and jumping!

But calming herself a bit and trying not to let her zipper pulls jingle, she took the priceless map from her backpack. As expected, the magical image had changed again. Now the desert was depicted in yellow-gray, and the purple circle was right in the center. Naturally, next to the circle was the funny little figure in a skirt – yes, a skirt and a girl, as always. The black line led upward to the large green blotch, as before, but inside the green, three colored arcs radiated from one point. And closer to the edge stood the same black ant with a small key above its head.

To the left of the ant appeared a blue square, and on the right – a dark triangle with two words in tiny gothic letters. Rina studied them closely and suddenly read the full inscription:

LA FOURMILIERE

Fourmiliere. Yes, La Fourmiliere! Rina knew the word from the movie Antz. She had watched it in French class at school. Twice. La Fourmiliere – the Ant Hill.

“The Ant Hill! That's right! Here's the tortoise, and she'll help me find the ant”.

Taking a deep breath to steady her nerves, Rina crept into the clearing and tiptoed closer until she stood nose to nose with the peacefully dozing Purple Tortoise. A smile rested on its enormous face, its eyes squeezed shut.

“How sad! I have to wake her up. But what else can I do? I'm in a hurry!”

Yes, of course, Rina understood that disturbing sleeping... uh... creatures wasn't right, because the Tortoise might be old or even sick – as the elderly often are. Perhaps at this moment she was enjoying a pleasant dream.

Rina also loved her dreams and hated her German alarm clock, which mercilessly woke her up on school mornings with unbearable beeps – “peep-peep-peep!” To avoid hearing those nasty squeaks, she tried to wake up early to deactivate the alarm by smugly smacking the shiny switch to “Aus”… In short, the big purple elder was a truly pitiable sight! But fate decreed that – whether she liked it or not – the Tortoise was part of this entangled and extremely dangerous business.

Sighing, Rina gently tapped the hard shell three times with her fingernail:

knock knock knock

The Tortoise didn't stir. Rina tapped again, much harder:

ka-knock! ka-knock! ka-knock!

No reaction. Still sleeping! Well, she picked up a hefty, egg-shaped pebble from the ground and tapped the purple shell again:

Ka-KNOCK!!! Ka-KNOCK!!! Ka-KNOCK!!!

The Tortoise twitched her nose but still didn't wake up. After several minutes of varied tapping and even punching, not too hard though, it became clear that this species of desert animal had an incredible capacity for sleep. You had to admire such powerful serenity! There was nothing to do but look for a more effective way to wake ‘the creature'. Pondering, Rina circled around the tortoise:

“She's really strange. Too round, maybe. Or oval-round. As if she'd been turned on a lathe – just the nose sticking out. And the tail. And heels... What? Heels?”

A mischievous idea struck her.

She rummaged in her backpack and pulled out her brush-pen that she'd recently bought on Rozetka – a special pen with a firmly attached stiff watercolor brush. Or maybe it wasn't for watercolors. But it was still a good brush anyway.

Rina approached and tickled a large pink heel with the brush. At that same instant, the Tortoise sneezed. Rina couldn't contain her glee! She ran around to the other side, looked – and her shoulders slumped in despair: the Tortoise was sneezing in her sleep.

“Wild! How can anyone sleep like that?!” Rina exclaimed loudly and went back to tickle the tortoise's heels again.

The poor creature kept sneezing furiously, and muttered something completely unintelligible – and very grumpy, by the way. She thrashed and kicked out all her legs, turned her head to all sides, twitched her tail, and snorted quite menacingly. But! She had no intention of waking up. So Rina approached the front, listened to the tortoise's breathing, and then, unexpectedly, tickled the reptile's huge lilac nose with the brush. And immediately the Tortoise burst into a squeaky giggle:

“He-he-he-he-he-he!”

And... woke up! Hooray! Rina and the Tortoise stared at each other with wide eyes, blinking together in perfect sync. Just like clockwork.

“Hello, good morning”, Rina said somewhat inappropriately.

What was inappropriate about it? The time! Morning had long passed – the burning sun was directly overhead.

“Hello”, the Purple Tortoise replied, surprised and still irritated, though in a beautiful female voice. After carefully inspecting the stranger from head to toe, she hesitantly suggested, “And you are... a boskryk”.

“Who?” Rina didn't expect such a statement.

“No”. The Tortoise shook her head. “Most likely you're not, but it's clear you're from the Steren Velen Verban Purden Borgen Pasten Basten Bemsky Wasteland”.

“Look”. Rina smiled. “I'm sorry, but I'm not from ‘Stere-vere-bere-pere'”. She couldn't pronounce it. “Not from the wasteland. I'm from Ukraine, from Kyiv!”

“From Kee-Wee Wee-Kee U-kee Ree-Kee”. The Tortoise tried very hard but couldn't spit out such a brutal mouthful. Yet she gathered her resolve and firmly declared, “A big special boskryk from Kee-Wee U-Wee!”

Rina laughed.

“From Kyiv. Kee-Yeev! And I'm no boskryk at all, I'm a human, a girl... well, not just a girl anymore, I'm a teenager. My name is Rina”.

“Rina”, the Tortoise repeated easily.

“Rina! Rina!” Rina exclaimed, beaming.

“And my name is Oluvila”, said the Tortoise, and added, “but that's not my real name. If you want to, if only you're curious and prefer to know everything as it actually is, my true name is: Olvo Leela Valo Veela Olvee Lala Luly Vala”.

“Oh!” Rina's brows jumped up for a moment. “I'll never remember that!”

“And you don't have to!” the Tortoise exclaimed excitedly. “Just call me Oluvila. I suppose you also have a longer name. You can't be just one single word, right?”

“A single word?” At such a weird question, Rina's nose even itched. “I... I don't know. Everyone calls me Rina. Some people – rarely – say Reana or Rayna, but I don't like it. Well, I have no other name. Actually, I'm just Rina”.

The Tortoise examined her guest again.

“Maybe, maybe”, she said doubtfully, slowly nodding her head. “Or perhaps you're just too small...” She suddenly remembered something very important and added unhesitatingly, “You're going to Triazis!”

“Tri Azis?” Rina echoed, deeply puzzled. “Well. Not exactly. I'm going to the Ant Hill, and that is why I need your advice”.

Oluvila goggled at Rina with huge amber eyes.

“Unique! We don't have any Ant Hill, no! There's the Sunset Hill, of course, and the Moon Hill, but both are very far – a five-day journey!”

Rina was frustrated: who could help her now if even the Purple Tortoise, Oluvila herself, didn't know where the Ant Hill was? Then she thought to take out the map. But immediately, it became clear that Oluvila had never seen a geographical map before. When Rina pointed to the purple circle and tried to explain that this mark was the Tortoise herself, Oluvila laughed, giggling non-stop for a good five minutes. Several times she quieted for a while, but looked at the map again and laughed anew.

“This is me? He-he-he! That's me? He-he-he-he-ha! Me! He-he-he-he-ha!”

And so on without end. Rina grew a little tired. Finally, after giggling herself into exhaustion, the Tortoise said in a serious, even authoritative tone:

“Oh! Listen! I'm not surprised anymore that you're looking for some hill that doesn't exist and never existed at all. Everything on this paper is backwards. It's unique! Look at me! Do you see how large I am? I'm already six hundred forty-one years, two months, and half a day old. Yet I grow, I keep growing! But here” – Oluvila waved her paw at the map – “here I'm tiny, like a May beetle! You think that's right?”

Rina decided not to argue. She knew from her experience that toddlers typically can't understand maps and get everything mixed up – but you don't scold them for that, you teach them and you love them. Oluvila, though she was truly six hundred years old and even more, behaved entirely like a preschooler. That was because nobody had taught her anything – all she knew, she had seen with her own eyes or deduced with her own mind.

“All right”. Rina did not insist. “Let the map be wrong...”

“Wrong, uniquely wrong!” the Tortoise confirmed.

“But if I go farther along this road, and if I walk for a long, long time, what color will the sand be up ahead? Green or black?”

“Green?” Oluvila opened her mouth wide. “No-o-o, the sand will be black, just like this”. She gestured around. “Because sand is always black. Or yellow. Or dark-black-and-yellow, or yellow-dark-black, or whatever color you like, but green sand, or white sand, or blue, or red – doesn't happen at all. It's forbidden. It's the Law of Nature!”

“Then please explain to me where this road leads”.

“Oh, this is not a road”, corrected Oluvila. “It's a ravine. Or rather, what remains of the Great Blue Ravine. Do you know how deep and grand it was only about three hundred years ago? Down there – at the very bottom – we even used to have water. Unique! Or you don't believe me?”

“I believe you!” Rina said, hurrying to agree. And sighed.

She'd walked and walked, searching for the Tortoise, had found her with much difficulty, barely woken her up – and it turned out the Tortoise couldn't help at all. Not only was Oluvila completely oblivious to how maps worked, and utterly blank on the Ant Hill, and evidently she had never seen green-colored sand, but now she was going to begin reminiscing about her “breaking news” from three centuries ago. And, by the way, there wasn't much time – Rina had to move forward.

“Fine!” she said impatiently. “Let it be a ravine! Or an ocean!” Oluvila wanted to point out the error – there had never been an ocean – but Rina continued. “Let it be whatever you wished three hundred years ago! But now, as far as I can see, it's a road. And I've walked along it for hours. So please, kind tortoise Oluvila, let me know where this way will take me?”

“To Triazis, where else?!” replied the Tortoise, a little hurt.

Of course she wanted to chat about those wonderful happy times when the Desert wasn't everywhere and there in that far-off corner of the world a small, densely populated valley was once prospering – a wide blue river flowed, palms grew on its banks, and she, Oluvila, a silly little tortoise-girl, had roamed across berry meadows with her siblings, rejoicing in the young, tender grass. But nobody wanted to listen, everyone was in a hurry – no time for her – and that was the most hurtful part of her long tortoise life.

“What is this Triazis?” Rina asked.

“Triazis? Oh, that's what we call it for short, you see? But if you want to say it the long way, it's Triple Ample Green Oasis”.

“Green?”

“Green”. The Tortoise nodded.

“There it is!” Rina unfolded the map again and pointed at the large, dark-green spot above, which, much to her joy, turned out to be an oasis and not sand.

“No-o-o”, objected Oluvila and cracked up laughing again, so Rina quickly hid the map behind her back so as not to waste another half hour listening to her giggle.

“Triazis isn't like that”, the Tortoise explained, slightly snorting, but thankfully without actual laughter. “There's grass and blue flowers, and two hundred eight palms – I counted them myself – and three colored brooks, and also...”

“Three? Colored brooks?” Rina exclaimed, accidentally flashing the map and pointing to the colorful strips amid the green spot.

“No! No-o-o! Ho! Ho!” Oluvila broke into laughter. “It is… He-he! He-he! Oh! Stop it! He-he-he! I can… he-he.. cannot! He-he-he!”

Rina quickly hid the map and frowned.

“It's all wrong, everything-everything! He-he-he! In real life these waters are fast, cold, and wet. They are just running creeks! But on your map... He-he! He-he-he!”

Rina let out a heavy sigh. Nevertheless, she'd learned something.

“Got it! First, I'll follow the road”, she thought stubbornly. “Then I'll find their famous Triazis with all its brooks, trees, and ant hills. And the map will show me what to do next!”

“Tell me, please, is this Triazis of yours large or small?” she asked.

“O-o-o!” The Tortoise's eyes widened tremendously. “It's ve-e-ery large! Gigantic! Two days long and one day wide! Uniquely so!”

Rina marveled at the enormity of the desert oasis and feared that finding the Ant Hill would be way tougher than she'd hoped – it would take quite some time. But then a reasonable thought struck he: a tortoise's “two days” might only be an hour at a brisk walking pace. Or maybe less.

Suddenly Oluvila asked:

“What are you able to do?”

Rina looked at her in surprise: what does “what are you able to” mean? Humans are able to do many things! How should she answer? But the Tortoise understood Rina's confusion in her own way and proudly announced:

“I can make a storm!”

“A storm?”

Rina was afraid that Oluvila had some magical power to summon real sandstorms.

“That's exactly what I need!” she thought irritably.

Meanwhile, Oluvila didn't waste a single second, she pushed off with her left legs, flipped upside down with a loud thump – nearly rolling over Rina's sneaker – then tucked back into her shell, let out a squeaky “Yi-he-ho!” and unexpectedly... began to spin. It was hard to believe – but despite the impossibility, the shell was spinning! At first slowly, then faster and faster. It spun on the sand much like a kid might playfully twirl a hard-boiled egg on a kitchen table.

Rina jumped aside because the spectacle looked quite hazardous – the Tortoise was big, and clearly very heavy.

Within a minute, her spinning accelerated unbelievably. Oluvila squeaked with delight inside her shell and seemed quite pleased with her homemade carousel. Sand flew out like rain in the wind – in all directions, showering Rina with dense, shooting streams of coarse black grit. Suddenly above the Tortoise a truly dragon-like, triple-spiral dust whirlwind rose and twisted menacingly.

“Yi-he-hay!” yelled the Tortoise. Her snub face was peeking from the shell, though each time flashing by so quickly that Rina could barely make it out.

Everything ended without warning. Oluvila braked sharply and stopped, the shell swayed and thudded back into place. The Tortoise, having immensely enjoyed the spin, popped her head out and asked happily:

“Did you like my storm?”

“Oh! Yes, I liked it very much!” Rina said, trying to shake half a pound of sand out of her hair. “I've never seen anything like that”.

Oluvila's face spread into a wide smile.

“O-o-o! If so, I will give you my Night Glass”.

The Tortoise retreated back into her shell, and soon, after some clinking and rattling inside, appeared again. But she got the exit wrong – her head now poked out from the spot where her pink tail had been moments ago – so Rina had to come around to the other side.

“Here! Take it!” said the Tortoise, extending a flat square of glass on her left foot. “My gift! For you!”

Rina cautiously took the little rectangle.

“What is it?”

“This is the Night Glass, I said”, explained the Tortoise. “If you ever have to walk through the dark of the blackest night, when you can't see anything and might get lost – just look through the Glass with one eye”.

“And what will happen?”

“It'll be like daytime!” exclaimed Oluvila. “You'll see for yourself!”

Rina thanked her and decided she should give something in return. But what? Flipping quickly through all the contents of her backpack, she found nothing suitable except for a small strap with a toy plastic compass attached.

Actually, Rina had two compasses: one real, made of heavy copper, with a mirror, numbers all around, and a plate with a long thin slit in it – that professional compass was in the outer pocket, Rina couldn't part with it, now she was a traveler, and as everyone knows, travelers don't go anywhere without a compass. As for the second one, the toy compass – she had brought it along purely out of a habit, “just in case”, and honestly, it was never needed.

Rina took it and put it on the Tortoise's finger – well, more precisely, on her claw. Oluvila was astonished, and once Rina explained how to use it properly, the Tortoise was absolutely delighted.

“Remarkable magic!” Oluvila even bounced slightly despite her horrifying weight. “An unseen wonder! Look – wherever I turn, the red tip points forward!”

“North”, clarified Rina.

“North! And the blue tip – backward!”

“South”.

“South!” exulted Oluvila. “Magic! Real magic! Unique!”

Rina thought, “Herx was right: what's ordinary for one seems wizardry to another”.

After playing with the compass for some time and admiring the new “witchcraft”, Oluvila said in a lecturing tone:

“If you go to Triazis, don't mess around and make sure you listen to the tuslins”.

“Listen to who?” Rina didn't get it.

“Tuslins! They sing, and you listen and obey, and go from one tuslin to another”.

“Why?”

“It's simple! All tuslins live on oasis roads. And if in any desert you hear a tuslin singing, follow its song and don't turn aside, and then you'll definitely reach the oasis you need – if, of course, you obey quite carefully. And in the oasis, there's always water and coconut buns. I mean nuts. That's what I call them. There's nothing tastier than coconut buns! Nuts, I mean”.

“I don't know”. Rina shrugged. “I tried one last winter and didn't like it”.

“That's because you haven't eaten nuts from Triazis! Only there do the real bun-coconuts grow, only in our Triazis! That's exactly why you absolutely must go there!”

“Yes, I understand. I'll go to Triazis, and I'll hurry”.

“Hurry?” the Tortoise was flabbergasted, she lifted all her legs in shock. “Is that how you hurry? When I'm in a rush, nothing can stop me! I've been hurrying for twenty-seven weeks already!... Or twenty-nine?”

“And where are you hurrying to?” Rina asked, just out of courtesy.

“Hurrying? To my uncle Obbaun, of course, where else?” Oluvila answered simply. “I'm hurrying for his Birth-Big-Year. This century, he turns forty. A jubilee! He's old, my uncle, he is very, extremely, overwhelmingly old! Three times older than me! Or four? Imagine! He's had thirty-nine Birth-Big-Years of forty years each. Beyond all belief!”

“Tortoises sure count time strangely”, thought Rina. “Is there even such a thing as a ‘birth-big-year?'“ But she preferred not to ask out loud, or else she'd be stuck for another fifteen minutes at least, listening to complicated tortoise explanations mixed with five-hundred-year-old memories – and heaven forbid, the giggles. Getting ready to head out, Rina said with sincere gratitude:

“Thank you very much, Purple Tortoise Oluvila! Now I know exactly where to go and which road to take!”

“Of course – along the Great Blue Ravine, for coconut buns!” The Tortoise smiled and added, “Unique!”

“Exactly, for coconuts!” Rina smiled too.

She felt calm and relieved. After all, the Tortoise was kind and funny – or better yet, sweet and amusing.

“Goodbye!” said Rina. She wanted to hug her new friend, but how could you do that? No arms would be enough. “Thank you for your help and for your great gift, the Night Glass!”

“Goodbye! And thank you for the compompalalas!” answered Oluvila.

Rina didn't correct her. She thought, “Really, what difference does it make what it's called – compass or compompalalas?” Especially since tortoises, as everybody knows, love long, tangled-up words. Let her call it whatever she likes!

Strapping on her precious little backpack, Rina headed down the road. After about forty steps, she turned around and waved both hands at Oluvila. The Tortoise lifted her front legs in response.

“That's it”, Rina thought. “Soon I'll find the ant, and then the golden key – the way out of the Desert. The Exit!”

That idea made her happier.

“Everything's fine”, she said aloud, picking up her pace, and no longer paying attention to the scorching sun or the stuffy, overheated air.

08 – GRI-GRI
part one

Maybe she'd gotten too lost in thought to notice how time had flown by, or perhaps Herx's map really wasn't all that accurate, just as Oluvila had claimed. But soon, Rina climbed the long, gradual slope, and as she emerged from the Blue Ravine onto the flat surface, she saw a green island with trees in the distance. Round, sandy dunes, pretending to be real ocean waves, hid the island's base behind their crests, but hundreds of fluffy palm tops – so far away they looked like thin blades of grass – peeked out invitingly, promising cool shade and a nice, relaxing break.

Rina estimated the distance at a glance – at least a two-hour walk. She was starving and, of course, terribly thirsty, so she decided to backtrack to a turn where she'd seen a ledge sticking out of the sandstone wall about twice her height off the ground. From that plain little overhang, a weak, pale shadow fell – but anyway, it was the only shade around. Rina sat down on the ravine's floor, pulled the sad remains of food from her backpack along with the still-full plastic balloon of flower water, and ate what barely qualified as lunch. Most importantly, she drank. The water was warm, but it tasted amazing. Then she rested for a couple of minutes, just sitting there leaning against the flat, rough rock, relaxing as much as she could in the hot air, and thinking fondly of her Kyiv.

“Everything's so weird”, she thought. “Here I am, in the desert. All around me just sand and heat, and at this exact moment in Kyiv, after the rain, cars are moving, people are out walking, and someone's sitting at Kartata Potata, looking out the window and making their own artisan pizza with triple cheese... Wild!”

After regaining some energy, she climbed back onto the smooth surface, which was patterned with gently sloping folds of low sandy mounds, and set off toward the green oasis at a brisk pace. But after a couple of minutes, she stopped and said in surprise:

“No! What's this? Some kind of your witchcraft?”

What startled her was that in just two minutes, the green island had come so close it seemed she'd already been walking toward it for a good hour and a half. The nearest trees were just about two miles away – or maybe even less. Looking closer, she could see not just palms, but also cypresses, grassy hills, and the tops of blooming bushes. Rina stood there, dumbstruck, then decided to keep going without looking directly at the oasis. If the road really was magical and had shortened all by itself, what was the harm? Better not to interrupt that kind of enchantment. Old fairy tales taught her that magic often works only when you're not looking at it.

“Yeah, better to watch the sand under my feet and count steps”.

She aligned herself carefully – right for the center of the green island – and began marching with her head down.

One two three four five six seven eight nine ten eleven twelve thirteen fourt...

Boom!

“Ow!” Rina cried out, grabbing her forehead – she crashed full-speed into a palm tree at the edge of the grove.

It may sound weird, but somehow, in just fourteen steps, she'd managed to cover far more than an hour's walk. And with no tuslins involved. Rubbing her forehead, she looked back at the sand and dunes – there, about forty yards away, right on the impeccably smooth surface, was a single footprint of her sneaker, as if she'd leaped across the entire desert on her left foot in hundred-meter-long jumps.

Dumbfounded, but unable to come up with any reasonable explanation for this trick, she stepped onto the thick, springy layer of grass – so even that it looked as if it had been recently mowed – and found herself in a cool, dark coconut forest. The dense, bluish shade cast by dozens of palm canopies, joined high above, blocked the baking sun. There, around Rina, the slender trunks stood like carved columns. The hot desert breeze still managed to reach in with short, angry gusts, but with every step, the coolness and humidity started to win out.

Soon, a sunlit meadow came into view, carpeted with a fine brush of young, emerald sedge. Beyond that were fluffy bushes dotted with clusters of canary-yellow buds and – what's this? – a narrow clay path peeking through the greenish foliage! Rina couldn't believe her eyes. Beaming with joy, she sprinted across the meadow, landing with both feet on the firm, well-trodden trail, feeling as if a real Everest had been lifted from her shoulders.

“People! There are people here!” she exclaimed, and took off running along the pathway, deeper into the lush thickets of mahonia.

But she was wrong. It's not only people who leave paths behind. And although there were never any dangerous animals in Triazis, there weren't any people either. Or rather, seven hundred years ago, an owl named Kailla used to tell her grandkids how five wanderers once entered Triazis, coming from a distant land they called the World of Morroddo. But where that land was, what kind of world it was, and who those wanderers were – Kailla didn't know.

Since then, Triazis had mostly hosted tortoises, ground squirrels, lizards, boas, hedgehogs, and toads. Occasionally, three cute, funny rattlesnakes – Shana, Shata, and Shada – would come too. And young foxes would show up, just out of their innate curiosity. Triazis was also a shelter for birds on their way to other oases. And a colony of red butterflies settled in the place, but they never considered themselves locals. Anyway, nobody knew who made this narrow trail that ran through both the Round Meadows and the Colorful Spring.

Rina ran along the path. The scenery changed quickly. The bushes were left behind, the palm trees thinned out, and then disappeared altogether. The path flashed between two pyramids of fossil shells, then timidly passed through a patch of spiky cacti, climbed a hill, dropped suddenly down the slope, and popped out onto a big, bright spot covered with sharp-leaved grass that Granny Anya calls “moon wheat”.

And there Rina witnessed an astonishing miracle: just a few steps off the trail, right in the middle of the clearing, there was a flat stone circle, and in its center, a shallow well with walls made of matching slabs of blue slate, and there, at the bottom, from three round holes, burst a strong, noisy, geyser-like spring. The water gurgled merrily between the stones, shooting off in all directions in two dozen crystal streams, and gathered into a mighty brook rushing back toward the palm trees.

Rina stepped closer to the water, leaned over, and touched it with her finger – it was freezing! She took off her backpack, lay down on the grass, and started drinking. The chill stung her mouth and throat, her teeth ached from the cold, but she kept gulping, feeling life itself flowing like a powerful current throughout her entire body.

A spring in the desert is real magic, way more meaningful than a shadow without a bird, or even a talking tortoise.

After drinking her fill, she rolled onto her back, closed her eyes blissfully, and lay there for a loooong time, not thinking about anything. She almost fell asleep. But a soft noise to her right disturbed her. Sluggishly and sloooowly, she turned her head, looked lazily... and leaped up like a scalded cat out of hell!

On a bent willow branch sat a giant blue parrot, cheerfully rocking and peering at Rina with its curious, gleaming eye. It looked just like the budgies they sell in Kyiv – kids call them ‘wavies' for the ripple patterns on their backs – but this one was a hundred times bigger – almost condor-sized, much larger than horned owls.

“Gree-gree!” trumpeted the parrot, swinging on the creaking branch like a seesaw.

In a horrible panic, Rina grabbed her backpack and rushed across the clearing full tilt, away from the monstrous bird.

The parrot majestically spread its enormous wings, deftly hopped up, and took off into the air. It made a wide half-circle, rose higher, and glided down from above toward the fleeing stranger.

“Greeeee!” it blared right above her head.

Rina was dreadfully frightened. Madly running at full throttle, she glanced up and suddenly, stumbling over a protruding root, she flew – head heels hands screams – right into the high barbed grass, painfully bumping her chin on something and scraping her knee on something.

“Go away! Shoo! Scram! Get lost!” she shouted, scared witless by the parrot monster circling above her. She grabbed a piece of dry bark lying at her feet and, with all her might, threw it at the bird.

The parrot jerked to the side and nearly crashed into an old palm tree standing alone, then turned sharply and shouted back:

“Hey! What are you doing?! Don't throw stones!!”

Rina was confused. Apparently, she had relaxed too much, too deeply, resting by the spring, and had completely forgotten that she was not just in a green oasis in the middle of a desert, but in a green magical oasis in the middle of a magical desert, and that the animals and birds there were not the kind of living creatures back home.

But the parrot's indignant speech, totally unexpected, brought her thoughts into some sort of order.

“Then why did you attack me?” Rina asked loudly in self-defense, already calming down though still standing in the tall, scratchy grass.

“I did?! Attacked?! You mean me?!”

The parrot was descending, preparing to land.

“Did I attack someone?” Finally, he found his mark and landed gracefully on a grassy mound. “If you think what I did means an ‘attack', then you are sorely mistaken. By Law, it's called – you know what? I know and I'm telling you – we call it ‘he approached and said hello'!”

“Said hello?!” Rina was baffled. She stepped into the open, meticulously smoothed back her hair, dusted off her sundress, and adjusted her backpack. This routine helped her fully regain her composure.

“Exactly!” exclaimed the Parrot. “I approached... or, I would say, I flew up closer and was just about to ask how you're doing, how's your health, your family, how everything is in general, and all of a sudden you threw a stone at me for no reason, as if I were a real tiger or a hippopotamus, I don't even know. And by the way, if you truly think that it's okay to just throw stones at non-tigers and non-hippopotamuses, then you are very mistaken. Very much so! I assure you!”

The Parrot resolutely came closer, and Rina saw that he was more comical than monstrous, and even, probably, defenseless. Most likely, he was young, possibly a teenager like herself, despite his extraordinary size.

08 – GRI-GRI
part two

The Parrot paused, took a deep breath, and continued:

“Just imagine, please, what this world would turn into if all of us, thousands of birds and thousands of animals, were to pick up stones, sticks, and... and everything that can be picked up in our paws, and start throwing them at each other. We'd smash everyone's heads! Eventually. Do you understand?”

“I didn't want to smash anyone's heads”, said Rina calmly. “I just got scared of you”.

“Scare-rer-red? Of? M-me?” stuttered the Parrot, totally stunned.

He stood there with his beak open, blinking his blue eyelashes, then lifted his wings and ran to the water, clownishly shuffling his orange feet and slightly hopping for speed.

In that way, he galloped through the glade to a little lake, full of rather deep and dark water. Actually, it was just a widening of the brook. The lake served as a mirror for some oasis dwellers. The Parrot looked into it, turned his left side, then his right, tilted his head here, there, and even managed to look at himself from the back. He then dropped his wings and, with an extremely sad expression, trudged back to Rina, who struggled hard not to laugh.

“Of course”, he sighed heavily. “No wonder you got scared of me. I'd be scared of myself too if I saw myself from the side”.

“But by the way” – he perked up – “if you think that I'm like this, I would say, messy not just today but daily, then you're mistaken, very mistaken! By law, it's not my fault! It's all Peglet! I haven't slept all night! Three nights! Look, do you see these ghastly bags under my eyes? And the awful color of my beak? Horrible! And all this because of what? You're still asking me because of what? Because how can one sleep through this screeching?! I'm telling you, it's all Peglet!” Each phrase made the Parrot bob up with anger and exasperation.

“Through what screeching? And who is she, that Peglet?” Rina was puzzled and thought that maybe he meant “piglet” but could not pronounce it the right way.

“Not ‘she', it's ‘he', I would say”, corrected the Parrot. “Peglet – he is a wet mouse. A wet boy-mouse, who's of no use at all, just a screecher! He says he's preparing for the contest. As a singer! Can you imagine? He's barely visible, zero size! But also wants to be famous! A screechy little wet nothing!!!” The Parrot yelled the last phrase furiously, turning his head toward the forest. “Washed-out opera!!!” he cried. “And if you think”, the Parrot said to Rina, lifting his wing, “He'll just keep singing the whole time, for weeks on end, and I'll suffer, never sleeping at night and being tormented by headaches, then believe me, you're mistaken, very mistaken!”

The Parrot huffed. Rina kept silent.

“And what can you say to that?” he asked, clearly expecting Rina's understanding or at least sympathy.

“I'm sorry”, she replied as politely as she could. “It's my first time in the Desert, I don't know anything in it yet, and I have barely seen anyone here, just a few... um.... persons. Besides, I'm in a big hurry. I have a very important matter to deal with”.

“An important matter?” The Parrot looked astonished. “Here? In our Triazis there are important matters?”

“There are! I'm looking for the Ant Hill. By the way, would you be so kind, dear Parrot, as to tell me where this hill is and how to get there?”

“That's all nonsense!” The Parrot brushed it off casually. “You can go there”. He pointed his wing somewhere to the right. “You can go here”. He pointed to the left. “All the same! The thing is, if I'm going to spend every night like this, and then fly all day with a headache until their stupid contest...” Here he stumbled and his eyes widened in horror at his own words. “Until the contest?
