The mice will screech every night until the contest?! No-o-o!!! That's hell and a nightmare! Can you believe it?!”

“I do, I can!” sighed Rina. “In our house, in Kyiv, a little kitten lived on the fifth floor, and at night it would meow. On the balcony. Every night. Of course, the kitten meowed very quietly, but his never stopping meow-meows were so plaintive that it made you want to cry. And I couldn't sleep at all. Then I'd walk around all day with a headache, just like you”.

“And what then? How did it end? I hope you tore him into slices and ate him up?” asked the Parrot with much interest.

“What are you talking about! How can you eat a kitten up?” Rina was shocked.

“Oh! Of course, I don't know a thing about kittens. Maybe eating kittens isn't very fashionable in your society, or perhaps they give you a stomach ache, so it's better not to touch them. You were absolutely right not to eat him immediately, maybe you'll slice him later when he grows up properly. But eating all sorts of wet mice is not a problem at all!” He shouted the last part, turning again to the forest. “Listen, I'm telling you honestly, if you think that I'll endure the torment for a hundred years until their ridiculous contest, and if you say it's forbidden to eat these genius singers, then I'll give it to you straight – you are mistaken, and...”

“And I know – I'm very much mistaken”, Rina finished for him.

“Exactly!” The Parrot jumped almost two feet high. “I'll gobble him down! You'll see, be my witness, I'll definitely swallow him in a wink! I have no choice! They left me no other option! It's the only way out, I would say, absolutely!”

“No need to eat anyone”, Rina suggested. “If you want, I can just go talk to him. Show me where your mouse lives, and I'll tell him everything. I bet he doesn't even know his singing is annoying you”.

The Parrot froze, his beak wide open.

“You will?! You really want to help me? Just like that, right away, you'll go and talk to Wet Peglet?”

“Of course I'll go and talk”, Rina reassured him. “I'll help you solve your problem, and you'll help me with mine – with the Ant Hill. Shake on it?”

“Sh-shake”, the Parrot answered uncertainly and shook his head, as if really trying to shake off a delusion.

In the end, both decided not to postpone important matters and to go to the mice immediately. Despite being a bird, the Parrot often enjoyed going on foot. He liked to alternate between the two ways of moving. So they walked along the path, Rina behind, and in front of her, the strikingly gigantic blue Parrot. Unreal! Almost shoulder-high!

“And why are the mice wet?” Rina asked after a long silence.

“Wet?” The Parrot turned around. “Of course they're all wet, all the contestants! Not dry, are they? They drink water”.

“So there are also dry mice that don't drink?” Rina guessed.

“Naturally, they don't drink! There's not a single drop of water there, so they're dry!”

“Where's there? In the desert?”

“In the desert, in the desert”. The Parrot nodded. “No one drinks water there! I mean absolutely no one! Not a single living creature! And where would they get it, this water, by law? Just sand and stones!”

Rina thought for a while and said, “Well, I think you are not exactly right. Some creatures still drink. For example, the Motionless Snake Skrtstn does”.

The Parrot stopped so sharply that Rina accidentally stepped on the tip of his tail.

“You know the Snake Skrtstn?” he asked in a half-whisper, slowly turning his head a full one hundred eighty degrees and staring at Rina with bulging eyes.

“I do”, she replied simply. “I met her recently, just last night, and she gave me water from the flower”.

The Parrot sat down on the path – simply collapsed right on the clay. He seemed to be speechless, as if he had never spoken before. A minute later, blinking his long eyelashes, he said sorrowfully, in a tragic, sepulchral voice:

“And you drank that water”.

“I did. Why?”

The Parrot sighed, stood up, and shook his wings.

“Oh, nothing, nothing, don't worry”. He started walking along the path again. Rina followed him. The next minute passed in silence.

“That's how everything happens in this cruel, shameless world!” the Parrot cried theatrically after three dozen steps. “Someone visits her for a hundred years in a row. He serves her, he pleases her, fulfilling her tiniest wishes, her subtlest – I would say – whims! Even, I must say it, he even humiliates himself! Yes, exactly! Hu-mi-li-ates! For a hundred years! Imagine! And then? What does he get for his efforts? Nothing! Literally no-thing! No attention, no gratitude! Zero! And some others come for the first time in their lives, just passing by, just dropping in accidentally for a minute, and they get everything right away! Right on the spot! Even the water from Shltstkn! Where's the justice? Where's the law? I'm asking you!”

“And what does this water from Shtl... shl... snts... do?” Rina asked.

“From Shltstkn?” the Parrot prompted eagerly. “Oh, nothing. Just nothing special. But the one who drinks it can move through the earth like the roots of Shltstkn”.

“And how do the roots of this shtl... tsn... khn move?”

“Roots? However they want, that's how they move!” the Parrot shouted somewhere off to the side and flapped his wings irritably. But soon he pulled himself together, glanced around with his deeply sad eyes, and explained in an almost calm tone:

“They move like this, for example, right now this root is here, you see? And a minute later – whoosh! – it's already ooover there! Behind those palm trees. That instant leap of the Shltstkn roots across any distance. Is that clear?”

“So now, you say, I can move wherever I want, like those roots?”

“Of course you can! You drank the water! Didn't she explain it to you?” The Parrot stopped again and energetically spun his head. “You absolutely can! Right now you're here, and then whoosh! – and you're already there!”

“And what do I need to do for that?”

“Do for what for that what?”

“Well, so that I'm here first, and right away I'm there?”

“A-a-ah! Nothing! You don't need to do anything. Just wish it, and that's all”.

“Wow! Now I understand!” Rina cheered. “Today I've already moved like the roots of that shl... shts... kn!”

“Like Shltstkn? Already moved today?!” The Parrot didn't believe her.

“Yes, you're right, today! I was walking to this oasis through the desert, walking and walking and walking, and it was so far away, the air was so hot, and my feet hurt so much, and then I really wanted the oasis to be just a little bit closer”.

“And what?! And what then?!” The Parrot impatiently hopped in front of Rina, shouting into her face.

“And I moved! Triazis became close! I walked farther, and probably wanted to get right inside the palm grove – I don't remember exactly what I wished for – and... and...”

“‘...and hit my forehead on a tree like a ram'“, Rina almost said, but caught herself in time and continued, “...and immediately I ended up right here, in Triazis”.

“In-credible! A-mazing! Marvelous! Fantastic!!!” The Parrot was jumping around in delight. But a minute later he drooped, fell silent, and lowered his wings.

“And how much of that flower water from Shl... ts... kn do you need to start moving like its roots?” Rina cautiously asked after a pause.

“Water?” The Parrot shook his head sadly. “Not much. Actually. One ksn. That's about two or three sips”.

“Hooray!” Rina shouted so suddenly that the Parrot jumped about eight steps back in fright. “I have it! I do have those two or three sips! Way more than two or three sips! For you!”

The Parrot blinked rapidly, not understanding anything, but Rina was already taking her amazing backpack off her shoulders. Untying the strings, she took out, carefully and gently, like a fragile treasure, a half-empty transparent plastic bag with warm magical water inside.

“What a W! I forgot to pour it out and take fresh water from the spring!” she thought, and then said aloud, “Here! This is the water from your Shlt... shn... skn!”

The Parrot shifted his frantic gaze from the bag to Rina, then down from Rina to the bag, and it was clear he literally couldn't believe his eyes.

“Take it! It's really that very water!” Rina held out the bag at arm's length. “I took some extra last night! Right from the flower. Take it, I don't need it anymore. I'll get fresh water from your spring”.

“No, no, no”. He swayed his head, deep in thought. “I walked for a hundred years... I served... I pleased...” The Parrot fearfully touched the shiny plastic with the tip of his wing and kept muttering as if in a trance, “Served... pleased... walked...”

“Take it, take it!” Rina insisted. “Drink it!”

“Drink?!” The Parrot suddenly snapped to attention, and then, with an unexpected lightning-fast strike, stabbed his sharp beak into the bag so hard that Rina could barely hold it in her hand. Within a minute, the bag was emptied to the last drop.

“And I always said this is exactly how it would end!” the Parrot proclaimed proudly, wiping his beak on the grass. “I have obtained the water of Shltstkn! I did it! No matter what they say, I obtained it!”

“Of course”, Rina agreed. “Of course you obtained it. And now, please, let's go to your little mouse Peglet. I'm really in a hurry”.

“To Peglet? To anyone at all!” he exclaimed. “Even to Golokaan if you wish! For your sake, I'm ready to go wherever you say, to the edge of the realm, to the Moon and beyond! ... By the way”. The Parrot looked at Rina strangely through a sly squint. “You and I, we still haven't introduced ourselves, and that's against the law”.

“Right, it's against the law”. She smiled. “My name is Rina”.

“And I'm Gri-Gri!” said the Parrot joyfully.

“And I knew that, actually!” Rina laughed. “When I was running away from you!”

“Gree-gree! Gree-gree! Gree-gree!” the Parrot shouted, hopping on one leg.

09 – LA FOURMILIERE
part one

The path led the friends out onto a dandelion field, and now, right at their feet, a yellow stream sparkled and danced – swift, cold water the color of ripe lemons. The little rivulet was so narrow it didn't need a bridge – a single step, and you were already on the other side. Rina asked:

“Why is the water yellow? Is it a pigment? Or paint?”

“Paint?! What paint?” Gri-Gri was startled, then dissolved into laughter. “Oh no! Ha! There's no paint here! This is our famous Yellow Flower Brook – it's always this color”.

“Yellow... brook...” Rina repeated thoughtfully.

A sudden idea from her geography lessons popped into her head: the Yellow River, the Grand Huang He, which flows all the way across the Great Chinese Plains, must start from just such a little yellow stream, too.

“Yellow! Yellow!” Gri-Gri nodded. “There's also a Blue and a Pink one – all three brooks run from that spring on the Spring Field, where we first met, remember?”

“Hold on!” she exclaimed. “I forgot, I have the map!” She jumped so high that even the Parrot was surprised. “Your brooks – the three colored lines!”

“Lines? What lines?” Gri-Gri didn't quite catch the word.

“I'll show you! Watch!” she said, slipping off her backpack and pulling out the map.

Unfolding the large, rustling square of paper, Rina gasped and stared in amazement: the map had changed dramatically – it looked like it had been swapped for another one! Now it was a map of Triazis – a marvelously depicted drawing with all the meadows, paths, fields, streams, hills, and dips accurately marked, and even the palm trees were shown as small green circles, each one separately. Gri-Gri raised both wings and stiffened in awe and silence. But not for long.

“Wow! It's beautiful! This is what beauty means! If you think I've ever seen anything this excellent, you're wrong, completely wrong!” he mumbled enthusiastically, tracing his beak across the varnished paper.

Three inches to the left of the center was the famous Colorful Spring. A gray stream flowed out of it, but soon it split into three thinner rivers, each a different shade: yellowish, bluish, and pink. A brown winding line ran to the Spring – the clay path. First, it turned eastward, then crossed all three streams one by one and led to the other edge of the map, where it ended in a round meadow dotted with dark green spots. Rina pointed at the path:

“So we're going to cross the other two brooks too?”

“Of course we will”, Gri-Gri confirmed. “The Blue Brook is right there, just past the cypresses, and the Pink one is a little farther on”.

“But how come your Colorful Spring isn't colorful at all – it's just regular clear water? So why are the brooks colored?”

“That I don't know”. The Parrot flapped his wings slightly. “I haven't been here long enough – only two hundred and three years. When I flew down here from the Southern Karlan-Narlan Valley, everything was already exactly the way you see it now”.

“And do you drink the colored water right from the brooks, or do you always have to fly to the colorless Spring?” Rina asked, for whatever reason.

“Why would I fly to the Spring?” Gri-Gri missed the point. “If it's too far, I drink wherever I want. When I'm thirsty, of course”.

“But the water – it's colored. That means there has to be something in it that gives it the color. You say it's not paint, fine, but there must be some kind of coloring in it anyway. What if it's harmful?”

“Harmful? Of course not! It's just flower water! Look – only yellow flowers grow along the Yellow Brook!”

Rina glanced and was surprised she hadn't noticed such a curious feature before. Sure enough, on both banks grew all sorts of flowering plants, large and tiny, tall and short, but every one of them was the same lemon-yellow color as the stream itself.

“And blue flowers grow along the Blue Brook”, said Gri-Gri in a lecturing tone.

“And pink ones are by the Pink Brook”, Rina guessed.

“Oh! How do you know our top confidential hush-hush covert beans?” the Parrot questioned in a mock-resentful tone and winked. “Of course they're all pink! What other color could they possibly be?”

“And if I drink the yellow water, will I turn yellow?”

“You know”, said the Parrot earnestly, looking around as if someone might be eavesdropping behind the trees, “I'll let you in on a secret – just don't tell anyone. About seventy years ago”, he went on, “a little green monkey named Mari-Rani showed up in Triazis. Before that, she'd lived somewhere in the Enimus Mountains – I don't know exactly where it is, but they say it's far away, a half-night flight from here. And someone over there, not knowing any better, told her about this colored water. Somehow she managed to make it through the entire Desert and reach our oasis. Well, Mari-Rani the monkey made a habit of drinking yellow water. Only the yellow. And a lot. From morning to night, every day. She even got sick from over-drinking”.

“But why would she drink so much?”

“Why?!” the Parrot cried. “To become yellow, of course! Just like the water!”

“It doesn't make sense”. Rina gestured with both hands in confusion. “What's the point of being yellow?”

“Ha! If you think I understand monkey nonsense, you're mistaken”, said Gri-Gri. “But it doesn't matter, I would say. The main thing is, it didn't work. Imagine! Didn't happen at all! She stayed as green as ever – not a single hair turned yellow, I swear”.

“And then? Did she go back to her homeland?”

“Probably”, the Parrot answered indifferently. “I was busy with many other things back then, and I don't remember what happened to her. I think she left Triazis with a pack of sand foxes, they travel west every five years. The foxes don't go into Triazis – they don't like it here, I suppose. They usually sleep on the sand by the Ravine of Red Cacti and stay around there for three or four days straight – resting, I would say – then they head quickly to Mount Katilum. They always have a gathering there. Didn't you meet them? This is the Fifth Fox Year, after all”.

“No, I didn't see a single fox”.

“Then you must have come from the other side”, Gri-Gri said confidently. “Otherwise you would remember the foxes glowing red”.

Rina was amazed by the news but decided not to ask any more about the glowing foxes. First of all, she hadn't run into them, and now they were heading toward the mountains in the opposite direction. Second, and more importantly, they weren't on the map. And if something's not on the map, then it's never needed... although the Parrot wasn't on the map either, but he was needed. In fact, he was absolutely required – the Parrot was indispensable! Rina fell deep in thought...

Soon they stepped over the Blue Brook, with its banks tightly covered with Chicory and Irises, and then over the Pink one. Along the way, Rina couldn't resist picking a strikingly beautiful and quite unusual blossom from a tiny tree by the Blue bank. It caught her eye at once – a camellia, her mother's favorite flower. Mom called camellias “the symbol of devotion and loyalty”. But – do blue camellias even exist? Rina gently placed it into the left pocket of her sundress. A keepsake, and something to surprise her family with, especially her Granny who's an expert with flowers.

At the Pink Brook, the companions paused for a minute. The Parrot stared, as if entranced, at the fast-moving barbie-pink water, as if remembering something important. Rina took advantage of the moment, rinsed her hand, scooped up a few sips, and drank – it tasted just like ordinary spring water. Gri-Gri didn't notice a thing.

She looked at him and asked, “Why aren't any mice marked on my map?”

“How should I know?” The Parrot shrugged, snapping out of his contemplation. “It's your map, not mine”. But after a quick moment of pondering, he added, “Apparently, the artist didn't draw the mice because maps always show only what's above ground, but mice don't live on the surface – they're underground animals”.

“Then what about ants? They're underground animals, too. Well, insects. Under-the-surface ones, but they're on the map”.

“No, not the same”. Gri-Gri shook his head. “Ants only sleep underground, but they still live under the sun. Mice are the opposite – they come out at night. So if you want to draw mice, you need to make a nighttime map, not a day one. But it's dark at night, right? Then you'd have to paint the whole paper totally black”.

Rina listened and felt slightly thrown off – Gri-Gri was so smart! And on Earth, in all the cartoons, parrots are either fools or dimwits. She thought that was unfair...

When they stepped out into a round clearing with a tall palm stump in the center, Gri-Gri immediately ran to it, jumped up on top, and announced from up there:

“Attention, I'm starting to get spinheaded!”

“Spin-what?” Rina didn't catch the word.

“Spin! Headed!” the Parrot repeated much louder. “We've been walking for a full half hour, and after an overly long stroll, you always get spinheaded. It's a body law! But don't worry, this will end very soon”.

Gri-Gri sat down, closed his eyes, and grew still. Then he slowly turned his head to the right. But he didn't stop – when his beak passed over his shoulder, his head kept moving behind his back and soon made a full rotation around its axis, meaning around the neck. Then the second rotation, then the third. With each moment, the head spun faster and faster. Rina watched this spectacle as intently as she could and started counting the turns without realizing it, but when she got to seventeen, she lost count, because Gri-Gri's head was spinning so fast there was no way to track the disappearing and reappearing beak. Rina got scared for the Parrot – what if he got sick with such a wild spin? But then she remembered the Purple Oluvila and her “sand storm”.

“Maybe spinning is a special trait of everyone who lives in the Desert. I wouldn't be shocked if sand foxes spin too”. She imagined a big red fox spinning her luxurious, bushy tail like a fan. Funny.

But after about three minutes, the Parrot's head started to slow down, and very soon, it came to a full stop in its normal position. Gri-Gri ruffled his wings, opened his eyes, and said with a deep sigh:

“It's all because of the lack of vitamin Q”.

“The lack of vitamins? That is why your head spins?”

“No, the opposite!” The Parrot jumped down to the ground. “The angular velocity was too low today, didn't you notice?”

“I did, I noticed”, she said, so as not to offend Gri-Gri.

“Later, I'll eat three leaves of the Barbudesh Prickle. Three whole leaves! Or better yet, five, I would say. I need the Q urgently, you know?”

And they went on. But as soon as the path ran over the next hill, Gri-Gri stopped, looked around, and stated:

“That's it, I'm not going down. I don't want to see him”.

“Who?”

“Peglet! Who else?”

“Peglet? He lives here?” Rina searched for a hut or something.

“No, not here, over there”. The Parrot pointed left with his wing. “Just past the turn, there's a big fallen palm tree. On the ground. A palm log, I would say. There, under it, you'll clearly see five little burrows. Holes, actually. The third one from the left is Peglet's, his home. And I live a little farther”, he pointed ahead, “there on that palm with the red vines”.

Well, there was no choice – she had to go alone. Gri-Gri stayed on the hill, and Rina ran down to the glade. She easily found the palm tree lying there with several round mouse holes under the trunk, and without delay tried to call the mouse by his name, but no one responded.

“Knock on the log! Three times!” she heard the Parrot, who, as it turned out, was silently circling high above her head.

Rina looked up at him, nodded, and tapped the log three times with her finger: knock knock knock. The trunk sounded like a bass drum. Right away, a sharp, lilac nose with white whiskers poked out of the hole. Beady eyes glanced at Rina, and the mouse dove back into the depths in half an instant.

“Peglet, don't be afraid, I need to talk to you!” she called out loudly.

No answer came from the hole for a long time. Rina waited. After a while of silence, apparently having thought it over, Peglet grew bolder and squeaked in a tiny voice:

“And who are you?”

“I'm a human. My name is Rina, and I came here from Earth to see the sights”.

“And what's that got to do with me?” came slightly louder sounds from the hole.

“Nothing”, she said. “But I have a friend, Gri-Gri the Parrot. I think you know him”.

“Your friend?” Peglet asked in surprise, still hidden from view.

“Yes, you're right, he's my friend! And he wants to sleep at night – like all normal parrots do, you know?”

“And does this have anything to do with me?” asked Peglet again.

“It does! It absolutely does! You sing!”

There was some rustling in the burrow, but no words.

“Peglet! Peglet!” she called impatiently.

“I... I sing?” Peglet asked uncertainly.

“For real! You do, you sing! Every night and a lot! And the Parrot, my friend, he can't fall asleep because of your singing. Do you understand me?”

“Yes. I do. But everyone… everyone here… they say that I sing... I sing... not too bad”, mumbled Peglet. He was barely audible, his snout sticking out, his face looking sad and rather offended. Rina shrugged.

“I don't know. I haven't heard you. Probably you sing well, but that's not the point now, sorry. You have to understand – if someone wants to sleep, and there's singing nearby, even if it's beautiful and wonderful singing, then...”

“I understand”, the mouse interrupted sadly. “I didn't know...”

“That's just what I told him – that you simply didn't know!” she exclaimed, scanning the sky for the Parrot. “But he shouts, ‘I'll eat him! I'll eat him!' Why eat you when we can work things out nicely and peacefully? Right?”

She looked down, but the mouse had already disappeared.

“Oh, I shouldn't have said the Parrot wanted to eat him”, she thought. “But it's too late. Well, what's done is done. At least from now on Gri-Gri will be able to sleep in peace. And besides, you could say, I saved Peglet from being eaten”.

The Parrot was absolutely happy about the successful resolution of his incredibly difficult problem. He walked in circles around Rina and jumped for joy. And of course, he repeated a hundred times that Rina would be very mistaken to think he could ever forget the kind thing she'd done for him.

“So, what now? Shall we go look for your extraordinary ant?” he asked Rina after she, at his request, had repeated the whole story of her conversation with the mouse for probably the tenth time.

“Yes, please, let's go if you don't mind. I'm in a hurry! I absolutely must find the ant today, the sooner the better!”

Gri-Gri didn't need much convincing. He crouched down, pushed off the ground, flapped his wings, and took off from the hill. “Just don't go anywhere!” he shouted, rising above the trees. “I'll be right back! We'll find your ant in two shakes of my tail! Relax!”

Rina waved to him, sat down in the shade, and pulled out her map again, carefully spreading it out on the young grass. According to Herx's map, the Ant Hill was somewhere near the border between the palm grove and the cacti, practically on the shore of the Yellow Brook.

09 – LA FOURMILIERE
part two

So she sat, and waited... and got hungry. She couldn't even remember exactly how many hours had passed since that meager lunch in the ravine under the ledge. Looking around, she saw all sorts of delicious edible treats and threw up her hands in amazement – how could she have walked through this dazzling berry-fruit orchard so hungry, never noticing a thing? And there really were plenty of marvels to see!

First, just ten paces away stood a thick, low-branched pear tree, laden with orange-red fruits like a Christmas spruce covered in toys. Secondly, to the left, tall raspberry stalks rustled with prickly leaves, glinting with a hundred large, maroon berries. Next to the raspberry, a patch of scarlet strawberries sparkled merrily. And above them – a slender palm tree, all made of joy, with her long green braids, weighed down by an impossibly heavy bunch of bananas miraculously supported by a thin stem.

Rina ate a little of everything – a decent snack! – and packed an “emergency supply” in her backpack. Just in case. Three bananas, four pears, and a dozen small but exceptionally sweet apples that she also found nearby. Then she returned to the top, lay down on the grassy carpet in the shade of the ceiba crown, and rested for a long time, listening to the birds and peacefully, in a Zen-like way, contemplating the ever-shifting shapes of little snowy clouds in the bright, high sky.

“It's so weird”, she philosophized. “Over the sands, the sky is totally dry, even dead. But here over the oasis, it's blue and full of water. It's completely different! Is it because of the angle? The sky depends on where you're looking at it from”.

Another half hour passed. Rina figured that Gri-Gri, with his amazing wings, could probably fly around the whole green island of Triazis at least eight times.

“Why isn't he coming back?” she worried. “Did something happen? No, that's not possible! Nothing could have happened. He's lived in Triazis for two hundred years without incident. It just can't be that something bad would happen today of all days – just when I came here”.

Yes, it made perfect sense, it was all reasonable – though how could she calm down? She peered into the distance, but coconut palms covered half the horizon. The Parrot could be anywhere. She even tried to apply the investigative logic from her favorite detective novels, thought it through, weighed the options, but all her deductions led exactly nowhere. Too little information.

After waiting a while longer, she couldn't stand it anymore – she stood up, threw on her backpack, walked down the path, and headed toward the palms.

“In the end”, she reasoned, “Gri-Gri will see me from the sky. And really, Triazis isn't that huge – you can't get lost here, and there's only one path!”

Rina convinced herself that the best thing to do in this situation was to start searching without wasting time. She couldn't forget why – and for whom – she was there in this desert. Gri-Gri was her close friend, maybe one of the best friends ever, that wasn't up for discussion. But somewhere beyond the worlds, her only sister was waiting for her. And no one in all the universe, no one but Rina would help her. So, in reality, there was no choice – she set off to find the Ant Hill alone.

Rina hurried down the path, and a few minutes later, between the trunks of coconut palms, she saw dozens of dull gray dunes in the distance. Triazis ended there. Coming right up to the edge of the last grassy clearing, she stopped and leaned against a warm palm tree's body. In front of her stretched the dreary, sandy endlessness.

“How cool!” she exclaimed. “I won't go back to these sands again! My journey through the Second World will end in this oasis”.

Rina had never grown to like the desert. Not just because of the heat, sun, and thirst. The desert felt alien, unfair, and even cruel. But Rina was right, in her life she would never see a desert again – not this one, nor any other in the worlds. Except maybe online or on TV. And the road to the World Number Three really did begin somewhere here, nearby, in the beautiful Triazis. But where exactly?

With these calming thoughts, she looked back at the forest... and cried out! Wow! Barely ten steps away, in the flickering shade under a small, pointy cypress, a pyramid-shaped anthill was looming. Just a minute ago, Rina had walked right past it – nearly stepped on it – but hadn't noticed anything.

She ran up to the ants and bent down low. Before her eyes seethed and bubbled a hectic, bustling life, such a tiny, yet unimaginably complex world, full of cares, joys, sorrows, sadness, and happiness. Many hundreds of glossy black ants were fussing around, never resting, never slacking, hurrying up and down their invisible ant streets and boulevards.

“I found it, I did it after all! This is my Ant Hill, my Fourmiliere!”

Rina didn't fully understand why she was so sure that some practically indiscernible, completely unremarkable forest ant heap could proudly be called a hill even with a French capital “F”. To get to the bottom of the situation, she checked the map again. Of course – the image had changed as expected. Now, in the center of the picture, a bold black triangle was drawn. The label had disappeared, but the ant had become larger, almost monumental, with long antennae and transparent wings with a reddish tint. Before, there hadn't been any wings at all.

Inside the triangle, the artist had skillfully drawn an old-fashioned key and generously filled it with thick golden paint. Below the black pyramid, right under the hill, was a blue square – also unlabeled. Overall, no matter how you looked at it, the map truly did seem to confirm Rina's discovery.

“Now I just have to find out where they hid the key!”

Of course, any map is just a convention – not even a sketch, let alone a photograph. The key might not mean a literal iron thing or, say, a golden one, but perhaps an entrance to something, a door, like that huge door on the pea field, or an object that serves as a door, maybe a hole, a cave, or perhaps a hatch or a staircase going down. Though it might not be a door, but a paper with a code, or even a papyrus scrap with a spell... Well, on other planets, anything could be the key!

In a quarter of an hour, the ant-heap had been studied top to bottom. Rina circled the cypress several times, then, spiraling outward from it, she examined everything around down to the tiniest speck – but found nothing except grass, sand, and dry, broken branches. She scattered a big pile of forest debris blown in by the wind, but then thought it was silly – the entrance to the Next World might have existed for centuries, and these leaves had only fallen a month ago.

“What should I do? What should I do?” she repeated, finding no answer.

In the end, she decided to get back and look for Gri-Gri. Whatever the case, the Parrot was a great expert on Triazis. Rina was positive that, being in a tricky situation like this, the best course was to seek help from locals. So, she returned, but found nobody. Having no other clues, she headed in the direction where she had last seen the Parrot. Twenty minutes later, after a tough hike through trackless bushes and thickets, she once again saw the gray dunes shining behind the trunks of old palm trees.

But she didn't make it all the way to the sands – thirty feet from the desert, right on the ground, wings spread wide and beak buried in the grass, lay Gri-Gri. He was motionless. Rina screamed and rushed headlong to him. Her blood went cold with horror – she was sure he was dead, perhaps killed by some wandering hunters – but then he stirred and pulled in his wing, settling in more comfortably. He was asleep.

“Gri-Gri! Gri-Gri!” Rina shouted, shaking him with all her might.

The Parrot lifted his head, looked around with a dull gaze, muttered something in a strange hissing language, and buried his beak back into dandelion leaves.

“What's wrong with you? Are you sick? Were you hurt?”

“M-m... me-e?” mumbled Gri-Gri. “N-no-o... Not h-hurt...”

“Then why are you here? You promised to look for the Ant Hill and come back. I waited for you! For a long time!”

“Pro-mised?” the Parrot wheezed and reluctantly turned toward Rina. “Promised... Yes... But I... I got so... so over... o-overwhelmed... that I... from the stress... I got this... what's it called... in-s-somnia”.

“Insomnia?!” Rina frowned, trying to get the Parrot's idea.

“No, no”. Gri-Gri shook his head. “I say over-somnia... It happens, it ha-happens...”

“Well, it doesn't happen to me!” she said, slightly resentfully. “If I promised to do something, I'll do it. Or at least I'll hit them up and apologize for not being able to”.

“You'll hit?” the Parrot also failed to understand. “With your fist, right?”

Rina sighed.

“Okay, never mind. What matters is you're here now. I'm happy you're alive and well and everything's okay”.

“Not well, and not okay!” Gri-Gri objected. “I've got oversomnia. From overstress. If you think oversomnia is nothing and not worth paying attention to, you're wrong. You can't treat your totally ruined health so carelessly!”

“Oversomnia will pass very soon, it's not dangerous”, Rina said, then suddenly remembered, “Hey! I wanted to tell you – I found the Ant Hill!”

“You did? The Hill? Where?” Gri-Gri instantly forgot about his oversomnia and leaped to his feet.

“Right here, nearby!”

“Show me! Let's go! Let's run! I want to see the Ant Hill!” the Parrot shouted, sprinting off in an unknown direction.

After a while, Rina found the path and led him to the ants. Gri-Gri studied the little black fellows scurrying back and forth, he looked at them intently for a long time, then clicked his beak thoughtfully, shrugged, and said with disappointment:

“Don't know, don't knooow. Honestly, veeery doubtful”.

“Well, I don't think it's doubtful. This is the Ant Hill I told you about, and we just need to find the entrance to the Next World!”

“But how? Where do we find it?”

Rina gestured helplessly.

They searched until dark, running around, arguing, even accidentally getting into a bit of a fight – though they made up quickly. The Parrot flew from palm to palm, tapping on the beige trunks with his sturdy beak to check the sound, then he rose to a great height, right up to the clouds, and from there he tried to see some signs, symbols, or geometric figures around the ant heap, while Rina dug round holes in the ground with a sharp stick, then measured the size and diameter of the ant house with her hand, and attempted to track the ants to see where they all were running to – in fact, the friends did everything they could think of to locate the key or that elusive entrance, but all they ended up with were five cute pebbles, an ancient-looking seashell covered with fancy patterns, and two large dried-up horned beetles.

“Listen”, said Gri-Gri. “On your map, the entrance – I mean that golden key – is drawn inside the anthill, right? So it must be inside!”

“No, I've already thought about that. The entrance can't be there”, Rina shook her head. “Maps are never created photographically, I mean like in real life. And besides, demolishing anthills – that's a crime called barbarism. Zinaida Abramovna says – barbarism and outright cruelty! I don't think wizards would hide the entrance right inside the ants' place. That would mean everyone entering would have to destroy the whole ant colony – the ants' entire city, you see?”

“Well then, your words mean we didn't find anything”, Gri-Gri said with frustration. “And anyway, I'm tired. Look around, it's evening already, almost night, it'll be completely dark in no time. I urgently need some rest. Haven't you read in your clever books that birds go to roost really early?”

Rina didn't argue. She herself was totally worn out after this endless, exhausting day. Naturally, she followed the Parrot to his home, to the palm tree with the vines. And there, under the palm, she made a makeshift bed out of two dozen bunches of dry grass scattered here and there. It turned out to be a kind of peculiar, maybe not stylishly designed, but a pretty decent, pleasantly smelling grass mattress. And she used her backpack as a pillow.

She lay down, settled in, covered her legs with soft, warm hay – and the fatigue took over almost instantly, not even three minutes passed before both tired companions, the girl and the Parrot, were deep in sleep under the gentle whisper of the desert wind, which was lazily finishing its games of hide-and-seek in the fluffy royal crowns of wearily capricious coconut palms.

10 – AUNISSA
part one

Rina looked down and saw a deeply unsettling scene: right in the middle of her splendid meadow, vibrantly painted with florets, blue cornflowers, and dozens of scarlet poppies, stood a large, solitary cone of dark-gray sand. And on top of that pyramid – an unusual flower shaped just like a daisy, but ten times larger and with violet-blue petals.

Curiosity overcame Rina. No wonder, all butterflies are inherently inquisitive, especially when it comes to flowers.

She circled it for a minute, cautiously, but finding nothing suspicious around, she deftly fluttered into the very center of the giant daisy and grabbed the yellow stamens with her quick, frantic legs. She smelled the nectar – rich and fragrant, and tasted the pollen – pleasant, sweet, but not cloying.

Then suddenly she heard a strange noise above, neither a hum nor a rustle, very close – somewhere over her head. And immediately after that someone landed in the flower at full speed. But who? Rina had never seen anybody like this half-butterfly, half-dragonfly. The unknown creature gleamed perfectly black, like an obsidian pebble. Its sharp abdomen was elongated, its elastic antennae constantly moving, and its transparent wings with ever-thinner veins were graceful. The next moment, a velvety voice proclaimed solemnly from the clouds:

“Behold the Queen!”

“The queen?” Rina exclaimed.

“Greetings, kind girl!” said the half-dragonfly, her voice a soft, deep soprano.

Rina was even more amazed, first, dragonflies never speak that way – it was one of the rare butterfly languages, actually an ancient secret tongue, and second, Rina was a Meadow Swallowtail, not at all a girl.

“I know everything about you. Or almost everything”, the half-dragonfly continued calmly. “You live on Earth, but you are now journeying to the Kingdom of Fire. Do not be afraid – there is no danger, my intentions are friendly”.

“Who are you?” Rina asked, utterly puzzled.

“I hope you heard it”, the half-dragonfly replied. “I am the queen. I am Aunissa the First, Great Magnificent Incomparable Queen of Ants. But to you, a guest from a distant world, I allow you to call me simply, Great Queen Aunissa. That will do. And now it's better for us to speak in our normal shapes”.

“What does ‘normal shapes' mean?” Rina asked.

“Just a moment”. Aunissa raised her wings and shook them forcefully several times. A whole star shower of white prickly sparks flew everywhere from the wing tips. Rina accidentally inhaled one of the little shimmers and... sneezed.

The dream dissolved in an instant. Rina was sitting on her grassy bed, the deep-blue sky high above, the bright moon a sharp crescent, and around – dozens of dark silhouettes of sleeping trees on a quiet, warm summer night. Suddenly someone lightly, like a feather, touched her shoulder. Rina jumped to her feet, turned around, and saw a beautiful young woman standing right before her in a long black dress, spangled with silver glitter.

“Don't be afraid, it's me, Aunissa, Queen of the Ants”, said the woman in a warm, soul-stirring voice.

Aunissa was beautiful. But the simple word “beautiful” doesn't even begin to convey her brilliance. You might call her fantastically gorgeous. Or perhaps devilishly marvelous. Though not with that paper beauty you can see in glossy magazines.

The Queen embodied the innate allure and regal grandeur of nature – the indescribable splendor of alpine meadows and the dazzling richness of the profound tropical sky, the refinement of sharply colorful July constellations, and the harmonious grace of May's stormy nights. Her huge, entrancing black eyes, her white, seemingly luminous skin, her gleaming coal-dark hair flowing like a waterfall over her elegant shoulders. Her hands were light and sensual, her smile was pure and radiant, her movements were precise and fluid, her charming, magical voice – tender and inspiring.

Rina thought that even if she didn't know who this woman was, she would have guessed it anyway. Yes, Aunissa was the Queen. Yet she wore no crown, and looked nothing like queens depicted in historical films or paintings. In her slender, dainty fingers she held a lacquer-black wand, flaring with the long blue rays of a large sapphire. Rina also noticed neatly folded crystal wings peeking out from behind Aunissa's shoulders.

“I have waited so many years for your arrival”, said the Queen. “But when I first saw you, I could scarcely believe it”.

“You waited for me?” Rina asked, amazed.

“I did”, the Queen smiled. “I counted the passing days, months, years. I waited and knew about you even before your birth, and believed your coming was inevitable. And of course I knew the day”.

“But is it possible to know someone before they're even born?”

“Yes, anything is possible”, replied Aunissa. “Such foresight comes easily to me”. Aunissa glanced at the stars, then looked deeply into Rina's eyes, and spoke more officially, “You are heading into the Fire World, risking everything to save your sister. That is honorable. And of course I will help you”.

“Oh! Thank you!” Rina exclaimed rapturously. “I really need some help, thank you so much!” And she added, “Great Aunissa, Your Majesty”.

The Queen nodded.

“I will certainly help you, kind girl. But you also, if you wish, can help my kingdom and my subjects”.

“Subjects?” Rina asked, but the next instant she realized the Queen meant ants. “I will do whatever you tell me. If I can, of course”.

“Oh, yes, you can”, Aunissa said. “It is not too hard and won't require any special skills or knowledge. Watch closely!”

Aunissa waved her black wand, and suddenly in the air, in the night void, a pale white rectangle appeared, the size of a newspaper page. Within moments it was filled with colored spots. Tiny speckles increased in number, quickly forming patterns, and very soon they became a clear image of Triazis from a bird's-eye view.

“Mini-led”, Rina thought instinctively. But of course it was just a regular softair set.

Meanwhile, Triazis on the screen drew closer, and Rina recognized the Yellow Brook carrying its floral water through the palm grove toward the endless desert sands.

“Now you'll see Sariiim as it looked exactly fourteen years ago”, the Queen said. “This is our capital – the largest city. Without Sariiim, the ant kingdom cannot survive”.

The next scene showed a dark anthill looming on the screen – like a common earthly one somewhere in Carpathian woodlands, or like the one Rina found at Triazis's edge, but ten times as high, far more massive and vastly wider. The Yellow Brook shimmered cheerfully just a few feet away.

“And then”, Aunissa continued, “a powerful hurricane struck us. We call it the Second Vortex, and it was stronger than the First one. The terrifying wind knocked down nearly forty palms and ruthlessly damaged three of my cities: Loriiim, Kariiim, and Boriiim. The storm broke the largest old palm in the Daisy Clearing. The tree fell into the Yellow Brook. That night the stream changed its direction, and since then, my capital, Sariiim, has existed with no water. My soldiers have to carry water from the new watercourse – seventy thousand ant steps a day. My proud city declines every year, and only you can help us”.

“Me?” Rina gasped. “But how?”

“It's not difficult. You must restore the original flow of the Yellow Brook. I'll show you now”, the Queen said, rustling her dress and wings as she stepped aside.

The screen went dark and dissolved. With her black wand, the Queen traced a double figure eight in the air and looked at Rina.

“Don't be afraid”, she said with a smile. “Just take one step”.

Rina inhaled deeply, held her breath as if ready to plunge, and stepped forward. She felt nothing but a faint breeze across her face. She opened her eyes. Everything had changed – on either side, young cypresses and fragile plumerias whispered to each other shyly. Before her lay a circular clearing lit by moonlight, sparkling with dew on daisy petals. The Yellow Brook hurried away across the glade. Its water glittered like thoroughly polished gold, and hundreds of moon crescents bounced off the sharp amber ripples. The stream looked even more lemon-yellow than in daylight. Queen Aunissa stood nearby, right on the bank.

“Look”, she said, “do you see that broken palm over there? The tree that fell fourteen years ago. It blocks the Brook. The water runs along the new ditch now. And here, to our left, is the original dry riverbed. We need the stream to return to that channel, and life will be as it was before the Second Vortex. Do you understand?”

Rina nodded and gave a small curtsey – she'd seen lovely curtsies in Twelfth Night at the theater – and slowly walked toward the water. In the center of the glade lay a long brown log crossing the current. Apparently, the log was heavy, it was literally sinking into the ground deeper and deeper year after year – no way to move it without a special machine. The poor Yellow Brook was forced to turn toward the forest, while another narrow, dry channel branched off, now overgrown with flowering grass.

“This is the old bed”, Aunissa explained, approaching Rina. “For the stream to return to its proper banks, we need to dig here”. She pointed with her wand, “and fill in there. Our task is to build a sturdy dam. Unfortunately, neither I nor my subjects can bring stones from the desert. We used sand and tried again several times each year, but the water is strong. It always washes away our work before it becomes noticeable. I believe only a human can handle this kind of construction”.

Rina paced along the bank, inspecting everything. Then she came back and said:

“All right, I'll try, of course. I don't know if I'll succeed...”

“You will”, the Queen assured her firmly.

They stood silently for a minute, watching the playful golden waves. Aunissa gazed up at the stars and said musingly:

“Tomorrow you will restore life to my legendary city, Sariiim. We'll have a royal celebration – balls and carnivals. You will be our guest of honor. I will appoint you the Valorous Keeper of Yellow Brook and give you the Key to the Third World”.

“So the key really exists?!” Rina exclaimed, breathless with excitement, but quickly regained her composure. “Your Majesty”, she added.

“Yes, the key exists”, the Queen confirmed, “though not in the form you imagine it. But like any other key in the universe, it opens doors”.

Rina pondered before asking, “Your Majesty, Great Queen...” Aunissa turned to her. “How did you know I would come to Triazis, and not someone else?”

“Oh, that's no secret”, the Queen replied. “We learned about you many years ago, even before the Cold Summer, when the black birds of the Fire Kingdom first appeared over the desert. Then, we heard your name and saw your future face in the clouds. That year, we also discovered the dangerous plans of the King of Fire”.

“Your Majesty, please tell me about the King of Fire!” Rina pleaded. “I'm going to him – to his world, his palace – my sister is there, but I know almost nothing about him. Nobody explains anything”.

“That is no surprise”, the Queen said, walking slowly along the old riverbed. Rina followed her. “It's perfectly natural they didn't tell you”, Aunissa continued after a pause. “And aside from that, no one will reveal the riddles of the Fire Kingdom to you, or anything about your true mission”.

“But why?!” Rina stopped, astonished.

“It's simple”. The Queen smiled. “These secrets involve your destiny. When a person knows too much about their possible future, sometimes they lose the ability to do what they must. You see, my kind girl, not all knowledge is helpful. Some information can prevent you from doing what's necessary. Imagine you need fire, but to get it, you must burn your best friend's letter – the only thing he left behind. You have no choice, and so you burn the letter... Do you understand?”

“No”, Rina admitted.

The Queen gently touched her shoulder.

“You are kind”, she said. “That is your greatest strength, your essence and value – do not lose it. Your heart is compassionate, but one day it may outweigh your reason, even your duty, then you will take the wrong road and fail in your purpose. So for now, let your heart worry about nothing. Tomorrow you will travel to the World Number Three. It will be hard there, sometimes very hard and even dangerous, but you will succeed... though only on one condition”.

“What condition?” Rina's voice trembled.

“The main condition is easy to keep in mind, never forget your sister. Whatever you face, whatever complicated choice stands before you, and whatever others say or advise – always choose the path that saves your sister”.

“But that's exactly what I've been doing!” Rina protested.

“Oh, yes, of course!” the Queen exclaimed. “Of course you have!”

They continued walking. Rina pondered what she'd heard and was about to ask another question, but the Queen raised her hand.

“It's time to rest – tomorrow brings work and a difficult journey. Let's not waste time”, she said, waving her black wand.

That same moment, Rina found herself in her grassy bed beneath Gri-Gri's tree. She glanced at the sky and fell asleep the next second. She dreamed. Again she saw the flower meadow and other butterflies – her friends and sisters, but in that new version of the dream she no longer remembered Aunissa, the Beautiful Queen of Ants. Rina the Swallowtail fluttered from flower to flower, sipping sweet nectar and rejoicing in the summer sun and playful breeze. Smiling peacefully and softly humming a trendy butterfly tune, she slept until dawn.

Morning came with the call of Gri-Gri. As all birds do, he went to sleep earlier and woke up earlier than others. Rina opened her eyes, recalled everything, and told the Parrot about her nighttime adventure – fluttering as a butterfly, meeting Queen Aunissa, and the old channel of the Yellow Brook. Gri-Gri listened intently and thoughtfully, clicking his beak, pacing back and forth, wings folded behind him, like a comical detective in a recent noir movie – but more serious than ever before.

Then he said:

“Understood. A complicated business, I would say. And what if the stream won't change course? If nothing works out for you, then what?”

“I asked that too”, Rina answered. “But the Queen says I'll do everything right”.

“Hah! I see... Okay... So when do we go to that log and clearing? We must study the brook and plan our work down to the tiniest detail!”

“We'll go. Soon. Right after breakfast”.

Rina picked up fruits, exactly what she wanted – straight from the trees, just like the day before. She found mandarins – many of them had fallen, but the ones on the tree were numerous and wonderfully tasty. On the right side of the path, most fruits were tropical, some she'd never seen and didn't know the names of, but she ate them with pleasure. Gri-Gri flew off eastward “for a few drops of time”, as he said.

“Just don't fall asleep on the way”, Rina said with a smile.

“Me? Asleep?” the Parrot sounded slightly offended. “Yesterday was a whole different story, I would say. By evening, I was totally shaken. Sorry, but if you think I've got nerves of flint, you're wrong!” And he took off.

Rina finished her breakfast, settled comfortably on the smooth grass under a fragrant magnolia – comically resembling a grandmother's green umbrella – and waited. She sat, watching the pinkish sun rising above the palm crowns, draped in a gauzy scarf of translucent pink. Some minutes later, Gri-Gri returned, and the two friends strode hastily down the path toward the Yellow Brook.

The actual amount of work at the stream proved to be far greater than it looked in the deceptive moonlight. First, the rushing water washed away the soil and clay Rina dropped from the bank. Second, they had no tools, and with only their hands it was literally impossible to dig the ground, hardened over centuries. Rina wandered across the clearing and got lucky – under the hazel bushes she found a sharp dry stick, gnawed by some animal, but good enough to use as a makeshift shovel.

To block such a fast-flowing watercourse, they needed stones – many heavy, large cobbles. Where to get them? The friends unloaded everything from Rina's backpack and headed for the sands, straight into the desert. It wasn't fast and easy, but there was no other way. Gri-Gri helped, scattering the hot sand with his strong legs to find stones the size of two or three fists, and Rina put them in her backpack and carried them to the glade.

Later the Parrot tried another trick – he grabbed one stone with his claws, and if it was not too heavy, he managed to fly up and bring it much closer to the brook. After four hours of work, an impressive mound of gray and black stones had been piled on the bank two steps away from the stream.

“That's enough”, said Rina, emptying her backpack for the thirteenth time, and collapsing onto the grass. Poor Gri-Gri wasn't used to such labor either – he was utterly spent and now sat silently, which was completely unlike him...

10 – AUNISSA
part two

The friends first decided to make a little trench in the yard-thick wall that separated the old brook bed from the new one. Once rested, Rina took the stick and started vigorously digging. The Parrot was grabbing pieces of soil with his claws and flinging them far into the bushes. In less than an hour, the first weak thread of orange water crossed the boundary of the two channels and ran into the spiky milkweed growing on the dried-up bed. Soon a second, and then a third thin trickle followed. Gradually, two channels formed from one. But the old bed lay slightly higher than the new one, and to force all the water into the right path, they needed a strong dam.

Building a dam would have been straightforward if you had the proper materials. Rina and Gri-Gri tossed stones into the water, golden splashes flew everywhere. With each passing minute, the stream grew deeper, the barrier rose. In no time, little yellow rivers flowed into the old bed. When the stones ran out, both laborers tried filling the rest with earth, sand, and clay – and this time, the weakened current couldn't carry away even a grain. The dam strengthened and widened, half an hour later the brook was fully blocked – only occasional puddles remained on the black muddy bed.

Rina rapidly filled her backpack with her belongings and, together with the Parrot, raced along the bank of the ancient streambed, following the yellow water that was merrily gaining its unstoppable power, and then they saw a grand black anthill beside the three thick, fused palm trees. It was truly a whole city, a real capital – over a meter high, six paces in diameter, with five peaks and a round platform between them. The water curved near the palms on the right side and babbled nearby with obvious pleasure – the Brook had finally returned to its rightful, native place! It was working now, diligently scrubbing the petrified bottom, carrying all kinds of forest debris toward the desert – leaves, twigs, sticks, bits of bark, clumps of clay, dry blades of grass.

Rina forgot all about being tired, she was thrilled! Everything had turned out well – actually it was a big victory! Smiling with joy, she cautiously approached the anthill, took a closer look, and right away she saw the Queen on the platform – a half-butterfly, half-dragonfly creature from her dream – standing in the very center, surrounded by two dozen unusually strong-looking ants. The Queen gracefully turned her pearlescent, raven-dark abdomen, stretched and folded her wings, moved all her legs, and waved her long antennae. It seemed she was dancing some kind of whimsical ancient ant dance. Maybe she really was.

Very soon, from every crevice and hole of the capital city, thousands of shiny black ants began crawling up and running down to the sandy surface. The instant they reached the ground, they started forming straight lines and marching toward the water. The Queen now stood at the very edge atop the pyramid and directed the emerging parade with her movements.

“The celebration is about to begin”, Rina whispered stepping slightly aside and watching the ant display.

Apparently as a warm-up, many ants ran across the sand in small groups, shaping themselves into black lines, squares, and circles as they moved. Everything kept shifting and transforming into something new. Then suddenly, the ant ranks arranged themselves into three powerful columns and began to march again, but before long the columns scattered into waves, and then the shapeless masses quickly formed complex geometric figures, mostly polygons, through which ran what looked like inscriptions in an unknown language.

Then, in the letters, many thin vertical stripes emerged and morphed into new symbols again. In this way, the scenes changed one after another – ceaseless running, marching, and countless regroupings. The ant maneuvers lasted for about ten minutes, maybe twelve. Gri-Gri stood by the triple palm tree, frozen, watching the ants. Though understanding nothing, he wisely chose not to interfere or ask anyone anything.

Suddenly, all at once, obviously at the Queen's command, the innumerable ant legions stopped running, stood still as if listening to someone's silent speech, then turned simultaneously to face the anthill – and in just half a minute, the great army, down to the very last soldier, disappeared underground into the black city.

Rina waited. Soon, thirty or forty exceptionally large ants, true ant giants, came out in a line through the main entrance. They were dragging a thread with a white, wire-like ring tied to it. With great effort, the ants pulled it across the sand right to Rina's feet. After that they turned around and ran back in the same formation. Rina watched them go, then squatted down and carefully picked up the little ring for a closer look.

At that very moment, a cold, dense shadow fell on the palm grove, as if a thundercloud had passed over. Looking up, Rina saw that the sun was covered by a dark shiny disc, hovering somehow in the air several hundred feet above. The disc looked metallic and motionless, but it was expanding visibly, covering more and more of the sky. The grove darkened, the wind rose, and birds cried out in alarm. Rina looked around nervously, seeking an explanation. Gri-Gri was genuinely frightened and ran closer to her.

In less than two minutes, the dark object was no longer just a disc – the entire sky had turned coal-black, like a starless polar night, with only faint rays of sunlight peeking through the edges and dimming every moment. Eventually, complete, absolute blackness fell on the desert – the kind you might find only in deep, abandoned catacombs beneath ancient castles.

And then, right in front of Rina's face, emerged a bluish, sparkling mist. She peered into it – there, inside the living, semi-transparent turquoise haze, tiny lightning bolts flashed like golden sparks no bigger than a vein on a butterfly's wing. Rina wanted to touch that microscopic thunderstorm. But suddenly, the blue cloud lit up brighter, drifted a few steps back, stretched upward, and then, with a flaming flash, in an instant transformed itself into Aunissa – just as beautiful as she had been the previous night. Though now, on her white royal shoulders was the thinnest silk cloak, grassy green like Tanzanian emeralds, and in her hand the Queen held not a black but a blue wand, shimmering with a multifaceted cyan topaz. A soft violet beam highlighted the Queen from above. Rina glanced up reflexively but saw no source of the light.

“My dear Kind Girl”, Aunissa said in a melodic voice. “I congratulate you on your great achievement. You overcame forces of nature. You created the proper and restored the demolished. Your selfless heroism will forever remain in our legends and songs. The Yellow Brook, the treasure of our nation, the famed golden current, has returned home. We thank you”.

Aunissa bowed ceremoniously. And Rina returned the gesture. She was rather embarrassed by so much praise and couldn't think of what to say back. The Queen paused briefly and continued:

“Put this phelenyne ring on your finger. Remember, it's not just a gift from the Black Ants. It is the sacred mark of the Yellow Brook Keeper, your eternal high title, which you will pass down to all your descendants for a thousand years”.

Rina put it on. Amazingly, the tiny ring fit perfectly. Aunissa looked at the anthill, then raised her hand and touched Rina's shoulder with the blue wand.

“I, the Great Queen, and my loyal subjects, the Black Ants of the Desert Planet, and all the ants of other planets of the Circle, are grateful to you. Wherever you are, if you need our support, take this ring off and place it on the ground. Ants are small, but they are strong and noble beings. They will help you”.

“Thank you very much, Great Queen, Your Majesty”, Rina finally said, fighting her shyness, and suddenly asked, “But where are the balls and carnivals?”

The Queen smiled.

“The balls and carnivals are already over. Our world lives by different laws, and our time flows asymmetrically here. What is an hour to you is a week to us, and your human month is like twelve years to my people”. Aunissa noticed the shadow of disappointment on Rina's face and added, “Don't worry about that. I know and I see it clearly – you will have plenty of balls, celebrations, and fireworks in your life, real triumphs and grand holidays... But now it's time for me to fulfill my promise”.

Aunissa took a quick step back and suddenly, in a loud royal voice, ordered:

“Bring the key out!”

Instantly following the command, from the inexpressible darkness into the blue cluster of light, four white spheres flew in. To Rina, they seemed to be made of marble – surely very heavy. On the spheres, somehow miraculously, rested a wide, flat dish, and on it sat a silvery box the size of a big apple. The spheres gently lowered the dish onto the sand at the Queen's left and silently floated back into the night.

Aunissa drew two figure-eight symbols in the air with her wand, the box opened, and out floated a small blue-glass vial, swaying as if on waves. Zigzagging, it flew to the Queen and landed squarely in her palm. Aunissa pulled out the stopper and poured all the contents onto the sand. Despite the dim light, Rina could see that inside the little bottle there were... ants, the same ants – many of them. As they hit the ground, they swiftly scurried in all directions and took up their designated positions, forming a clearly outlined black circle. Rina and the Parrot watched the performance, astonished and silent. But the main miracle was still to come.

The Queen said something to the ants in a strange, consonant-only language, then she bent down and stuck her blue wand into the sand at the very center of the ant circle. At that moment the ants rearranged themselves and depicted a new shape with their bodies – a square. As soon as the formation was complete, the sand figure, outlined by the ants, lit up with a sapphire fluorescence. The glow quickly grew brighter and soon became a blinding searchlight, shooting a thick column of blue rays into the sky. A low hum came from the shining blueness, like that of a massive electrical device, the ground trembled underfoot, and the column was slowly but relentlessly growing.

“Farewell, Rina, the kind girl”, said the Queen with a shade of warm sadness in her voice. “It is time to say goodbye”.

Rina looked at her, at Gri-Gri.

“That's all? This is the entrance?”

“The blue light of Auni is your key. The door to the Next World will remain open for eight and a half minutes”, the Queen replied.

Rina turned to the Parrot.

“Gri-Gri!” she said in distress, tears welling in her eyes, “I'm leaving, I must go... nothing can be changed, there is no choice... You've no idea how much I don't want to leave you, I wish I didn't have to!”

The Parrot grew anxious, trying his best not to cry – he stayed silent, blinking his eyelashes sadly – so very sadly. Making an effort, Rina focused, calmed herself down, and turned to Aunissa.

“Thank you very much, Great Magnificent Queen, Your Majesty!” she said with deep gratitude – glittering droplets on her eyelashes. “You are so kind. I will never forget you. I've loved ants since I was a little girl”.

“We know that”. The Queen smiled. “On Earth, not many understand ants the way you do... Unfortunately, we have no time for pleasantries. Perhaps I'll be able to visit you in the future, but now you should go. Go boldly, go courageously! And fear nothing. You will get through everything and overcome it all, just you remember your goal... One little thing: please, when you meet Aoao, tell him we are grateful and have not forgotten him... Farewell, my dear girl!”

“Goodbye! Goodbye, Queen! Goodbye, Gri-Gri! Goodbye, everyone!” With wet eyes, Rina looked at Aunissa's beautiful smile, drew a long breath, and stepped straight into the column – into the powerful blue stream of dazzling light.

Immediately, the blue began to fade until it disappeared completely. Around her – black, impenetrable darkness.

“Your Majesty? Gri-Gri?” she called out. No one answered.

“I see. They're already far away”, she whispered. “I've been transported... Why is it so dark here?”

Suddenly a large drop of cold water fell on her head. Then another one, and a third. And more, and more, and more. Heavy drops beat against her shoulders, arms, and hair. Rain! Water was falling from the black, invisible sky. The rain grew stronger and harsher. Very soon she was drenched to the skin. But the downpour didn't stop – the water kept increasing. Rina felt salt on her lips. She was surprised: there was no such thing as salty rain! Meanwhile the rain turned into a torrent, and a minute later it intensified so much that now, instead of drops, entire waterfalls came crashing down from above, whole rivers of icy water. Finally, all the rivers merged into one and pushed out the air. Rina was underwater. Not knowing where, or how deep – darkness all around. She pressed her lips tightly together. Panic surged through her. She pushed off the bottom and desperately tried to swim to the surface. The water squeezed her down with a crushing pressure, there was no escape. Holding her breath was no longer possible. Rina screamed – air burst out of her lungs like a wild geyser. She realized she was drowning, realized she was dying. She swallowed the disgusting water, inhaled deeply, coughed, and... began to breathe...

It was so terrifying, so frightfully scary, so mad, shocking, and unheard of, but at the same time so... pleasant – Rina was breathing water with her mouth wide open, she was in water that no longer felt as cold, she exhaled it gently, sensing that it was real breathing, like inhaling cool, dense air in a forest glade early in the morning – but it wasn't air, it was seawater, ordinary and regular. It was filling her lungs with thick and heavy, life-saving streams. Breathing water was strange, not too easy, and totally unfamiliar, but Rina understood the main thing – she was alive! She hadn't died! This World was the world of water, the world of endless oceans, in which not only fish could live, but people too...

It all happened astonishingly fast – but that was even better. Rina simply didn't have enough time to become properly afraid – that mortal terror lasted for less than half a minute, and it took just as long to return to a more or less tolerable state. She was almost calm, almost relaxed. Moreover, the most important task had been completed exactly as intended – the inconceivable transition into the Next World was fulfilled!

True, this World wasn't just water and salt – it was darkness too. She had never known that.

She was slowly sinking down to the bottom, and as soon as her sneakers touched it, she tried to walk, but the attempt failed – her footwear instantly plunged deep into the sticky silt, and every step turned into difficult, pointless labor. She stood still for a few moments, then pushed off, waved her arms, and glided forward with sudden ease".

“Funny...” Her tension reduced enough for her sarcasm to return. “Back on Earth”, she thought, “I never learned to float, even six paid lessons didn't help, I couldn't swim at all, literally – and here, look at me, I'm swimming like a white shark. Well, like a denim one, to be exact...”

She rose higher, farther from the dirt and murk, and accidentally drifted into a warm current – a quiet, gentle flow caught her and carried her forward.

“To the Unknown”, she thought with a dark smile.

Anyway, she didn't resist. She lay calmly in the soft, enveloping layers of the underwater river, looking into the matte black veil and waiting for – something. There was no anxiety in her soul, no real fear – she simply wanted to know what would come next. And she didn't have to wait too long...

WORLD THREE – OCEAN
11 – BLASTA

Somewhere in the distance, barely visible blue dots began to shimmer, like the pale lanterns of desert fireflies. The sluggish flow was carrying Rina toward them. With each passing minute, the little lights grew brighter, took shape, and after a while turned into a dozen strange-looking little fish: orange scales, humped noses, chopped-off tails, short fat fins, bulging colorless eyes. Funny little fish, very much like the rubber toys used to distract toddlers during frightening shampoo-and-soap activities. On the forehead of each fish glowed a toy-like but convincingly bright living lamp, lighting up the darkness for many paces around.

The fish had noticed Rina long ago and cautiously spread out to the sides, though still trying to maintain their dignity. With five strokes, Rina pulled herself out of the current and stopped, slowly descending to the bottom.

“I wonder, can they talk or not?” That was the question that worried her most.

For a minute or two, the fish hung motionless and stared at the stranger with surprised expressions on their faces. They were a bit scared, of course. After all, they had come across an unknown creature – a rather large one – but as usual, curiosity won out over fear. One after another, the fish began to circle around Rina, approaching from all sides as if by accident, as though not noticing her, still lighting her up continuously with their foreheads like tiny spotlights.

“Hello, I...” she said and trailed off.

There was no voice. And there couldn't be. She was only opening her mouth, just like the nearby fish, but the word “hello” didn't come out into the water – it was just deep in her head, and yet with more than enough volume and clarity.

“‘Ello!' ‘Ello!'“ came from the left and right, and it was clear no one had spoken aloud. The fish were saying it directly at Rina's mind, right at the very center of it, though the sources of the sounds could be located by both direction and distance easily. A rather unusual sensation – but Rina was pleased. The ability to communicate with locals gave hope for a quick finding the entrance to the next world.

In ocean depths, animals do not speak the way their relatives do in rivers and lakes. Of course they make different sounds from time to time, especially when emotional, but not words – they prefer sending entire phrases directly to others' heads. Rina heard the fish just like voices in pre-dawn dreams. She was surprised, though not too much. Apparently, over the past few days, she had got accustomed to the weirdness of other worlds – she considered it normal.

“My name is Rina”. She tried to smile at each fish present. “I'm a tourist, traveling through different worlds and countries, just sightseeing – and now I'm looking for the entrance to the Fourth World”.

All the fish even stopped moving their fins. As if struck by a powerful blow, they all froze with their mouths open, turning for a few seconds into actual two-dollar playthings from a rubber bath toy distributor. But the next moment, the fish all at once, as if on cue, turned and darted away at surprising speed, creating a vortex behind them, and three seconds later they vanished into the darkness. Rina watched them go, bewildered.

“What did I say wrong? Did I scare them?” But after thinking it over, she decided not to mention the Fourth World directly any longer. In conversations with ocean dwellers, it would be smarter to draw out all the information through veiled hints and roundabout questions. She had to be clever – really! That was the conclusion she came to. Especially since, who knows, maybe this world is at war with its neighboring planets, and any mention of enemies is strictly forbidden.

Swimming through the obscurity in search of the lost current, she spotted another glowing dot – this time it was red, almost poppy-colored. The dot grew before her eyes, and soon it became clear that it belonged to a long, bulky fish with a large flat head. The fish braked sharply and was about to turn around and flee, but Rina said her calming “hello”, and the fish stiffened in still anticipation.

“How have you been?” Rina began with the universal pleasantries. “How are you doing? What's new in the ocean? I came from the Desert”. She chose not to mention Earth either, just in case. “I'm traveling and I'd like to see your world, all the most interesting parts. I'm a tourist”.

The fish remained silent.

“Perhaps you know, dear Fish, what special tourist attractions are found in your area that visiting foreigners usually come to see?”

The fish stared at her with pale-violet eyes full of interest, but obviously wasn't planning to answer. Rina thought that perhaps not all the underwater creatures could talk, but suddenly the fish opened her mouth and pronounced her long-simmered phrase clearly and in a pretty modern Western Ocean dialect:

“Ouni loves Diuni”.

“Diuni?” Rina repeated.

“Diuni boo-boo-boo!” the fish declared quite seriously, and moved a little to the side.

“Boo-boo-boo”, Rina thought with a smirk. “Either she's very young, a baby – that would explain it – or so dull she doesn't even know the simplest words”.

“And where is that Diuni, the one that boo-boo-boos?” she asked the way people talk to one-year-old infants.

“Boo-boo there, Diuni there”, the fish answered eagerly and wagged her tail, just like a joyful puppy. It was clear that the conversation brought her great pleasure. But Rina sighed and thought:

“Aunissa was right – this world isn't easy. If everyone in the waters is this clueless, who's going to help me get out of here?”

After several minutes of perfectly pointless dialogue, Rina tried to get away from the useless companion – she told her “thank you very much”, waved her hand, and swam off, leaving the poor little dusky fish in confusion and disappointment.

For at least half an hour Rina swam through the amazingly thick darkness, trying to move perfectly straight, arms extended forward in case she collided with something solid, a rock, for instance. The black water didn't frighten her – on the contrary, it brought a sense of calm and a kind of unfamiliar carefree detachment. It was easy and nice to float in emptiness, feeling neither the weight of the water nor the cold, not even her own body. It seemed like thoughts and emotions were dissolving into the eternal oceanic night, like sugar in tea...

But then, ahead, a bright flash flared. A strong, white beam – like a powerful aircraft landing light – pierced the tight blackness of the seafloor. The ray stayed motionless for a minute and lit up stones, coral fragments, fish skeletons, empty shells, and all kinds of marine junk scattered in abundance across the bottom. Rina had never suspected the sea could be so dirty and neglected.

As for the white light – at first, thinking it was a submarine or another underwater machine, Rina was thrilled. Even if there weren't exactly humans inside, operating such a complex apparatus meant these beings must be intelligent and educated, so she could talk to them in a human-like language or something similar, and eventually ask about the cherished entrance to World Number Four. Smiling, she rushed headlong toward the white light, which itself was also approaching at a good pace.

But soon she realized her major mistake and was dreadfully frightened – there was no real submarine. Instead, scuttling quickly along the bottom with massive thick limbs and raising towering clouds of black muck, came two enormous octopuses, the first one shining with his mighty headlight. Both were the size of delivery vans, both had dull grayish skin and looked extremely aggressive. Their every movement radiated tremendous energy and a readiness to attack and kill anyone in an instant. Rina stiffened in terror.

Very soon, the first octopus noticed her and, shooting forward a long, strong tentacle, grabbed her, wrapped her entire body so tightly she could barely breathe, and dragged her off to who knows where, without stopping for a second. She didn't try to break free – fear paralyzed her will, thoughts scattered from her mind like a frightened swarm of fish, and only her heart kept pounding loudly in her temples.

Being in that state of stupor, she completely lost the ability to sense space or time. It wasn't clear how many miles the octopuses crawled along the muddy floor and for how long – it could be half an hour or half a week, who's counting. But every journey comes to an end.

At first, the gray cephalopod monsters had been looking for someone. They had most likely grabbed Rina by accident, without any particular purpose. They were really interested in someone else – they checked behind every rock, every little hillock, shoved their arms deep into cracks and round holes on the ocean bed, thoroughly rummaging, searching for the target only they knew about. But after a short while, the octopuses got tired of this tedious work and gave up, clearly putting off their hunt until a better time – and without distraction, sped along a deep canyon, dramatically lighting up jagged coral ledges with the lifeless white beam.
