Rina swayed side to side, pinned by a slimy octopus's arm to his enormous nape. And though she had come to her senses, terror still kept her from linking her wits into a chain of logic. Anyway, she was sure they were just going to eat her – and that would be the end of the adventure. But the octopuses had an entirely different thing in mind...

In the end, they arrived at their destination and stopped all at once. With difficulty, Rina lifted her head and saw a vertical stone wall rising into the dark. There in the flat gray rock, loomed a big oval hole – possibly an entrance to underwater rooms. The octopuses were waiting for something, lazily stirring their muscular tentacles.

The wait dragged on, and the octopuses were clearly losing patience, their activity quickly increasing – until, at last, from the murky blackness crawled out two giant crabs, whose very appearance made Rina's knees quiver – the red crabs, each the size of a large dining table! Their gleaming pincer-claws were huge, like the gaping jaws of crocodiles, and their blood-hued shells bristled with sharp, hard spikes jutting in every direction. The crabs moved in sudden jerks, causing the octopuses to shudder with their whole bodies each time.

The larger octopus holding Rina shifted his grip to her waist and gently placed her on the sandy floor. She was petrified and tried not to breathe. Without hesitation, one of the crabs jumped toward her, its bony legs clicking. She curled into a ball and squeezed her eyes shut, expecting a bloody attack – but the crab delicately grasped her by the left sneaker and slowly dragged her into the dark burrow. The second crab followed closely behind his boss.

A minute later, the crabs laid her on a large smooth stone somewhere deep within the cave. After standing there for a moment, they left. She lay motionless, focusing intently on the fading clatter of the crabs' steps. Very soon, all was silent. Darkness – no sound, no movement, no light...

Unexpectedly, the cave walls lit up with a faint yellowish glow – it looked like a church candle appeared in the corner. Rina slowly turned her head and saw an astonishing variety of sea creatures – fishes of every shape, color, and size, squids, several purple octopuses, two blue moray eels, and many others, most of which she had never seen before and couldn't even imagine existed – on Earth or anywhere else.

The cave was inhabited. Wall-to-wall. Rina had become just one more resident – a prisoner, actually. It was immediately obvious that the Gray Octopuses and their friends the Red Crabs were using this place as a prison. Rina sighed, sat more or less comfortably on the stone, and began staring into the unblinking eyes of her fellow lifers crowding across from her, all curiously observing the stranger. And everything in complete silence.

Then a large white fish swam up, with an orange lantern at the very tip of its nose. The fish was as long as Rina herself, if not longer, and its sharp tail, black-tipped, reminded her of the tiny tails of clown loaches – the pet fish of Rina's best friend, Zlata Sorochenko. Rina scooted back a bit, just in case. She clenched her fists and braced to defend herself. But the fish wasn't planning to fight. In an even, feminine voice, she asked in fluent Mid-Oceanic:

“What's your name? Who are you?”

For half a second Rina was surprised that she understood yet another language – but really, what difference did it make which language was being used? What mattered now was something else entirely. Instinctively, she realized that the smartest thing in this situation was to tell the truth, answering politely but firmly, and without fear.

“My name is Rina, I'm a human, I came from the Desert”.

“And my name is Blasta”, the fish said, trying to smile. “I'm a Palakila fish and I live here in the Dark Ocean... lived”, Blasta corrected herself with a trace of gloom.

“What do they want?” Rina asked after a short pause.

“Who?”

“The octopuses!”

Blasta glanced toward the entrance and answered:

“They don't want anything. They just catch us and throw us in the cell”.

“But why? Are you criminals or something?” Rina didn't get it.

“Are you a criminal?” Blasta asked in return.

“Me?! No! I didn't do anything!”

“We didn't do anything either”. Blasta waited, then continued, “Basically, to the Belmons, it doesn't matter whether you're a criminal or not, a bandit or a law-abiding citizen. They grab you and drag you here. We should at least be grateful they don't execute us or starve us. Belmons are stupid and cruel. They believe this is how to maintain order in the country, less freedom – more discipline! That's their favorite slogan. The Belmons have no brains”.

The crowd of prisoners murmured in agreement, clearly supporting Blasta's view.

“Belmons – those are the gray octopuses?” Rina clarified. Blasta nodded. “But if they lock everyone up, who's left in the country?”

“They don't care, they never think about things like that”. Blasta swam over and gently settled onto the stone next to Rina. “Like I said, the Belmons are empty-headed. They don't need anyone. They never build houses, not even burrows, they sleep immersed in warm silt – that suits them fine – and feed only on algae”.

“Algae?!” Rina was stunned. She was sure the vicious octopuses were savage predators – no doubt.

“Yes, algae”, Blasta confirmed with a ‘told-you-so' face. “They eat white scemitta, which grows everywhere here, and the roots of the intulis plant – their favorite treat. They're herbivores. Otherwise, there wouldn't be any prison. If it weren't for the algae, the Belmons would've eaten all of us in a minute!”

“But when will they let us go?” asked Rina.

A stifling silence fell over the cave. Blasta sighed, silently lifted off the stone, and drifted a few yards toward the exit. Suddenly she spun around and exclaimed angrily:

“Haven't you realized yet? They'll never let us go!”

“What do you mean, never?” Rina's soul rejected the very idea of it.

“Exactly that. Never!” Blasta snapped.

The crowd buzzed again, but soon quieted down and began slowly spreading out into the cracks and corners of the cave.

“Listen!” Rina said. “I don't have much time. I can't stay in your country for too long, I simply cannot afford it! Because I have a very important mission – time is running out – I must reach the Fourth World...”

The moment she said “the Fourth World”, every creature in the cell stopped moving at once – as if this was a cartoon paused with a remote. Blasta froze momentarily too, but then quickly came to life, darted up to the baffled Rina, and hissed, utterly agitated:

“Don't ever say that again!”

“But why?” Rina whispered in shock.

“Because there is no Fourth World. And there never was. The Fourth World doesn't exist, all the Worlds are just in your imagination – a fantasy, a myth, a void!”

Rina gestured with her hands, ready to argue, to prove that Blasta was wrong, that the Universe held not only the Fourth World, but the Fifth, the Sixth, even the Seventh one, and that... but Blasta frantically shook her head and yelled:

“Quiet! Quiet! Stop talking! Now!”

Rina grew scared and fell silent. The fish hovered right in front of her face, staring into her eyes without blinking.

After a moment of quiet, Blasta said:

“Remember, if you want to live, it's best not to ask questions like that. Don't forget – we're prisoners here, that's true – but we still kind of enjoy being alive”. Blasta nodded toward the other inmates sitting around, and the yellow-orange ray of her lantern drew a looping pattern on the stone wall. “If the Belmons hear even a single word about other worlds”, she went on, “they'll just knock us out with hyglyphia leaves and bury us deep in the mud somewhere in the Rotten Dale”.

Of course, Rina desperately wanted to know why talk of other worlds was forbidden, but looking into Blasta's sorrowful eyes, she decided not to press the matter.

Everyone calmed down, fell silent, and hid in their corners. Many turned off their headlamps. It was dim in the cell. A long, depressing stillness settled around...

12 – THE BREAKOUT
part one

To distract herself from heavy thoughts, Rina began, as she put it, a scientific inspection of the cave. She wanted to study the place from top to bottom, to examine the cell down to the tiniest crevices.

But soon she discovered that not every detail was easily accessible, because each ledge and hollow was already occupied – little faces peeked out from every crack. Even some of the stones on the floor weren't just slate, but the roofs of hidden shelters. Moray eels, crabs, octopuses, sea stars, oysters, scallops, and mussels, along with other mollusks, kept to the bottom. A bit higher, squids, shrimp, crayfish, cuttlefish, and lobsters inhabited every niche and burrow alongside creatures totally unknown to Rina. And above them were all sorts of fish – some didn't even look like fish, and only their rhythmically moving gills gave away who they really were.

The cave's population mostly consisted of small sea animals, each about the size of her palm, but a few of the larger fish, two lobsters (two giant brothers), and one unidentifiable being had claimed massive flat rock slabs as their beds. And among the largest, as one of the cave's most influential residents, was Blasta.

“At least I found a decent place”, thought Rina. “Sleeping on the bare floor, even underwater, isn't exactly great”.

Meanwhile, as she mused, Rina was gradually drifting closer and closer to the black tunnel, floating almost inside it. Suddenly, a sharp wave struck her from behind – someone grabbed her, spun her, and forcefully pushed her back into the cave.

“Where are you going?!” Blasta yelled in outrage. It was obvious she was the one who had shoved Rina, trying to hurry her away from the entrance. “Have you lost your will to live?!”

“What happened?” Rina was shocked. “What did I do?”

“Nothing. Just a little more and there'd be two of you! Half the size each!” Blasta snapped, darting aside and twitching her tail tip anxiously.

“I don't get it!”

“Crabs! Red crabs! The jailers!” Blasta shouted throughout the cave. “Didn't you notice? They guard the tunnel. You saw their claws. Blades like that can slice you in half in an instant! Crabs are thick-skulled – they hear us, but they can't even speak! They were ordered to guard the exit, so they guard it. They don't care whether you tried to escape or just drifted sleepily toward the gate caught in a cave draft. They'll slice you up, end of story!”

Rina stared at Blasta, her mouth open. A chill shot from her neck to her toes. She didn't know what to say – except maybe to thank the fish for saving her. But Blasta wasn't waiting for thanks. She was swimming circles around the cave, clearly still rattled and trying to calm down.

“Sorry”, Rina finally said, pulling herself together. “I didn't know. Besides, there's no sign or plaque – nothing to show where you can actually swim or what's off-limits”.

“Why is it so unclear?” Blasta asked, baffled. “The closer to the exit, the more limits you get!”

“True enough”, thought Rina. “So simple...”

This brutal prison simplicity filled her with such hopeless grief that she wanted to cry. But crying salty tears into salty water was pointless anyway...

Attempting to ease the tension, she decided to change the subject:

“So... when will we be fed?”

“Soon”, Blasta replied curtly, but after relaxing a little, she added, “In about an hour, they'll bring it”.

Rina noticed that some fish were also circling the cave, still unsettled by the recent incident.

“And what will they treat us to?” Rina smiled, emphasizing the word “treat”.

Blasta didn't take it as a joke.

Rina clarified, “There are so many here, and everyone's so... different. I guess you all eat various dishes?”

“Dishes?” Blasta's jaw dropped at the idea. “What dishes are you talking about? They'll drag pieces of vura-vura in here and that's it. Vura-vura is a soft coral – a type of hybrid growth. Everyone can eat it because some consider it a plant and others an animal. Of course, it doesn't taste like much, but you'll get used to it. At least we're not starving to death in here”.

Suddenly there was a tapping sound as if someone were clicking their fingernails on a big piece of dry wood:

“Tlee-tlee-tik. Tlee-tlee-tik”.

Rina listened closely.

“What's that?”

“Crab music”, Blasta grinned.

Rina gingerly peeked into the tunnel and there, in the dim light, she saw two dark, grotesque silhouettes of huge crabs sitting at the entrance. One of them had lifted both claws and was knocking them rhythmically against his companion's back as if playing a xylophone. The crab's shell gave off three different tones, and the result really did resemble a kind of music – something like the exotic sounds of Brazilian timbau drums. Rina had been lucky enough to hear a full batucada in Sao Paulo during the trip there with her dad. Of course, the crabs' timbau was much quieter:

“Tik-tik-titik! Tik-tik-tlik! Tik-tik-titik!”

The crabs were clearly into it, their rhythms becoming sharper and more expressive.

“Usually this lasts half an hour. Or longer”, said Blasta. “They're brainless. Nothing in the world interests them except mud-crawling snails and this drumming. When they drum, they forget everything – their shell color even changes! Once I tried swimming up to them during their concert – they didn't notice me. Didn't see me at all!”

“But if the crabs don't see anyone while they're playing music, then... then you could sneak through the gate, slip past and swim out, right?”

“That's exactly what's impossible”, said Blasta sadly. “Look! Before they started tapping, the crabs moved into the tunnel, right up against the gate. Know why? To block the whole exit with their bodies. Next time look at them – on their heads, they have an entire forest of sensitive spines and hairs. If you so much as touch one, they'll snap awake and slice you into paper-thin strips in an instant!”

Rina thought for a moment and asked:

“Is it always two crabs drumming, or could there be three?”

“There could be three or four, as many as you like”, said Blasta.

“And they'll all sit there and listen and notice nothing?”

“Probably, yes... Why?”

“Oh, no biggie”. Rina smiled. “I haven't come up with anything yet”.

But in truth, a brilliant idea had occurred to her. She simply wasn't ready to share it with her friend until the entire plan was completed. “A worthy project is solid inside and out”, like Rina's Dad says...

In any case, after thinking hard and planning carefully, she decided to carry out the planet's first underwater psycho-music-engineering experiment – in those exact words. She got up, swam low along the floor, and picked up two small stones that looked like basalt. One was flat, about the size of an espresso saucer, the other was round and light brown – it looked almost like a walnut. Then she returned to her spot. Blasta watched, mouth agape. Rina took the saucer-stone in her left hand, the walnut-stone in her right, and gently tapped the “nut” against the “saucer”:

“Tik!” Then again, “Tik-tik-ti-tlik!”

The sound was almost indistinguishable from the tapping on crab shells – only a bit sharper and without bass tones. Blasta grasped the idea immediately. She stared at her new friend, her huge Palakila eyes wide and unblinking. The totally stunned fish was slowly sinking to the bottom, forgetting to move her fins.

“You're a ge-e-enius...” she whispered and flopped belly-first onto the stone floor.

The two companions felt triumphant! But after pondering for a moment, Rina asked:

“Wait, do you know a popular crab tune they especially love?”

“Of course!” Blasta wrinkled her face. “I have to listen to these dumb serenades every day in here!”

“And can you sing one for me?”

“Sing?” Blasta spread her fins. “You can't sing that. You can only tap it out!”

However, unlike humans or crabs, fish didn't have hands or claws. After struggling and attempting every possible method of knocking, Rina and Blasta together invented an astonishing, perfectly original fish technique called Teeth. Blasta had never had any interest in music, but this time she took the plunge – and was amazed. Who would have thought that her very own jaws, which she'd used actively since her early childhood, could be turned into a universal and quite handy musical instrument?

“Clack-clack-cla! Cla-cla-clack!” Blasta chattered her teeth.

“Tik-tik-tlee! Tlee-tlee-tik!” echoed Rina, tapping the round pebble against the dish-shaped stone.

In just half an hour, they had managed to learn the all-time favorite crab knocking tune, which, by the way, was far from simple, but fortunately, its pattern greatly resembled the New Year's classic “Jingle Bells”.

“Ti-ti-tik! Ti-ti-tlik! Tli-ti! Tli-ti! Tik!”

After quietly tapping the jingle around ten times in a row, Rina and Blasta agreed to test the power of music magic right after lunch.

Soon the crabs completed all the numbers in their musical program and, after standing motionless for a few minutes – probably coming to their senses after the “aesthetic enjoyment” – crawled off in different directions. And a little while later, both of them returned, carrying two huge loads of freshly sliced grayish-pink chunks of vura-vura coral on their flat backs. Like two robotic dump trucks, the crabs offloaded the coral bricks in the middle of the cave and left. The entire prison community floated out of their burrows and holes and swarmed toward the long-awaited food.

12 – THE BREAKOUT
part two

Surprisingly, the vura-vura coral proved to be tasty. Though perhaps that was just because Rina had gotten rather hungry by then. Still, the flavor really was quite pleasant – somewhere in Europe, this kind of food would definitely be considered a delicacy. Either way, the coral meat was remarkably nutritious and resembled something between a French cheese soufflé and those vegan “Vici” crab sticks Mom used to buy at Varus. Satiated, Rina took out a handkerchief, carefully wrapped up a fist-sized cube of coral, and hid it in her pocket, “Might come in handy”. Blasta paid no attention to this maneuver. Rina asked:

“Well, can we do it now? Are we ready to start the show?”

“No, we aren't, still too early”, Blasta answered, straining to chew her twentieth piece. “The crabs will be back soon – they're going to identify us”.

“What do you mean, identify?” Rina didn't follow.

Blasta explained. Within a minute, Rina found out that red crabs, despite being so thick-skulled and witless in every other way, possessed one unique ability – perfect visual memory. They couldn't count, so keeping written records of prisoners was out of the question, but they could spot the absence of any inmate instantly and then identify who they were – what species, color, size, and so on. And then transmit the image directly into the minds of Belmons. After that, it wasn't hard for the octopuses to hunt down the runaways – unless, of course, the fugitives had gotten very far. Every day, the crabs made the prisoners line up in ranks to be “identified”.

That's exactly what happened that evening, the crabs opened the grate and, barely squeezing through – their shells scraping against the tunnel walls – crawled into the cave, oddly enough, not one by one, but as a pair, literally belly to belly, and began clicking their jaws, waving their claws rather aggressively. The inmates reluctantly shuffled toward the center.

The process of identification didn't take long, but it was a grim and humiliating ordeal: the crabs stared at each cellmate with their dead white eyes, and that ghastly gaze made Rina's heart freeze. Finally, they lumbered away.

“Now's the time!” Blasta whispered conspiratorially. Rina nodded, scanned the cave as if Belmons' spies might be hiding somewhere, picked up her priceless pebbles, and tapped, “Ti-ti-tik! Ti-ti-tik!”

All the inhabitants watched the strange, and for most, totally incomprehensible performance without blinking.

“Louder!” Blasta shouted, peering into the darkness of the tunnel.

Rina knocked harder:

“TI-TI-TIK! TI-TI-TIK! TI-TI! TI-TI! TIK!”

“Again! Again!” Blasta demanded.

Caught up in the excitement, Rina pounded the “nut” with rising fervor, swept away by each bar of music. The sharp, sonorous tapping rang in her ears, the pebbles tried to slip through her fingers. The rhythm of the kids' song, familiar since her childhood, consumed her fully, and she even began humming it:

“Jingle bells, jingle bells:

Jingle all the way:

Oh, what fun it is...”

“Faster, faster!” Blasta kept ordering.

“...to ride in a one-horse open sleigh, hey!”

A minute later, Blasta finally dared. She cautiously swam up to the grate, peered through it – and wildly ricocheted back as if she'd been stung by a sea wasp. A glow of victory shone on her happy fishy face.

“They! Are! Listening! They're listening to us!” she shrieked with such joy that Rina faltered for a second and almost stopped tapping. “They're listening! They're listening!” Blasta yelled across the cave, unable to contain her excitement.

Rina rose from her berth and, still tapping, swam with difficulty toward the exit. Through the tunnel, she saw the two giant crabs frozen in the yard five steps from the grate. The crabs weren't moving – they looked like wax exhibits in a natural history museum, their shells yellowed, dimly gleaming with amber traceries.

“They're asleep! Our music put them into a coma!” Blasta whispered in awe – and then... she slipped outside through a wide gap in the grating.

The crabs didn't even stir. They saw nothing and heard not a sound except Rina's pebbles. Without breaking the rhythm, Rina followed her friend's lead and gently floated out to the outer platform in front of the entrance. She stared at the crabs, barely believing her eyes – they both stood motionless on their hind legs, goggling at each other with cloudy, glassy eyes, their horrific claws spread wide...

“Keep tapping!” shouted Blasta. “I'll be right back!”

The fish made an ecstatic spin and lunged back into the cave. Rina's fingers were beginning to ache, but she continued tapping just as clearly and rhythmically. Very soon, a flood of prisoners came pouring out of the tunnel. They all had worried, yet joyful faces. Blasta hadn't needed to convince them for long. As soon as they saw the fish swim out and come back unharmed, the convicts understood everything – and ran! They rushed off at full speed – with all their legs, tentacles, and fins – thrashing, jostling, and scattering in all directions as fast as possible. Blasta flew up to Rina:

“Hurry! Let's get out of here! Now!”

But both of Rina's hands were occupied with pebbles. She was swimming, though her turtle-like momentum clearly didn't satisfy the fish. Blasta quickly looked her friend over from all sides and, finding a more or less workable solution, slipped the tip of her snout under the strap of Rina's backpack and began to pull.

“Louder! Tap louder!”

Rina, with completely numb fingers, kept tapping the “saucer” as hard as she could, slipping out of rhythm two or three times, but the crabs didn't notice anything. Suddenly, after a particularly strong strike with the “nut”, the saucer-pebble cracked in the middle and split in two. From behind Rina, Blasta couldn't see the catastrophe that had occurred. She shouted:

“Keep playing! Tap it! What are you doing? Don't stop! They're close!”

“I can't!” Rina cried out desperately. “The instrument broke!”

Blasta was a clever fish. Though she didn't entirely grasp what exactly had broken, she understood the main problem, the crabs were still nearby! And the only thing that could save the fugitives was speed.

But using her snout to drag a big, heavy, and completely un-hydrodynamic girl was too difficult for the fish. Blasta dived under her friend and commanded:

“Grab onto me!”

Rina instinctively shoved the nut-pebble into her pocket and gripped the fish's smooth, firm fin with one hand. Blasta surged forward with such mad force that Rina nearly dislocated her forearm – but by some miracle she managed to hold on to the very edge. Blasta was speeding at a crazy velocity unheard of in the underwater world, rising up along a steep spiral, higher and higher, farther and farther from the prison.

After seven minutes of this wild vertical sprint, the fish stopped and, gulping water greedily through her large mouth, asked:

“Every! Thing's! Good! Are? You? All? Right?”

“I'm okay, I'm fine. Just my fingers hurt”.

Blasta caught her breath, thought for some seconds, and said:

“No need. To rush now. No crab or octopus can ever make it. This high. If you want, climb onto my back. I think you'll feel more comfortable there”.

“I'll try, thanks”.

“Thanks? What are you talking about? It's me who should thank you! If it weren't for you, for your invention, I'd have spent my whole miserable life rotting in that cell!”

“Yeah”, Rina nodded, “we both would have rotted there... But still, look at us, we really pulled it off!”

“Pulled it off? More than that – we did great! You couldn't ask for better!”

“It was like a movie”, said Rina, straddling Blasta's back cowgirl-style.

“Like what?” Blasta couldn't make sense of the word.

“Well... like a fairytale”. Rina changed the comparison, assuming they likely didn't show movies in the ocean yet.

“Exactly, a fairytale!” Blasta agreed readily. “And just imagine what the crabs are doing now!”

“Oh, I can imagine! They're thrashing around the empty cave like raging lunatics, biting the walls with their claws out of fury”.

“Totally! And tomorrow they will get it!” Blasta added gleefully. “They're going to catch hell! Belmons are ruthless, they never joke around – they've got no sense of humor whatsoever!”

And thus, happily chatting, the two lucky friends swam off into the darkness, putting more and more distance between themselves and that dreadful, eternal slammer. Their spirits were higher than the largest ocean mountains. Rina was telling some cheerful nonsense about life at her summer cottage, Blasta was laughing, and both were certain that the worst times were finally behind them, and only success and victories awaited them ahead.

Life will be bright forever, life will be wonderful with no shadows ahead! It was unbelievable – they had defeated the Red Crabs and the horrible Belmons. There, in the entire Dark Kingdom, no one could even dream of such triumph.

But of course they were both wrong – there were far more difficulties and dangers in the Underwater World than anyone could imagine. The next trap was already visible: deep below, a pale, foggy, golden patch of light began glowing.

“Boz!” Blasta exclaimed. “My hometown! My beloved, my precious city! Boz!”

And turning sharply left, she began her spiraling descent.

13 – THE CITY OF BOZ
part one

Although Boz was rich and famous, on the map it was just a typical undersea settlement, one of dozens. It was inhabited mostly by fish, squids, crabs, lobsters, starfish, sponges, and other bottom-dwelling creatures. The word “boz” was a distorted “bossoz” in the ancient language of palphins, and it meant ‘number three'.

Why three? Very simple. Long ago, this seabed was nothing but silt in the darkness – light-glowing corals don't live in muck. But one day, burgundy stars appeared, though no one knew where they came from – they never said anything about themselves, and their origins were a complete mystery.

When the burgundy beauties reached this place and found not a single enemy here, they decided to stop wandering and declared the wasteland their national territory. Sure, this violated the law, but the muddy lot was of no use to anyone, the authorities did not oppose. Fine! The stars settled. But living in darkness was difficult and uncomfortable. Then, to the great joy of all, Keyona, a young cousin of the tribe's chieftain, stumbled upon a yellow, self-luminous citrine stone. Soon, the stars washed hundreds of such citrines out of the muck, and the golden stones, like festive lanterns, lit up the dark valley and its surroundings. Life got better, life got merrier!

Over time, thousands of yellow citrines found throughout the area were arranged into light pyramids on new plazas and streets. And when the star colony grew stronger, they constructed a star monument – a huge Column adorned with golden lights laid out in patterns in strict accordance with the mystical worldview of the three-rayed creatures.

Soon, the totally useless backwater had been transformed first into a neat village, then a town, and gradually into a decent city – a natural center of underwater activities. Inevitably, the place began attracting folk of other settlements – many ocean floor dwellers headed there. And the wise stars didn't make relocation difficult. They offered space, light, comfort, and orderly urban life to everyone who wanted in. However, every newcomer had to accept the absolute superiority of the burgundy starfish over all others, no matter their wealth or position.

Well, living in Boz was easy. The only irritating clause there was the Star Time – stars had biological timers inside, they would wake up, eat, work, rest, and go to sleep at precisely scheduled minutes. And naturally, they expected everyone else to live by the same scheme. Many of the incomers couldn't withstand the rigorous rules and left the city, but most stayed.

Over the years, Boz grew, prospered, and became a local capital. The neighboring areas kept merging into the starfish realm one by one. And quite spontaneously, perhaps for the first time in the Galaxy, a new state was born within the vast empire with no destruction, no violence or wars. Later it was named The Golden Duchy of Boz. Predictably, most of the stars held positions of ministers, generals, and other high-ranking officials. The country was ruled by a former “Miss Sensation 66”, now considerably plumper and older, but still ambitious and clever Grand Duchess Aditta.

Blasta was born in Boz. Though due to her restless nature, she was a notorious wanderer and often swam a hundred miles here and there – sometimes toward the Tepid Currents, or to the White Mountains, and even as far as the Plain of Mercy, where she managed to survive in strong currents of icy water. But each time she returned to her hometown, where, as a long-time resident, she had a rather cute underground apartment.

Blasta fully accepted the stars' routines, paid her taxes, and never clashed with the authorities – except for one incident when she was caught eating in the wrong place at the wrong time. But after paying a vexing fine of ten red shells, poor Blasta was once again listed among the law-abiding citizens of the glorious city of Boz.

Rina sat atop the fish, her eyes wide as she gazed at the approaching underwater wonder. She could already make out straight, narrow streets, perfectly round plazas, ideal checkerboards of meticulously arranged parks and squares, and the angular zigzags of roads disappearing into the darkness. All of this was in a pure amber light. From above, the city looked like a mosaic, assembled from tiny plates of the finest gold. The sight was magnificent. Hard to believe, but most of the creators – the stars themselves – had no idea of the elegance and splendor they had built. Stars disliked swimming. Instead, they busily crawled along the sandy lanes, never glancing to either side.

“It's so awesome here!” Rina exclaimed.

Rina had almost forgotten the Belmons and red crabs, together with the fears that had so recently tormented her. She didn't want to think about that. She looked at Boz with amazement and wanted only one thing: to go down as quickly as possible and walk along the golden streets.

Her little dream came true in just a minute. Blasta landed in the middle of a square, at the center of which, a sharp-pointed five-foot-tall pyramid glowed with the charming light of polished yellow stones, illuminating everything around. As Rina would learn later, they were held together with a special compound that the stars kept as a state secret. But no one except the stars knew that the very top stone of every pyramid was always left unglued – perfectly in line with the romantic ideals of the New Holy Star Commandment...

Rina stood up, stepped onto the sand – it crunched under her sneakers like the first snow – and, full of excitement, she declared:

“This is like magic! This doesn't happen in real life!”

Blasta didn't reply – she was busy inspecting the square and peering into the streets. The only thing that mattered to her at the moment was the question – what time it was now? Unlike the stars, Palakila fish couldn't tell time, and Blasta had no desire to pay an absurd, unfair fine for improper use of her own body.

The square was empty, and Blasta concluded that it was night in the city – the citizens were asleep in their burrows according to schedule.

“We'd better hurry”, she said with concern. “I live on Three Lights Street – it's not far”. Blasta pointed with her snout. “About five hundred of my lengths. If you're tired, I'll give you a lift”.

“No, no, thanks, I'd rather swim by myself”, Rina replied. “I want to see it all, it's so beautiful!”

“Yeah, beautiful – waking time, sleeping time, administration, patrol squids, penalties...” Blasta grumbled under her breath.

Rina wasn't listening – she swam almost like a dolphin, turning her head left and right, unaware of Blasta's nervously twitching tail as the fish impatiently waited for her too-slow friend.

Fortunately, Blasta's home really was nearby, and they encountered nobody along the way. Blasta slipped into one of the round holes, carefully decorated all around with tiny bluish shells. She poked her snout out and whispered:

“Hurry, please! This way! Swim in!”

Rina entered. Almost immediately after the wide entrance, the space narrowed into a tight corridor, and at the bend her backpack bumped into a protruding corner, forcing her to squeeze through. But just beyond the twist, the tunnel opened into a spacious oval room. Rina looked around with great curiosity – it's not every day one gets to visit a real home of an ocean fish, let alone at this unimaginable depth.

The room was furnished modestly – it was practically bare, if you didn't count two cylindrical stones of unknown purpose and a large flat piece of basalt – clearly Blasta's bed. But everything – walls, floor, and ceiling – was studded with holes and recesses of all shapes and sizes. And in the center of the ceiling, a big glowing cube-shaped citrine was attached, serving perfectly as a chandelier.

“This is how I live”, Blasta said a bit bashfully.

“I love it!” Rina was delighted. “If I hadn't seen it myself, I would never have believed that fish live in such large, cozy apartments!”

“Well, when it comes to size and comfort, I wouldn't go that far”, Blasta replied, wrinkling her snout. “You see, I'm no rich fish – my income is average, and so is my average home in an average district. Now, if you ever visited someone on Triple-Three-Light Street, that's a different story”.

“I don't want any triple-threes, I'd rather stay here with you. And honestly” – Rina looked around the room – “I do really like your place”.

“No kidding?” Blasta asked skeptically.

“Oh, you're so suspicious – it's almost insulting!” Rina exclaimed, swam over and hugged the fish tightly.

Having studied the apartment, now being rested and relaxed, Rina thought about the map. So much had happened that day, so many incredible events – all so fast and dramatic – that the map hadn't crossed her mind even once. But now, having fully recovered and being at peace, Rina returned to the main question: where was the entrance to the next world? The map! She had to check it immediately.

She took off her backpack, which she hadn't parted with for even a second all this time, and untied it. Blasta stopped breathing – literally lost the ability to move her muscles. Rina noticed her extreme surprise and asked:

“Something's wrong?”

“You... you...” Blasta mumbled. “You fell apart!”

Rina looked at the backpack and burst out laughing.

“Oh! Nobody fell apart! That's not me, not a part of me, it's a thing, you understand? A thing that you wear on your back, and you put other things inside it. If they fit, of course”.

With bulging eyes, Blasta hovered at a cautious distance, but finally dared to come closer – though she didn't stop worrying until she had touched the backpack with every one of her fins and thoroughly examined everything inside.

Amazingly, the map was still intact – it hadn't soaked through and wasn't water-damaged. In fact, it felt dry – at least it had the same glossy surface as on land.

“This isn't paper”, Rina thought. “But why am I even surprised?”

Checking the map was a bit scary. Rina squeezed her eyes shut and unfolded the smooth cardstock by feel. She opened her eyes, looked – and went numb. The map was blue. Completely! From edge to edge, a solid coat of indigo. And of course, the little ‘figure-in-a-skirt'. This time, the girl was made of white dots – a dotted outline. What did that mean? No one knew. But no path was shown, no symbols, no writing. Or wait – there was something! Not on the blue background, but along the thin white border, almost at the very edge, yes, something was written in tiny violet letters, she had to swim closer to the chandelier to read it.

lighT oceaN

Exactly like that – with a capital “T” and a capital “N”, that was all the information! The little figure in the middle – the girl in the skirt – and to her right, lighT oceaN. Not a single other note. And how was she supposed to interpret that? Well, okay, the main thing was clear – the entrance to another world was somewhere in the Light Ocean. Fine. But how to get there?

Blasta didn't know where the Light Ocean was located, let alone in what direction. Yes, she was a prominent traveler, but even her boldest voyages had rarely gone beyond a hundred miles (later Blasta explained that “a mile” was just one thousand of her own lengths, so all the miles in the kingdom were strictly personal).

“Well, the inscription is to the right of the girl”, Rina reasoned. “And she is facing me, feet to the South, head to the North, which means her right side is West”.

Rina grabbed her compass and opened it.

“Hooray! It works!”

The whole time, Blasta had been watching her guest with undisguised confusion and a touch of awe. The fish didn't comprehend a single thing – not in the slightest. The only explanation that came into her clever head was that her friend was performing some kind of paranormal ritual – or maybe even witchcraft – and it was best not to interfere.

Finally, having decrypted the map and put it back in her pocket, Rina decided that it was a good time to tidy up her so-called vestments (in Aunt Vera's jargon). Some of the things were dirty (though there was no soap anyway), and the top button was hanging by a single frayed thread – hanging on by pure stubbornness, as Grandpa-2 would say. Everything needed repair.

She took out a needle, but the moment she pulled the jacket off her shoulders and was about to start sewing the button back on, Blasta slipped back into her deep coma-like stupor. Rina had to spend much time explaining that the denim jacket wasn't her skin, and that taking off clothes wasn't too painful or life-threatening…

An hour passed. Blasta, in turn, showed Rina her oddities and valuables, which, predictably, were tucked into holes and nooks everywhere around the walls. There were colored pearls, colored corals, blue, black, and green shells, purple pebbles, two dwarf shark skulls, pieces of volcanic glass, bones, claws, and even a petrified eye of a giant white spider, which had lived in that province ages ago. And she showed her personal bank: right under the basalt bed there was a hole concealed under a plate of gray slate. There were two hundred eighty-seven red shells (Blasta was good at counting), and forty-two transparent ones – a rather significant amount of money, by the way.

But her most prized treasure was a scarlet bomur antenna. A bomur is a large crayfish. Once, Blasta spent a month on the slopes of Mount Beu-Oss, where she met a highly educated old crayfish named Rinsho. The day Blasta decided to move back home, Rinsho gave her one of his antennae as a present.

Bomur crayfish are known to shed their antennae every ten years, the old ones fall off and new thin sprouts grow in their places. At the moment of detachment, the fallen antenna changes from dark brown to piercing red. Nowadays, bomurs are rare, almost extinct, and their antennae are considered pretty expensive jewels.

Next, Blasta treated her friend to sea macaroni, which was in fact nothing more than young shoots of Cayoma. When Cayoma is a baby, it protects itself by mimicking the shape of the poisonous jellyfish Agamia. Later, it grows long limbs and becomes a copy of the dangerous octopus Tastod – though much smaller. And as it matures, Cayoma stretches even more and transforms into a charming underwater tree. Once a season, large fish snap off its fragile new shoots to eat them raw, but if you hide them under rocks for a week – they become softer and wonderfully flavorful.

So, the two friends were lying on a warm flat stone, chatting without a care, laughing and chewing the fabulous macaroni. Life was good! But they didn't get to finish lunch: suddenly, right above the entrance to Blasta's apartment, an ear-splitting howl sounded – just like a steamship in old movies. Rina jumped up in fright.

“What's that?!”

“Don't be scared”, Blasta smiled. “That's just the ‘Reveille' call”.

“Reveille?” Rina remembered a boy named Andriyka from the fourth floor – a freckled, white-browed lad who always told stories about his scout camp, where mornings started with ‘Reveille!', and evenings ended with ‘Lights out!'

“REVEILLE FOR EVERYONE!” Blasta called out playfully, then explained, “In the whole city, only the stars have clocks – no one else can tell the exact time. So we have a crew of signal-squids. They send signals six times a day, and the signals are different. Right now, it's for the morning routines – everyone must get up, have breakfast, and get ready. After that comes the Announcement Time – people gather at the Square of Rays and the Star Administrator announces the beginning of the new day. Later the squids signal again, it'll be time for work and study. And so on”.

“And you live by the signals?” Rina asked, bemused.

“Of course!” Blasta was thrilled at the chance to share the utmost brilliance of the city's signaling system. “You can't imagine how convenient it is! They signal, and you do. No need to think, no need to remember – just listen to the signals and that's it. There's nothing more rational than living by signals and schedules – especially if you don't have a clock. Or do you disagree?”

“I don't know yet”. Rina shrugged, confused. “I haven't tried your schedules so far”.

But in truth, as a Ukrainian girl, raised in a society based on rights, democracy, and freedom, she felt very differently – she just didn't want to offend her new friend. Rina imagined how people in Kyiv would kick this signal-sender and chase him out of the city with dirty brooms, if he dared to tell folks when to get up and what to do next. Very funny!...

13 – THE CITY OF BOZ
part two

A little later, the two friends were moving side by side along a brightly lit central street. Dozens of other fish and sea creatures were hurrying toward the Square of Rays.

“So, right now your star is going to announce the new day?” Rina clarified.

“Totally! She'll make the announcement. We should not be late. You'll see how beautiful she is! She can raise her arms like this, can you imagine? And she flies like this! And she can curl up into a ball! And right after that the new day starts”.

“Interesting... And what if your star doesn't announce anything, doesn't raise a single arm, doesn't curl up – what happens then?” Rina asked with a naughty smile. “Does the new day still begin, or does it not start without her at all?”

Blasta halted so abruptly that Rina found herself two feet ahead.

“What do you mean she doesn't raise her arm?” Blasta's eyes grew huge.

“Well, I don't knooow”, Rina drawled, holding back her laughter. “Let's say she had stuffed herself with rotten coral last night and now she has a stomachache. Terrible constipation, you see? Or, simpler yet, someone didn't notice her in the dim hallway and stepped on that arm, and now, poor thing, she's stuck in bed for the whole week, moaning and groaning. Or maybe she fell off her wardrobe”. Rina could hardly contain her giggling. “Yes! Imagine this: your Administrator takes her chamber pot in her teeth, climbs up the wardrobe just to take a short break from all the announcements and flights, and then – ka-boom! – she crashes down from the height right onto the sharp rocks! And breaks the pot! Full of... you know”.

Blasta's eyes, now crimson, seemed ready to shoot out of their sockets. She opened her mouth wide, glistening with a hundred prickly teeth, and froze, paralyzed by the rebellious words. But soon she recovered more or less, and, choking on her fury and rage, shouted:

“How could you? How dare you say such things about our kindest Great Administrator?”

Two dozen fish swimming past stopped instantly and turned to examine Rina with curiosity. Blasta got a grip on herself, swam closer, and whispered:

“Listen, you don't understand anything! It's not like that! It's all completely... completely... not like that!” She glanced at the onlookers and added, “Let's get out of here, quick!”

Rina sighed and followed...

Soon they arrived at the Square. It seemed rather different from all other squares not only in size, but also in shape – it was triangular. According to Blasta, there was no more than a minute left before the beginning of the announcement procedure. Practically the entire city population had gathered in that packed area, and those who came too late had to crowd onto the adjoining streets. Rina looked around for a platform, a podium, or at least a big rock from which that large star might address the people. But the square was as perfectly flat as a beaver's tail.

Then everyone fell silent at once. Rina searched for the cause of the sudden hush, glanced up – and gasped, right above the square, a hundred meters up, something ominous began to glow. Her first thought was an alien spaceship – triangular, dark-red, and apparently, very heavy. Descending sinisterly fast, at that.

Before long, she could see that the ‘ship' was made up of numerous glowing fish, scarlet with multiple black stripes. The fish were large and arranged vertically – packed tightly into the shape of a three-pointed star. And sitting right there, perched on the fishy snouts, was the star herself. The fish moved their tails in rhythmic coordination, trying to keep formation as they descended, until they reached the ground.

Rina had the impression that the fish-ship had landed right on the heads of the townsfolk standing in the center. Maybe it really had. From where Rina and Blasta stood, it was hard to see the finer details.

However, within a minute, the red-fish-platform lifted off the square again, allowing Rina to get a better look at the Great Administrator, a sea star of impressive girth, dark cherry in color, her arms dotted with two lines of beige suction cups. She had spread herself regally across the fish – not on their sharp noses anymore, but on their broader, more comfortable backs. The fish had shifted position and now their long bodies formed two layers of something resembling a floating stage.

The star silently surveyed the hushed crowd of loyal subjects with a victor's gaze – and then arched her back just like a cat, standing on all three of her rays, and in this strange pose sang out in an astonishingly beautiful, enchanting contralto:

“Boooz weeelcomes yooou, my friends! I see you've rested weeell! And are ready for useful and noble deeeeds!”

The crowd hummed with awe and approval, but immediately fell silent again.

“We hereby declare and celebrate the commencement of the one hundred, six hundred, nine hundred and fifth day of the Big Counting! Let the new day begin! Let us return to our worthy responsibilities, to work and study, to all the jobs we do in the name of our incomparable Duchy, and to the glory of Lady Boz the Grand Duchess!”

The star lifted off from the live platform, and like some unearthly three-winged bird from Martian fantasies, soared upward. Rising above the spellbound crowd, she froze for a few seconds – then suddenly she shot out her rays in three directions like lightning, and instantly curled into a tight, dark crimson sphere, and then dropped from there like a massive boulder back onto her stage.

Obviously, the audience had been waiting for that exact trick. The crowd practically exploded, fish, crabs, octopuses, and squids – all jumped and shouted, thumping tails and arms, clacking claws. Rina was terrified: the horde had gone totally insane. She stared at the folk, her eyes wide. Then she spotted Blasta – and didn't recognize her. The always clever, judicious, and calm Blasta was now frenzied, spinning like a whirligig and squealing in an absurd, high-pitched, non-fishy voice.

After about three minutes, the crowd began to tire of the excitement and gradually quieted down. Rina looked toward the center of the square, but the burgundy star was gone. When or how she had swum away was up in the water.

So then, the new day had been proclaimed. The crowd of citizens, now slightly subdued but smiling contentedly, began to disperse – swimming and crawling off to go about their business. Rina silently trailed behind Blasta, looking at the streets thoughtfully. The radiant, golden, magical city no longer seemed so wonderful to her. On the contrary – the shinier the lavishly decorated burrows, arks, columns, and grottos were, the more unpleasant and repulsive they appeared. Rina felt a growing disgust for everything around her, she wanted to swim away from this horrible place and forget it forever.

She looked at Blasta – and an unbearable wave of pity pierced her heart.

“The deceitful stars are fooling the folk!” Rina thought mournfully. “They've tricked Blasta! She doesn't understand that the stars aren't needed at all. New days begin without them. Everything happens without them. They're just like all others – no better, no worse, no one is obligated to obey them. Everyone should live in freedom!”

Blasta twitched, as if she had heard Rina's indignation. The fish turned around, surged closer, and said sharply, though trying to keep herself steady:

“You may think whatever you want. But you're wrong”.

“Listen! You are...” Rina began loudly.

She was ready to argue and debate, she wanted to explain what the stars were doing to the inhabitants and why. But she glanced into the hollow fish eyes and remembered Blasta spinning and squealing at the sight of a cheap circus stunt. Rina stumbled mid-phrase and fell silent. She only sighed hopelessly and swam on...

Someone touched the sole of her sneaker. She turned quickly and screamed – behind her, elegantly swaying his springy limbs, floated a large, pink, thick-bodied octopus covered in raspberry-colored spots. He looked at her with serious, unblinking eyes, gently bobbing, almost as if dancing.

“Excuse me, my lady”, he said in a deep, rich bass, slowly lowering himself almost to the sand, “I carry a special message from Her Highness for you. Could I invite you to visit our Star Palace?”

Rina had no idea what was going on. Palace? What palace? Why?

“Her Highness the Grand Duchess offers you a brief audience”, the genteel octopus said, bowing on his tentacles.

Blasta hovered nearby. Though she had heard everything, she didn't dare come closer. Rina looked at her for support, but Blasta quickly turned her gaze away.

“An audience?” Rina repeated.

The octopus bowed once more, and remained in that waiting posture. Rina mentally raced through all possible responses, found none appropriate, and asked:

“But... but when... when will this audience be... taking place?”

“Oh!” the octopus exclaimed, straightening and swaying. “Immediately following the Social Stroll and preceding the First Ducal Luncheon!”

“I don't know where your palace is. I've never been to this city before. And besides, my watch has stopped, and...”

“There is no need for madam to worry”, the octopus interrupted with a third, maximally courteous bow. “A personal Ceremonial Carriage will be sent to you by Her Radiance the Administrator”.

“W-well, all right”, Rina said uncertainly, and after a pause, added – doing her best to keep up the formal tone:

“Please convey to Her Highness... uh... that I do accept the invitation to the brief personal audience”.

The octopus made a pompous, deliberately exaggerated bow, scraping one of his limbs – well, technically a tentacle – across the floor in an old-fashioned way, and asked without straightening up:

“Would the most esteemed lady be so gracious as to state her most deeply respected full name? For the record”.

“Sure, for the record...”

She assumed an air of playful grandeur, lifted her chin, cleared her throat (in her mind, of course), took a deep breath, and solemnly declared:

“Writeth thou this doun! I am Rina, thay Champion of Pan-European Races, thay Conqueror of Black Sands, und thay Keeper of thee Yellow Brook!”

The listed titles crushed the octopus hard – he had never been so profoundly overwhelmed in his life. He bowed much lower now, dripping with servility. After enough groveling to satisfy protocol, he managed to squeeze out a proper phrase, switching from a booming bass to a nasal tenor:

“Please, permit me, Lady the Greatest Keeper Conqueror, to most humbly withdraw and inform Her Grace, the Grand Duchess, of your acceptance”.

“I permith thee!” Rina intoned regally, barely keeping a straight face.

The poor octopus, bent so low he was practically sweeping the floor with his forehead, backed away, tail-tentacles wiggling, and without turning around, began slinking off toward the Square of Rays.

“Unheard-of honor!” Blasta shouted, rushing up to Rina. “Such trust! Such reliance! You can't even imagine what this means, and what a...”

“...what a load of nonsense!” Rina finished. “As for your duchess, honestly... I couldn't care less! I'm only going so I can figure out which way to swim next. I suppose if she doesn't know herself, there should be some brainy crab, or squid, or whatever else in the palace who knows something about the worlds and the ins and outs”.

“A-a-ah!!!” Blasta jumped back as if she had been pricked, she turned even paler – though it seemed impossible. “No-no-no!” she shook her head desperately. “Just don't say that! Never! Don't even mention the worlds! I'm begging you!”

“Okay, okay! Calm down! I'll ask some other way. No worlds. Chill, please”.

Back at Blasta's apartment, Rina asked:

“Didn't the Administrator say everybody must go to work?”

“She did. So what?” Blasta blinked.

“Then why aren't you rushing off somewhere?”

“Oooh, that's what you mean!” Blasta flopped onto the basalt and stretched out full-length. “Doesn't apply to me. I'm a fish – and you know, fish don't work. I mean, some do, if they feel like it, or if they suddenly need to pay for something. I also take gigs from time to time. But generally, no”.

“Why not?”

“Fish are a free people!” Blasta declared, with a weirdly proud tone. “I live in this city, I'm a local, but I have the right to swim off wherever and whenever I want. I do whatever I please. Others cannot”.

“I still don't get it”, Rina said. “You're not explaining anything”.

“Oh, but it's super simple! In our city – and across the whole duchy – anyone born with six directions is independent! Fish and stars can go left, right, forward, backward, up, and down. How many is that?”

“Six”.

“Exactly! Six! But almost everyone else only gets five – four main ones plus down! Their problem is that five doesn't divide by three!”

“So what if it doesn't?” Rina was totally unable to grasp this fish logic.

“What do you mean, so what?” Blasta leaped up on her stone. “Dividing by three is the key to everything!”

“They really are, I don't know… dumb, maybe”, Rina thought with annoyance. “They care about things that don't matter at all. The pointless stuff normal people wouldn't even notice. And the much larger things that really matter – they don't even see them...”

Waiting for the fancy carriage – which Blasta claimed was decked out in ultra-rare pearls and was the priciest ride in the whole duchy – Rina decided to take a nap. She was just wiped. The day had been too much, too heavy, too weird. And even though napping like this was actually against the city's schedule, Blasta took pity on her guest and didn't say a word. She just pulled a special shell over the chandelier, filtering the light into soft, warm amber illumination that made the room feel pleasantly dim and magical – like one of those summer nights at her dad's Carpathian friends' cabin in the woods. Rina yawned, rolled from side to side the way she always did, curled up, closed her eyes – and immediately fell asleep.

Silence and drowsiness – those were the perfect words for the next two dreamy hours. Blasta hovered under the ceiling, wrapped up in thoughts, while Rina snuffled softly through her nose. Everything stayed like that, flawlessly still – until a big-muzzled orange squid burst into the room like a tornado, paying absolutely no attention to the shaded light, and loudly summoned Rina – her heart detonating against her ribs – to his carriage. Or more precisely, to his seashell.

Hardly able to keep her eyes open and bumping into every corner, Rina swam out of the apartment and, still half-asleep, plopped into the shell that looked like a ten-gallon enamel basin – slippery and surprisingly uncomfortable, but sprinkled all over with pearls and all sorts of shiny sea trinkets.

Blasta swam closer and exclaimed in rapture:

“I'm so jealous of you! There you are, going! To speak with the Duchess herself! With the Great Duchess!”

Rina didn't reply. For one thing, she was not completely awake. For another, she didn't want to hurt her friend's feelings, but truth be told, she wasn't exactly brimming with excitement over the upcoming audience.

“Come back quick!” Blasta pleaded. “Don't forget, our alley's called Three Lights Street. Ask around – anyone will point you there”.

“No need to w-worry!” snapped the official-sounding squid, clicking his tentacles into a salute. “Im-mediately after the audience, the L-lady will be returned by a special ess-cort to the exact l-location!”

Blasta sighed. Rina waved. The squid barked something unintelligible to the three long gray fish strapped to the shell. They rose, pulled against their straps. The shell creaked along the sand, tilted – and suddenly lifted off, sailing above the grottos and glowing pyramids, carrying Rina deep into the most distant corner of the undersea capital. She held tight to the rounded rim, gazing down.

She wasn't thinking about anything... well, not really. One weird, sleepy, utterly ridiculous thought managed to sneak into her head:

“I've never flown in a helicopter”, she thought. “Of course, this isn't a copter at all, but I bet the view is pretty much the same”.

The Duchess's whimsical carriage – a glistening shell – rocked gently in the slow opposing current, and floated farther and farther away, leaving street after street below.

14 – CHAOS IN PALACE
part one

Funny as it was, the Grand Duchess had completely forgotten about the invitation. Truth be told, she'd been blessed – or cursed – with what they called “childish forgetfulness” ever since she was a baby. Plus, on that particular day, Her Highness was entirely preoccupied with the final moves in a game of starfish checkers.

The thought of losing to Marquis Bien gave her a backache, and although she longed to win, all her advisors could clearly see a brutal defeat coming. Some of them were so afraid of her imminent wrath – they had not slept, frantically sketching hundreds of possible strategies in the sand. Naturally, Marquis Bien himself had no intention of clashing with the ruler, but on the other hand, his position was high, his authority absolute, and his wealth and estates – more than vast.

So he didn't have much to lose. He figured if fortune was kind enough to hand him the win, then teaching the old shrieking despot a little lesson at the checkerboard might actually serve the state's best interests. At the very least, it would be the first notable palace event in years. And the palace needed events the way deep-sea astarias required fresh currents of salt water.

Since the Creation of the Ocean, the palace's main ritual had been the Snail Juice Ceremony, a gathering of nobles around white ceremonial stones, sipping sour juice from long spiral shells while engaging in overly intellectual small talk.

Juice is juice – but what do you talk about? For three years straight, all the palace folk had been discussing the underwater hurricane that swept in a bunch of strange creatures now housed at the city zoo. These beasts, unlike anything anyone had ever seen, had a bizarre talent for mimicking any sound they heard. Which quickly turned them into a comical attraction – just say a few words near their cage and the hobnons (as they were called) would immediately belt out whatever you said, loud and in your own voice, no matter how inappropriate the initial phrase was.

The Duchess often visited the cage. She never spoke to the hobnons – too undignified – but she sure enjoyed it when one of her courtiers whispered some flattery through the bars, and the hobnons, who had no ability to whisper, bellowed it back at full volume, utterly unaware of what they were saying.

But since hobnons were just dumb animals with no real brains, the repetitions weren't always accurate. That led to all sorts of mishaps. Like the time the Highest Secretary of the court whispered to them for Her Highness:

“No deep-sea roses compare to your beauty...”

To which the hobnons loudly repeated something like:

“No cheapsy noses tom chairs to your booty!”

The result? The Duchess, roaring with laughter, ordered the executioner to award the poor fellow twenty cheapsy slaps on his booty immediately – to the rollicking delight of the crowd. The Secretary couldn't move his left ray for three days and hobbled around pitifully, which made the whole court laugh even harder.

Before long, there were some clever tricksters who made up gibberish all day long and deliberately pronounced it to the hobnons either too fast, indistinctly, or too quietly, causing them to repeat the phrases with funny distortions. And for a while, the utterly boring Snail Juice Ceremony turned into a riotous affair full of jokes and gags, with the hobnons as the unwitting stars, clueless, brainless, but ridiculously weird.

Then the weeks rolled on. The jokes got stale. Everyone stopped laughing. The courtiers ran out of ideas, the humor dried up, and the hobnons became so tiresome that just hearing their name made people groan. The gray days crept back, filling the halls with a dull green boredom.

And then – out of the black – Her Radiance the Star Administrator returned from the equally dull ritual of Announcing the New Day and brought the Grand Duchess a refreshing little tidbit, a mysterious creature had appeared in the city, never seen before, indescribably strange and extraordinary – apparently much weirder than the hobnons themselves! At the very least, the Administrator was confident that this entity could perform some rare stunts, play pranks, or provide any other pastime – that was essentially all the Palace expected.

The only problem was, no one knew where the creature had come from. What if it was from the Light Ocean – that is, from the enemy? Or worse, what if it had drifted in from beyond the Warm Currents? No one in the entire duchy had the foggiest idea what horrors lurked past those waters. Long story shucked, the Grand Duchess was intrigued and had no choice. She had to summon this ‘whatever it was' with all due ceremony (well, with minimal ceremony, of course), just to see if it could be useful. Maybe it would turn out to be good for something – say, social entertainment. Or maybe not.

Without delay, she dispatched the octopus Mullibu, Junior Master of Throne Procedures, with orders to bring the creature in for an audience.

But then a squid-messenger showed up from Marquis Bien with the next move scrawled right across the squid's forehead. The Duchess made that move on her board – and gasped. The Marquis was threatening a double kinging! She instantly forgot everything else and called an emergency session – not just with her highest advisors, but also with their assistants, their squid-consultants, and shrimp-secretaries. Because in her Duchy, she might have tolerated anything – even war – but a third loss to that dandy upstart Bien? Never!

So when Rina finally spotted the palace buildings at the end of her long flight, and her carriage touched down on astaria-white sand in front of a lavishly patterned arch glowing with fifty yellow stones, and the squid officer politely bowed and pointed toward the entrance with the tip of his tentacle – a wall of crab-guards suddenly burst out of a tunnel, blocked her path, and nearly skewered her with their bone-tipped spears.

Clearly, not only had the Grand Duchess forgotten the audience, but the entire palace staff had too. Totally caught up in the checkerboard crisis, the personnel hadn't informed the guards. And if the Star Administrator hadn't happened to hear the commotion outside her window, Rina might eventually have been turned away. Not that she had any idea where the palace even was in the duchy or how to get back to the Street of Three Lights.

In the end, the Administrator sent two housemaids, and the whole embarrassing incident was resolved in the easiest way: by scaring off the guards. Waving long, mysteriously glowing red sticks and shouting something like “ba-ya-ba-ya!”, the squid maids drove the guards back underground and escorted Rina up with proper respect.

The moment she swam into the Administrator's quarters, she was surrounded by the bustling office fish and various subordinates. They were all talking at once, pushing and poking her with their noses – and Rina could have sworn that one or two of them were even trying to taste her ear. But then the Administrator slipped inside, and the frightened minions scattered in all directions, like beetles under a lit lantern.

“Good day”, Rina said as politely as she could, bowing immediately and deeply, thus skipping other formal preliminaries.

“Good New Day”, the Administrator replied, circling Rina for a full inspection. “What reason compelled you to come to our glorious city? Where are you from?”

“I'm a tourist”. Rina launched into her prepared line. “I travel from place to place and have come here from the desert to explore your famous duchy and all its sights”.

“The De Zert?” The Administrator widened her eyes. “I've never heard of such a kingdom De Zert. Is it beyond the Currents?”

“No, it's above”. Rina pointed at the ceiling.

The Administrator glanced up at the stone arches, thought for a second, and said:

“I see. And you say you travel – yes?”

“Yes, I do. That's true”.

“So then, up above, you traveled too. Meaning, you don't actually belong to that De Zert. Is that correct? In other words – you're from somewhere else. So where are you REALLY from?”

“This is turning into an interrogation”, Rina thought, slightly irritated. Aloud, she said:

“You're right, I'm not from the desert. I live in another... another...” The Administrator leaned in. Rina continued, “in another place”.

“Ah! Another plaaace!” the Administrator exhaled in relief. “That's fine. Very fine... Well, no matter. Are you familiar with the conditions of residence in our city?”

“I am”, Rina nodded. “Everyone must live by the signals”.

“Exactly. Though that's not all”. The Administrator raised her right ray and now waved it in sync with her words. “The main condition is that the citizens of our glorious city must obey the stars – and follow their every command. Every command, literally every one, you understand? Even if it seems unclear, incorrect, mistaken, or anything else – you MUST still do exactly what the stars say. Immediate and absolute obedience to all star directives and recommendations! Do you agree to these terms?”

Rina sighed and thought to herself:

“It's okay. I'll be here for maybe five hours, tops. I can survive that”. And aloud, she said, “Yes, I agree”.

“Excellent! All that's left is to complete your city registration, and then you can stay here as long as you like. Although...” – the Administrator gave Rina a thoughtful once-over – “Although, your size. I suspect the Management will have some trouble finding a suitable spare apartment”.

“Thank you, but I don't need one anymore”. Rina smiled, shaking her head. “I'm staying with Blasta”.

“Blasta? Who is Blasta?”

Rina explained. Naturally, she said nothing about the prison. No names had been recorded there anyway. Yes, of course, the Red Crabs had burned her face into their memories for the rest of their lives – but they couldn't talk.

A few minutes later, having gathered all the necessary details about the foreign visitor, the Administrator personally escorted Rina to the Upper Hall, which served as the reception area for honored guests. The Palace, which looked so dazzling and luxurious from the outside, turned out to be nothing more than a massive coral hill riddled with tunnels, passageways, caves, and grottos – many of them completely undecorated, and some not even lit.

“Wait here”, the Administrator whispered, nodding toward a broad flat stone – probably very comfortable for stars.

Rina decided to act a little more like a real tourist, since that's what she claimed to be. She floated leisurely along the hall, studying the abstract-looking statues placed along the walls and the even stranger drawings carved into the niches – no idea what tool had been used. It was actually pretty interesting. Like a museum tour...

Some time passed. Soon she grew bored and started thinking it wouldn't be too bad to pick up her interrupted dream where she'd left off once she got back to Blasta's. In that dream, she was a hungry fawn wandering through the autumn forest, diligently searching for mushrooms – the white ones, especially...

She couldn't recall the entire dream – there was a noise, and at that moment, a swarm of tiny beige squids burst out from a square opening in the wall. One soon separated from the group – the largest and plumpest of them all, obviously the squid boss. Wriggling, he floated up to Rina, stopped, hovering at a respectful distance, and announced in a well-trained childish baritone, loud enough to make him blush a little:

“Her Highness the Grand Duchess Boz, Patroness of the City of Glowing Stones, Sovereign of the Blue Current, Ruler of Mount Beu-Oss, grants you, Lady Rina” – he stressed the ‘na' – “Conqueror of Deserts and Keeper of the Yellow Brook, a brief audience in the Red Hall!”

Rina listened carefully and then followed the haughty little squid in a calm, unhurried manner. The others lined up behind in two rows, forming something like an honor guard.

In this bizarre order, the entire procession entered the tunnel and emerged into a truly scarlet room – not very large, but well-lit by glowing red stones. For a moment, Rina remembered Uncle Olezhko's physics lab, which she had visited a few times, where cherry-red lanterns always burned, making everything look like another planet. (“Our own little Mars”, Uncle Olezhko used to joke.)

14 – CHAOS IN PALACE
part two

On the left side of the hall stood a multi-ton light-pink stone with a flat top – resembling a giant Frisbee – but its edges were trimmed with a fringe of fine needles that glistened silvery in the shifting light. Reclining on the stone was a star – presumably the Ruler herself – who differed from the Administrator in that she was perhaps half the size, and looked considerably older. Rina bowed and glanced around. The walls radiated a heavy ruby glow. It was impossible to stay in such a place for too long – an eerie, ominous-looking room with the dark red ceiling looming menacingly overhead.

The squids rose higher and hurried back into the tunnel. Rina paused at the entrance. The Administrator was whispering something to the Duchess, who kept glancing at the visitor with great interest, eagerly twitching the tips of her rays.

“Come closer, Lady Rina”, said the Administrator, and glided three paces away from the Duchess.

Rina obeyed and swam forward. The Duchess stared at her for a long moment, motionless, and then suddenly asked:

“What are you able to do?”

“That's odd”. Rina smirked to herself, “a sea star looks nothing like a tortoise, but asks the same kinds of questions”.

“I can do many things”, she replied. “What do you mean?”

“What do you mean, Your Grace!” the Administrator corrected in an insistent tone, slithering back to whisper a few more phrases to the Ruler, of which Rina only caught the words “much” and “very”.

“You can spin. Rotate, I mean”, declared the Duchess with confidence.

“Again?” Rina said in surprise.

“What do you mean ‘again'?” The Duchess looked utterly surprised and sharply turned to the Administrator.

“I mean you need me to spin and rotate. Again!” exclaimed Rina. “Nobody knows why, but on every planet, people are always spinning and rotating”.

“On every WHAT?! Every PLANET?! What planet?!” the Administrator asked sternly, raising all three of her rays.

Rina's heart stumbled. She panicked and, slightly stuttering, tried to cover her slip:

“N-no... not planet... I said... splannet. It's a mirage, an illusion – people call mirages ‘splannets' in our desert. And they love spinning and rotating in mirages. I'm from the desert, and you must know – deserts have tons of mirages. I mean, no true desert can even exist without mirages. No one could survive even a day without mirages”.

“Mirages?” the stars echoed in unison, exchanging glances.

“Yes! Definitely! Mirages! Splannets, as we say – optical illusions, splannets. Imagine, you walk there and see a tiny lake in the distance. You think it's real and you run toward it, but when you get closer – it shimmers, then poof!” Rina snapped her fingers. “Gone! Evaporated! Because there was never a lake or anything. It was just a desert mirage, a splannet, you see?”

The stars didn't understand a thing. How would they? Stars know nothing about deserts, let alone mirages or lakes. Still, the Administrator clearly relaxed – her rays kept lowering. The Duchess said:

“Even so, I hope you really can spin and rotate”.

“Rotate?” Rina smirked. “Of course, I can rotate”.

She floated to the middle of the Red Hall and spun six times on her axis.

“Aaah! Ooo!” the stars cried out as if someone had poked them with scissors.

“Amazing! Astonishing! Magnificent!” the Duchess exclaimed, deeply inspired by Rina's performance. She eagerly wriggled down from her stone and flopped onto the floor, narrowly avoiding tearing her belly open on the sharp, shiny needles. “Tell us! Tell us!” she shouted. “How many spins can you do at once, in one go?”

Rina shrugged. No one had ever asked her such a silly thing. She hadn't expected this nonsense from noble people, but out loud she said:

“I don't know, actually. I've never counted. The hardest part is not getting dizzy. But if I really tried, I guess I could do twenty. Maybe twenty-five”.

“Twenty-five?! Twenty-five!!! Can you imagine?!”

The Administrator was beside herself with joy. “Can you imagine? Gosh! Tomorrow, at the Announcement, I'll bring her with me and she'll spin twenty times in a row! In a row!!! Just picture what the crowd will do! They'll lose their minds forever!”

“Listen! Listen!” the Duchess cut in excitedly. “We'll create a new ducal rule, every day, the number of spins will equal the date divided by three!”

“Brilliant! Outstanding!” the Administrator rejoiced and flew up to the ceiling in celebration of the upcoming festivity. “And the populace, the whole Square – everyone will count the spins out loud!”

“Yes! Exactly! They will! Out loud! And in the Palace she'll rotate in the Black Park, and we'll bet on the spins – how many she can make!”

“A new ducal game!” the Administrator squealed in utmost excitement. “One pearl for every missed spin!”

“Two!”

“Three!”

“Maybe more!”

“But we, I hope, we will know in advance how many spins she'll manage, right?” asked the Ruler.

“Of course! Otherwise, what's the point of the game?”

“Wonderful! Terrific! Excellent!”

Rina stood between the two delighted stars, turning her head left and right, not entirely following what they were talking about. At last, she couldn't stand it anymore.

“Excuse me”, she said. “But I didn't come to your country to spin in public squares. I'm a tourist!”

The stars fell silent mid-word. The Administrator lifted herself onto the tips of her rays, turned gracefully toward Rina, and responded, enunciating each phrase in a drawn-out, almost ceremonial tone – much like she had that morning during the Announcement, only a bit faster:

“Esteemed Lady Rina! It is clear that you have not yet fully understood or felt the immeasurable fortune that has befallen you. The Grand Duchess invites you to permanent employment at the Palace as the First Assistant to the Administrator of New Days. Is that correct?” – she looked to the Duchess, who nodded in confirmation – “This is an enormous honor, you'll agree”, the Administrator continued, pacing the floor of the Red Hall with her rays, “Few have ever experienced such luck in their lives! What's more, you'll be granted your own room within the Palace walls and a third-class carriage, and in time, a second-class one. In addition, you will be permitted to attend the Thursday Games! Did you ever dream of such success?”

“No, I didn't”, Rina admitted honestly.

“There, you see!” the Administrator exclaimed. “I'll give the order at once to proclaim the Supreme Decree of your appointment, and shortly after...”

“Wait a second!” the Duchess interrupted. “Tell me, I don't understand – on whose behalf will she be rotating?”

“Clearly on our behalf!” the Administrator answered. “She will represent the unceasing rotation of the Ever-blooming Stars!”

“But that's impossible!” the Ruler objected. “She isn't even a star!”

The Administrator looked at Rina carefully.

“Well, she's not a star, but she looks like one. From a distance, very much so”.

“No”, the Duchess disagreed. “Stars have three rays, and she has four. That's just disgusting. Four! Think about it!”

“It doesn't matter...” the Administrator began.

“It does matter! It matters a lot!” the Duchess cut her off. “For the games it may not, but for the Announcement – it's critical! You seriously think no one among the community will notice? Don't delude yourself, darling! The very first day, they'll see she has four rays. We'll be ridiculed to the point of extinction! It would be a provocation – a ready-made act of treason! An attack on state authority!”

After that pronouncement, both stars stopped talking, staring at each other. Finally, the Administrator decided to reason with the Ruler.
