“Forgive me, sister, but I think you're exaggerating a bit in this case. The folks? Let them look, let them scrutinize! Ha! If this Rina came here with me, then she was a proper star. Period! Who would dare argue with us?”

“Exactly, exactly...” the Ruler sighed, climbing back onto her spiny stone. “I've always said you were raised without enough discipline. I mean really... How can... How can...” – the Duchess caught her shell on a needle and now, slowly and meticulously picking her way across the spines, was shifting ray by ray – “How can we allow someone with four rays to be presented to the entire Square as a real star?!”

“Then I don't know what to do”, the Administrator confessed, flustered.

“But I know!” the Duchess declared in a commanding royal voice, finally untangling herself from the dangerous spines and settling comfortably on her beloved stone. “Yes, I know! If she had two rays, that would be much harder. But four? We understand what we can do about that!”

The Administrator caught on to what the Duchess was implying and soared up to the ceiling again in a burst of admiration.

“Of course! You're right! It's so simple! Brilliant!”

“True brilliance is simple”, said the Ruler modestly. “Snip it – and there you are! We'll cut it off today, the sooner the better!”

“What?! Cut what off?!” Rina finally grasped the meaning of the Duchess's idea. “What do you mean, cut it off?!”

“Don't worry, my dear!” the Administrator cooed in a soothing, gentle tone. “We'll call in a very experienced red crab specialist, Rakkr, and he'll amputate it, he'll snip away the excess instantly and professionally! This little ray right here!” – the Administrator pointed to Rina's right arm.

Rina stared at her hand in horror, then yanked it behind her back and screamed:

“No way!!! Are you all totally insane?!”

“Clearly, you just haven't had enough time to fully understand the situation”, the Administrator purred even more sweetly. “Losing a single ray is almost painless. Snip – done! And best of all, only once. By tomorrow, you'll be proclaimed a new star! And the day after, you'll be my Assistant and a Personal Confidante of the Grand Duchess herself! Just think – who were you before? Nobody! Absolutely no one! An ordinary ocean animal...”

Rina tried to object, but the Administrator decisively waved her ray and continued:

“Tomorrow, you will be one of the Great Three-Rayed Stars! Chosen ones! Every inhabitant in this city and the whole duchy will bow to you. Every word you say, even the slightest hint, will be respected and instantly obeyed and acted upon by the Duchess's subjects. Wealth, fame, and honor await you, think of it. And more, you'll become part of the sacred Announcement ceremony. You'll hold a position that many true-born stars don't even dream of. Just picture it – even the Great Stars will envy you! And that's not all. You'll be admitted to the ducal games! Such an invitation is an unheard-of, unimaginable honor! All these benefits will be yours as soon as you become three-rayed. I mean – what difference does it make to you whether you have three rays or four? Is it actually worth fretting over a pointless, insignificant, and, let us be frank, absolutely useless little extra ray? Do you agree, or do you need time to think it over?”

“I do not agree! And no, I don't need time to think! Do you really believe I'm going to let you cut off my arm just to get all this straight-up absurdity you're pitching me?” Rina glanced at the exit, focused, drew a deep breath, and said as calmly and confidently as she could, “I'm a tourist. I came here by your invitation. They promised a brief audience to me, and I think it is now over”.

Rina turned to the Ruler and, with the utmost solemnity, gave an expressive bow.

“It was great meeting you, Grand Duchess. Allow me to leave. Please”.

“So you refuse our generous and magnanimous offer?” the Ruler asked sternly.

“I refuse”.

“So you reject the enormous honor We have chosen to bestow upon you?” With every word, the Duchess's rays jerked higher and higher.

“I reject everything!” Rina answered boldly.

“That is your final word, your well-considered, definitive decision?” the Ruler hissed.

“That's final”.

“Guards!” the Duchess shrieked. “Guards!!!”

At that very moment, a crowd of massive fleshy squids burst into the hall one after another, each the size of a big pillow.

“Catch her!!! Seize her!!! Imprison her!!! Jail!!! Jail!!” the Duchess screeched, her voice rising from a shriek into something ultrasonic.

Watching Rina with their dead, unblinking eyes, the squids surrounded her and pounced on her like wild dogs. In an instant, cold, stiff tentacles, as tight as ropes, wrapped hard around her arms and legs.

“Jail! Jail! For life!! Forever!!!” The Duchess did not spare her throat.

Without the slightest delay, the squids dragged Rina out of the hall, swept her through several rooms and tunnels in a single powerful wave, turned into a dark corridor, and soon emerged outside the Palace, surrounded by two dozen more squids from the external guard and five monstrous red crabs.

“Is this even possible?” Rina thought, unable to believe her eyes. “To get thrown in jail twice in one day? That's not even funny”.

And no, it wasn't. Stars were vastly more intelligent, cunning, and devious than the dumb, nasty Belmons. A star-run prison might be secured a hundred times tighter than that primitive little cave, and in that case, the Duchess's sentence of lifelong imprisonment for a disobedient foreigner might turn out to be more than just the rage of a crazy old creature. The dreadful sentence could become a reality, and the prison – a forever home. But... But thinking about that was too heavy. Moreover, Rina wasn't about to give up that easily.

“All right, it's not over yet”, she told herself. “I'll wait and see what can be done”.

And indeed, things were unfolding impressively fast – not even two minutes had passed before the whole squid procession reached the endpoint of their journey. The prison turned out to be just a part of the Palace's rear utility structures. The squids, now moving without haste, carried the detainee over a dimly lit but clearly well-known path that led from the halls, offices, and reception rooms through a series of corridors straight to the prison courtyard.

As Rina had suspected, the prison cells were just large, artificially made holes in a long outcrop of chalky rock – the very rock from which the Palace itself had been built. Only, unlike the Belmons' prison, the walls of these hollows were densely wrapped in a thick netting of twisting seaweed, whose leaves glinted with sharp little spines. And yet, the entrances had no doors, no bars – just nothing.

The guards lowered Rina onto the gritty sand not far from two such holes. The squids backed away several meters and froze.

“Wait here!” ordered the lead squid.

“All right”, she replied. But feeling a weakness in her legs, she sat down tiredly on a round stone sticking out near the wall. The squids didn't object.

Rina had been right, star customs and procedures were many times more complex than those of the Belmons. Instead of just shoving her into one of the cells and being done with it, the guards began to dance. At least, what the purple squids started doing a few minutes later outwardly resembled wild dances without music – the convoy seemed to have completely forgotten about the prisoner, and nothing interested them now but twitching, hopping, diving, and weaving loops and zigzags.

In complete silence, puffing and turning maroon from effort and exertion, the entire squid troop was showing off something unimaginable. There was no rhythm in their tireless pirouettes and leaps, nor any visible order, but at first glance, it was clear – everything was happening according to a well-written and carefully rehearsed script.

After darting and flailing themselves into exhaustion, the guards finished their insane boogie-woogie with a frenzied vibration and suddenly scattered in all directions like billiard balls – but immediately regrouped, fashioning a sort of hemisphere with their bodies, inside of which was their dark-hued leader. He swayed heavily side to side, like the pendulum of an antique clock.

But soon the construction collapsed, the squids sank to the seafloor, and there they lined up in two rows, while the lead guard, tracing a knotty spiral above everyone's heads, floated toward the entrance of the nearest hollow, flipped himself upside down and hung there, glaring at Rina with his bulbous orange eyes.

15 – HONORARY COURT
part one

At that very moment, from the unbreakable, solid darkness, a sweet, winsome pair emerged – two horrible red crabs. Like vintage wind-up toys, the crabs marched side by side in perfect sync, pausing for half a second after each step, apparently playing honor guard. On their backs, they carried a book. Or more precisely, a Book! Better yet, a BOOKZILLA! For a moment, a recently seen sci-fi movie came to mind, where in long, abandoned underground vaults, the main character discovered alien books the size of a garage. The film was funny, but cheesy. Reality was tragic and terrifying. The most striking part of the entire scene was the huge, awful claws, gleaming with a golden hue and shimmering like saber blades.

“The Honorary Session of the Honorary Court of the Honorary Prison is hereby declared open!” proclaimed the Senior Guard from above with inflated pride. Right after that, the three-meter-tall piece of blue slate, leaning against the white wall, moved to the side as if by magic and then crashed to the floor, raising an immense cloud of sandy dust and dark silt.

A few minutes later, as the murk, carried away by a sluggish current, began to dissipate, Rina saw that behind the stone was an oval niche, crudely carved – or more likely gnawed – deep into the rock. Nestled inside was an unbelievably fat black octopus, shiny as new rubber boots. It sat, staring blankly into the distance over Rina's head, while the tips of short sausage-like tentacles lazily scratched its bloated, pitch-colored belly, swollen from the recent second lunch.

In perfect mechanical synchronization, both the crabs stepped toward the niche and turned around. As before, Rina thought of automated tractors maneuvering in the field. Unexpectedly, the crabs parted, stepped aside, and the gigantic book plopped onto the floor right in front of the black octopus's belly. So, everyone had to wait again for the muddy fog to settle.

“Suspect!!!” the octopus screeched like a cat with its tail crushed under a door.

Rina even looked around, searching for the source of the sound. It was hard to believe that an animal the size of a rhinoceros could have such a kitten-like voice.

“Suspect! Where is he?!” the octopus squealed piercingly.

Rina cleared her throat and said:

“Ahem... y-yes?”

Eager to get rid of the octopus as quickly as possible, she decided not to make a fuss about whether she was a ‘she' or a ‘he'.

“I don't care”, she thought calmly. “All this will be over in five minutes”.

“Suspect! Your name, species, position, rank, place of work, place of residence, occupation”, the octopus creaked in a rattling rush.

“All right then”. Rina began counting on her fingers. “My name is Rina. But if you want me be precise, I am Rina Volodymyr. Because Volodymyr is my father. That's one. My species – human. That is, I'm a humanoid person. Humanoid teen, right? That's two. Now position”.

“Position! Position!” the octopus confirmed nervously.

“Well, I'm not sure. Most likely, I can be listed as a student. Because I go to school every day”. At these words, the octopus fidgeted impatiently in his niche. “Rank? I don't have one. I'm not old enough to have a rank. And I'm neither a soldier nor an officer. What's next?”

“Place of work!”

“Oh yes, I forgot, you said, my place of work. Cool. Let's say I'm employed at the same school, also as a student. Or is that not acceptable? Position, student, job, student... No? Probably, that's not okay...”

“Residence!” the octopus interrupted.

“Residence is the easiest”, Rina said a bit wearily. “I live in Kyiv. Kyiv is a city, very large, like Boz, though a hundred times bigger, and only people live there. Well, not just people, of course, also dogs live, many, and cats, many as well, and birds, even more, I suppose”. She paused for a second to scratch her head. “And, definitely, butterflies. Millions of them. Especially on the river, not in the river, of course, but along the embankment. And besides, in our forest park, there are about two hundred squirrels. Or maybe three hundred. Who knows? Squirrels run everywhere, no one counts them, it's too hard, you see. And definitely, fish live in the river, also millions just like in your ocean, only ours have no light on their noses. And I think you won't believe it, but even crayfish are found there, thousands of them, just imagine that. Although it is funny, crayfish never live in the river itself. They don't like rivers – I have no idea why – they always prefer crawling in the warm ponds somewhere near the river. Despite the dirt. And honestly, we also have a lot of...”

“Enough!” the octopus screamed. “Your crime?”

“Crime?” Rina was surprised by the word. “What crime? I didn't do anything wrong! I never break rules. Not guilty!”

“Everyone. Says. That”, the octopus drawled with a bored tone.

“Perhaps you're right”. Rina shrugged. “But I'm telling the truth, I haven't done a single thing”.

Still hovering above the cave entrance, the Senior Squid suddenly came to life and declared in a solemn baritone:

“Brazen insult! To Her Highness! The Grand Duchess! And Her Grace! The Chief Administrator!”

“Ah, so that's it!” the octopus gloated, rubbing its front upper tentacles. “And such terrible, unacceptable, unlawful actions you don't even consider a crime?”

“But it's true, I didn't insult anyone! I just said that I refuse to...”

“Cease the debating rigmarole!” he shouted.

“Cease what?” Rina didn't know the word ‘rigmarole'.

“Stop arguing with the judge! Arguments are prohibited!” the octopus pronounced louder, slightly hopping, and only then did she realize he wasn't alone.

The judge-octopus sat as if in a chair in a rounded depression on the head of another octopus, of the same color but gigantic, puffed up like a balloon and with short, thin appendages on the sides. The bloated octopus, despite his occasional movements and incessant scratching of his belly, was asleep! He was totally lost in his sweet dreams and had no idea about the ongoing trial.

“He's probably a backup. Or the assistant”, Rina reasoned.

“I'm not arguing with you”, she replied aloud, “I'm just telling you how it actually happened”.

“We're familiar with your case excessively enough!” the judge squeaked abruptly. “We have no questions whatsoever!”

“Suit yourself, I don't have to tell you then”.

“And no need! The Court knows everything. Entirely!”

From somewhere behind his back, he pulled out a thin white arm, similar to an elongated silver teaspoon, though covered in bright-pink suckers, and began flipping through the pages of the colossal book.

“We know all... justice... this is... you're not... you don't think...” he muttered. “The page... the page… here! On page eight hundred four hundred and six...”

“There's no such page”, said Rina.

“Whaaat?” The octopus twisted his muzzle as if he'd swallowed vinegar.

“Never mind”. She waved her hand and thought, “Let it be page one thousand three thousand, whatever!” She no longer had the will to fight.

“Here it is! We found it!” stated the judge and lifted the edge of the paper so the defendant could also see the law on page eight hundred four hundred and six. Rina gasped – the page was blank. That is, completely blank! Not a single letter, not a symbol, not even the smallest hieroglyph – a clean, totally innocent sheet!

“But there's nothing written there!” she exclaimed in total astonishment. “Your book... your book is fake!”

“What did you say?! How dare you, pathetic miserable accused, pronounce such offensive, inappropriate words?!” shrilled the octopus, overflowing with professional outrage. “Insult to the Great Book of the Bottom Laws! Insult to the Honorary Ducal Court! Insult to everything!”

“Can't you see it yourself?” she said, amazed. “It's empty, absolutely white paper – not a single line...”

“Accused!” the judge interrupted with an impatient jerk. “We advise you not to make your already difficult situation worse! Answer the question, Do you plead guilty?”

“No!” answered Rina firmly. “I'm not going to plead anything!”

“All the worse for you! All the worse!” The octopus was impatiently bouncing with each exclamation, nearly falling off his inflated colleague. “I hereby! Declare! The honorary sentence! According to Article Thirty-Twenty, you are sentenced to eternal imprisonment. And then, according to Article Forty-Fifty, your sentence is doubled. And according to...”

“But how can you double eternal imprisonment if it's eternal?” Rina couldn't believe that courts could be this nonsensical.

“We can, we do!” squealed the judge. “According to the Article!”

“Ah, according to the article”, she said, already weary. “Better to stay quiet”, she thought. “No point in this whole mess anyway”.

But the octopus himself was already exhausted too from the complexities of the “honorary” session. He mumbled the article numbers again – this time they were completely different – and explained that the sentence was final, highest, supreme, foremost, paramount, and top, and that is that! Therefore, it could not be appealed or discussed, or revised, or changed, or improved, or anything! With all his cephalopod might, the judge tried to scare the foolish convict with the strictest punishments, but Rina stubbornly remained silent and didn't react at all.

Then he pulled a sour, twisted grimace, waited half a minute, and asked:

“Convict, would you like to say a final word before your eternal incarceration?”

Rina looked at the stony, indifferent snouts of the half-asleep squids and, with a defiant spark, said:

“You want my final word? Here you go! My final word – kefir!”

“What is ‘kefir'?”

“I won't tell you!”

The octopus was taken aback for a second, but years of courtroom practice took over, and he continued:

“Well. In that case, you are taken into custody immediately! The term of your eternal imprisonment begins this day, this hour, this minute, this... uh... definitively!” He exhaled with relief, then turned to the squids, rose up on his tentacles and ordered:

“Honorary guards! Execute the sentence!”

The squids instantly woke up, grabbed Rina and dragged her toward the left cave entrance. After swimming four or five meters, they stopped. One of them cautiously approached the seaweed thickets on the wall, peered into them attentively, and suddenly squirted out a whole cloud of inky fluid. As stated on Wikipedia, it is a national tradition among sea squids to release ink when frightened. The black-blue fog crawled in slimy streaks across the wall, covering the seaweed leaves with a dark coating.

“Bring in the convict!” barked the Senior Guard. The squids let go of Rina and, sluggishly wiggling their palps, lined up behind her like a purple fence.

There was nothing left to do but enter the tunnel. After swimming a few paces, she looked back, the squids' smug grins filled the entire inlet.

“Too bad my uncle's Rottweiler Rex isn't here with me”, she thought. “He would show you clearly what an honorary court should look like! You'd remember him for the rest of your silly lives!”

The tunnel was long and slightly winding. Somewhere ahead a yellow dot shimmered – it proved to be a simple piece of glowing citrine, about half the size of a matchbox. Rina swam into the cave and looked around, a small room, walls roughly and clumsily carved, two flat stone bunks – one by the back wall, the other in the center and... and nobody. Absolutely no one!

“So this is a solitary cell?” she asked into the emptiness.

Recently, she and Nina went to the IMAX on Berkovetska. The movie was called “Moon ‘B'”. Actually, Rina had convinced Nina to go see the action flick because she'd read a story with the same title. But she was mistaken. The movie was about some radioactive mines and dumb robots. And there in that film, a young woman spent five years in solitary confinement deep underground. And the cell – rock-hewn and round – was just like this one...

Methodically searching the cave from beginning to end, Rina sorrowfully confirmed her complete, desolate loneliness. Not a single little fish or a shrimp, not even the tiniest shell or snail was to be found anywhere. She sat down on the rock and listened – impenetrable silence.

“Hey, maybe everyone's been gone for ages and there's no guard there?” she called out loudly into the empty cell.

The tunnel entrance yawned black across from her. A few steps away, in the dim light it looked like a big smear of soot haphazardly slapped onto the wall. Rina decided to check. Moving her arms as smoothly as she could, taking every precaution, she swam toward the tunnel and fluttered inside like a moth, fanning only with her palms.

She moved slowly. The tunnel curved slightly to the left, and after about five meters, its walls were tinted by a faint bluish light filtering in from the prison courtyard. The closer to the exit, the thicker the algae wove across the walls. Rina brushed one of the leaves by accident, and it stung her pinky sharply. A deep-sea nettle!

Something flashed like a mirror three inches from her face. Rina jerked back, bumped her head on the ceiling, and let out a cry. Only then did she get a good look at that “mirror” – a little fish, about four inches long, with silver scales and stubby red fins. The fish had no glowing spots, but its head was grotesquely large, like you were looking at it through a magnifying glass. And there was something wrong, something off in its whole appearance, but Rina couldn't figure out what it was just yet.

The strange little fish flitted nervously in front of her nose, but soon calmed down and darted back into the tangled thickets.

“Well, you sure scared me”, said Rina. No one answered. “All right, nothing special”, she added. “Just some small fish living in the tunnel. What's so unusual about that?”

15 – HONORARY COURT
part two

Pushing off the floor, she headed for the exit again. But at that same moment, another silvery fish shot out of the weeds and zipped right at her face, barely missing her forehead. Rina froze in confusion and for a while watched the wild darting of the fish with astonishment.

“Shoo! Go away!” she shouted and waved her hand.

In response, three more silvery big-heads burst out. They zoomed around so frantically that even a simple collision with them could easily leave a bruise. But then one of the fish slowed down, hovered about a foot from Rina's face, and everything became clear – it wasn't just the head that was enlarged, but the jaws too! The agitated fish was breathing heavily, opening its gaping mouth, full of the sharpest teeth – like shards of broken glass, all triangular and deadly.

It turned out that these strange little fish were the prison guards! Rina looked at the increasingly thick and overgrown nettle on the walls and thought that by the exit, there could be a hundred, two hundred, or as many of these glass-toothed fish as you like – and with jaws like those, they could shred anything to bits. They weren't attacking only because she hadn't come close enough yet. But everything in their behavior said clearly – they were ready for a fatal strike.

Rina lingered a bit longer, watching the furious fish frenzy to her heart's content, then turned around and swam slowly back. Glancing over her shoulder, she spotted one of the fish trailing a few meters behind her.

“What do you want? Don't you see I'm going back to the cell? Not breaking any rules. Leave me alone! Everything's fine!”

But the fish didn't leave and never responded. Most likely, she could not speak any languages. Not surprising indeed – who needs brains when you've got jaws of this size? Back in the cell, Rina sat on the flat rock by the wall again. The gloomy fish swam into the cave and immediately settled on an angular protrusion on the opposite wall. Undoubtedly she knew that little shelf very well.

“Watching me now?” Rina asked with distaste. “Fine, watch all you like. Warden!”

The fish stared blankly with her empty, motionless eyes and had no intention of going anywhere. Rina decided to distract herself with something useful, to shake off her dark thoughts somehow. She took off her backpack, opened it, and started taking inventory of her personal belongings – pulling out items one by one and neatly laying them out on the smooth rock.

“Ha! The magic is over”, she said with a mournful smile, placing a transparent plastic pouch onto her palm.

While working with Gri-Gri at the Triazis dam on Yellow Brook, she had filled one plastic bag with yellow flower water – making a funny-looking bright yellow balloon. But now the water was colorless. And, on top of that, completely useless. She unwrapped the packet, squeezed out every last drop, and carefully folded it into a square.

“And what am I even going to do with these fruits?” she asked the fish.

The oasis pears, apples, and bananas looked amazingly fresh, but Rina was sure they must have spoiled in ocean water.

“Well, I'll try a little bite”.

She peeled the tip of a banana and cautiously took a nibble. Astonishingly, it tasted great, exactly like a fresh one, and smelled perfect too – no sourness at all.

“All right, you win. I'll keep the fruits, and eat them later”.

Peeking out of a long pocket was her favorite flashlight with the pea-sized bulb.

“But the battery's dead”. Rina sighed, took it out, and pressed the black button.

A blinding yellowish-green beam sliced through the darkness like a carving knife. Rina squeezed her eyes shut. She had gotten so used to the underwater darkness that even those dimly glowing city stones had seemed bright – but now, dazzled by the crazy blast of true, earthly brightness, her eyes couldn't take the overload for a second.

“Whoa! What a light! And the battery's still working! Unbelievable!”

The beam danced around the cell. Surprisingly, the cave wasn't that gray at all, but mottled and vividly colorful. Crystals and flecks of mica glittered like diamonds. Red and orange mineral streaks ran like snakes along the walls, and on the ceiling gleamed a blue crescent-shaped spot she hadn't noticed before. Rina shone the light toward the fish – but the jailer was gone.

“Aha, makes sense! She got scared of the light!” Rina said with a laugh.

Suddenly, it hit her like a golden ticket win.

“Scared of the light?!” she echoed in disbelief.

And answered herself:

“Of course she did! Naturally! Those fish have never seen light like that! So, that means... that means...” Rina felt a mighty volcano of joy erupt in her chest. “...that means I know what to do!”

Without wasting a second, she hurriedly stuffed her precious possessions into her backpack, slung it over her shoulders, thoroughly adjusted the straps and folds, grabbed the miraculous flashlight – turned into a weapon by a twist of fate – and headed resolutely into the tunnel.

Just a meter short of where the first warden-fish had appeared a few minutes earlier, Rina turned vertically, planted both her feet firmly on the floor, and hit the button.

The thin, searing beam cut through the sharp-leaved little jungles. That same moment, deep in the seaweed, nutty chaos ignited – fish thrashed violently, slamming into each other, crashing into the stalks of the plants. Rampant panic spread among the prison guards instantly like wildfire.

Rina pressed forward, undeterred, sweeping the relentless beam over every branch it could reach. The thicket trembled, madly pulsing with uncontrollable energy. Finally, the fish couldn't take it any longer – one by one, mortally terrified, they began bursting out into the open.

Totally insane with fear, stunned and blinded, they darted every which way, viciously bumping their bony snouts into the stone floor and ceiling, fleeing as fast as they could from the terrible, searing, burning blaze of the light.

Rina marched forward.

No one stood in her way.

No one tried to stop her.

“Hurry! Move! The battery may die!” an agitated thought flared in her mind.

To pick up speed, she pushed off the ground and swam, never worrying about the unlit shrubbery. The horror among the fish had reached such a level that the wardens were exploding out of the foliage not just in front of Rina under the deadly blasts, but even behind her – in the darkness, where they were no longer in danger at all.

A few vigorous strokes – and Rina emerged through the open entrance.

Freedom! My sweet, glorious, beautiful freedom!

But there was no time for celebration.

The jailers, shocked beyond reason and fully out of their minds, could easily attract the attention of the palace guards and staff – not with words, but with their frenzied, hysterical behavior. And soon, red crabs would come scuttling into the prison, closely followed by the Belmon octopuses – gray shadows of death. Belmons are phenomenal swimmers, nobody can escape. Against them her little flashlight would be no weapon at all, just a useless toy.

Rina raced on. Faster! Higher! Farther from the prison! Farther from the Palace! Getting away from this wonderful, terrible, sinister city of Boz!

Glancing here and there, she swam over the amber-lit palace gardens. No one was chasing her. No one even noticed her.

Following the rules of her favorite detective stories to cover her tracks, she dipped down toward a blue shale platform and skimmed over it for about a minute, heading toward a cluster of massive coral columns. A couple of huge veil-tail fish and three black pikes stopped and gawked at her, mouths open in astonishment.

Then she ascended higher, spun sharply to the right, and took off at double speed, carried along by a kind, chill current that seemed to be helping her flee.

Maybe the Duchess's palace was far outside the city limits, or perhaps the water below wasn't as clear as before, but within just minutes, Rina couldn't see a thing. Only dense, murky darkness and some blurred, grayish smudges.

The golden lights of the famous city had faded into nothing – forever.

Nearly half an hour passed. The current pushed and pulled her forward diligently, but escape takes its toll – she was worn out from the unaccustomed underwater marathon. She decided to land somewhere quiet, catch her breath and think.

Rina descended a couple hundred meters. All she could make out were some formless blue blotches scattered across the empty black sea floor. No immediate danger, it seemed. Probably just remnants of ancient deep-sea coral still glowing faintly.

“Well, that's that – no more fear”, she said to the darkness, encouraging herself. “I'm dangerous and armed like James Bond – I've got a plasma gun! The only thing... I kinda forgot a spare battery...”

Soon she was standing on the soft sediment at the bottom. Looking more closely, she spotted a big grayish stone jutting out of the brown silt about twenty meters away. But besides that boulder – nothing at all.

“Okay. Now the main thing is not to get caught by the Belmons or the stars' servants. That's death one hundred percent. The stars won't forgive. But more importantly – I have to figure out the right direction”.

Her compass showed that she had been swimming far to the North. No problem – she turned to face due West right away. There it was, her path to safety. But what lay beyond – neither compass nor map could say. And there was no one to ask.

All around, as far as her eyes could see in the dim murk near the bottom, stretched an endless, flat lowland. Nothing but mud, basically. Only a few smoldering fragments of coral gave the dreary scene of this long-forgotten oceanic backwater some sign of life. The perfect emptiness.

16 – BALUBIBO
part one

Having rested and grown accustomed to the blackish-violet haze, Rina gathered her will into a tight fist and said, as loudly as she could:

“Everything's fine! No danger at all!”

She wasn't trying to play tricks on her own mind. But with no one else around, what choice did she have? Who else would support her? Who would inspire her in a place like this? Only herself.

Just as it had been long ago in the waterless desert, the ocean floor in the World of Water was a total wilderness – no roads, nor the slightest hint of a trail. There was no sign of intelligent life, either, unless you counted the dimly gleaming fish skeletons scattered here and there.

“This is so stupid. I don't even remember which way their city was”, she whispered irritably.

A heavy, unpleasant thought took root: if she were particularly unlucky – considering how many times she had twisted and turned while fleeing – she might accidentally end up back in the Duchy of Boz. There, something far more terrifying than a life sentence awaited her. A torture chamber, perhaps.

“Fine, we'll see. I know one thing for sure – this time I'm heading the right way”.

And so, she walked. Walking on the seafloor was pretty uncomfortable, but Rina was tired of pretending to be a fish – or, as the Administrator had insultingly put it, ‘an ordinary underwater animal'. She wanted to be a human being again, if only for a minute. She walked upright, head held high, despite her agonizingly slow pace and the nasty sludge clinging to the soles of her sneakers.

Some minutes later, having trudged less than a hundred yards, she looked back and gasped. Above every footprint hung a mushroom-shaped cloud of black sediment. Worse, the trail she'd left wasn't the straight line she had intended, but a complex, winding curve.

After a moment's thought, Rina conceded that “playing human” on the ocean floor cost too much time and strength. She pushed off, rose a bit higher, and swam. The problem of speed and direction was solved.

The tedious, monotonous “Dark Wasteland”, as she dubbed it, stretched for miles. The flat surface didn't rise or fall an inch. Luckily, the hated yellow lights of Boz were nowhere to be seen.

At a height of about three body-lengths, Rina swam a quarter-mile at a time, then descended to the floor to rest for a few minutes. This accidental rhythm proved to be her smartest way to move. After a dozen such breaks, she noticed the mud thinning – in places, the sludge turned a mustard color and was no deeper than half an inch.

“Sand soon, probably. Not that it matters”.

But a short while later, a leaden fatigue set in. She forced herself to swim another hundred meters out of sheer stubbornness before sinking helplessly to the bottom. Here, the floor was solid – stone-hard sand – and nearly clean, with only a light dusting of silt. She also noticed dozens of deep holes, each about the diameter of a glass. The hollows looked suspicious – too round, too even, as if someone had drilled them with a giant auger. At first, Rina was afraid that snakes might live in them, but she quickly calmed down – she realized that so many serpents would never survive on a lifeless plain like this.

There were no large rocks around, so she rested standing up, ‘horse-style', as Grandma-1 would jokingly put it. Rina pulled the stored piece of vura-vura coral from her backpack and ate it. However, it didn't fill her up. She took out the smallest Triazis apple, examined it carefully, and, finding no defects, ate it too. Out of habit, she imagined a cup of coffee – but only out of habit – she wasn't thirsty.

“I'll catch my breath and then swim another four miles bit by bit”, she said loudly, just to break the pressing silence.

“And I'm not swimming anywhere”, said someone behind her.

Rina turned abruptly – and saw a large jellyfish sitting on the smooth bottom just a few steps away and glowing faintly as if warming up like a retro TV. A minute ago, there had been nobody. Magic? Or had it simply sneaked up on her so noiselessly?

Meanwhile, the jellyfish “heated up” completely and lit the sand with oval patches of colored light, each about a meter long.

“Who are you?” asked Rina, trying to quickly gauge the threat.

“I'm a Baanoon”, it answered – or rather, he answered, because his voice, somewhat childish, was definitely male, boyish, perhaps, though sounding too smooth and polished, like most anime voices.

“Baanoon?” Rina repeated, using Lida's trick of repeating to buy time to think.

“Not quite, sorry”. The jellyfish blinked, seemingly a little offended. “Not Baa-anoon, just Ba-aanoon. And you, if you don't mind, who are you exactly?”

Rina decided not to clarify how many “a”s there were in his word. She said:

“Nice to meet you. I'm a human. And my name is Rina”.

“Veeery pleased to meet you, madam”, the Baanoon replied pompously, bowing his round, orange-green-blue... well, multicolored head. “My name is Balubibo. That's my nickname, the local fish call me this. But when I was little, my mother used to call me

********

You're probably wondering why there are eight asterisks instead of a name here. Most likely because no letters in human alphabets could capture those sounds. The Baanoon, still holding his bow, let out eight distinct, unvoiced, nasal “bloops” – each different from the last – as only Baanoons can do, no one else in the world.

Rina replied:

“If you don't mind, sir, I'd rather call you by your nickname. It's easier for me”.

“Very good!” the Baanoon agreed, blinking a green light on top of his head. “Call me Balubibo – I've been used to it for years”.

“Do you live here?” Rina asked, trying to begin a delicate conversation, which, in her plan, could somehow lead to some questions about the Light Ocean.

“Oh yes! Unquestionably! This is where I live, right here at this very spot. And it's been... it's been...” – Balubibo did some mental arithmetic – “forty-six years already. Oh, my! Would you say forty-six is a lot, if you don't mind my asking?”

“It is”, Rina agreed, but then caught herself and quickly added, “Actually you don't look it – maybe thirty, or thirty-three at most”.

“Really? Ooooh! Thank you! And if you don't mind – how old are you? Just pure curiosity, sorry”, he added apologetically, tilting his head.

“Me? My age?” For some reason, Rina felt like hiding her real years and blurted out the first number that came to mind, “Seventy-seven”.

“Aaah! Ho!” exclaimed Balubibo, his eyes widening in astonishment. “Very respectable, if you don't mind my saying! And my compliment must be far greater than yours, but I'm an honest person, so I will tell the truth – you look no more than... uh... with your permission and my respect... no more than forty-five”.

“Wow, what a compliment – forty-five!” Rina laughed silently to herself, but aloud she politely thanked him for such a flattering guess.

An awkward silence followed. The conversation wasn't taking off, and they couldn't find any common ground. Balubibo sat blinking bright, colorful patches and patterns on the back of his head. Rina pretended to be searching for something in her pockets.

“Excuse me...” The Baanoon broke the pause, unable to stand it any longer. “You're not heading to Benabag, by any chance?”

“No”, she answered. “I've never even heard of a city like that”.

“It's not a city, I'm sorry, but Benabag is an underwater forest”.

“A forest?” Rina repeated in surprise. “I don't know, not sure. I was actually swimming around to visit all the local sights. But is your Benabag Forest worth checking out? I mean, is it cool to see?”

Balubibo dimmed his lights and thought hard.

“Unlikely!” He lit up again a minute later. “To be honest, I've never been there, excuse me, but those who have say the water in Benabag is too cold. A health risk, if I may say so”.

“Oh, then, you know, I'll probably skip it. Still, dear Balubibo, do you know which way the glowing pink corals are?”

“No, I'm sorry”. The Baanoon shook his head – it wobbled like jelly. “The only glowing pink thing I know is my own forehead. Hee-hee-hee! Excuse me”.

Rina smiled, as if to play along with the joke. But the conversation still wasn't coming together – they couldn't find anything worth talking about.

And then suddenly, from somewhere above, just like snow falling down from the sky, came a flurry of fidgety little fish. They bustled and jostled, squabbling among themselves, clumping into tight bunches and then bursting apart like fireworks, again and again – until finally, they managed to surround Balubibo in a sparkling, restless ring. Rina had no idea what was going on, but the fish weren't paying any attention to her. They were completely focused on Balubibo.

From the side, the school of fish looked exactly like a swarm of moths circling a streetlight. True, in this case the light was alive, but that didn't change much.

Balubibo seemed to forget all about Rina as he watched the tiniest movements of every single fish. It wasn't hard for him, as Rina noticed, since in addition to his two large orange eyes, he also had numerous miniature, frequently blinking little eyes scattered across his entire head.

“You know these fish?” she asked.

“Know them? I've known them forever!” the Baanoon answered enthusiastically, without taking his eyes off the fish for even a second. “If only you knew how much I love and adore them all! Excuse me, of course”.

Soon, the fish settled down a bit and hovered about a meter above Balubibo's rainbow-shimmering head. The Baanoon drew in a big stream of water, then, in a tender – if a bit sugary – third-grader's voice, declared:

“My dear friends! I am so happy to see you! Today I will tell you the wondrous story of the one and only Striped Pearl”.

The fish froze in place, teeny mouths hanging open. Balubibo continued:

“One day, King Bono-Bono went hunting for lemolises. But he had no luck – the hunters fell into a shark ambush. Only the King survived. The rest of his retinue was torn to pieces by Ambit sharks”.

The fish let out a collective “Aaah!” and quickly reshuffled themselves within the group, then froze again, waiting.

“The King wandered the Gray-Green Sea for a long time”, Balubibo sang, “until he met the sorceress Miroula. She helped him and gave him a carriage pulled by blue pikes. And in return, the King rewarded her with the only incomparable Striped Pearl in the world. That Pearl could speak”.

“Oooo!” cried the fish, shifting their formation again.

“Yes, yes! Picture it, the Pearl is smart, she knows one hundred, three hundred, and seven words in two languages. But unfortunately she is small and frail – she has no legs, no fins, and you won't believe it – not even a little tail. So even the smallest crustacean can hurt her”.

“Aaaa...” the fish sighed sympathetically, slowly shifting again.

“But the sorceress Miroula was locked away in a prison by Prince Barredod. Miroula didn't want her beloved Pearl to fall into the tentacles of Barredod, so she entrusted it to her best student. Guess who it was? Yes, my friends – it was my great-grandfather!”

“Ouaaa!” the fish jumped from shock and changed formation mid-leap.

“Truly I say to you!” shouted Balubibo like a seasoned preacher. “It was my great-grandfather! The Prince never released Miroula. So, in the end, my great-grandfather passed the Talking Pearl to my grandfather, and he gave it to my Papa, and when I grew up, my Papa entrusted the Pearl for safekeeping... to me!”

At those words, the fish burst into a frenzy, darting about chaotically, mixing themselves up, then each one had to scramble to find its proper position. It took some time. Balubibo waited patiently. Eventually, the fish got themselves sorted and completed their reshuffle successfully.

“Ever since that year, the Talking Pearl has lived with me!” the Baanoon sang out grandly. The fish moved again – this time more elegantly and with dignity.

“I want to show it to you! But...”

The fish began a new reshuffling, though they paused at the ‘but'.

“But...” Balubibo continued in a half-whisper, “please, don't scare her. I beg you – no sound, no movement! Do you agree?”

“Yes! Yes! Yes! We agree! We agree!” the fish muttered eagerly, continuing to rearrange themselves – smoothly and solemnly.

“Get ready! Now!” Balubibo shut his tangerine eyes (although all his other tiny quick peepers kept blinking, following every fish without stopping), and then he opened himself – like a tulip bud unfurling in the morning, though instantly, in one single move.

“Quiet! Quiet!” the Baanoon hissed. “Look!”

From the center of the jellyfish flower, a tentacle emerged – thin, white, with a flat paddle at the end. And there on the paddle, like on a cushion, lay a large pearl painted with pink and blue stripes. Rina instinctively stepped a couple paces closer to get a better look.

“Do pearls even talk?” she thought skeptically. “A pearl is just glass. Or bone? I don't remember...”

But the fish had no doubts. Their crystal-backed bodies shimmering, the entire school of fish stared, spellbound, at the treasured gem, watching the hypnotic sway of the white tentacle in breathless awe.

16 – BALUBIBO
part two

Finally, once they had absorbed the moment, Balubibo stated:

“You're in luck, my friends! The Pearl's speaking! I can hear her!”

The fish immediately swam toward the Pearl – so close that a few accidentally bumped her with their snouts. As if to protect the priceless artifact from clumsy spectators, Balubibo began slowly pulling the tentacle back inside his body-flower.

The enchanted fish, oblivious to everything else, followed it obediently – their attention locked on the marvel.

“She's speaking! Do you hear? She's speaking now!” Balubibo encouraged them, still drawing the tentacle slowly inward.

The poor fish jostled and jammed each other trying to push closer to the treasure. The white paddle disappeared beyond the rim of the flower. Many of the fish, without a second thought, swam inside, full of passionate desire to hear the voice of the legendary relic.

Rina felt something was wrong and opened her mouth to object, but Balubibo suddenly shouted:

“Listen, listen! She's talking about the Warm Currents!”

The fish sank even deeper and, a second later, every last one of them slipped into the bud. And then the inevitable took place – the jelly flower snapped shut!

It all happened so fast and with such treacherous perfection that Rina was petrified and for some time she couldn't even speak. She just stood there staring at the Baanoon with her eyes wide open.

The Baanoon, for his part, was extremely busy – the fish had finally realized their mistake and were now desperately trying to get out. They pounded from the inside so wildly and furiously that Balubibo's head trembled and wobbled like gelatin. But Balubibo, red-faced and grunting, clamped the petals of the flower tightly shut with all his strength.

The fish were doomed.

“What are you doing?!” Rina managed to snap out of it. “Let them go!”

“Who?” Balubibo asked with a crooked, dumb smile.

“Let the fish out! Right now!”

“I don't know any fish”, Balubibo lied shamelessly. “And as for you, by the way, I've never seen you before in my life!”

“Oh really? Well then, I warned you!” She yanked off her backpack and pulled out her secret weapon – her trusty flashlight.

A sharp, piercing beam of light struck the Baanoon, dancing across all his eyes, but he only grinned and said:

“Great! Thank you. Much appreciated. I'd been trying to find my chewing pebble all day long. You helped me”.

This time, he wasn't lying – a small round piece of agate was gleaming on the ground three feet away.

“Perfect! You asked for it”, Rina threatened. “I'll go straight back to the Palace and tell Her Radiance, the Lady Administrator, what you've done, and she will send a dozen red crabs here. To investigate your crimes”.

“Re... Re... Red?!” Balubibo stammered painfully, turning pale and dim. “Cra... Cra... Crabs?!”

“Yes, exactly, crabs! Red Crabs!” Rina shouted, rolling her R's with terrifying loudness. She was gaining momentum. “You don't know who I am!” she hollered, advancing like a battering ram toward the trembling jellyfish. “I am the Race Champion. The Yellow Brook Keeper. The Owner of the Desert”. She had meant to say “conqueror”, but this came out even better. “I am the Chief Assistant to Her Most Serene Lady Administrator!”

“Blub! Blub! Blub!” burbled Balubibo, shaking feverishly.

“The Judge of the Duchy is my cousin! The Head Guard is my uncle! And I am the Grand Duchess's personal friend!”

That was the deathblow. Balubibo couldn't take it any longer.

“BLAAB!” the Baanoon gurgled with all his might – and gaped wide!

The fish shot out like lightning, bolting toward freedom and never looking back, vanishing into the blackness.

“BLE! BLE! BLE!” Balubibo choked and started to cough as if firecrackers were going off inside him. And then a violent convulsion racked his body:

“BL-BL-BLAAAB!”

The Striped Pearl flew out of the jelly flower like a bullet.

“AB! AB! ABAB!” Balubibo bubbled furiously, all his eyes bulging out.

But physically bound to his burrow, he was powerless there – his precious pearl twirled about four paces in a high arc and plopped into a little puddle of soft silt.

In half a second, Rina darted over, grabbed it, and held it up to show him.

“There! You! Look at this! From now on you will never trick poor fish again. You're a liar. Your phony stories are over. Forever!”

“BL! BL! BAB!” Balubibo coughed, staring at the Pearl in Rina's hand in absolute horror and desperately flapping the petals on his head.

Suddenly, the entire wasteland lit up like an Olympic stadium. Rina couldn't believe her eyes, from every round hole, flowers were growing – fantastical buds of multicolored glowing jellyfish! They opened up and burst into bright festive lights – red, blue, green, yellow, pink. Hundreds of dazzling blooms! Rina stared at the spectacle with her mouth open, completely forgetting about the Pearl and the Baanoon.

The jellyfish-flowers looked back at her – and all smiled. Then a blue jellyfish, the closest one, growing right at Rina's feet, spoke in a wonderful opera-like voice:

“Thank you! We thank you, kind stranger! You have saved the honor and glory of our lineage, the entire nation of the flower jellyfish!”

“Me?” said Rina in surprise. “I just let the fish out”.

“Exactly! And you took back the Striped Pearl, which was stolen from us by that... well, let's forget his pathetic name! What you have done is very noble and deserves a great reward”.

“Oh, no need for rewards”, said Rina modestly. “I just don't like tricksters and I can't stand to watch little ones being bullied”.

“Bravo! Bravo!” shouted the jellyfish-flowers from all sides.

“The Striped Pearl is one of the symbols of our family”, said the green jellyfish on the right. “There are very few like it in the Ocean – we only know of five such pearls. And for those who receive them honestly, the Striped Pearls bring happiness and good fortune. This one is yours by right – keep it!”

“No, I can't take something so rare”, Rina objected.

“You've earned it! You've earned it! It's yours! Yours!” the jellyfish insisted.

Rina looked at them and sighed with a smile.

“Well then... if you insist... Thank you, of course! ... And what will happen to Balubibo?”

“Don't worry about him”, answered the purple jellyfish on the left. “We'll take care of his re-education. Without the Pearl, he's nobody. He'll have to obey the laws of the flower jellyfish and give up his predatory ways. He'll become a peaceful water-filterer just like us”.

Rina glanced at Balubibo – he had shriveled up, hunched over, gone completely grayish and dull – a pitiful sight.

“And you won't hand him over to the Belmons?” she asked.

“Definitely not!” assured the yellow jellyfish on the right. “We'll douse him in the juice of blickusta, return him to childhood, and raise him anew. Everyone knows jellyfish are the best educators in the world!” The jellyfish-flowers rustled again and flashed with approving orange lights.

Wasting no time and seizing the moment of the crowd's enthusiasm, Rina asked:

“Kind jellyfish, could you tell me how to get to the Light Ocean? I really need to see King Bollung”.

“We'll help! We'll help!” the jellyfish chorused.

“We can't swim”, explained a lilac jellyfish. “We are bottom-dwellers. But we have friends – the floundulas. They are wise and know everything about everything – they learned the secrets of both oceans. Wait, we'll call them”.

And right then, the multicolored jellyfish began disappearing, one by one, into their deep burrows. Very soon it was dark and cool again, and just as the last one dimmed and faded away from the surface, Rina heard – or rather, felt through her feet – the ground beneath her start to tremble rhythmically.

“Doo! Doo! Doo!” rumbled somewhere in the earth. “Voo! Voo! Voo!” echoed back from distant cliffs.

A few minutes later, it stopped, and the jellyfish began to reappear – one by one and in groups – emerging from their holes, lighting up as they rose and blooming into their luxurious, wondrous flower shapes.

“They'll be here soon, very soon”, said the closest yellow jellyfish. “The floundulas are diligent and serious fish – they won't let you down”.

“The most honorable fish in the whole Ocean!” agreed the other jellyfish.

A moment later, all fell silent and looked upward, searching for the floundulas. Some time passed in quiet anticipation.

“Maybe they...” Rina began to ask.

“Coming! They're coming! The floundulas!” everyone shouted at once.

No matter how hard she tried, Rina couldn't see anything – no lights, no shapes. Apparently, jellyfish had stronger darkness-adapted eyesight.

“Floundulas, what a strange word”, Rina thought. “Floundulas... Floundelas... Flounderas... No! Flounders!” she guessed.

Until that day, she had only seen flounders in pictures and couldn't really imagine how fish with both eyes on one side ever managed to swim. But very soon she saw for herself that flounders were magnificent swimmers.

Finally, Rina saw them. Nine large snow-white fish – she counted them. From afar, they looked like a flock of Pacific albatrosses – mighty white birds in the night sky. Their strong, elastic movements, their proud bearing, and their uniquely elegant, streamlined forms inspired involuntary respect.

Unfortunately, the flounders turned out to be mute. They could hear and understand everything, and they even knew three oceanic languages, but they couldn't speak at all.

The flounders approached and hovered ten meters above the welcomingly blinking jellyfish. The fish kept together, gently moving their muscular fins to stay at the same height. The largest of the nearby jellyfish, radiating an especially intense ruby-red light, turned to the fish and briefly explained everything that had happened there. Also, the jellyfish introduced Rina to the flounders. She bowed. In response, the fish descended and, turning toward her, nodded their heads slowly and meaningfully.

Rina had no idea how to behave properly according to the local etiquette, so she was just smiling and waving both hands as politely as she could.

Then, on behalf of all the jellyfish kin, the Ruby Jellyfish asked the flounders to help guide the foreign traveler to the realm of King Bollung. The name of the ruler of the rival ocean initially unsettled the fish, but after exchanging some silent gestures and signals with the jellyfish, they calmed down and began nodding again.

“The floundulas will escort you to the Blue Current and help you enter it”, said the yellow jellyfish on the right.

“Why do I have to enter a current?” Rina asked, slightly confused.

“The Blue Current is the Great Ocean Road – it once connected both main palaces. But now this part of the Current is under the control of the Duchess of Boz, and entering it is forbidden, though not for everyone. The white floundulas have known these waters since childhood – they'll lead you along the safest path”. The fish hovered just above Rina, bowing left and right as if to confirm the jellyfish's words. “Follow them, kind foreigner, and have no doubt – the fish will help you!”

“I have no doubts, I'll do exactly what you said”, Rina replied seriously.

The flounders were done with their sweeping bows, and now, using fancy gestures to bid farewell to the jellyfish, they began slowly rising above the undersea meadow.

“Goodbye! Thank you!” Rina exclaimed, looking around at the flowers. “Thank you all! I'll never forget your kindness!” She turned, pushed off, and swam after the fish.

“Farewell, good stranger! Farewell! Farewell!” The jellyfish blinked with a soft, sorrowful-white parting light.

The flounders gained altitude, and Rina struggled to keep up. Looking down, she saw hundreds of white lights scattered across the pitch-colored seafloor like white pearls on black silk. A magical sight! But there was no time for awe and gasping – the flounders moved slowly, though steadily. Rina picked up speed, and with each stroke, she drew a little closer. Where was the Blue Current? Was it near or far? And would the flounders wait when she got tired?

“Everything happened so quickly”, she thought. “I didn't get a chance to ask anything... Though, how would I – they're mute”.

At that moment, one of the flounders separated from the group. Only now did Rina get a clear sense of the true size of these unusual fish – more than two meters long! The flounder stopped and nodded several times. Rina approached and carefully touched the fish with her fingertips. The flounder nodded. Rina understood and grabbed hold of the fish's smooth, firm fin.

The flounder flicked her tail and confidently pulled Rina along.

“Thank you”, Rina said, but the fish probably didn't hear.

A minute later, they were in the middle of the white group. Rina gently pulled herself up and lay down on the flounder's flat back, pressing herself against it and holding on to both side fins with her hands (she somehow figured the fish would feel more comfortable this way). Immediately after that, the flounders, no longer held back, picked up their pace.

The forceful currents roared in Rina's ears, and her hair streamed behind her as if in a strong wind. Carefully letting go of one fin, Rina adjusted her slipping backpack and touched the chest pocket of her sundress – there, warm and safe, lay the Striped Pearl.

“I'll be at the Palace, and if I have to give a gift to the King, there's nothing I have that could be better than this pearl”, she thought.

Then a strange idea came to her – just a week ago, she would never in her life have believed she'd be casually meeting and speaking with queens, kings, and duchesses. It all seemed so surreal that the royals and nobles, along with these giant white fish, now felt like a scene from a children's dream.

“But no”. She sighed sadly. “A dream is something you can wake up from. I'm not dreaming. Nina is in captivity in the Seventh World – the farthest one – and I am still in the Third”. She looked into the distance, finding only darkness there. “We're moving toward the Blue Current, which means toward the Light Ocean – straight west, just as the map commanded. And that's what matters. Not a dream – it's my reality”.

The flounder seemed to catch the drift of Rina's thoughts – the fish inclined her flat body and appeared to smile. The distance to the Blue Current was nine thousand two hundred flounder-lengths.

17 – THE LIVING WALL

The nine flounders moved in unison, huge, powerful white birds cutting through the water. Rina lay on the back of the largest of them. With each stroke of sturdy fins, both the fish and the girl rose and fell in rhythm, slowly tilting to the left, then to the right, like a cradle. The steady, repetitive motion, the darkness and the silence all around made Rina want to close her eyes, relax, and drift off to sleep. She shook her head – no! She would fall off the fish's back in a second. To fight off the persistent pull of sleep, she kept biting her lip – a proven trick used by her friend Svetlana, a notorious sleepyhead, during every test...

The flight had already lasted more than two hours. Rina shifted position a hundred times, twisting this way and that to settle herself, but nothing helped. Her arms and fingers went numb, turning into dead, lifeless sticks. Her back was stiff, and whatever strength she had left was fading – melting like snowflakes on a cheek.

“A little longer and I won't make it”, she thought in despair.

At that very moment, just as Rina, exhausted and weary, was about to ask the kind fish for a brief rest, the flounders – all at once, without any visible signal – leaned left in sync and began to spiral downward. Rina lifted her head and peered into the depths. There, at the very heart of the black abyss, a faint glimmer appeared – like a small golden cross beneath a piece of frosted glass. The flounders stopped beating their tails and were now falling almost vertically.

Soon, Rina realized it wasn't a cross below, but four yellowish dots. A minute later, she could see they were pyramids of citrines – the same kind used in Boz on every corner. Most likely, these markers had been placed by the Duchess to establish the boundary of the Blue Current, or maybe one of its entrances.

The flounders kept descending until, finally, they slowed their fall, glided down, and effortlessly touched the sandy ocean floor with graceful precision. The fish spread out and lay still for a few minutes.

“Tired, poor things...” Rina thought, sliding down from the flounder's back and cautiously swimming toward the nearest pyramid.

“I've collected a bunch of souvenirs, but I still don't have a yellow stone”, she said aloud, trying to steady her nerves and regain her balance after the long flight.

At the very top of the pyramid lay an oval, elegant pebble, about the size of a big grape. Luckily (according to the starfish religion), it wasn't glued in place. The stone turned a bit brighter the moment Rina touched it.

“A fire gift for Mom. I just hope it'll keep glowing back on Earth...”

She glanced at the fish. One by one, they were lifting themselves off the sand and slowly gathering into a circle. Two of them began moving their front fins quickly.

“That's how they talk”, Rina guessed.

Then one of the fish gave a strong thrust of her tail, glided over to the pyramids, and pointed her snout somewhere ahead. Rina looked in that direction and noticed a faint yellow dot, barely visible to the eye.

“More glowing stones?” she asked.

The fish nodded.

“Are we going there?”

The fish shook her head ‘no-no-no' and indicated the opposite way – into the dark, no lights at all. Rina understood that was their target.

Soon, the other flounders joined in. Once they were all together, they lined up in a new formation. With gestures and nods, they placed Rina at the very end of the flounder convoy. She couldn't figure out why they were doing it and expected some kind of ancient fish magic, but nothing unusual happened.

The minute they were all in line, the fish set off like a freight train, one after another, the smallest flounder first, the largest one at the end. Rina followed the pattern, waited a second for the ninth fish to pull ahead by three feet, then pushed off the floor and hurried after her white friends.

For a while, the flounders glided just above the sandy surface, neither rising higher nor rushing ahead.

Then something cracked loudly in the emptiness – as if a dry branch snapped underfoot in a sleeping forest – and instantly, a red crab appeared in front of the lead flounder! It wasn't as huge as the guard-crabs, but still, the mere sight of it left Rina frozen in shock.

The front flounder dashed aside, and the crab, blinded by rage, charged after her, stabbing its sharp bony legs into the sand with each yanking stride. The youngest flounder weaved and zigzagged, luring the crab farther and farther away until both of them vanished into the blackness.

The remaining fish paused for a minute, hovering in place, and then turned to look at Rina. Naturally, she was still shaken by what she'd just witnessed, but she understood the clever trick – the crab was being drawn off so the rest could move forward. She waved her hand. The eldest fish nodded, and the whole troop resumed its journey.

Barely three minutes had passed before another crab attacked the front flounder – an oversized, dimwitted crustacean, wild with anger, snapping its claws as it charged after the agile fish. A minute later, everything went quiet. The flounders moved on.

It happened four more times. Now, only the three most experienced remained in the lead. They slowed their pace and settled into a calm bottom current. But after drifting no more than half a mile, they turned and grouped into a triangle, silently watching one another with large, gray eyes.

One of the fish swam off to the side and beckoned the others with several nods.

Rina approached too – and only then did she understand what they had stopped for.

Right in front of her, a colossal, unbelievable wall of blue water rose up, extending into infinity in every direction. The glossy surface shimmered, glinting from within as if faint blue beams were refracting through dozens of distant sapphire prisms.

Rina took a step – and suddenly saw herself, as if in a dim mirror. It was like looking into the dark window of an old village house on a moonless, rainy night. She could feel the motion of a million-ton mass. The wall was flowing like rivers on Earth – like the Dnieper back home. And it was warm. Rina sensed it with her skin, her face, her palms – the inviting, living warmth of the unimaginably powerful inter-ocean stream.

“The Blue Current”, she whispered in awe, turning to the flounders.

The fish were waiting just a few steps behind. It seemed they wanted to say something.

“Thank you!” she said, her voice trembling. “I wish I could stay and thank you properly, to explain how grateful I am, but I'm out of time – I really have to hurry”. The fish nodded in understanding. “I have to go – it's important. Someone is waiting for me, someone needs me very much... Goodbye, kind flounders! I won't forget you. Never! Thank you so much, guys! Bye!”

The silent fish hovered motionless, sadly looking into Rina's eyes. She sighed, faced the Current, and without glancing back, stepped right into the blue wall. The soundless water caught her in its gentle, strong hands, lifted her up, and carried her off into the unknown blue. Rina glanced down – the flounders were gone, and so was the sea floor.

“I'll be at Bollung's palace soon, and that's what matters most”, she told herself, calming down. “Everyone says he's a solid man. That's good. In fairy tales, kind kings always help little girls”. She smiled. “How I wish this were a fairy tale. But...”

The mighty stream treated her tenderly and delicately. The quiet water felt safe, warm, and familiar.

“Like Grandma”, Rina thought.

She was so exhausted, so worn down – so many events had happened to her in this unbelievably long, sometimes nightmarish, sometimes funny, but anyway really difficult underwater day. Now, all her thoughts were wrapped in a thick fog of fatigue. Rina no longer had a clear sense of where she was or what she was doing. With each passing minute, the world around her seemed more and more like a dream.

Drifting in and out of deep drowsiness, she lay on elastic currents as if in a featherbed. She fidgeted a little, rolling over, looking for the most comfortable position, and eventually closed her eyes. The warm water rocked her lovingly, lulled her gently in its soft watery palms.

Rina smiled.

She saw her Grandma.

And Gri-Gri the Parrot with Aunissa the Queen. The three of them were walking barefoot on a sun-heated clay path, laughing, chatting about some cheerful nonsense, and munching on honey pancakes. Aunissa was in the lead. But as soon as she approached a large, wide-branched tree with reddish leaves, she gradually turned into Herx the Wizard – yet her eyes never changed, remaining fixed on the wizard's face without bothering him.

He stood there a minute, thinking. Then came closer – so close his face filled Rina's entire vision – full screen. Herx winked mischievously like a playful boy, but stated rather solemnly:

“You know, girl, I never doubted you. And look how skillfully you are doing everything – much better than anyone could have imagined”.

“I'm doing what better?” Rina asked, in Grandma's voice.

“Just everything!” exclaimed Herx. “And honestly, I didn't expect you to be so fast. Can you believe it – you have crossed a world and a half in six minutes!”

“Six minutes?” Rina repeated in surprise. “Why? It's been several days!”

“Six minutes, six days – what's the difference?”

Then Herx suddenly remembered something very important:

“Listen! I won't be able to come to you anytime soon. I'm busy, you should understand. Some complications. But don't worry – everything's going to be fine. Just one thing: don't believe Aien. Not a single word. Keep in mind – Aien is a liar, a blabbermouth, and a swindler! A big, sneaky liar! Got it? And whatever you do, don't touch the Ae flower”, he paused and added, “Well, I mean – if you absolutely have to, if there's no other way, then all right, you can touch it, nothing terrible will happen. But it's better not to risk it. So, don't forget the formula: Aien is a liar, the Ae flower is dangerous. It's simple. Do you hear me?”

“I hear you. But who's this Aien guy?”

“What Aien? Where's Aien? When is Aien?” Herx suddenly squawked in a horrible, raspy voice.

Where the old man had stood, there now perched a bird, perhaps a starling – yes, a starling, clearly, but so scruffy and disheveled, feathers sticking out on all sides, with an uncombed forelock standing up on its head.

“Who are you?” Rina asked, bewildered.

“No entry without clearance!” barked the starling, flapped its wings, and flew away fast. Rina watched him fly, and saw her yard in Kyiv. On the purple bench beside the jasmine bush sat her mother, explaining something to a boy standing in front of her.

“Mom! Mom!!!” Rina called and tried to run closer – but couldn't move.

Mother didn't hear her.

Rina recognized the boy – it was Herx's student, Yunni. In the bright sunlight, his blue face looked like pure aquamarine. But from the distance, it was impossible to tell whether his eyes were open or shut.

“Yunni!” she yelled, hoping he would hear her.

And he did.

Yunni turned his head, saw her, and raised his blue hand.

Rina wanted to wave back – but she had no arms.

“What's happening to me?” she panicked.

In that same instant, the yard darkened and vanished as if it had fallen into a bottomless well – and in its place appeared a wide, round meadow dotted with delicate, spindly fir saplings. In the center of the circle, five smiling riders sat atop bay horses. Rina stepped forward with purpose.

The riders were dressed in medieval armor made of thick bull-hide and blackened plates of iron. In their hands were long lances with blue and yellow ribbons at the ends.

One of the riders bowed and declared with as much of mock pomp as he could:

“Zivela Kralica, darca nieba zauviek!”

The horsemen giggled.

Rina gave a courtly bow too, deliberately weighty, and replied:

“Kralica priima tvoi naklon”.

The riders exploded with mirth.

Then everything glittered and sparkled – as if dozens of camera flashes went off at once – and within seconds, the people and their horses dissolved into the air. All that remained was the empty meadow – which, before her eyes, began turning into a large, hilly cloud, pink on one side, turquoise on the other, with a big hollow at the top.

But it soon turned out that Rina had been lying right in that weightless crater, in the fluffy little dip – merely resting, arms spread wide, gazing up dreamily at the violet sky. A dream within a dream.

In a minute her unruly eyelids dropped again, and her body, like fast-melting wax, filled every corner of her imagination. Now the third dream slipped into her soul, flooding it generously with a benevolent sea of deep serenity.

Rina savored it, luxuriating in the soft, ephemeral pillows of the steam mound. She was sound asleep in the second dream, slowly floating through the third one – carried off either by a sky-high wind or by a calm tropical river. But which layer of dream she was in now was hard to tell. Surrounded by the warmth of innocent reverie, she slept peacefully on until... a firm, persistent tentacle roughly tapped her on the shoulder, and an unfamiliar voice, harsh and abrupt, shattered all three dreams in an instant.

“Hasn't anyone reported this to Baruvil yet?”

18 – KING BOLLUNG
part one

The words were in a new language, yet Rina understood them easily. Still not quite awake, without opening her eyes, she answered:

“Better report to the king”.

“The king?!” the voice asked in confusion.

She shuddered, opened one eye, then another – and found herself face to face with a jelly-like, shapeless monster, trembling disgustingly, with a dozen short limbs growing everywhere. No, she wasn't too frightened – but out of instinct, she backed away a little. Only then did she fully make out the giant jellyfish, as tall as a grown man – thick, many-legged, topped with a puffed-out wide cap molded like an old toadstool. But unlike the similar creatures on Earth, this one had enormous black eyes – round and bulging, just like a deep-sea lemnia fish. His eyes were half-covered by long white lashes, obviously made of that same jelly material.

“Report to the king? Report what to the king?” asked the jellyfish, laboring to tilt its fifty-kilo “sombrero” from side to side. Rina noticed he had nothing resembling a mouth – he simply radiated his thoughts into the water, effortlessly, without bothering to imitate real speech like many other Ocean dwellers did.

“You should report to King Bollung directly!” Rina insisted, unexpectedly firmly, standing up from the flat coral she'd apparently been laid on. “Inform him that Her Shiness the Keeper of the Yellow Brook and the Mistress of the Desert requests an urgent audience. On a matter of importance”.

The jellyfish's eyes grew rounder still. Turning to the side, he muttered quietly:

“No, I really can't proceed with this without Baruvil. Let him figure it out. After all, whose department is this – mine or his?”

“His”, squeaked a voice in reply. “But she's right – we'd probably have to inform the King anyway”.

Rina looked and saw a tiny golden fish, no bigger than her pinky. Obviously, despite its miniature size, the fish was in royal service and clearly an important figure.

“Fine”, Rina agreed. “Take me to your Baruvil. But mind you – I don't have much time, and His Majesty won't be pleased with unnecessary delays”.

“What am I saying?” she thought. “Why am I talking like this?”

But neither the jellyfish nor the golden one found her words the least bit strange. Without another word, the three of them set off – gliding over a sandy path into a misty pink fog. The jellyfish led the way, as if he were the senior officer. Rina followed. The golden fish swam behind, playing the role of the detainee's escort.

Rina looked at the little procession, barely stifling a laugh.

“Back to prison again?! This is turning into a comedy”.

And in this whimsical mood, she decided to tease her bumbling convoy – she suddenly kicked off the ground with both feet and zipped straight up.

“Wait! Wait! Where are you going?” squeaked the fish, flashing past Rina's nose.

“Catch her! Catch her!” yelled the jellyfish, struggling to chase the escapee – his massive mushroom-cap made any real speed impossible.

Rina sighed.

“Oh, you! Pitiful guards! Okay, you won, I give up”. And slowly floated back down.

The indignant jellyfish flailed his little legs in outrage. The golden fish screeched something incomprehensible right into Rina's ear, forcing her to wave him away like a pesky mosquito.

“It's all right, relax. I won't do that again”, she promised. “Take me to your prison”.

“Prison?!” the jellyfish croaked, his eyes jumping up past his forehead. “What prison? We have no prison at all!”

“You don't? Then where are we going?”

“To the Bureau of Registration, of course. Where else? Unless you already have your Eight-Sided Mark”.

“What mark?” Rina was lost.

“The Eight-Sided kind! Like this”. The jellyfish bent forward and, as best he could, revealed the crown of his cap. Right in the center was a beautiful design – something like a multi-triangular snowflake.

“No”. Rina made a face. “I don't have anything like that”.

“There, you see? But we all do! And soon you will too!” the jellyfish said cheerfully.

“Me?”

“Of course, you! They'll make you a proper mark, and you'll become a full member of our great, world's best society of Free Swimming. And you'll become a happy subject of the kindest King Bollung! And definitely...”

“You can't!” the fish squealed. “I have to report it first!”

“Exactly!” agreed the jellyfish. “First, the Eight-Sided Mark. Then Benden reports to Baruvil and the department. And after that you'll be named a happy subject!”

“And what if I don't want to be a happy subject?” Rina objected. “And I don't need any of your stupid marks either! I'm a tourist. And by the way – I'm the Keeper of the Yellow Brook and Mistress of the Black Sands!”

“Uhhh...” the jellyfish moaned.

“You have no right to detain me!” Rina went on indignantly. “I demand an audience. Immediately! Her Shiness the Keeper and the Desert Mistress hath the urgent state-level information for the King to beest thither presenth without delay!”

“I forget what movie that was from”, she thought. “But it actually sounds pretty cool”.

“Uhhh...” the jellyfish closed his eyes, possibly entering a trance. But soon he stopped moaning and declared:

“In that case, I must... No! I am absolutely compelled to pass your message on”. He gave a slight bow. “To pass it on to the Senior Master of the Court, Mr. Be-Ovoh, in his private chancery”.

“Then do so even but now!” Rina granted graciously.

Eventually, after nearly fifteen minutes of this travel, interrupted by pointless stops and the jellyfish's lectures on civil etiquette, the fish squeaked:

“That's it! We're here!”

Rina looked around: a deserted sandy spot, strewn with all sorts of marine debris, dimly lit by rare pink corals. And a round burrow – or rather, a big hole in the floor – about five feet wide. And that was it.

The jellyfish hurried over to the hole and gleefully announced:

“Here's Ka Do! In honor of the 40th Pinkening, we are permitted entry through the Ceremonial Class-Three Door! Meh-meh-meh!”

“Meh-meh-meh!” squeaked the golden fish in response.

Rina thought they were mocking her, making fun of a stupid foreigner – but she was wrong. They were completely serious. The jellyfish furrowed the flesh above his unblinking eyes, even managing a sort of frown, and insisted:

“Meh-meh-meh! Come on now! What are you waiting for?”

“Come on what?” Rina shrugged. “What exactly am I supposed to do?”

“Nothing, just say it! That's all!” the fish prompted.

“Say what?”

“Meh!”

“Meh?” Rina didn't believe it.

“Meh-meh-meh!”

“Fabulous!” But after a moment's thought, she decided to go along with it. “Fine, if that's what it takes. And honestly, what does it matter what I say? Let it be meh-meh-meh, or baa-baa-baa – I don't care!” So she approached and said:

“Okay, take it! Meh-meh-meh!”
