“Ohh!” the jellyfish exhaled in relief, and continuously groaning, began squeezing himself into the hole. Finally, he succeeded.

Rina peeked inside the tunnel. The burrow resembled an unfinished well – dark and totally unequipped, but at least its walls were smooth, they glowed faintly with a pinkish hue and looked soft as if made of thin plastic. The jellyfish descended as if in slow mo, his epic mushroom-cap sinking steadily. “Elevator”, Rina thought.

“After you”, the golden fish piped up politely, right in front of Rina's nose.

The descent was dizzyingly unpleasant. The shaft felt bottomless. And worse – the jellyfish, being the slowest of the group, constantly got in the way, so Rina kept accidentally kicking him in the head with her sneakers – well, in the hat, technically.

“Sorry”, she said each time.

And the jellyfish muttered even more crossly.

But after about ten grueling minutes, the shaft widened significantly – until at last it turned into a vast vertical hall. The space grew lighter, and the water became warmer. Soon the silence ended too – sounds could now be heard from below. Rina couldn't resist. She overtook the sluggish jellyfish with ease.

“You mustn't! Etiquette! Chamber Master!” he yelled after her – but she was already far ahead.

Below, somewhere on the bottom, a bright device gleamed – long and narrow, resembling a giant neon lamp. The rumble grew louder. For a split second, Rina thought she heard a harpsichord playing amid the noise.

Then she turned upside down and swam like a little frog, paddling with her arms and legs at the same time, leaving the poor jellyfish far behind – apparently he was deeply insulted by the crude and unforgivable violation of palace ordinance, paragraph three, section eight… or seven. Rina looked back at him and laughed.

Rina was approaching the base of the shaft and soon spotted two large cream-colored fish and a red jellyfish that looked fairly ordinary. When there were only about twenty paces left to the bottom, the waiting red jellyfish opened his splendid umbrella and rushed up to meet her. Rina paused in mid-descent out of caution.

“Welcooome!” greeted the red jellyfish and quickly added, “Bousing, Second Assistant to the First Chamber Master”.

Rina waited until he rose high enough for her to see his eyes – but found none on his face. She gave a slight bow and responded with her practiced introduction formula:

“hello-i'm-the-keeper-of-the-yellow-brook-and-conqueror-of-the-black-sands-my-name's-rina”.

The jellyfish bowed back, then turned silently and floated down with solemn grace. Why solemn grace? Most likely because his stylish poppy-red umbrella was covered in sophisticated silver patterns, and as he swam, a long train of a hundred fine red tendrils streamed behind him, making him appear richly and ceremonially dressed.

Just then, Rina heard shouting and commotion above her. She looked up and saw her old acquaintance – the mushroom-cap jellyfish – still discontented and still indignant. But the moment he spotted Bousing, he fell silent mid-word and, glancing guiltily around, sank his way past, doing his utmost to look uninvolved. And though Bousing had no eyes – or even a face – Rina could clearly feel his coldly mocking, scornful glance. The mushroom-cap jellyfish shrank to half his size under it.

“This way, please”, Bousing said, turning to Rina before quickening his pace.

Moments later, she stood upon a marble floor in a colossal hall as vast as Mbovva Cave. It was empty, but grandly illuminated by countless pink and blue coral lamps that cast long, two-toned shadows from thin white columns.

“His Majesty is presently occupied with matters of extreme importance”, Bousing whispered, flicking his enchanting umbrella. “But if you are inclined to wait, you may – perhaps – be granted the honor of beholding the Great King in this Hall of Doubles, or wherever else His Majesty deems fit”.

He paused, expectant.

“Thank you”. Rina nodded. “I am inclined”.

“In that case”, Bousing perked up, “permit me to announce your arrival. What may I send to His Majesty's First Secretary?”

Rina took the word ‘send' in the Earth sense and replied uncertainly:

“Like… I mean… please send… um… my best regards to the King”.

The jellyfish made a lightning spin, evidently expecting to see the ‘regards' in Rina's hand. Finding nothing, he blurted out, in a blatant breach of etiquette:

“Where?”

Realizing her blunder, Rina unzipped the chest pocket on her sundress and pulled out the Striped Pearl. Under the bi-colored lamps, the sky-blue and lilac stripes gleamed like festive fairy lights.

Bousing stopped fluttering his umbrella and began to float upward, completely unaware, until he bumped his head against the stone ceiling. From above, he gasped in admiration:

“I've never seen such sincere, such marvelously flawless regards to His Majesty...”

Rina didn't answer. Deep in her girlish heart, she didn't want to give away this one-of-a-kind pearl. Not out of greed – of course not! But really, where on Earth could you ever find anything so rare, so exquisite? Nowhere! Not for any price. Don't even try.

For a moment, she imagined the Striped Pearl set in curls of golden wire, with a tiny clasp at the top – to pin it to her dress, to her midnight-blue graduation dress all sequined and sparkling...

But! The value of unlocking the Fourth World's secret was worth a thousand times more than even a handful of such pearls!

So Rina simply placed her treasure on her palm and, in a pompous theatrical manner like in a homemade play, declared:

“Honored assistant to the chamber master! I ask you to present this – my sincere esteems – to His Majesty Great King Bollung”.

Bousing snapped out of his trance, flipped upside down, and in three swift strokes darted over to Rina's hand. He immediately snatched the Pearl with his long, grasping tentacle, and then, without a word, he straightened, tightened himself like a spring, and launched off, torpedoing his way toward the dark columns at the far end of the hall.

Rina decided to stay where she was.

She figured she'd done everything she could. Now the only sensible thing left... was to sit and wait.

Clearly, the Striped Pearl had convincingly outweighed all the most important state and royal affairs. Pretty soon, literally in a few minutes, a bluish light shimmered between the black columns, and from there, out of the luminous cloud and bottom dust, emerged a crowded group of diverse under-the-sea creatures. Among them, you could easily distinguish fish, jellyfish, anemones, and even snakes, but mainly, they were octopuses of all possible colors and conceivable sizes. However, Rina noticed that none of them was white.

The crowd reluctantly glided over the polished tiles toward Rina, chatting noisily, occasionally laughing, and one could imagine that they were all classmates or pals, returning from a boisterous party or having just stepped out of a movie theater where they had watched a long but hilarious comedy.

Suddenly, the group stopped and fell silent. Now, only two flatfish with luxurious smoky tails and thin white horns on their round foreheads approached Rina. Stopping about ten paces away, they turned and stood vertically, snouts up, maintaining this position with rapidly fluttering transparent fins.

Rina stood, looked at them, and decided that this was most likely an honor guard. Then she lifted off the floor and swam between the fish, as if through a triumphal arch.

“Probably, this entire batch is the royal courtiers”, she reasonably assumed. “And where there are courtiers, there must be the king”.

And although that is not always the case, there was no other option – let them be courtiers, even such as these, at least it's better than jailers and judges...

The retinue was silent and, without blinking, watched Rina's movements, ludicrously clumsy from their point of view. In another situation, the palace dwellers would have laughed at her and arranged their favorite Bi-Wi-Co – a brief wit contest, as they called rude mockery and outright backbiting toward newcomers and strangers. But after the Striped Pearl, and against the backdrop of all of Rina's unheard-of titles, the courtiers were not entirely sure who exactly had appeared before their eyes, and therefore, for the time being, preferred to remain silent.

Rina swam closer, looked at everyone, and suddenly, absolutely out of the blue, inexplicably and unexpectedly to herself, addressed them in a frivolous, overly familiar way, pretty boldly and without any trace of ceremony:

“Hi, folks”, she said, “do you know how to find the King up there?”

The retinue started rustling, humming, buzzing, all agitated, all exchanging frightened glances and whispering feverishly to each other, barely opening their mouths. Rina could have stood on that spot for as long as she wanted, no one was going to respond to such an unprecedented, audacious, insolent question. And if it weren't for the red jellyfish Bousing, there was no telling how all this commotion would have ended and what exactly the extreme irritation of the palace nobles might have turned into. Perhaps, into arrest again.

“His Majesty is expected to arrive any time now”, he announced bombastically in an inspired manner, rising above the broad backs of the courtiers.

Rina sighed and prepared for a long wait. She, of course, knew from her books that “any time” with kings easily transforms into any number of hours. But to her great luck, the king, although extraordinarily foolish, was naturally curious, and therefore he hurried with all his might to see the amazing stranger who was generously distributing such immense treasures left and right.

True, the royal “with all his might” actually meant a long series of routine operations necessary to appear before the public in full regalia. Just burnishing the huge royal forehead by rubbing it against a large shell took much time and effort, not to mention sharpening his calloused tentacles and tinting his aged suckers white. But finally, His Majesty somehow managed to spruce himself up and crawled into the hall backward through the special narrow tunnel, built exclusively for him and members of the highest family. At the sight of their benefactor, the loyal retinue spun around, jostled, and obsequiously bustled.

Bollung was old, and as noted earlier, foolish. Not so old by octopus standards, to be honest, but yes, foolish beyond infinity, to unprecedented and unimaginable levels. Though his foolishness was never pathological, like that of true idiots, nor childish, which arises either from a lack of life experience, or more frequently – from a kind of naive immature mind. No, Bollung possessed significant knowledge, was well-educated, and had quite extensive practice as the Emperor. His foolishness was of another sort – convinced, solid, and unbreakably stable, impenetrable like granite rocks. The only thing that saved the ocean citizens from the dangerous stupidity of their monarch was the king's harmlessness.

Nevertheless, harmlessness is not the same as kindness. Bollung was not particularly kind. By his nature, he was too pampered, spoiled, and lazy for great deeds, and even for medium ones, and for the smallest ones too. He preferred not to get involved in the complexities of state affairs, nor in court intrigues, and no one could ever see him engaged in politics, or schemes, or machinations.

18 – KING BOLLUNG
part two

Since early childhood, young Prince Bollung had realized that by agreeing, never arguing with anyone, forgiving everyone and forgetting everything, by simply closing his eyes to any trouble and not interfering in any activity, he could live quite happily, effortlessly, and in complete pleasure. It's hard to believe, but this homespun formula bore him golden fruit, the Light Ocean indeed flourished, prospered, and bloomed, freed from authoritative pressure and state malice. Although the Great King himself was totally unaware of this. He did not go into details.

“Show her to me!” demanded Bollung, barely entering the room, impatiently looking around and not noticing his bowing courtiers. “Where is this Keeper?”

“I am the Keeper!” said Rina loudly and swam forward.

The octopus ballooned his white porcelain-plate eyes, waiting for her to approach, then abruptly stretched out his snout, and suspiciously looked over Rina's shoulder, as if someone could be hiding there. After that, he extended his thinnest tentacle and cautiously touched the tip of Rina's left sneaker. Apparently, this inspection of the foreigner satisfied him, because in the end he said something like “uff-foo-foo” and smiled crookedly. Rina also smiled in return and bowed politely enough.

“And so, are there many of them in your yellow waters?” asked Bollung without preamble.

“Many of what? The pearls?” she clarified.

Bollung turned around in confusion, seeking support from his advisors, but they, unfortunately, were delayed for some reason. Then Rina answered her own question, that is, she lied – out of her innate stubbornness and teenage disobedience, perhaps.

“Yes, very many, Your Majesty. Three thousand three hundred pieces!”

“Three thousand?!” echoed the courtiers.

“Eeee...” the King drawled, “Then why did you give me only one piece? Are you stingy with gifts for the King, huh?”

“I'm not stingy, Your Majesty!” Rina bowed again. “To tell the truth, this pearl is the best of all three thousand!”

“And what makes it so best?”

“The stripes”.

“But aren't the other pearls striped?” the octopus didn't quite catch it.

“Yes, they are also striped”, Rina fantasized passionately and wholeheartedly, “But all the other pearls have equal numbers of blue and pink lines, as they should, and this is the only pearl in all the oceans that has more pink stripes than blue ones”.

Bollung held the Pearl with two tentacles and twirled it in front of his bulbous eyes. Suddenly, he howled like a steam engine:

“BRABAON! Where's Brabaon?!”

From the crowd of courtiers, a little orange crab shot out as if on rubber bands and jumped up to the King.

“Brabaon! Count the stripes!” the King commanded loudly.

The petite ten-legged bookkeeper grabbed the pearl with trembling claws and began diligently tapping it with his left antenna. Half a minute later, he squealed in his ecstatic-sonorous style.

“Exactly! Thirty! Seventy!” he announced this news to the entire hall.

“More pink ones than blue ones?” the King asked sharply.

“More! Many more! Very!” yelled the mini squealer joyfully, clearly ready to scream absolutely anything if it meant pleasing his King or any other superior.

Bollung stretched into a contented grin.

“Well then. And what do you want for it, the Keeper?” he asked, plucking the pearl from the crab's claws.

“I'm looking for an entrance to the Fourth World, and I need Your Majesty's counsel”, Rina replied in a calm, steady tone.

“Counsel?” the King asked in disbelief. “Counsel?! Well, all right, counsel, sure. But what else? Something more substantial?”

“Nothing more, just your counsel”, she answered honestly – and a little sadly.

“Nothing? More?” Bollung repeated. “She asks for nothing more! Just counsel!”

“He-he-he”, giggled someone in the crowd in a thin, unnatural voice.

“Guh-guh-guh!” chortled Bollung gleefully.

“Ha-ha-ha-ha-ha!” echoed a large gray fish to his left.

“Fun! Nee!” ordered the King.

In an instant, the courtiers burst into violent, unrestrained, madly loud laughter. Fish, crabs, octopuses, snakes – everyone spun and twirled, tangling in each other, writhing and shrieking with much gaiety. And at the very center of the witless mess, booming like a bass drum and bouncing on the marble floor, danced their large, round King, thoroughly magnificent – in every sense of the word.

Rina didn't laugh. She watched them, gloomily, waiting for the hilarity to run its course. All of a sudden, Bollung stumbled mid-bounce and asked, dead serious now, as if he hadn't just been laughing at all:

“And why do you need the Fourth World?”

The entire palace fell silent in a heartbeat, swallowing their leftover chuckles in a single gulp. Rina looked straight at the octopus, no jokes, and said with firm confidence the very first thing that popped into her head:

“I'm going to my grandmother's”.

“What grandmother?” the King blinked.

“My grandmother. She lives right in the realm of the Fourth World. I'm going to visit her because it's my summer break, and it's her birthday this month”.

Bollung furrowed his brows and began to think very hard. Then he turned (his advisors had finally swum over) and asked:

“Do we have a grandmother?”

“We do! We do! We've got everything!” shrilled the Chief Advisor – who, to be fair, had been nothing but an ordinary shrimp since childhood. The King relaxed.

“Fine”, he said, patting his belly like he'd just devoured one of his courtiers. “So, according to you, you're going to visit your grandmother. Because it's her birthday. But if that's true”, he paused, thinking, “If it's true, then what are you bringing her as a gift?”

Rina got into character. She nearly said she had a basket of pies, but quickly thought better of it. The King might take her Red Riding Hood story literally and demand a pie sample – and then what? In half a second, she came up with some glorious nonsense and delivered it with flawless certainty.

“I'm not bringing my grandmother anything, Your Majesty, because gifts for grandmothers are strictly forbidden by the Second Blue-Green Law, and you, Your Excellency, you know that perfectly well”.

The King was caught off guard for a moment, then quickly turned to the advisors again, who were eagerly nodding their empty heads like roly-poly toys.

“Anyone who enters the Fourth World”, Rina continued improvising, “must prove they're not hiding any gifts. To do that, they have to do three somersaults, shout the magic word ‘hara-bara-tambara', and turn out all their pockets”.

“Pockets?” Bollung was surprised.

“Yes! Exactly! Like this!” Rina reached into the largest pocket on her sundress and, with a swift flick, turned it inside out.

The King stared blankly at the thick denim seams, then with his tentacle suddenly grabbed the spot on his face where a human nose would be, gave it a sharp twist as if it was a faucet, and shrieked like a cat:

“Vyaow!!!” he yowled. “How do you do that?! It hurts!”

“Well, I just have to, no choice”, Rina sighed, struggling not to laugh. “Nobody should argue with the laws”.

“Terrible, terrible laws!” Bollung grumbled amid a wave of disapproving murmurs from the staff and courtiers, all sincerely outraged by the unfair customs of the Fourth World. “Then why did your grandmother settle in such a dreadful place?” the King asked, puzzled.

“I haven't the faintest clue”, Rina replied with difficulty, clamping her hand over her mouth to keep from exploding with laughter. She suddenly pictured her Grandma Fima, flipping pockets and somersaulting in the air.

This time, Bollung fell into deep thought again. Rina could feel the heat radiating from his enormous round head – the strain of supreme royal reasoning. At last, after much deliberation and whispering with his advisors, he declared:

“We have decided!” He winked. “Decided to help you reach your grandmother. And since today is... um...” he glanced at the retinue inquiringly. The advisors hissed a long, twisted phrase. The King didn't catch it, waved a tentacle at them crossly, and carried on, “Because today... is today!” Tentative applause fluttered through the court at the brilliance of this new royal revelation. “And so, you – the Keeper...” he looked again at the advisors, who all whispered “brook, brook” in unison. “Brook?” the King repeated, baffled. “You must... um... and that's that!” he proclaimed loudly – then fell silent.

Rina tried to extract some meaning from this royal decree, when suddenly, as if from nowhere, an orange jellyfish appeared and, in a sugary-sweet melodic voice, announced:

“His Majesty, Great and Invincible King of All Oceans...” Rina blinked – “All oceans? What about King Bellung then?” But of course, she said nothing aloud. “His Majesty”, continued the jellyfish, practically singing the phrases opera-like, “King of All Oceans, in His infinite mercy and immeasurable generosity, grants permission to our dear guest, the honored Keeper of the Brook, to proceed to the Fourth World toward her most respected grandmother”.

“Yes!” the King confirmed. “To proceed! I grant it!”

Having received the royal support, the jellyfish – clearly he was the Chief Doormaster – sang even more drawlingly.

“His Majesty, King of All Oceans, Seas, and Currents, bestows upon our honored guest a Special Ceremonial Mother-of-Pearl Carriage and a pair of riding pikes, complete and together with the coachman!”

“Me?! I?! Bestow?!” Bollung exclaimed, astonished – but the advisors hissed at him so sharply, so insistently and in such perfect sync that he just batted his lashes and looked around, boundlessly dazed.

“Thank you very much, Your Majesty!” Rina said with a long, dutiful reverential bow. And at the same time, she thought, “Great, I wonder how many other Majesties and Highnesses I'll have to meet in these worlds before the end of my journey?”

Despite the King's extreme displeasure – his total inability to grasp why and by what twist of fate he was giving something away, let alone one of his favorite carriages – there was nothing to be done. The machinery of royal gifting had been set in motion. Immediately a formation of heralds swam into the hall – a dozen long-snouted blue fish started blasting their lip-trumpets in celebration of the supreme generosity and wisdom of the most luminous and the most magnificent. And right behind them came a troop of blue crayfish, led by an officious Lobster-Watchmaster.

From the lobster's long-winded and hopelessly entangled speech, Rina was able to gather just one thing: the blue crayfish served as coachmen and handled the carriages. The rest might as well have been in Martian – it was impossible to decipher no matter the bribe. And then, at last, the carriage itself appeared.

As Rina had expected, it was a shell – huge, flat and long, polished to a chromium gleam. But unlike the Duchess's ten-gallon enamel basin, Bollung's shell was much larger and had no ornamentation whatsoever, unless they counted the royal monogram on the front and odd red ribbons dangling from the back.

The carriage was harnessed to two enormous black pike-fish, each the size of a half-ton crocodile. One of the pikes yawned, slightly parting its mouth, revealing, to Rina's horror, a row of glittering dagger-shaped teeth – razor-sharp and monstrous, like combat paratrooper knives, shining with a silvery gloss. But the pikes were well-bred and perfectly trained. They obeyed the slightest command of their crayfish driver, who suddenly whistled with a trill, made a sharp turn, and brought the carriage to a fancy stop right before the still-sulking King.

“There... that's for you...” the King muttered with a sour face.

Rina bowed again, and not wasting a second, stepped into the shell. She sat down there, grabbed the sides with both hands, and just kept sitting quietly and smiling.

Of course, she knew and felt it. She was breaking etiquette – obviously, at the very least she ought to deliver some kind of a thank-you speech or something of the sort, but she was so entirely fed up with palaces, oceans, and all their inhabitants that she resolved to finalize the ceremony, wrapping all this up as quickly as possible.

Luckily, the coachman wasn't overly devoted to protocols either. Once he saw that his passenger was securely settled, he let out a melodic whistle and raised both claws. The pikes took the signal and lunged forward so suddenly that Rina nearly flew out of the slick shell. The carriage lifted and shot across the hall.

“Fweeu! Fweeu!” the driver whistled.

“Vrrr! Vrrr! Vrrr!” buzzed the perturbed courtiers.

But it was too late – the carriage made a wide loop under the ceiling, soaring above the retinue's heads like a big snow-white bird. Rina managed to spot only the King, thanks to his hippopotamus size. And she also caught a crimson flash of the red jellyfish Bousing. She smiled and waved at him. Whether he noticed it or not, she had no idea. Besides, it didn't matter any longer.

The carriage flew on, burst into a wide corridor, banked through the courtyard, and veered sharply upward. Rina saw a round hole in the vaulted ceiling rushing toward her. Within seconds, the shell zipped into it, miraculously avoiding the sharp edges, and rocketed up toward the seabed surface. Rina pressed herself against the cold mother-of-pearl, trying to dig her aching fingers into the shell's sides.

Of course, she was scared – like any other girl would be – they could smash to pieces against the rocky ledges flashing past with savage speed. But still, oddly enough, she trusted the old crayfish's driving skills, and soon she calmed down, more or less.

“It's all right. No real danger”, she was reassuring herself, “but where are we flying? Does this fellow even know the way toward the entrance to the Fourth World?”

Naturally, the blue coachman knew nothing about the destination, nor of the purpose of the trip. He was simply caught up in the ride, waving his claws and whistling like mad – so wildly, in fact, that the poor, dutiful pikes were completely losing their minds, hauling the carriage forward with every last ounce of their pikepower.

On the other hand, like all service crayfish, he possessed the ability to sense his master's slightest whims. He was used to not thinking, not reasoning. He had spent his whole life carting nobles around, always assuming that the high-born passenger surely knew very well where they were going, and where they weren't.

At last, the terrifying tunnel ended, and the carriage broke free into the open space above a pink sandy valley. The driver leveled out the trajectory, and the tired pikes began to slow their pace. Rina finally allowed herself to breathe easier.

“Excuse me”, she said, the crayfish was momentarily stunned, but soon regained his composure and cautiously turned around to look at the passenger, though never daring to say a word. “Excuse me”, Rina repeated, looking at the driver's face. “Would you happen to know”, she tried to construct her question as carefully as she could, “where the entrance to the Fourth World is around here?”

Probably, if she had asked whether there was a hardware store to exchange one American dollar for a sack of diamonds, the crayfish wouldn't have been so shocked. The very mention of the Fourth World literally paralyzed him. He had raised his left claw halfway – and stayed frozen in that pose. The pikes, interpreting the claw as a signal, obediently turned left, tracing a perfect arc. Rina even started to worry about the driver's health. But a minute later, he began moving again. Nervously twitching his exceptionally long mustachios, he asked uncertainly:

“Madam... desires?”

“Madam desires to go to the Fourth World right away!” Rina stated, firmly and without hesitation.

The poor crayfish shriveled a little in place, but at least he didn't freeze this time. He simply gave a loud click with all six of his jawbones. Then he raised both claws high above his head, the pikes jerked their black snouts upward, and, picking up speed with every beat of their tails, they launched the carriage up nearly vertically.

After some time, the shell had climbed to such a staggering oceanic altitude that all around, in every possible direction, there was only thick, invincible darkness. Not a trace remained of the rosy fields of the Light Kingdom. Rina didn't disturb the coachman. She kept her eyes forward, trying to recall Herx's words, and wondered:

“How could I forget a thing like that? What an idiot, what a total lalabot I am!” she scolded herself. “What comes after the Ocean? Ice? Butterflies? Or was it Clouds?”

Sure, she could try asking the Crayfish, but he looked so tense, so on edge, that a question like that might send him spiraling right into a full-blown coma. Which, in this risky situation, would be both dangerous and too unreasonable.

“Though it'll be even funnier if he drops me off straight into the snowfield. And I'm soaked! In the frost! I'll turn into the prettiest little icicle ever!” Rina thought, smiling grimly and already shivering from the imagined chill.

Meanwhile, the carriage kept climbing.

But everything ends eventually. Before even half an hour had passed in this extraordinary ascent, something finally appeared overhead: far above in the heights, a faint, slightly oval-shaped glow, colored grassy green.

Rina peered so intently, narrowing her eyes until they ached, but all she could make out was a blurry, formless smudge – something that could mean anything. The impassive coachman paid no attention to the mysterious light. He sat motionless at the very top of the shell, looking like a carved figurehead on an ancient Roman ship. But the pikes had noticed the green clearly. They grew restless, fidgety, and began to reach their maximum speed.

WORLD FOUR – AIR
19 – AHAA
part one

The glow above grew stronger, and five minutes later Rina realized what it was: dozens, no, hundreds of parallel light-green bands. Waves! The surface of the Ocean!

Her chest tightened at the thought that she might finally see the sun, the sky, the clouds. These dark, cold depths suddenly felt lifeless, foreign, unbearable. She yearned for warmth and air, her soul ached for real sounds, not imagined ones, and for true smells, not water-muted ones.

Of course, Rina worried about how breathing would work. What if she ended up like a fish flung onto the shore? But her experience in the previous two worlds told her that the transition from water to air would be – well, magical, or, as people say nowadays, technological – and the problem would sort itself out...

Another three minutes passed, and the scene transformed dramatically. Bright greenish light flooded the space around her. The vibrant, shimmering waves gleamed like living mirrors, twisting and curling, sending hundreds of sharp, slender rays plunging into the deep. Rina felt on the verge of bursting into song.

But.

Just twenty meters short of the radiant swells, the pikes stopped moving. Without the slightest pause, the heavy shell began to sink slowly, drifting downward. Rina had to swim out of it.

The Crayfish remained perched at the rim of the carriage, as if glued there. He glanced at Rina and muttered in a guilty tone:

“Madam's order has been fulfilled”.

“Yes”, she replied. “It has, we've arrived”. Then she tried to clarify. “So... is this the end of your World?”

The Crayfish didn't reply. He probably wasn't allowed to talk like this with nobles. Or maybe he simply didn't know the answer. Rina waved to him:

“Right, cool. Bye then, I am gonna head out. Farewell, time for me to go”.

And she swam upward. After a few meters, she looked back – the shell-carriage was vanishing into the dark without a sound. The pikes didn't move their fins. They stared up at Rina, snouts tilted high, watching her intently.

Once again, she was alone. But this was a very different kind of solitude – eager, exultant, soaked in sunlight, and with the waves murmuring above. Yes, the waves are close now, unbelievably close! Twelve strokes away.

The surface was right there. One stroke, another, and another, another, again, again, again, and again! The watery mirror was rushing to meet her. Rina lunged forward with everything she had, and her outstretched arm pierced the thin membrane – the shimmering emerald boundary between water and air. And then...

... a mega explosion rang out.

The word ‘explosion' wasn't enough – everything exploded, absolutely everything! The water, the sky, the sun, the entire world. In a cosmic catastrophe, an insane, apocalyptic blast hurled billions of tons of green water into the sky, tore Heaven to ribbons, turned the sun black, and shook the planet to its core. Inside the towering column of water, a blinding fireball flared.

The ocean boiled, roaring with thousands of giant fountains of raging foam shot from the depths. The surface heaved, mile-high waves surged furiously in all directions. Countless monstrous bolts of lightning rained from celestial heights through the deafening crash...

But Rina didn't see any of that hell. She only caught, in the very first moment, a vague crimson flash – and then... everything was over. Time stopped. Space disappeared. Darkness again. And silence. Along with an unpleasant sensation of her body's absence. Yet she was not scared. No frustration, no despair. In fact... were there even thoughts? No. Those had vanished too. All that remained of her was “It” – the thing that had always lived deep inside her, at the very foundation of her being.

Rina's ‘It' was calm, cold, and wise. Unfazed by past events, untouched by future troubles. ‘It' knew no sorrow, nor weariness, no joy or thrill, no love or hatred.

The apathetic ‘It' was weak. The ‘It' could not resist her emotions beginning to seep out through the armor of icy indifference. Rina did not remember the rules of emotions, but she could listen to them.

“Is it me?” asked the nearest emotion.

“Yes, it's you”, confirmed a distant one, barely audible.

“Am I... dead?” the first emotion asked with a sigh.

“No, you are not”, the distant one replied positively. “You're alive”.

“Then where am I?”

“Above”.

“Yes, you're all up above”, echoed the ‘It', with its flat, empty voice.

And silence again...

Someone's warm, tender hand softly brushed her cheek. Rina opened her eyes. There, high above her, a deep sky stretched in serenity, rich with a violet hue, lovingly adorned with massive snow clouds that looked like castles from a dream. She sat up and looked around. No Body. And No Thing.

She was sitting on the surface of a cloud, on the endlessly flowing currents of dense steam. She reached out and touched the mist, it felt elastic, like a very thin foil – the steam behaved like a layer of tightly stretched white silk threads.

Gathering her thoughts with great effort, shaping them into the simplest meanings, she told herself not to jump to conclusions yet. She tried to push back against the onslaught of questions pounding inside her mind, she decided – absurd as it sounded – to “explore the cloud” first. So she stood up and walked.

Rina walked just like she would on Earth, with her legs – no swimming, no flying, no floating. However, she wasn't putting the slightest effort into her steps. It felt like she just wanted to go, and her legs, obeying her quiet command, moved on their own, as if detached from her will. It was less like walking and more like steering her body from somewhere deep inside.

The cloud was changing constantly. Here and there, rounded mounds of steam would rise, stack up on one another, swell and jostle like living things, only to pop with a hiss some moments later, shooting up little geysers of pale mist that quickly sank and merged back into the foggy substance. The cloud quivered, slowly swaying from side to side, occasionally letting out deep, almost inaudible sounds like a distant bass. Rina could feel it breathing and heard the steady throb of a massive cloudy heart...

With each second, she felt more like herself. Her thoughts arranged themselves into neat patterns, memories clicked into place, and soon she was nearly back to being the Rina she'd known since her early childhood.

“But what had happened?” she asked aloud.

She wanted to hear her voice. It trembled slightly and the sound was weak, but still the voice was real. And only then did she remember the explosion. Somewhere at the very distant edge of her mind, she saw huge, terrible red flames – and felt, with her whole being, the searing pain. But remembering pain isn't easy. Painful memories are a kind of torment...

Rina stopped on a freshly formed bump of steam and studied her hands in detail – yes, the skin was paler than usual, but that would pass. Her palms were just... palms. Her fingers were fingers. She touched her face, neck, hair, shoulders. With a flicker of regret she remembered that she hadn't brought a mirror. She reached around to check her backpack, without taking it off – it seemed to be fine, too.

“The main thing is, I can breathe. Nothing hurts. Nothing is lost. Arms, legs, everything is here... I hope. Clothes are dry... So, I'm okay?” And she answered herself, “Yes, I'm okay. And I know where I am. In the World of Clouds”.

She wandered across the cloud for a long time, with no plan in her head, not even the slightest idea. What was she supposed to do now? Where to go? And how do you go, anyway? The cloud was big, sure – but still it was just a cloud. She felt tired and sat down on a plump tuft of mist, filled her lungs with the cleanest, coolest air ever, and let herself relax.

She sat there for a while, resting, entertaining herself by watching the playful dance of two little cloud-children nearby – sometimes they would approach each other, which would take a quarter of an hour, and then they would jump in opposite directions – another quarter of an hour...

Suddenly, there was a rustle – like someone had crumpled a sheet of thin paper. Rina turned around in alarm and saw something unbelievable, a long, dark-gray cloud was floating toward her. It looked rather dense and heavy – almost solid, it even resembled an ancient zeppelin. But what truly stunned her deeply was that people were standing on it! She gasped, leaped to her feet, eyes wide with astonishment. The people saw her, too – and they were just as surprised and puzzled. Many began shouting, running around, calling out in a language she didn't understand. Some of them jumped down into the interiors of their ‘zeppelin'.

“Into the hold”, she thought.

The cloud was coming fast, but gradually it slowed, and eventually the cloud-zeppelin came to a full stop. Its underside hovered directly overhead – close enough that, if Rina really stretched, she could probably touch it with her fingers. But should she? And the bigger question, were these strangers dangerous or not? What exactly should she do in this weird situation – try to run away and hide, or just wait and see what happens?

She didn't have to wait long. Just ahead, in the lowest part of the cloud – right in the belly of the zeppelin – a large six-foot-wide hole opened up. Not a doorway or a hatch, but a hole, with uneven, rippling edges. Thick gray mist came pouring out of it, and as soon as it touched the firm surface of Rina's cloud, it quickly gathered into a solid structure that looked just like a staircase of an old aristocratic mansion.

Out of the hole stepped a girl – or rather, a very young woman – probably about sixteen. Tall, slender, graceful. She looked attractive: a lovely face, large blue eyes, and voluminous white hair tied back with a blue ribbon. She wore a long, pearly gown with a high slit and a gauzy veil that flowed from her shoulders to her shoes.

“Ai aa?” the girl asked softly, smiling.

“H-hello”, Rina replied, a little unsure.

“Oh! You speak Yulret!” the girl exclaimed in surprise. She darted down the staircase, landed on the cloud and walked toward Rina, her heels barely touching the swirling steam. “Hello! What's your name? Who are you? Where did you come from? You showed up so unexpectedly!”

“My name is Rina”, Rina said. “I'm the Keeper of the Yellow Brook”, she added, which made the girl raise her eyebrows. “I'm from the planet Earth, and I came here... well, I'm just visiting as a tourist. Right now heading for the Fifth World”.

“Fifth World?” The girl's thin eyebrows went even higher, arching in black crescents. But catching herself, she added, “And I am Ahaa, Princess of Air”.

“Pleased to meet you”, Rina said with a bow, thinking with a smile, “I was right – these worlds are full of royalty”.

“We're flying to the Second Palace, far to the North”, the princess said. “If you'd like, and if it doesn't interfere with your plans, you're welcome to join us”.

“Thank you very much, Your Majesty”, Rina said with another bow. (The princess whispered, ‘Your Highness'.) Rina had to bow again. “I'm flying to the World of Ice and Snow”, she continued. Yes, she said ‘I'm flying'. Because it didn't seem right to say ‘I'm heading' while standing on a cloud. “I don't know exactly where it is”, she added, “but usually ice and snow are found in the North”.

“Why?” Ahaa asked, slightly perplexed.

“Because it's always cold there”, Rina answered confidently.

“Cold? Oh no, not quite”, the princess objected. “The North is actually hot. If it weren't for the Upper Winds, I wouldn't last a single day in the Northern Islands”.

Rina thought for a moment and said:

“Then I guess I'm looking for a different North. But either way, I'm happy to fly with you. Thank you!” And she finished with the already familiar, “Your Highness”.

The princess nodded and, still smiling, gestured invitingly for Rina to enter the ship. Rina approached, carefully stepped onto the misty stairs – oddly firm and solid, like reinforced concrete – and boldly marched upward. Ahaa followed, her gaze curiously studying Rina's clothing.

19 – AHAA
part two

Inside, just beyond the entrance, servants and footmen stood in two neat rows. As Rina appeared, they all bowed their heads, eyes fixed on the floor. She walked past them and stopped in front of another staircase – narrow and very steep. The princess approached and said something that sounded like “ao ae au”. Instantly, the room lit up, and the staircase began to grow and stretch until, within seconds, it looked just like the grand stairs at the entrance.

Rina and the princess climbed side by side. The second floor opened into a spacious hall, though the low-hanging ceiling didn't exactly inspire confidence. Ahaa led her guest to the right-hand wall, touched a small protrusion there, and at that moment it sank inward, forming a dark little recess about the size of a fist. Then the princess simply stepped forward, walked through the wall, and vanished into an unusually dense gray steam.

Rina, deeply surprised by the trick, stopped in confusion. It was scary. The wall looked as if made of rock – firm and solid. What to do now? Of course, Rina decided to go too – she shut her eyes and took a step. All she felt was a brief rush of damp cold air on her face. She walked through.

When she opened her eyes, she found herself in a cozy, light-filled room with rounded corners and a vaulted ceiling. The walls were made of the same misty substance as the rest of the zeppelin. All the flat surfaces in the room were overlaid with a fine blue design that loosely depicted clouds and sunsets. Overhead, three strange orbs glowed brightly with a slightly bluish light – they slowly rotated and sparkled inside with electric discharges like microscopic thunderstorms.

“This is your cabin”, said the princess. “We live simply aboard the ship, nothing too fancy. I hope it's comfortable enough”.

“It's very nice – thank you”, Rina replied sincerely, but after looking around, she asked, “Where's the window?”

“Window? You mean the porthole? Here it is”.

Ahaa stepped over to the left wall and touched a white speck at the center of a spiral pattern. The dot glowed softly for a second, then began to grow until it turned into a perfectly round opening about three feet wide. Instead of glass, the porthole was made of a faint, nearly invisible haze. Rina walked up to it and saw heavy clouds far below and a flawless violet sky stretching into infinite space high above...

Not long ago, during spring break, she had flown with her father to Italy on one of his business trips. The plane behaved like any other, but for some reason, less than an hour in, it began to ascend – higher and higher, as if scaling a mountain – until it climbed above all the clouds, even the transparent ones. And that's when she saw the miracle, a dark violet sky. And on that smooth, matte violet – an enormous, sharply outlined sun, blazing with a pure, indescribably white light...

Rina asked:

“What altitude are we flying at?”

“Altitude?” the princess echoed with a baffled look. “We don't have altitudes here. We are in the air”.

Rina could not believe it. She leaned her forehead cautiously against the misty windowpane – cold and solid like regular glass – and peered down. As far as her eyes could reach, the space beneath the ship was endless, a bluish-gray void scattered with huge, slow-floating clouds suspended at every imaginable depth.

“There's no ground down there?” she asked, still trying to wrap her mind around it.

“Ground? What ground?” Ahaa tilted her head. “No. There's air. And clouds. Sometimes with rains”.

“But rains always fall to the ground”.

The princess laughed and shook her head.

“Not quite. Rains fall on lower clouds”, she said confidently, like a geography teacher straightening out a confused student.

Rina stared into the vastness below, trying to comprehend the sheer scale of it all – the world filled only with drifting clouds and open air. She tried. But she couldn't.

“We'll be arriving at Ayeon Island soon”, Ahaa said, clearly looking to steer the conversation elsewhere. “Actually, it's not really an island. Islands are hard. This is more of a motionless cloud. There's nothing much there, but people say it has the most fragrant air in the whole Province of Oai. The servants usually collect it for the rest of the journey. You can, too – for your cabin”.

After a pause, she added, “And we also play aaoo there. Do you play aaoo?”

“Me? No, I don't”, Rina replied.

“Oh, it's easy and fun! You'll love it”, the princess beamed. “I'll teach you”.

“Thank you”, Rina said with a bow.

Ahaa walked over to a little ledge in the wall.

“Make yourself comfortable. Rest. I'll send someone for you later. If you need anything, just touch this bell”. She pointed to a dark hemisphere fastened to the wall beside the window. “You'll have what you need right away”.

“Thank you, Your Highness”, Rina said, bowing again.

The princess lingered for a moment, as if expecting some traditional farewell or something in accordance with protocol. When nothing came, she gave a smile and walked out straight through the wall.

Rina was left alone.

“Well... looks fine... but where exactly am I? Yes, the World of Air – but which part of it?” she wondered. “And am I a guest here? Or a prisoner?” She turned around, scanning the room. “Okay, I did agree to fly with them to their North willingly, didn't I? It was my own decision”, she said loudly. “So, no – I'm not a prisoner at all. I'm just a new passenger. And this is a common passenger cabin. Basically... a guest room. Definitely not a jail cell. Jail in the clouds – a good title for a movie”. She chuckled at the idea.

Still, now that she looked at it closely, the apartment was a bit too small and too plain. At first glance, it could easily pass for a standard mid-range hotel room. That is, if you ignore the fantastical view outside the window, and the glowing orbs floating under the ceiling, and the walk-through walls, and the fact that the whole thing was made of mist, and that the concierge was an actual princess. Other than that – yep, a pretty standard budget hotel.

Rina wandered back to the window, took another look down – and suddenly, a funny theory popped into her head:

“If there's no ground, then there's nowhere to fall. You just keep falling and falling, and the sky never ends. So... I guess here in the World of Clouds, a true air crash is just a dream”.

At last, she took off her backpack and set it down on a flat ledge that looked just like a marble shelf. But as soon as she did, the shelf began to shrink until it turned into a perfect square – just big enough to hold the little backpack. Rina shrugged, picked up the bag by both straps, holding it in the air – and the smart shelf instantly grew back to its original size.

“Automation”, she muttered the silly word with a sneer.

There wasn't a single visible wire, or an electrical gadget, or a control panel in the room. Still, with self-adjusting shelves, with the walls you could walk through, and the staircases building themselves on command, Rina was pretty sure this wasn't magic either. That's just how the Fourth World works – the laws of another nature. You had to get used to them, and stop being surprised like a child.

In the far corner stood a thick, pale-bluish cabinet. Or wardrobe. Or locker. Hard to tell. It had no doors, no handles, no openings – none of the usual features that separate a normal closet from a big plywood box. Out of curiosity, Rina walked over and touched its smooth, icy surface.

“Fridge”, she said.

And immediately, she felt hungry. She remembered the last proper meal she had – back in the first prison, with Blasta. The vura-vura coral. And later some macaroni at Blasta's place. But that was so inconceivably long ago, and so many unimaginable events had happened since, she couldn't believe how long she'd gone without a real breakfast, lunch, or anything even remotely resembling meals.

Her stomach demanded food. Something edible. She tried to open the “fridge” the way Ahaa had opened the door to this cabin – with a simple touch of her palm. Rina pressed her hand against the “refrigerator”, tried it here, tried it there, felt all over the surface from top to bottom – nothing happened. The thing remained stubbornly shut.

Annoyed, she gave it a small punch. “Take that, automation!”

Then she remembered her emergency stash and rushed over to the shelf. In the backpack – her miracle fruit supply from Triazis, pears, three bananas, and some wild apples, all picked from the trees – that felt like a lifetime ago. And amazingly, they were fresh – the fruits were perfect and fragrant. Not even the saltwater of two oceans had spoiled them.

She couldn't find a sink, so she wiped the fruits off with her handkerchief and, with pure bliss, ate them all – pears, bananas, apples, every last one.

After that delicious feast, she felt a warm wave of sleepiness washing over her. She was calm and relaxed. Her eyelids grew heavier, her thoughts jumbled, and all her body wanted was to lie down for half an hour. Her legs and back were longing for something horizontal.

Rina looked around – but there was nothing even close to a bed. Not even a couch. “Then how do they sleep here, anyway?” A little while later, she noticed a thin slit running along the wall about half a meter above the floor. She walked over and carefully touched it. And jumped back, just in case.

Instantly, thick white steam began to billow from the entire length of the slot. Within a minute, it filled more than a third of the room, then it began to condense, pulling itself closer to the wall, thus compressing and gathering until it was reshaped into a wide, soft-looking shelf. And in fact, soft to the touch too.

There were no blankets or pillows – nothing – but one glance was enough to understand, this was really a bed. Rina sat hesitantly on the edge – no, it didn't sink or give way. The mist lay like a featherbed, yielding just enough.

“Okay... but what is the schedule here?” she asked loudly, half-mocking, addressing the glowing orbs overhead. “Is napping during the day allowed? Or strictly forbidden? What if the princess walks in and it turns into an international scandal?”

She gazed sadly at her wind-up watch – her father's gift, a perfect vintage Roamer – still frozen since that night in the Desert, beneath the Yellow Star. Hopefully, the watch survived both oceans. Rina didn't know that for sure, she didn't have enough courage to try winding it up. But even if the Roamer was still alive, what time was it actually on the ship? And was it possible to guess it from the sun's position? She went back to the window – the sun was low, off to the left.

“If noon's already passed”, she said aloud, still talking to the glowing orbs, “then maybe it's about three in the afternoon. But if it's still morning, then it is nine or nine-thirty... Though, who even knows? What if the sun in this world doesn't move at all?”

She went back to the bed. Finally, she kicked off her shoes and lay down.

A shy, soft mist gently covered her feet, tickled her neck tenderly – the bed, despite its plain, simple design, was comfy, cozy, and warm. Rina's eyes closed on their own. And without any kind of lead-up or preamble, sleep crept in quietly on tiptoe, slipped into her mind, and immediately washed away her fears and soothed her emotions, plans, and doubts – rounding their edges and quieting their restlessness.

But even in sleep, one stubborn thought never left her: Rina was waiting for Herx. She'd long since realized the wizard was honestly trying to keep his word – and more often than not, he was somewhere nearby. Of course, talking to him was possible only in the realms of dreams. Well, perhaps that was the only technology he could use in the distant worlds – the only thread that reached her there...

It was nice on the beach – at least, not as cold as before. Rina wandered aimlessly along the shore, but the sand was too loose, her sneakers kept sinking in. Soon, she settled onto the rough-cut edge of a thick tree stump, clearly washed ashore by some powerful storm. Sitting there was not very comfortable, though fortunately the stump was warm and dry. She sat and stared out at the sea, musing, slowly rambling through her memories.

But ocean weather is never stable for long. A wind picked up, bringing an unpleasant, early autumn chill. The recently clear blue sky quickly spoiled itself with fast-moving dark gray clouds. Even the white seagull bobbing close by on the steely waves seemed a little downcast. It floated on the water in silence, watching Rina intently without blinking, as if the bird wanted to ask something – but obstinately stayed quiet.

“Well, where is he?” Rina asked, growing impatient, arms wrapped around herself – too wintry in just a sundress.

Still no sign of Herx. Yes, of course – she remembered – he'd warned her. Said he'd be busy for some time, there were problems. Fine! Everyone has problems – that's life. And surely she could wait, she had been good at waiting since childhood. But anyway, couldn't he come for just five minutes? Three minutes, even?

She needed him badly, desperately. Things had changed a lot – she was in a whole new world with no actual idea of what to do next. Was she right to accept the invitation to fly with the princess? And how was she supposed to look for the clues leading to the Exit? She needed answers. And advice. And support. There were just too many doubts and not a single explanation in sight...

Rina was sitting like that, pondering and shivering on the bleak, unwelcoming shore throughout her long, dreary dream. The Seagull kept rocking on the waves, evidently waiting for Herx too, until something startled her. The bird flapped her wings and flew away. Left alone, Rina lingered on the beach for a while, trembling with cold, grumping and complaining quietly. And then decided to wake up..

20 – ALMOST 300 DIVIDED BY THREE
part one

She opened her eyes and saw orange stripes across the wall – sunlight was breaking through long, wind-torn gaps in the sprawling thundercloud hanging beside the ship. The zeppelin was quiet, the only sound was a soft, low hum – probably from the rigging or the inner mechanisms of the aircraft.

The floor, along with the bed, swayed gently. The air in the cabin was cool and crisp, like in a clearing in the forest just before dawn. But it wasn't cold – gracious waves of homey warmth, generously radiated by the glowing orbs, were spreading in all directions. Rina felt refreshed – rested, crystal-minded, and much stronger physically. She stretched, rubbed her eyes, looked around... and exclaimed happily:

“Yes! The sink grew!”

That's exactly how she said it – grew – because how else could she explain the sudden appearance of a beautiful porcelain-like basin with two shiny faucets?

She ran over and turned the chrome handles. Water poured from both taps – perfectly transparent and icy cold.

“Too bad there's no hot”, she muttered and began freshening up with relish.

It didn't take long to notice there was no soap, no towel – not to mention toothbrushes – but such trivial things couldn't spoil her good mood. She felt psyched (yes, psyched! That was the word!) to finally wash up with water that wasn't salty like the Ocean's, or flower-scented like in Triazis, but good old-fashioned real water. Spring water, she figured. Or more likely rainwater, she guessed. Which was even better.

Having made herself presentable and even cute, especially after combing her hair, Rina suddenly felt an irresistible desire to have hot, strong coffee with a bun. Her throat ached with how badly she wanted some.

Coffee! Give me coffee! And a croissant would be nice too. Or at least a bagel.

She glanced over at the “fridge” and said with mock offense:

“You're not a fridge – you're a bank safe!”

She laughed, then remembered the princess's suggestion about the little hemisphere on the wall.

She walked over, inspected the rounded nub from all sides, and without hesitation gave it a firm, vigorous, morning-strong press with her full palm.

What happened next felt like something out of a bad movie, or a silly cartoon.

Instantly, as if summoned from thin air, all kinds of people began to appear just a few steps away. Tall, short, stout, thin, young, old – all kinds. Rina stared at the sudden crowd, eyes wide. But she quickly realized they weren't guests – just footmen. Or servants. Or... whatever. She honestly didn't know the difference between a footman and a servant anyway.

In moments, half the room was filled with bowing people. The nearest one, bent at the waist, began to speak while folded in that awkward position. He asked the most classic footman question:

“What would the gracious lady desire?”

“Coffee!” Rina ordered without skipping a beat. “The lady would desire coffee. Please. With a bun, if possible!”

The bent-over footman straightened up so quickly that for half a second he was levitating in the air. Everyone else was petrified – round eyes, mouths shut.

“Oops. I probably said something wrong”, Rina thought, a little embarrassed.

But the footman simply said “at once” and nodded to a maid in a white lace apron. She immediately fell through the floor. Literally. As if a trapdoor had opened beneath her slingbacks.

The other footmen weren't fazed in the slightest and looked at Rina expectantly, ready for more orders.

And yes, she had another one – her second issue.

“Could someone tell me what time it is?”

“Time! Time? What time? Time what? What is?” the footmen echoed, glancing at each other with long faces and puzzled shrugs.

“Got it”, Rina sighed. “You don't use clocks here”.

Then a young, completely bald man in the front row beamed and said in a high-pitched, childlike voice:

“If the gracious lady permits, I could consult with Junior Scientific Advisor Mr. Ashea on her behalf. Mr. Ashea is a well-known philosopher. He wrote a book about the refraction of light in the raindrops under a half-covered sun”.

The other footmen let out a low, affirming hum, as if backing him up.

“What do I need your rain specialist for?” Rina thought but didn't say anything out loud. The bald one waited a moment, then tucked away his smile.

Suddenly, a bell rang. It sounded just like a school buzzer at recess – though much softer. All the servants folded in half, bowing at perfect right angles.

And then, just moments later, Ahaa flitted into the room – playful, mischievous like a little imp, full of energy, smiling, in a short, pale-blue dress, twirling something cube-shaped in her hand.

“Oh, hey! What's good? How are you? Did you sleep well? Did the light wake you? Bet you weren't cold, right?” she rattled off a stream of questions. “And these guys...” She gave a casual nod toward the staff. “...they weren't tripping, were they?”

Rina was a little caught off guard by the princess's shift in manners, and by the fact that she had suddenly switched to the casual street tone. No more formalities. Though what could Rina say? Ahaa was older and royalty, after all. Rina decided to let it slide.

“No one was bothering me, Your Highness”, she said politely.

“Good. Come on, let's head up to the Upper Deck. Everyone's gathering there”.

Just then, the maid in the lace apron shot up through the floor. She was holding a massive box – the kind you'd expect to find a mini-bar fridge packed in. She almost elbowed the princess without noticing her.

“What's that? What's that even for?” Ahaa scowled, eyeing the bowed servants.

“It's probably the coffee”, Rina said with a shrug.

“Kah-fee? What is kah-fee?” The princess spun toward the maid, agitated. “Where did you get it?”

The frightened woman dropped the box, went sheet-white, and began babbling something incomprehensible while bowing so deeply her chin nearly touched her shoes.

Then the bald, child-voiced footman stepped forward again, smoothly shifted his bow into a graceful arc, and declared:

“Your Highness, the Gracious Lady requested caaffi be delivered to her cabin. The order was carried out promptly and precisely”.

It took just a minute to get to the bottom of things – in the Second Ardartian language, the word “caaffi” means “something large to store other items inside”. The princess's servants – and those of the high court officials – were actually well-educated and typically spoke several languages, necessarily including both Ardarts and at least one of two Yulrets. The absurd misunderstanding with the box was cleared up in seconds. But actual coffee – let alone the bun – was never brought to Rina, and that was something to regret.

Ahaa made a quick hand gesture, dismissing the servants, then said, spreading her hands helplessly:

“There's nothing I can do with them. I try to train them the right way, but it's useless. Ever since my poor magnificent father left, everything in our unfortunate kingdom has unraveled. The people don't love their queen as they should, and they're not too eager to obey their princess either. You just saw that for yourself. And believe me, I have no idea what the right thing to do is in these situations. My Second Secretary, Auan, suggests setting up an ice cell to frighten the lazy ones. Meanwhile, my Senior Science Advisor, Aien, thinks the best punishment is to deprive them of sunlight and open air for a whole day”.

She paused for a moment (her eyes landing on Rina's backpack), and said, “Tell me, what do they do in your kingdom to raise proper subjects?”

Rina wrinkled her forehead. Indeed, what did they do back on Earth to raise loyal citizens? Lots of things!

“We have special people called police. They catch and punish hooligans”.

“I'm not interested in hooligans”, the princess replied quickly, inching nervously closer to the shelf.

“Then the rest, when they're still little, are raised by their parents”, Rina added.

“Ah!” The princess threw up her hands. “So it's the same in other kingdoms, too – no new ideas, and all the hard work dumped on luckless parents! How stupid and unfair it is! Do you really think parents can raise genuinely worthy citizens of a kingdom? Of course they can't! There's nothing to argue about”.

Rina had no intention of arguing.

Ahaa cautiously reached out and tapped the backpack with the tip of her pinky, then quickly pulled her hand back as if she'd touched a hot stove.

“All right, we must go!” she said briskly. “Better not provoke the court any further – they're moody enough already, and I've had too many problems with them”. She turned sharply and headed toward the wall. Rina followed.

As before, passing through the wall took barely a second and was completely effortless. In the next room, Rina saw that the entire left wall was now transparent, like a panoramic movie screen playing a never-ending film called Floating Clouds.

The princess picked up the pace. She walked right into the opposite wall without so much as glancing at it. Rina followed closely, but at the last second she instinctively squeezed her eyes shut, bracing for an impact that never came – the wall simply vanished and reappeared behind her, and a corridor opened up, ending in what looked like a wide marble staircase.

On the Upper Deck, in a bright, spacious salon filled with tall, black chairs, sat the top advisors, secretaries, and other members of the Lesser Court. Officially they were the princess's helpers, but their primary duties were to supervise the princess and spy on one another.

Once a week, each of them drafted a classified report and sent it by the special courier to one of the Queen's residences. But all the messages were intercepted, read, and many of them destroyed by the princess herself. Long ago, she had simply bribed the Chief Courier and his assistants.

Ahaa played a complicated, risky game at the Palace – often balancing on the edge of scandal with the court and full-on falling-out with her mother. But she was a strong, seasoned player – and almost never lost, at least in the important stuff...

The moment the courtiers spotted the princess through the glassy wall, they rose noisily, bowed their heads, and plastered on dull masks of respectful humility.

But a few steps before reaching the door, Ahaa spun around to Rina and said:

“Hey, I just thought of something. I've got to sit through a painfully wearisome debate about state matters. I think you'll be bored to the core. But I have a solution for you – go for a walk instead. It won't take long – I think we wrapped most of it up at the last meeting. Well, you have no idea how stubborn they are when they start arguing... I'll come find you later and introduce you to everyone”.

“Okay”, Rina nodded. “I'll take a walk”.

“That staircase over there, the blue one, it leads to the Second Open Deck. It's beautiful – the best deck on the whole ship. I go up there too when I need air, especially when these ones...” She rolled her eyes, clearly meaning the people in the salon. “These ones start getting on my nerves too much”.

Rina gave a small bow and headed toward the staircase.

Just as she reached the steps, she hesitated – pure curiosity. She just wanted to take a peek at the royal meeting. But there wasn't much to see, Ahaa was speaking, everyone else listened silently, then one courtier stood up – a very thin man with a sharp, fashionably trimmed beard – and spoke through a clever little smile. Then he bowed and solemnly sat back down. Ahaa gave him an approving nod and continued her speech, now with a new expression on her face – unnaturally smooth and doll-like...

The Open Deck, situated at the very front of the ship, really was beautiful – almost majestic. Towering cumulus cloud-mountains loomed just beside the ship, close enough to touch. Above them the impossibly clear crystal-violet sky stretched in all directions, trying to cover the entire universe – apparently firm, solid, and seemingly smiling.

All around, endless space, with nothing even remotely resembling a horizon. Far-off clouds overlapped each other and blended into a grayish haze. The sky didn't curve at the edges – there was no dome. It looked more like a perfectly flat, flawless ceiling.

The zeppelin cruised forward slowly – or so it seemed from this platform.

The deck was covered in an excellently polished layer of hardened mist, smooth as a mirror. On three sides, low white railings – just slightly above knee height – lined the edges. The fourth side was sealed off by a strange flat surface, no doors, no windows. In the far corner stood two small latticework benches, shaped and colored just like the ones you'd find in old European parks back on Earth.

Rina strolled back and forth – it was truly impressive, as if she was on an airplane stopped somehow in the sky and with the roof and walls completely removed. Then she sat for a while on one of the benches and finally decided it was time to head back to Princess Ahaa. Or at least to check whether the Very Important Government Discussion was over.

But then she heard a grinding metallic noise, glanced over – and gasped. Off the starboard side, about a hundred meters from the zeppelin, a tube-shaped cloud was gliding past. It was dark, nearly black-gray, and visibly heavy. In its very core was an oval opening, crackling and snapping with sharp electric flashes.

“It must be a shuttle”, Rina guessed.

There were no people on it nor any sign of civilization except the shape and discharges. The tube-cloud was outpacing the ship, moving, as they say, on autopilot. Suddenly, something inside it began to rumble, producing thumping and crashing sounds, and the shuttle gracefully flipped over belly-up, executing an elaborate maneuver. That's when she saw something written underneath in thin white letters – they looked like English or French. She tried to make it out. The second word read something like “barr...”

“Barr... Bar-ru...” she whispered. “Bar-ri... Bar-ri-er?” Rina cried out with sudden delight. “Barrier! It says ‘Barrier'!”

Squinting and cursing herself again for forgetting her father's binoculars, she tried to decipher the rest. But aside from “barrier”, there were just two legible words, the first one vaguely resembling “walls”. The cloud kept drifting farther away.

Totally caught up in trying to read the title and not watching her step, Rina walked the length of the deck, trailing the passing shuttle, eyes locked on its underside. Suddenly, she banged her knees against the low railing, slipped on the slick floor, and... tumbled overboard.

It happened so simply, so casually, as if she'd just planned it and sincerely wanted to fall. She didn't grab anything, she didn't hit anything, she didn't even scream. Her ship just flashed before her eyes and shot upward, fast and soundless. The wind began roaring in her ears, her hair whipping across her face. Like a wounded bird, she was falling, lips tightly sealed, arms stretched wide. She stared into the blue abyss below her with a kind of iron calmness, as if this had all been scheduled and planned, as if she had been waiting for the right moment. No fear of impending death, no panic at the growing speed – nothing disturbed her. Except just the wail of the hardening wind.

To an outside observer, it might even look funny. The classic old joke, someone goes out for a promenade, and... And that's it! Just the sky, the wind, and the blindingly white snow of clouds rushing toward her with the velocity of military jets. But the clouds didn't matter. What bothered her the most was the air itself, too icy, too hard, crushing her chest, scraping her cheeks, and freezing her fingers. At that moment, her first thought popped up – just as dumb as everything else around there:

“I left my backpack in the cabin...”

Rina closed her eyes and silently answered herself:

“No need for backpacks anymore... Won't be required any longer...”

And then, right after the sad words, snippets of dozens of different movies began flashing through her memory – scenes of people falling. Famous Hollywood actors were falling... from what? From everything – skyscrapers, rooftops, trees, cliffs, helicopters, planes, horses, spaceships, chairs, and even elephants – you name it! In every clip they screamed, flailed their limbs, shouted something with their eyes wide, full of despair and fear. But she didn't want to shout. Or despair. Or even think.

The fall with its inevitable tragic end didn't scare her. She was wrapped in a kind of stupor, a strange detachment from her own fate. Somehow, she felt it wasn't even her falling from this immeasurable height into the pit of hell. It was someone else. A stranger you didn't have to care about. She was sinking deep into the soul-numbness that philosophers call “the acceptance of destiny”.

And the fall went on. And on. Way too long. Rina had frozen to the core. Her hands were blue, her nose, cheeks, and ears totally numb. She curled up into the tightest ball and shivered uncontrollably. But nothing was changing – neither below, where gravity was mercilessly pulling her, nor above, where the useless sun blazed like mad. The white and grayish clouds filled the space with eerie consistency, rushing past in copy-paste uniformity.

20 – ALMOST 300 DIVIDED BY THREE
part two

Again and again, Rina pierced through cloud after cloud, each time engulfed for a second in milky whiteness, damp air, and a train-station roar – then blasted out into the light and wind again. Several times she fell through rains, where hard pea-like droplets slapped her face cruelly like knotted whips. But the torture lasted only moments, vanishing as instantly as it began. Within seconds, her clothes were dry, the water evaporated without a trace.

Eventually, her indifference settled into something like Zen. An irrational sense of peace. But the excessive calmness gave way to logic – Rina began to think.

She noted that there was something wrong about this fall, something unnatural. And suddenly realized it. Every fall she'd ever seen in movies was about one thing: ground. That was the endgame – ground as the final mark of any fall. But there was no ground here, just clouds, just skies everywhere, half-shielded by steam mounds. And yet, the very fact that gravity existed must mean there was a huge mass somewhere below. At least, that's what they taught in school, and it made sense.

Her memory helpfully dragged out a pile of crumpled images and began arguing with her imagination – was there a ground? Any solid bottom at all? Or did this endlessly deep sky just turn back into ocean?

“Yeah, the ocean would be good”, she thought. “If I fall into water, I won't break”.

But her younger, more unruly thoughts – wild and ill-mannered – quickly sketched a giant island with a wide sandy beach dotted with scattered rocks here and there. Just one second later, the imaginary island transformed itself into Earth, but not the real planet – a global map with continents, oceans, and atmosphere.

“Yes!!!” Rina yelled inside in unexpectedly high volume. “The atmosphere! I've been falling what – fifteen minutes? Or twenty. At what speed? Two hundred kilometers an hour? Or three hundred. That means... that means...”

And to her own surprise, she started math in her head. Like she'd gone four years back to do a third-grade revision test.

“Okay, twenty minutes – an hour divided by three. Speed's three hundred per hour, by three too. I've traveled one hundred kilometers. One hundred? That's insane! The atmosphere is never that thick!”

And just then – she sensed it. A faint slowing. As if she was riding a full-speed train down vertical rails and the conductor had started braking – he spotted the lights of an approaching station. Moment by moment, she could feel it more clearly – the clouds around her slowed significantly. The wind wasn't yanking at her hair as much. And best of all – miraculously – the air became warmer.

Suddenly, the whole world flipped upside down! That's exactly how it looked to Rina, first everything tilted sideways, and a second later the sky was below her. Now she was falling headfirst, straight toward the sun! The new flight felt rather smooth – much slower than before. The speed dropped gradually to the level of a bicycle race. And more importantly – she wasn't falling any longer. Quite the opposite – her descent to hell had morphed into a rise to the heavens.

“Totes! But what happens when I reach the very top? The last foot. Will I stop right there? Or fall again?”

Rina imagined herself trapped in this world forever, ascending and falling like the pendulum in her grandfather's clock. An endless up-and-down.

She shaded her eyes from the sun and kept drifting along, lost in distressing thoughts. And then she saw something new ahead – an unusually dark cloud of abnormal shape, as if it had been lathed on a giant celestial machine. The cloud looked dense, practically solid, and perfectly even. Rina was heading straight toward that ominous monolith. The collision seemed inevitable, and it could be catastrophic. Rina tensed to the limit, pulled her head into her shoulders, and braced herself as best she could, preparing for a terrible impact.

But twenty paces from the hulking monster, the fall unexpectedly stopped. Effortlessly and lightly – like a feather on a breeze – Rina landed on the very edge of the cloud.

The strange mass wasn't as stone-like as she had feared – it felt more similar to dense, wet cotton, soft and yielding. Her fingers plunged through the doughy substance without a hitch. Still shocked, Rina didn't dare to stand upright. Instead, she got on all fours, watching her palms sink some inches deep. Her knees also went into the thick mist, but even so – after that nightmarish flight into the netherworld – this cloud felt somewhat reliable, something she could hold on to, at least temporarily.

“Fine!” she said aloud, trying to sound upbeat, suppressing her emotions. “At any rate, I won't be falling up and down anymore. But now what?”

At that moment someone called her:

“You're still here?”

Rina glanced back – and nearly tumbled off again. A cute blond head was peeking out of a large opening she hadn't noticed before. It was the princess.

“I'm here”, Rina answered automatically, not quite trusting her eyes.

“Excellent! I was delayed a bit”, said the princess, climbing the stairs, “but our council meeting, as they call it, is just wrapping up. As promised, I'd like to introduce you to my inner circle. Come!”

Rina sat down on the floor, straining all her mental reserves to comprehend this impossible situation.

“This can't be happening. This can't be happening”, her mind repeated over and over again. But it was happening, it was real.

She gathered her strength and chose to go along with what had occurred.

“Okay. I'll think it all through later”, she told herself and smiled at the princess.

“Are you feeling okay?” Ahaa asked, approaching with a mixture of concern and disbelief in her eyes. “Why are you on the marble? Are your legs hurt?”

“No, not hurt, thank you. I just... for some reason, I can't walk”.

She tried to stand up but instantly sank ankle-deep into the cloud's surface. It was soft like a plowed field in March. The princess opened her mouth and watched the miracle in great surprise. She herself was standing perfectly well on the smooth, polished tiles, her pearly slippers barely brushing the deck.

“I don't understand...”

“Me neither”, Rina admitted.

Stumbling terribly and plunging her feet into the fog, she somehow made it to the stairs. She looked back – the holes her sneakers left in the glossy slabs quickly closed up, vanishing in a few seconds without a trace.

“What's happened to the deck?” the princess murmured, still not grasping the changes that had unfolded in her guest.

With much difficulty and the princess's help, Rina reached the salon where the high courtiers were gathered. From there, things went more or less as planned – Ahaa introduced Rina to each noble, and each noble was introduced to Rina.

The courtiers smiled their movie-perfect smiles, the kind they learned in early childhood, bowed with precise angles, offered their hands, barely touching Rina's fingers. But they could not help it – every one of them cast suspicious glances at her sneakers, which were now embedded in the cloudy floor four inches deep.
