Ahaa did her best to smooth things over, casually hinting at the peculiarities of foreign footwear. The courtiers didn't believe a single word of it, but nodded politely anyway.

At last, without saying boo or hearing much of anything, Rina made her awfully heavy way to the exit. Everyone understood that the princess had to cut the ceremony as short as possible and was now escorting the invitee back to her cabin.

While they were in the hall, another nuisance occurred. The princess had to say something to one of her secretaries, so she stopped halfway, leaving Rina a little ahead. Just four feet from the exit, Rina turned to glance at Ahaa, took a step, and tripped over a ledge on the floor, crashing headfirst, arms extended, straight into the wall. She punched right through it. As it turned out, there wasn't any hidden door there. Rina had pierced through a wall that was totally impassable to Air People.

“Something's happening to you”, Ahaa said anxiously as they reached the room.

“Yeah, something is happening... I just don't know what”, Rina replied.

“But I do”, the princess said firmly. “You're becoming heavier. And denser”. She paused. “Tell me, there in your world – were you heavy?”

“Heavy? Me?” Rina repeated amazedly. “Not really. Last winter at my checkup, I only weighed thirty-nine kilograms. In boots and underwear”.

“What's that kil er gramz?”

“It's a unit of weight”, Rina answered the school way.

“But what about clouds?”

“Clouds?”

“Yes, clouds!” Ahaa peered into Rina's eyes. “How much does a normal cloud weigh where you're from?”

“Nobody weighs them, really. I don't know. Maybe three hundred or four hundred kilograms, if it's a big one”.

The princess looked up at the ceiling.

“So, you weigh about as much as a small cloud”, she mused. “If you keep getting heavier, you'll fall down through the ship”.

“No!” Rina exclaimed in horror. And thought, “No way! Anything but this!”

Fortunately, the increase in weight stopped quickly. In fact, her weight was even decreasing, although gradually. She was still sinking into the floor, but not as deeply as she had right after landing. However, even with those reassuring signs, walking around had become much harder. And besides, Rina couldn't touch anything properly. For instance, she could no longer use the water taps – they crumpled under her fingers like dough. The bed, now soft as warm butter, got punctured by the slightest touch and didn't bounce at all.

Even her backpack – never having fallen anywhere – had gained so much weight that it went through the shelf and crashed to the floor, continuing to plunge into the hardened mist. Rina had to spread all her belongings evenly across the room to make the backpack lighter.

But despite the hard-to-explain incident with the guest, “the flight proceeded in exact accordance with the ship's itinerary” – the best retro phrase to describe the weird situation. Before long, Rina grew used to her atypical new feature and hardly noticed it anymore – except, of course, for the extra effort she had to put into every step.

Princess Ahaa held a brief discussion about Rina's phenomenon with her closest people. The second advisor shared his theory on the matter, but it didn't sound scientific, so the talk rapidly drifted back to the far more depressing concerns – the heated debates surrounding a wildly controversial law proposed by the Legal Council with the intention of spiting the Parliament and thus seriously undermining the Court...

Only the servants and footmen didn't bother to hide their curiosity. They followed Rina's every move with rapt attention wherever she went. Some of them even started to panic, terrified that the earthling girl might accidentally crush the entire airship. But the senior footman threatened them with immediate dismissal, so the worries and agitation dissipated into thin air that same evening...

Rina liked to watch the clouds. She sat in the chair and looked through the porthole. (The chair was slowly melting under her weight, and the window was no longer glassy – it became sticky, warm, and easily breakable.)

Ahaa had stopped by recently to announce that the expected detour to the island of Ayeon had been canceled by the captain due to some sort of unfavorable winds – Rina didn't quite understand what that meant – but thanks to the corrected flight route, they'd be arriving at the preeminent island of Avaal a full day ahead of schedule. Interestingly enough, the air on Avaal was also said to be healing – and delicious. Ahaa had actually said that word, “delicious”.

“Avaal is real”, the princess explained. “It's not just an ancient cloud like Ayeon, nor an artificial steam assembly. Avaal is eternal and solid. Much harder than our ship. So you'll be able to walk around just fine. If you want to, of course. And besides, I have something unusual to show you there. And by all means we'll play aaoo, if we have time. But the walk – that's the main pleasure there”.

Rina thanked her, and Ahaa left.

“Ha, the walk”, Rina chuckled. “We will go for a walk on the island – the greatest pleasure, no cap”. She sighed. “Well, nothing else to do anyway”.

From the princess's second secretary, who popped in to brief her on the situation, she learned that the large island of Avaal was practically uninhabited, except for twelve settlers – now rather venerable graybeard old-timers – who had once tried to establish a major colony there. But for various political and geographical reasons, no one else had ever joined them.

The settlers lived not too far from the pier, and the secretary suggested visiting the village – as a kind of tourist curiosity. With a sad sigh, Rina vividly pictured a little cluster of feeble, weary old folks perched on a long, cloudy bench.

“Awesome. I'm a foreign tourist”, she thought with a bitter smile, “and the elders are the must-see point of interest of the Sky Kingdom. Disneyland! Wow, what an amazing vacation this is turning out to be!”

But she was wrong. Sky dwellers, even in their extreme age, are never weak or helpless. The fresh air, the youthfulness of the sun, the blueness of the sky, and the absence of bad habits – as well as the absolute impossibility of animal-based food – preserved both their bodies and spirits in clarity and strength. Right up until his last hour, an air elder remains full of energy and vigor. Plus wisdom – which is no small thing. Plus knowledge and experience – exactly what Rina would need to get from them for her trickily-knotted journey...

An hour and a half passed. Restless in her cabin, Rina decided to take a promenade through the airship, hoping to head down to the lower decks where the sailors and servants lived – not for any particular reason, just to amuse herself and maybe gather some new impressions. She was a tourist, after all. But very soon she grew tired of the sticky, clinging floors and returned to her room.

A few minutes later, a funny little pageboy in all-purple garb burst in through the back wall. He was wearing narrow-toed lilac slippers that looked oddly girly, and, with great seriousness, announced to Rina that the island of Avaal was now within sight. Indeed, the cloud-ship was making a leeward approach at a steady pace. But Rina still hadn't seen it, the island lay off the port side.

She sat by the window again, bored as she waited for the landing, completely unaware of the overwhelming, staggering, unimaginable discovery that awaited her just ahead.

21 – ISLAND OF AVAAL
part one

The ship turned its mooring side toward the island. Suddenly, Avaal was there in the porthole – out of the void, as if by magic. The zeppelin hovered about fifty meters above the pier and was now performing a complex maneuver against the wind, somehow guided by means unknown.

From this angle, at least, the island looked like a scoop of vanilla ice cream lightly dusted with crushed nuts. In the middle of the mound rose a sharp spire, shimmering with pure ice and bending slightly to the right, as if it had begun to melt. Rina stared intently but could find nothing to compare the size of this needle with – no rocks, no trees, certainly nothing man-made. Observing the mechanically precise movements of the airship, she realized it would be landing within minutes and decided to get ready to disembark.

What worried her most was the upcoming encounter with the island's natives. Rina didn't quite admit it to herself, but somewhere deep inside her soul, a vague concern began to grow.

A maid burst into the room. Rina flinched, still not used to the servants' annoying habit of showing up unannounced, popping out of nearly any wall, always on the run and whenever they pleased. The maid, a pretty girl of about fifteen with oddly pale-pink skin, wore a very short white dress with absurdly large, rustling bows on either side. She nodded and went to the “refrigerator”. Rina watched her intently.

“I need to prepare your cabin for air replacement”, said the young servant apologetically and breathed onto the “refrigerator” the way little kids do onto frozen fingers after a snowball fight.

The “refrigerator” opened – its front panel simply evaporated, and behind it was a mechanism of two dozen woven ice tubes. The maid reached deep into the contraption and flicked something, then stepped back and waited for the panel to reappear from nowhere. With a short nod, she turned and, her huge bows swishing, left the room through the same wall.

By that time, the ship had already descended, its belly nearly grazing the pier. Rina stood with her forehead pressed against the glass, carefully observing the bustle below. Immediately, the window began to melt. Workers on the dock were finishing the mooring operations. The platform looked like a long gray extension of the mountain and was scattered with large, angular fragments of something unidentifiable. Rina, for some reason, started piecing them together mentally and ended up with a cube.

Then the floor gave an unpleasant shudder, there was a metallic clang, and finally, the airship stopped, bumping its nose dully against a thick, translucent outcrop.

“Her Highness invites you”, a quiet yet insistent voice said.

Rina turned around but saw no one. Either the speaker had already vanished into the wall, or it was the ship's intercom. Not that it mattered – she had expected something like this and was fully ready. Slipping the tightly folded map into her pocket, she stepped out of the cabin.

The servants, unable to tear their eyes from her sneakers, helped her down to the lower perimeter and then onto the gangplank. Rina peered out from the entrance arch: snowy, gentle slopes, a peak soaring dizzyingly high, speckled with faint pink and blue patches.

Twenty steps from the staircase stood Ahaa, giving firm instructions to a deeply bowing footman in dark livery. Spotting Rina, the princess gave a short, cryptic wave and returned to her lecture. Rina waved back and once again couldn't help but notice the dramatic change in Ahaa's appearance and demeanor. This time, Her Highness was dressed unusually plainly, in something like casual loungewear – a light, straight-cut robe with no details or ornaments. Her hair was loose, the wind eagerly tangling it, forcing her to constantly fix her rebellious strands with both hands. As for her conduct, Rina marked an unusual sternness and authority in the princess's face and gestures.

She went down the staircase, pulling her sneakers out of the softened fog-concrete with each step, and carefully touched the surface of the pier with the tip of her shoe. The island proved to be quite solid as promised – slightly springy though, like a thick Persian rug rolled out over hardwood. She approached the princess.

“First, we'll go to the Royal Pavilion”, Ahaa said without preamble.

“Pavilion?”

“Yes. It's not far, just beyond the Blue Cliff”. She pointed to the left. “I want to surprise you with my everlasting statue”. She smiled. “There are lots of statues there, but mine is the happiest – you'll see”.

Without waiting for agreement and completely forgetting the humbly hunched footman, the princess strode off along a smooth, wide path. Rina barely managed to keep up. After a few minutes, they reached a flat, sheer vertical ridge about twenty meters tall. Without breaking stride or looking back, Ahaa walked straight into it, as if entering any ordinary door on the airship.

Rina didn't hesitate either, just closed her eyes for a second, and stepped in after the princess. The cliff, which looked like diamond-hard granite, offered no resistance. But unlike the thin walls of the ship, this one was deep – many meters thick.

She held her breath as if diving into water, just in case. When she opened her eyes, she found herself surrounded by an incredibly dense, cold, wet gray fog. She couldn't hold out and inhaled it – and instantly remembered Odesa and her great-aunt Anya. It was winter, early February, and the entire city had been blanketed in that exact kind of fog people say you can't see your own hand in. Little Rina had stood on the balcony, white, milky air flooding the world from ground to sky. Now, inside the cliff, it was the same, though the mist had a grayish-blue tint.

“Just walk straight ahead!” came Ahaa's muffled voice. “They cleared the way last year, no bumps or ledges. Don't worry, you won't trip – keep going forward”.

Thankfully, the foggy passage didn't last long. Half a minute later, the cliff ended, and sunlight struck her eyes. Ahaa looked around and, pointing her chin toward the peak, said with frustration:

“That mountain gets darker every season. I'm afraid in a hundred years it'll be completely black, and we'll have to move the Pavilion to the island of Agao”.

Rina looked at the dazzling slope and shrugged.

“If today it is considered darkened, then what did it look like before?”

The Royal Pavilion was just beyond the cliff, across a short yard – twenty paces wide. To be honest, despite the grand name, the Pavilion was simply a cave, hastily carved into the mountain's southern shoulder a few centuries ago, but dressed at the entrance with colored cloud-glass for aesthetic purposes.

In the middle of the yard, Ahaa dropped to her knees and covered her eyes with her hands. Rina was startled and stopped five steps away. But soon, the princess removed her palms and burst into laughter.

“He said... he said this is the third time... I've visited him this year. He'll reward me by putting three new freckles! On my nose!” she said, barely getting the words out through her giggles as she rose to her feet.

“Who will?”

“Him, Aaot!” The princess pointed to the left slope.

Rina looked and saw a large statue of an old man in a Roman-style tunic, standing on a distant ledge, hands on hips, smiling down into the valley.

“My great-grandfather”, the princess explained. “He always jokes when I come. And his jokes are... childish. But I don't mind. My father told me he was a cheerful prankster all his life, more interested in carving transparent taaoi from morning fog than in running the government. He never harmed anyone, never even spoke rudely, and helped many people who used to come to him for support. He saved and freed hundreds of prisoners falsely accused in the days of the Big Riot, you know – I'll tell you about that later – and he expelled the corrupt and cruel from every government office, and then improved half the laws in favor of the people.

“The subjects adored him. They built him the largest statue in the Kingdom... And now, I'll show you the eternal sculpture of my father, the Great Aatan. He was immeasurably wise and perceptive, though... people don't always understand their rulers”. Ahaa was about to add something, but thought better of it and silently stepped into the cave.

At first glance, the Pavilion looked like a big storeroom of a grand art museum, a basement filled with never-displayed exhibits. Statues crowded the hall, in every spot and of all sizes – from human scale to massive, distorted giants – standing in complete disarray. Some looked well-maintained and polished, while most were coated in a thick layer of porous, ancient mist. Two darkened sculptures had even toppled to the ground, one so awkwardly that the girls had to crawl over it. The princess was in a hurry and paid the inconvenience no attention whatsoever.

21 – ISLAND OF AVAAL
part two

Soon they stopped before a two-meter-high statue of a young man resting his fingertips on a stone slab. The sculpture was crafted with astonishing skill – exquisite, precise, and inspiring. And if not for the pale, lifeless color of the marble, one might have mistaken it in the cave's dim light for a living person. Rina was struck by the artistic mastery of the sky people and was about to ask who had made it when Ahaa suddenly dropped to her knees again.

But she didn't cover her face this time. On the contrary, breathing heavily, eyes wide open, she stared up at the white figure, whispering fervently in an unfamiliar language. It went on for several minutes. Without rising, Ahaa reached out and lightly touched the pedestal with her fingernail.

Suddenly, the statue turned its head and looked straight at Rina. She screamed in terror and jumped backward. But Ahaa noticed nothing – she gazed pleadingly at the stone face, waiting. The statue began to speak. A deep, low baritone filled the vast hall, echoing expressively off the walls.

The statue said:

“...and all attempts to return our kingdom to the path of growing the height of the blue deep-layer clouds turned out to be a waste of time and energy. For many years, our ancestors pursued a fruitless and irrational goal, which has left us not only with a noticeable reduction in the actual area of the Lower Layers but also, inevitably under such a policy, with a slowdown in development of new bi-normal cloud technologies. This kind of inexcusable delay...”

The statue continued speaking – eloquently, convincingly – but Rina had no idea what it all was about. The stone face shifted expressions, becoming at times thoughtful, stern, then inviting, then mocking. It was alive! The stone lived with a programmed life, recorded in it. Ahaa had risen long ago, now she stood, unblinking, staring into the statue's eyes – and crying.

After a while, the marble speech came to an end. The sculpture turned its head, lowered its arms, and returned to its original pose.

The princess took out a handkerchief, dabbed at her tears, and said:

“I've heard it a thousand times, and I still bawl like a little kid. No one in the whole world ever had such a loving father. And I loved him. And Mama loved him too”.

Ahaa drew in a full breath and exhaled slowly.

“My poor father made too many enemies in his lifetime. Anyone doing great, honest work always has enemies. And if that work bears beautiful, flawless fruit, then everyone around becomes a jealous rival”.

She looked at other statues. They stood in silence, their lips pressed.

“Let's go on”, she said, snapping out of her thoughts. “Just past that ledge is the philosopher Aael. We won't activate him – I just want to stay near him for a while”.

Aael turned out to be a stooped, stocky old man with tightly shut eyelids. Rina asked why his eyes were closed. The princess answered, gently stroking the stone sage on the shoulder:

“Aael was a genius. He used to say the only truly reliable support is emptiness, and that even the mightiest cloud is no bigger than a child's palm. I didn't understand those strange words for a long time, but now I think I get it. He was my mother's teacher. When I turned five, I got my own tutor – a younger one – but Aael remained at the palace, and I often listened to his poetry. Yes, he wrote poems. He was considered a powerful master of rhyme – his verses were beautiful. Though I preferred his sayings. He never wrote them down, he just gave them freely to anyone who needed them.

“But one day he went silent and closed his eyes. The last thing he ever said was, ‘Let them not look at me, for I do not look at them'. You know, I think that was his best line. A few days earlier, he said, ‘Do not seek white patches on black clouds, for those who cannot dispel the darkness will find nothing'. Since then, I've tried to live that way – to dispel the darkness. And when I fail, I don't make excuses for myself”.

The princess fell silent. Rina studied the statue, rendered so vividly and skillfully it seemed the old man might raise his hand and touch you at any moment. The uncanny thought made her uneasy and cold.

“My eternal statue is over there!” Ahaa shouted. “By that wall!” And, quite excited, she dashed ahead. “But we won't activate it either. I say such silly things there, it's better not to hear it. When I get the Great Crown, I'll redo everything in here”.

The statue of the princess was astonishingly beautiful, marble Ahaa smiled, big eyes full of life and wide open, lips slightly parted as if she was just about to fire off a playful quip in response to a funny joke.

“Do you like it?” the princess asked.

“I love it! It looks alive!”

“Yes, alive”, Ahaa agreed, “but they made me too doll-like. Look how much taller I am!” She stood shoulder to shoulder with her stone self. “Am I taller?”

“Yes, a little”.

“That's why I'll redo her too – she has to be improved. But not right away, some other time. And I'll change the words”.

About ten minutes later, after wandering through the Royal Pavilion a bit more, Rina and Ahaa headed for the exit.

“On Earth, we put sculptures in parks and plazas, in open places with lots of people”, Rina said.

“That's not too wise”, Ahaa replied. “People might deface and damage a statue of someone they don't like. Or even smash it”.

“That's true. Sometimes they do”.

“See? But here in my Pavilion, the entire collection is perfectly safe”.

“But no one guards them”.

“Guard them?!” The princess was stunned. “Why guard something sealed inside a mountain? Only members of the royal family can pass through the Blue Wall. And their guests and friends, of course”.

A bit later, after passing back through the cliff and walking a quarter-mile along the snow-white path, they reached a small rise.

“The settlers live over there”. Ahaa pointed toward the base of the mountain.

Rina saw a row of round houses, the same thick milky-white color as everything else, looking very much like Inuit snow huts. The road sloped gently downward. Good weather – no wind, the sun was beaming, and the walking was easy. Her sneakers didn't sink at all into the cloud-firm surface.

As they talked, Rina and the princess gradually approached the first house. Unlike the Inuit cabins, it had no visible entrance – just a smooth white dome sitting on a polished marble surface. There was no indication of which side to approach, where to knock, or how the owner might emerge. But Ahaa made no move. She stood silently beside it, waiting.

Less than a minute later, the wall shimmered, darkened – and out stepped... Rina's mother. And right behind her – Aunt Vera, all in white.

Some impossible, supernatural force helped Rina stay on her feet and not collapse onto the stone floor. She didn't faint, didn't scream or run. Shocked and bewildered, she stared into the beloved faces, unable to even blink. It felt like her heart had stopped for a few seconds. Perhaps what really saved her was the constant expectation of miracles, and the unshakable awareness that this wasn't Earth – no, around her was an entirely different and in many ways irrational world.

Ahaa didn't notice Rina's frightened eyes and saw nothing strange in the settlers' appearance.

“Aa ae oe iaa”, she said shortly, and the two jolly women began talking over each other, addressing the princess in their tuneful language without a single consonant.

“This is terrible!” Rina thought in despair upon hearing their voices – undeniably her mother's and aunt's voices.

Everything was like a painful delirium filled with illness and injections, when strangers in your dream wear familiar faces, and your loved ones are unrecognizable, when everything is absurd and illogical – you speak your native language, but no one understands, and you're unable to make out a word from your own mother. Be that as it may, seeing your Mom living on a cloud island in the heart of the Fourth World – that's too much. One needs nerves of steel to endure such a shock.

Finally, after learning everything she needed from the settlers, Ahaa turned and said:

“No news on the island, I'm satisfied. Now we'll visit Avvan. It's become a ritual, I've always stopped to see him since the year Aael left us”.

Relieved, Rina hurried after the princess, trying not to think, not to look back at the two women – they both stood smiling happily and bowing as they saw off the noble guests. But after she walked for a few dozen steps in silence, Rina couldn't help herself and asked:

“Your Highness, is it possible for people in different worlds to look exactly the same? I mean, like identical twins?”

“Which people?” Ahaa, deep in her thoughts, hadn't quite heard the question.

“Those ones!” said Rina, pointing back. “Those women. They look just like my mother and my aunt. Even their voices are the same”.

Ahaa stopped, looked at Rina, deeply astonished – and suddenly burst out in ringing, childish, uncontrollable laughter.

“Don't tell... don't tell me my servants... they have been so indolent they didn't explain you anything about the law of resemblance? Lazybones! But it's so simple”.

Rina stood there, just blinking in response. The princess shook her head.

“Don't worry, I'll explain everything as we walk. Come on”.

And they walked on, while along the way Ahaa revealed to her guest the most wondrous law of the Cloud World: anyone with a strong will and patience could transform surrounding objects into what he wanted to see.

For instance, if one looked at a chunk of hardened mist and needed something round, say, a model of the Sun, then very soon the mist would turn into a sphere by itself, and it would even start to glow and become warm. Rina remembered the washbasin that emerged mysteriously from nowhere.

“Exactly! That was the Law of Resemblance in action!” confirmed Ahaa.

“But the sink didn't just resemble sinks from Earth – it had water, the faucets worked like real ones”, Rina said doubtfully.

“So what? The Law of Resemblance is powerful. If your Earth sink is supposed to have water, then our copy of that sink will absolutely have water too”.

Rina thought for a moment and asked:

“And those women – are you saying they weren't real? They appeared from the hardened mist, like the washbasin?”

“Oh no! With living beings, it's not that simple. Mist can only become mist, in its different shapes – we just alter its features. But life, no, life is much more complex. People can only take on the forms of others for a few minutes, and even then only if there's someone nearby who's inclined to see them like that. I think that when the women appeared in front of us, for the first moment they were just themselves – two ordinary elderly ladies – but your deep, constant desire to see your mother and aunt was so strong that their faces changed instantly”.

“And their voices?”

“Their voices too, of course – according to the Law of Resemblance”.

“But now that we've left, have they gone back to their real look?”

“Naturally!” Ahaa smiled. “Why would they stay looking like someone else? They could have changed back earlier, but they didn't – out of politeness, so as not to offend us, their visitors”.

“But that... I mean... this changing faces like that... does it hurt?” Rina asked, suddenly alarmed that her intense feelings might have caused discomfort or even pain to the kind strangers.

“Don't worry, it's almost unnoticeable”.

“And your face too? Can it... be changed?” she asked, totally horrified.

“Mine?!” Ahaa exclaimed cheerfully. “Sure it can! Certainly it can! But who would dare touch the face of the princess? Unless they're making a copy of me – that's allowed. If I permit them. You saw my copy – my eternal statue in the Pavilion”.

“So all those statues were made the same way – using the Law of Resemblance?”

“Well, sure! How else? Or perhaps you know of other methods for making exact copies of people?”

Rina didn't answer – she just shrugged. Clearly, she did know such methods, at least two of them: a talented sculptor could recreate a person's appearance... or, much simpler, you could print a 3D copy instead.

But she didn't go into details. Rina smiled and remained silent, she figured that was enough as a reply. The princess seemed perfectly satisfied with this reaction, evidently deciding that while Rina was an intelligent and reasonably educated girl, she understood little of naturalistic art – let alone the subtle craft of shaping stone replicas and marble doubles...

22 – AVVAN, MAP, and TABLE
part one

Meanwhile, laughing and chitchatting, Rina and Ahaa approached the house where, according to the princess, the famous elder Avvan had been living for three hundred and forty years. He was once the official historian of the royal family, though now he preferred to call himself “the obsolete philosopher”.

As before, the princess stopped a couple of yards from the wall and waited. Rina stood beside her. Less than a minute later, the white panel dimmed, trembled – and out stepped a smiling, pleasant-looking young man of about twenty-five. He gave a standard greeting and bowed deeply to the space between the two girls.

The princess turned and said:

“He speaks Old Yulret too”.

“Hello”. Rina bowed in return, briefly puzzled by the “elder's” youthful appearance.

“Ohh!” he exclaimed. “How delightful it is to speak once more in my beloved antique language! There are only two of us ‘classical Yulretians' on the whole island – me and Adaon. The others learned the second Yulret, you know. But we are classics! Though I speak it better, because Adaon learned from books, and I had real practice – I visited Earth in my youth!”

“Avvan, don't talk nonsense”, the princess chided gently. “You've never been anywhere, you've lived on the island nearly your entire life”.

“Exactly, Your Highness – nearly, nearly!” The old man practically bounced with excitement. “And before that ‘nearly', where did I live?”

“I don't know”, said Ahaa. “But my records manager does. I'll ask him after our walk”.

“Your Highness always displays the greatest wisdom. However, if it pleases you, I can easily prove what I say. I saw an Earth dog!” Avvan announced with ceremony.

“A dog?”

“Yes, Your Highness! A real dog. Spotted!”

The princess turned briskly to Rina and winked mischievously – as if to say, “He's making it up”.

“Spotted?”

“Unbelievably spotted, Your Highness!” He jumped up joyfully. “Spots all over and all the way to the very tail!”

“Tail! Spots!” The princess laughed, and it took her some effort to calm down.

Nevertheless, after Rina questioned him thoroughly, it turned out that Avvan really had seen a dog, and not only seen it, but even touched it, patted it on the head. Judging by his colorful description, it was a Dalmatian being walked by a dark-skinned maid in a chestnut grove – apparently in a park on the outskirts of some old, possibly French or Swiss town.

“Very strange”. Ahaa looked rather unsettled. “No one ever reported your traveling to other worlds”.

“Ohh!” the elder said wistfully. “That's because I visited neighboring worlds a very long time ago, Your Highness, when your esteemed father was still a little boy”.

“Perhaps, perhaps...” muttered the princess, not entirely pleased, thinking of the second secretary whose obvious oversight it was.

“Excuse me”, Rina addressed Avvan. “But if you traveled to our Earth, does that mean you know the exact exit points from this world and the way to return?”

“No, my lady, I don't know the exit points. You see, it's hard to explain, but in my case, I joined the travelers more or less by accident – by a silly mistake, if you like”. He smiled sheepishly. “It all happened on the spur of the moment, in a rush and haze of emotions. The so-called ‘entry point' slipped completely past my attention, as I was rather preoccupied. Same with the exit ones. You see, there was a woman there – a lady, very attractive – and I was young, impulsive, uncontrollable, you understand? So I can't say anything about the point itself. But the journey to Earth – the transition process, if you will – I remember that part very well, it stuck in my head in every detail. Not just the trip to your beautiful planet, but also the path to the Desert World. True, there was not a drop of water in the desert, only blistering sand, and I nearly died on the very first day, because I was alone and, you know...”

“And you met Aunissa!” Rina interrupted eagerly.

“Aunissa? I don't remember. Who's that?”

“The Queen of Black Ants”.

“Ants?” The elder – or rather, the young man – frowned, trying hard to summon faded images from the distant past. “No, sorry, I don't recall any ants. But there was the giant bird Gherdeleon, I remember him like he was here yesterday. And two monitor lizards, the Lonovil brothers. Very strange ones, I must say. They talked a lot, but you couldn't make sense of it. And the great Oluvila”.

“Oluvila!” Rina exclaimed and clapped her hands. “The Purple Tortoise!”

“Exactly! The tortoise! Completely purple!” the young man – the elder, of course – confirmed, still riding the same wave of joy.

“And from there you went to the Ocean World!”

“Not quite, my lady”, Avvan corrected. “From the desert, I returned home – right here. To this place!”

Rina paused, thinking.

“Then I guess in ancient times, the worlds were connected in a different way”, she concluded logically, as she always did.

“Ancient times?!” the elder repeated, slightly offended. “What do you mean, ancient times? Not ancient – my youth!”

“Of course, of course”, Ahaa quickly soothed him. “In your youth. Our guest Rina means time flows differently – on Earth, time shifts at another rate, doesn't it?”

“Yes, that's right”, Rina picked up the thread. “A completely different rate”.

The elder was happy to drop his offense and instantly returned to being a bright, cheerful young man.

“Have you ever flown to the World of Fire?” Rina asked suddenly.

“World of Fire?!” the elder and princess echoed in unison.

“No, never had the chance”, the young man answered. “And I wouldn't advise you to visit that land, my lady. Our scientists say there's no way back from the World of Fire, not a single exit, just an entrance”.

Rina sighed.

“Maybe you're right. But I have to go there”.

“Why?!” Ahaa asked in shock. “Are tourists obliged to travel through dangerous places?”

“I'm not a tourist”, Rina admitted. “I am obliged”.

“Not a tourist?!” the princess and the young man repeated in chorus again.

In short, Rina told them everything. Well, actually an “edited” version – because the full account of the past week's innumerable events would've taken a whole day. She also showed them Herx's self-updating map, which she'd studied in her cabin, but now, the paper pictured nothing except white, gray, and blue smears and stripes, without a single label on them. Even the familiar little skirted figure was gone. At the same time, however, a bold black dot had appeared close to the upper right corner. Rina understood that such a clear mark likely meant something very important – maybe even the entrance to the next world or something like that – but where it was, how far, and by what means she could reach it, those questions had no answers.

The elder and the princess were astounded by her incredible story. Ahaa was visibly shaken. But even more to them was the map itself, which struck them as both a brilliant masterpiece of alien craft and, at the same time, as plain, simple, and obvious as the foreheads on their faces.

“That's Aarun”, Ahaa said, pointing to a blue stripe in the center.

“And this one is definitely Aoot. I lived there for two years with friends when we got our first job”, said Avvan, tracing a grayish blot in the lower corner with his finger.

“Then what's this here? I can't figure it out”, the princess said, touching a small white smudge shaped like a butterfly.

“Your Highness!” The elder spread his hands. “That's your family island – Aaenn!”

“You think so?”

“I'm sure! That's it! The Summer Palace is right at this spot, see? In the southern part, just below the sharp ridge...”

Rina looked at the childishly excited Avvan and the happily smiling princess and could hardly believe her luck. That morning, she hadn't even hoped to use Herx's map in the Cloud World. What good was the paper covered in meaningless streaks? But now, these blots and blurry shapes turned out to be well-known locations to the air dwellers – and not just known, but even dear to them!

“This dot here, what does it mean?” she asked, pointing to the small black circle.

“Dot? What dot?” The elder squinted, leaning so close that his nose nearly touched the map. “Probably some cartographic symbol, I suppose”.

“No”. Ahaa also leaned in to look closer. “It's not a symbol. I think... I think... Yes! It's an azimuth angle – a direction marker for the midday sun! I've seen it before – not on a map, but still – I'm sure. It's a solar azimuth!”

“But pardon me, Your Highness”. The young man smiled indulgently. “Do directions really get marked with dots? Airographers always use arrows. Usually”.

“You're right”, Ahaa agreed, pretending to concede, her eyes gleaming with childish naughtiness. “On aerial maps, they draw arrows. But on Earth maps – they use dots!”

“My map isn't from Earth”, Rina objected cautiously. “It's Herx's map, and he doesn't live on Earth”.

“And where does he live?”

“I don't know. Herx never said”.

“Then it follows that the bold dot is a direction to the sun!” insisted the princess. “An azimuth! You see it yourself”.

Arguing with princesses – even cloud-born ones – is a thankless and largely pointless endeavor. In the end, both the elder and Rina gave in and accepted Ahaa's version – that the mysterious black mark could indeed signify the direction to the midday sun, whose position is critically important on all navigational maps of the World of Air. Still, this didn't solve the main problem, the entrances to the Fifth World remained undiscovered.

Then Ahaa suggested:

“You know what? No one can sort out these academic subtleties better than my advisor Aien. Today, I'll release him from his court duties a little earlier and order him to assist us. Of course, no guarantees, to this day, Aien is considered the most knowledgeable historian and astronomer. And entrances to other worlds – that's both a historical and likely astronomical matter. If even Aien can't manage it, then as soon as we reach the Second Palace, I'll summon the chief librarian Annad. He's a man of science – he reads a full cabinet of books a year and knows more than all my advisors combined. At any rate, his books list everything found in the nearby worlds and, for sure, the entrances to the next World are in there too”.

22 – AVVAN, MAP, and TABLE
part two

With that, Rina's dilemma was sort of postponed. Soon afterward, Ahaa announced she wanted to walk to the game field, where visitors traditionally trained in aaoo. The elder – still maintaining the image of a sprightly young man – politely declined to accompany Her Highness, citing his age and hopelessly aching legs. So Ahaa bid him a warm, friendly farewell, promising to visit him again at the first opportunity.

“Well, now I won't be able to sleep for six days!” the princess suddenly proclaimed as they stepped onto a wide, slightly fog-dusted road.

“Why?!” asked Rina, astounded.

“Because I'll be thinking about your sister and worrying. And when I worry, I don't sleep for six days straight”.

“Please don't worry, Your Highness!” Rina said heartily. “I will find her, and I'll come back home with her. Everything will be fine. The wizard Herx is helping me, he's the most experienced and powerful magasther in all the worlds. And many others are helping me too. Including you”.

“No, what help can I offer? We're just flying in a long arc across the Kingdom, and we might be moving farther from your entrance to the next world with every hour”.

“Maybe”, Rina sighed. “That's okay. Then I'll fly back”.

“Better yet, we need to speak with my scientific advisor immediately”. Ahaa said decisively, pivoting almost military style on her heels and turning down another path, heading right toward the pier through the empty lot.

Rina followed. But they hadn't walked even a hundred steps when a breathless boy in a junior servant's uniform came running out from behind. Without properly stopping, he bowed and shouted:

“Ao ee o! Ao ee o!”

“Ie ae oua?” asked the princess.

The boy nodded rapidly and blurted out, “Ae u oi! Ai a ia o aui!”

“Oh, perfect!” Ahaa exclaimed with delight. “The chef announced that the Jubilee Feast can be held right after the Time of Short Shadows. Wonderful! The sooner we disturb Aien, the better for all of us. You can't imagine how important and slow-moving he is. If it weren't for my Mother's patronage, I'd have banished him from the Palace long ago for his lack of respect toward the Princess”.

The boy bowed again and dashed off without waiting for a reply. Apparently, Ahaa had given him some kind of hand gesture, though Rina didn't catch it.

In fact, hand gestures in this world were a common practice and served the population as a fast and convenient second language. What was most astonishing – this hand-talk was known on other planets too, and the dialect was almost the same everywhere. Ahaa was happy to say that those hand signs were invented by the Alzeans, a prehistoric tribe from the only island on the Ocean Planet. They called their land the ‘World of Morroddo'.

Rina immediately recognized the word from her conversations with Gri-Gri. Ahaa wasn't surprised and said that in old times the Desert Planet had to use hand language too when dealing with foreigners. Rina, as an Earthling, didn't know a single gesture – she just spoke all the local languages easily, often without an accent, as if automatically (yes, that silly word again), but even that didn't amaze the princess in the slightest.

“It's because”, she said confidently, “you were sent on the journey by the wizard. I think he gave you all the languages in advance”.

Rina agreed – that electric strike to her forehead from Herx's finger was unforgettable.

And so, drifting a bit into the topic of linguistics, the girls walked along the ‘shore', right at the edge of the cloud island.

Rina found the princess interesting to listen to, but the sheer drop and the slow-crawling gray clouds far below, which she glanced at now and then with growing horror, didn't exactly inspire comfort. Ahaa noticed her frightened looks and turned onto a parallel path, slightly higher up. Reaching the top of a small rise, she turned and said:

“Such a shame, but we won't have time to play aaoo today. The practice field is over there, where the two pink hills are”.

Rina looked back, but saw no pink hills. Or rather, quite the opposite, there were more than enough hills scattered across the valley. Though they all looked exactly the same. She searched for a pair that looked even slightly different from the rest – but found nothing.

“Tomorrow morning, we won't stop anywhere – we'll go straight to the field and finally play. Do you agree?”

“Of course I do!” Rina smiled. “I love sports”.

Ahaa didn't know what ‘sports' were, but just in case, she clarified:

“It's not a complicated game. The rules are simple, although it takes focus and quick reflexes. I'm sure you'll do well. And then – a little practice, luck, and agility – and you are the best player in the kingdom”. The princess winked.

Chatting casually, they reached the airship. On the parade stairs, the footmen and servants – already changed into festive violet attire – were lined up in two rows. Rina remembered that Ahaa had mentioned some celebration and asked:

“You said ‘jubilee dinner'. Is it someone's birthday today?”

The princess raised her eyebrows:

“Oh no, not at all! Today is the eighteenth day since the beginning of the movement of the lowest clouds, which officially makes it the First Day of Summer. And eighteen days from now, summer will end”.

“That's strange. Our summer lasts a whole ninety days. Or ninety-two, I think, if I'm not mistaken”.

“Nothing surprising”, the princess reasoned after a moment's thought. “The Earth World is many times larger than ours, and so your time is both wider and longer. But a holiday is a holiday. A change of season means a change in winds, and a new wind brings a turn of fate. At least, among the people, the First Day of Summer is considered the third most important holiday – after New Year's and Coronation Day, of course... At the table, you'll sit next to me on the left. Sorry, but the right seat is permanently occupied by the State Secretary, according to tradition”.

Rina sincerely thanked her for the invitation...

The table was set on the Second Open Deck – the very one from which Rina had recently managed to fall, and to which she had somehow miraculously returned. There she expected to see flowers, cakes, flags, an orchestra, ribbons, garlands, champagne, and smiling ladies in gowns – everything typical of a lavish celebration, especially a royal one.

But reality was much simpler – so much simpler, it was almost insulting, not only were there no decorations or orchestras – believe it or not, there weren't even dishes! No utensils, no napkins, no glasses. Just a long, bare table of black glass, and on it, as the only “dish”, four or five dozen large, crudely torn chunks of pink and white mist, piled carelessly into stepped pyramids. And that was it!

However, in anticipation of the grand feast, Rina had been feeling a dull ache in her stomach, hunger had reached an unbearable level, and now, honestly, she was ready to swallow just about anything – even clouds. As she and the princess climbed the staircases and crossed the walkways, she had imagined the most luxurious royal delicacies – though she understood that in another world, the dishes would be unfamiliar, but surely delicious, because after all, they were royal. And yet instead of treats, hors d'oeuvres, drinks, and appetizers – an essentially empty table.

For a while, she still hoped the cook and his helpers would appear with soup tureens and hot plates on trays, but then Ahaa nodded, and the courtiers, standing respectfully nearby, made their way to their chairs. Without waiting for everyone to take their seats in accordance with protocol, the princess briskly approached a small portable throne. Four servants, in perfect coordination, swept it back. Rina hurried to the table too, her sneakers merging into the floor as though the marble was melting slush.

Ahaa sat down, looked carefully at everyone gathered, then stood up abruptly and said in a deliberately authoritative, commanding tone:

“Aea a oea! O iao i ue a aia!”

The courtiers smiled and shook their heads in denial. Ahaa, smiling too, slowly sank back into her throne and reached for the nearest pink morsels twitching slightly in the breeze. She scooped up a full handful of pinkish mist, stuffed it in her mouth, and swallowed it all in one gulp. Rina hesitated for a moment, unsure of the local dining etiquette, and watched the princess's actions intently.

“Please! Take from my aia”. Ahaa nodded toward the pink heap.

Rina tore off a bit. To the touch, the edible mist resembled sponge cake, or more precisely – wet plastic foam. She brought it subtly to her nose, sniffed – no smell. She bit into it, chewed. The taste was bread-like, similar to plain, simple wheat rolls, though somehow too artless – not sweet, not salty. Bland undercooked wet sticky cold stale bread, basically. But her unrestrained appetite took over, Rina tore off a hefty portion the size of a large pastry and shoved it into her mouth whole. The mist instantly melted like cotton candy – but watery and without any sweetness.

From there, things went better – one chunk after another. The courtiers wasted no time either – exchanging cheerful remarks consisting mostly of “a” and “o”, pushing pink slices into their mouths with evident delight, barely chewing the porous mass. Ahaa got into it, too, grabbing and cramming damp blandness with both her hands.

After about eight or ten minutes, rather sated with the pink, Rina decided to try a little of the white pyramid, hoping it might be slightly tastier. She reached over, pinched off a corner. It was significantly firmer than the pink. Interesting! But just then, she noticed the courtiers had stopped chewing and swallowing. They sat frozen, mouths full, as in a comic photograph, staring at Rina with unblinking eyes, as if seeing her for the first time in their lives.

She was terrified she'd unknowingly committed some gross and offensive blunder. Eyes wide with panic, she looked to the princess.

“It's all right”, Ahaa reassured her. “You're allowed. You're a guest and a foreigner”.

“I just...” Rina had wanted to say she hadn't known about the white mist, and that... but the princess stopped her with a gesture.

“White aia is for the Second Suite of Courtiers. Pink is for the First”, she explained. “On the other hand, we're all among friends here. These are my best people – entirely equal”. Here she raised her voice so the far end of the table could hear too. “Of course, we can't violate the kingdom's regulations or our ancient customs. And so, even my closest friends, if they belong to the Second Suite, would never touch the pink aia. That's the law. And every order must rest on firm law. Our ancestors! They created the Rules of the High Ones! And it is not for us to change them!”

These last three lines Ahaa delivered even more emphatically, like slogans on a poster, but the courtiers fully agreed and responded with overly enthusiastic exclamations.

“A princess's guest”, Ahaa continued, “at the royal table is treated as First Suite. But a guest always has extended privileges, including the right to the white aia. I think we can consider the incident closed”.

Silence fell. Rina still didn't know whether she was supposed to finish the unfortunate piece of white mist or not. Or maybe she should just drop it to the floor? But what if discarding food – of any color – was strictly prohibited, even for guests? So, to defuse the tension, Rina popped the poor bit into her mouth and quickly ate it. Astonishingly, the white mist had a top-tier flavor – something like a luxury ice cream with natural strawberry syrup.

Both suites of courtiers let out a collective sigh and resumed their feast as if nothing had happened. Ahaa smiled at everyone at the table, then leaned toward Rina and, shielding her mouth with her hand, whispered conspiratorially:

“So how is the white? Is it good? I've never tried”.

“It's good”, Rina admitted honestly.

“A pity”, replied the princess almost soundlessly.

At last, the pink and white mist pieces were completely gone, and without waiting for permission or saying a word, the courtiers began dispersing. Soon only three people remained at the table, Ahaa, Rina, and the hopelessly silent State Secretary. Rina's stomach was full, she felt properly fed, even slightly overstuffed – despite the suspicious airiness and transparency of the meal.

“All right, now we're going to talk to Aien”, the princess announced, rising energetically from the table.

The State Secretary flinched all the way down his spine. He pulled his face into a ridiculous servile smile and bowed low, not even trying to stand, but almost bumping his chin on the black tabletop as he gazed at the princess with adoring eyes. The princess didn't look at him – she headed straight for the staircase. Rina said “excuse me” to the secretary and hurried after her, immediately noticing that her sneakers didn't sink into the deck anymore, it felt like walking on a thin carpet, with hardly any effort.

“I'm light again!” she thought joyfully. “Now I understand why air people are so weightless – they eat clouds”.

The girls crossed the hall and headed to the lower decks. The princess hurried down a narrow curved staircase to a blue mirrored landing near the exit, swiftly took off her left shoe, and suddenly hurled it downward over the railing.

“I'm angry at him”, she said. “At the advisor. I'm going to limp so he remembers. He'll be ashamed, but he deserves it!”

Indeed, the princess's left leg, now missing its high-heeled shoe, became much shorter – Ahaa hobbled terribly, swaying rather dangerously to one side with each step. Limping like this, she went into the doorway hidden behind a thick gray curtain beside a big greenish window.

23 – AIEN
part one

Aien's cabin was situated on the auxiliary deck at the very front of the ship. Originally, it was a technical room cluttered with suspicious black pillars and various mechanisms, but Aien persuaded the crew to swap the equipment for his belongings in his former cabin. This simple maneuver effectively separated him from the rest of the court staff.

However, his new “quarters” remained strictly utilitarian, and consequently, there was no proper access to the room. To get there, one had to climb a flimsy spiral staircase, which unexpectedly transitioned into a thick wire tube with sharp protrusions. How the heavy, corpulent Aien managed to scramble up there several times a day remained a mystery to the entire retinue.

Under the formal rules of ship etiquette, the princess could not inquire directly about her advisor's new eccentric dwelling and dangerous staircase. Personal matters weren't addressed bluntly, she merely hinted playfully, but Aien pretended not to understand the hints.

He was indeed stout, bulky, and seemingly ungainly. Through his sluggish movements and heavy clumsiness, he deliberately conveyed the impression of slow thinking and a burdensome mind. In reality, that was a meticulously trained ruse. Despite his size, Aien was extraordinarily quick both mentally and physically. The contrast between his tortoise-like demeanor and his lightning-fast strikes – delivered at any moment from any position – along with his dazzling remarks and aphorisms, precisely timed and intoned, served him well.

His phenomenal quick thinking, intellectual boldness, and mental flexibility propelled him from a neglected, completely useless secondary office first to the Royal Chancellery, and from there straight to the Court Academy, where he studied for less than five weeks, but that was enough to ignite deadly wars among everyone there and charm the College patrons – a dozen aging ladies – who unanimously recommended him to the Palace.

In such a cunning way, Aien entered the close circle of the future queen. Once inside the Palace with legitimate rights, he effortlessly outmaneuvered weak and lazy competitors. To him, they were merely tools and toys. He swiftly ascended the hierarchical ladder, replacing one functionary after another. Eventually, through years of intrigue, forgery, betrayal, and behind-the-scenes battles, he reached the administration of the infant Princess, firmly securing his position as her Senior Advisor – the role he had long aspired to.

Remarkably, the rest of the courtiers accepted his self-appointment as natural and evidently reasonable. None had a clue that Aien was actually manipulating the entire retinue as he pleased. He was a true architect of the unseen and a master of illusions within the Palace.

The only thing he hadn't managed to master was the princess's unyielding character. Though she was not yet of age and occasionally allowed herself to become a naive child for a minute or two, the strong, ambitious, merciless blood of her royal father was increasingly and inexorably overpowering the fragile genes of the Aenons – semi-legendary knights and near-mythic heroes of the ancient Aen lineage. The young Princess Ahaa was irresistibly transforming into a stern, unapproachable, often ruthless and formidable ruler.

Nevertheless, Aien was not the least bit troubled by this. Even if he couldn't control the princess herself, holding her entire flock in his cold, rigid grip brought him immense satisfaction. As for the foreigner who had recently appeared on board, he simply didn't think about her, well aware that Ahaa was incapable of forming genuine friendships and would lose interest and forget her guest soon enough.

What is more, Aien planned to ensure that Rina stayed in the capital, eventually blending into the city, settling among the townsfolk, and never meeting the princess in the future.

After miraculously climbing the wildly swaying, impossibly twisted staircase, Rina and Ahaa found themselves before a large oval opening in the wall, resembling a submarine hatch, yet adorned with a semi-transparent, sparkling curtain.

“Aea-e!” the princess commanded loudly, and without waiting for a response, passed through the icy fabric.

Aien's cabin was surprisingly cramped and modestly furnished. Its main feature was the table occupying nearly a third of the room. On the table, piles of miscellaneous oddities were jumbled carelessly, mostly ‘objects of obscure purpose', as an inexperienced investigator might note in a crime scene report. Aien stood by the window, respectfully bowing his head upon his mistress's arrival and smiling subtly.

“Aieu ou ai?” he asked without raising his gaze.

“No”, the princess replied, “we'll speak in Old Yulret, the language of my guest”. Aien bowed slightly lower, still adhering to ship protocol.

“This concerns...” Ahaa continued.

“I know everything, Your Highness”, he gently interrupted with a perfect smile. “I learned of the aim, purpose, and objective of your visit from the Administrator and have prepared some useful and, I believe, valuable information regarding your esteemed guest's issue”.

“Good”, Ahaa replied coldly, sitting down with a sharp motion. “If so, I'm listening”.

Aien detached himself from the wall and approached the table.

“I have been informed, Your Highness, that your highly respectable royal guest is traveling to distant worlds on personal matters and is currently seeking entry into the next world, the Fifth One, counting from the beginning of her journey, with the primary goal being the World of Fire”.

Rina stood three steps away, feeling both intrigued and awkward, as neither Aien nor the princess had looked at her once, speaking of her as if she weren't there at all. This manner was part of palace standards, but it was still an unpleasant sensation.

“I am unaware of the reasons that prompted the esteemed Ms. Rina” – like most, he stressed the second syllable in her name – “to undertake this journey”, Aien continued. “It is not my habit to meddle in others' affairs, however, I must note that visiting the World of Fire, even briefly, can be a highly dangerous endeavor, potentially leading to unpredictable consequences for Your Highness's personal guest”.

“We are informed of all dangers”, the princess replied in a frosty tone. “My guest is free to make any decisions she deems fit”.

“Oh, undoubtedly, Your Highness!” Aien exclaimed with a bow. “I merely considered it my professional duty to remind Your Highness of the treachery and cruelty of the World of Fire, as well as all other worlds beyond our beautiful, radiant, and unique Air-Cloud World – the best of the best and foremost of all possible worlds. I also believed that it was my direct responsibility to mention the risks and threats that abound in the spaces beyond our Kingdom, so wisely governed by our dearly revered Great Queen”.

“Aien!” Ahaa said irritably. “Please get to the point, we don't have much time”.

“With your permission, as your Advisor of Sciences and Affairs, I always speak directly to the matter, Your Highness”. Aien made an annoyingly obsequious, saccharine face and, twisting his neck, bowed even lower, as much as his immense belly allowed. “At least, I do everything in my power to faithfully serve the Great Queen, Your Highness, and the entire Kingdom”.

Saying this, he reached with his left hand to one of the piles of miscellaneous items on the table and began deftly rearranging boxes and bundles.

“For me, the Princess's desire is an unalterable and inviolable law, Your Highness”.

“Let's not touch upon laws, please”, Ahaa retorted sharply. “We intend to discuss geography, not politics”.

“Of course, of course”, Aien muttered and suddenly exclaimed, “Ah, here it is!”

“What is there?” Rina and the princess asked simultaneously.

“The Aenel Lens!”

“Why do we need a lens?” Rina asked.

Only then did Aien look at her for the first time, and even then, only sideways. Rina thought he had no eyes at all, just two deep, round holes beneath his eyebrows.

“I have been informed”, Aien said in a lowered tone, “that the Esteemed Guest possesses a certain magical map that changes as the journey progresses. Is my information accurate?” He glanced at Rina again and, after a pause, bowed slightly.

“Yes, I have Herx's map”, she confirmed. “But it doesn't show anything, only clouds and islands”.

“Would the Gracious Lady allow me to look at the marvelous map?”

“Of course”. Rina pulled a copper button, unfastened the pocket, and took out the folded paper. Aien took the map, barely touching it with his fingertips, carefully unfolded it and said:

“Everything is exactly as I anticipated. This map is significantly incomplete because... because... because...” He brought it close to his face. “Because...”

“Because what?” Ahaa couldn't hold back.

“Because at the beginning of summer, the shapes of the islands, cloud formations, and wind directions are insufficiently stable and cannot yet be precisely determined”, Aien rattled off like a memorized lesson.

Rina stepped half a pace closer and hesitantly said, “There... in the corner... a black dot. Could you explain what it means?”

“A dot?” Aien was surprised. “I don't see it”. He bowed and presented the unfolded map to the princess.

“You probably covered it with your finger. Accidentally”.

“Where?” Aien removed his hand from the map and showed it to the princess again, then turned to Rina, but there was no dot on the paper.

“But how...” Rina began.

“That's normal”, the Advisor smiled sourly. “I've already had the honor of reminding you that in the early days of summer, forms are unstable, and therefore dots and any other objects and symbols can appear, disappear, or change as they please. If we were to look at your remarkable map in seventeen or eighteen days, then...”

“Then the entrance to the Fifth World would be visible?”

“You see, your map has too small a scale. Entrances and exits are unlikely to be discernible under such conditions. Unless, of course, they are specifically marked. But as far as I've noticed, the entrance to any subsequent world is not yet indicated here”.

“So what?” the princess asked. “You can't help us?”

“I didn't say that, Your Highness. I merely had the audacity to state that on the existing map, entrances are not evident to the naked eye, but that doesn't mean they can't be detected by other means”.

“Other means?” Rina was surprised and thought with a grin, “X-rays, perhaps?”

“Exactly!” Aien exclaimed. “Other means! For example, this Aenel Lens. With this device, we can change the scale of your map and examine all the small features and nuances. With your permission, of course”. He bowed to the princess.

“If there's no threat to the ship”, she replied dryly.

“Have I ever posed even the slightest danger to Your Highness's property?”

Ahaa didn't answer. Instead, she demonstratively stuck out her bare left foot. Aien was taken aback for a moment, coughed, and sidled back toward the window. But after standing there for a few seconds, he again said, “With your permission”, and, with difficulty bending down, laid Herx's map directly on the floor. Then he took a tiny snow-white handkerchief from his vest pocket and carefully wiped the lens with it. Exhaling, he bent down again and placed it in the very center of the map.

At that moment, a crackling sound was heard. It was like dry twigs being broken by a strong hand. And there was the smell of hot iron. Rina tensed, Ahaa sat with an impassive face, and the Senior Advisor smiled happily, showing small gray teeth. The crackling intensified. Rina felt that the map lying on the floor was radiating streams of heat like a blazing campfire. The cabin trembled slightly, and a second later, the map began to grow! Its corners and edges came to life, stirred, flapped against the marble, and stretched in different directions. The map rustled, clicked, expanded, and gradually covered the entire floor with a mottled paper carpet.

The lower edge of the map reached Rina's feet, she was about to jump aside but didn't have time – the cardboard slid under her sneakers, and Rina found herself on a large, spreading blue blot. Ahaa, along with her chair, was now on a wide white stripe, probably indicating one of the air currents.

But the map continued to grow and soon, pressing against the cabin walls, impatiently pushed them. The walls yielded to the force without visible resistance and moved apart in different directions, increasing in length and height simultaneously. The princess raised her hand, intending to object, but the Advisor merely nodded and made a calming gesture with his palm. Ahaa shook her head discontentedly, though she remained silent.

Very soon, the walls and ceiling had moved so far away that the small cabin had turned into something resembling a hockey stadium. Echoes rolled through the immense hall, but the stripes on the floor kept spreading with the same persistence. Fortunately, the main law of the Universe is implacable – everything has its end. Two minutes later, the blue, gray, and whitish spots, which had been moving in waves under Rina's feet, stopped and settled.

“Magnification complete!” Aien announced ceremoniously, like a circus ringmaster. “If you allow me, I will show you some details, Your Highness”.

“Are you sure our ship is all right?” the princess asked calmly, rising from her chair.

“Absolutely, Your Highness. What happened here pertains exclusively to my modest, if I may say so, quarters. Meanwhile, the rest of the ship remains untouched”.

They walked across the map, which now covered thousands of square meters and no longer resembled a map, except for the scattered inscriptions, signs, and symbols here and there, which in total formed an uncountable quantity.

“You may notice”, Aien continued enthusiastically, addressing only Rina, “your map has many visible details and elements, provided the scale was properly adjusted”.

“Yeah”, she agreed, “but after you have zoomed it in like this, I see only those details, without knowing where they actually are”.

“Why without knowing? Here”, Aien pointed to the left with a school pointer that had appeared in his hand from nowhere. “Here is the city of Aail, it cannot be confused with any other – it's round and empty inside just like Her Highness's ring on her pinky. And over there”, he pointed to the right, “a resort island known as Old Asaa. Do you recognize the island of Asaa, Your Highness?”

The princess had stopped at the image of the island. Looking closely at the picture, she suddenly squealed and leaped back, as if a mouse had jumped out of the map.

“Everything is moving there!” Ahaa shouted in fright.

“Please, calm down, Your Highness”. Aien showed his little teeth in his rather satisfied smile. “The dimensions of this map have increased so much that you managed to see the movement of the clouds”.

“No, not the clouds! There are ships!”

“Of course, ships. Summer has begun, vacation season is underway, and many of Her Majesty's subjects have flown off to resorts. The island of Asaa is a charming place to relax – you can't deny that”.

“But it's just paper!” the princess exclaimed, glancing around.

“Yes, paper, though please allow me to remind you, it's magical paper. Forgive me, but you seem to have forgotten for a minute that we are standing right on the surface of a map belonging to a certain Herx, who, according to your esteemed guest, is a famous and powerful wizard”.

“But I stepped on them! Accidentally!” Ahaa added in horror.

“It's not dangerous, Your Highness. You happened to step on the drawing of the ships, while the actual cruise liners are flying far away from here. Their passengers have no idea what an honor it was – for a moment they were beneath the exquisite shoe of the Magnificent Princess of Air”.

“Ah!” Ahaa said indignantly. “You always find a way to scare me. Clearly, you enjoy it immensely”.

“Not at all, Your Highness”, Aien replied with a bow. “I merely carry out your supreme will”. He bowed again, this time significantly lower than protocol required.

Rina looked at him and thought that the Advisor bowed so often not out of respect for the princess, but to hide his gloating smiles. Surprisingly, she was right, the Senior Advisor was having an unexpected and quite significant celebration – he had managed to shock the princess. Twice!

23 – AIEN
part two

Everyone was walking across the map. Aien recited the names of islands, currents, cloud fields, and deep air hollows. He frequently stopped, bent down, poked at the paper with his pointer – he behaved like a seasoned tour guide, as if he had seen Rina's map two hundred times before. Occasionally, the paper beneath their feet trembled, like a living, yet deeply sleeping beast. Or just a kind, flat, peaceful animal.

“Like a white flounder”, Rina thought.

Finally, after strolling for quite a while, Ahaa asked:

“Advisor, do you still have no idea where exactly we should look for the entrance to the Fifth World?”

“No, my Gracious Lady”, Aien bowed ceremoniously. “But in my opinion, this entrance should be depicted somewhere along the edge”.

“Then why are we trudging around the center now?”

“Because I examined the central part of the map, Your Highness, to confirm the validity and soundness of my latest assumption”. The Advisor bowed even lower.

The princess waved her hand hopelessly, turned away, and said to Rina, who was carefully studying a small dark gray spot:

“Let's go to the far end. Aien believes the entrance will be at the perimeter”.

“Look!” Rina said in amazement. “That's our ship!”

“Our ship?!” repeated Ahaa, rather bewildered, and then, limping desperately, hurried over to her, nearly slipping on the slick paper puddle of the air lake Aalolo in the province of Aenta.

“There it is!” Rina pointed. “Here! This is our ship! It's flying!”

Indeed, a sharply outlined gray object in the shape of an elongated drop was moving forward, although barely noticeably.

“Quite possible”, the Advisor grumbled, approaching from behind. “At the very least, it's a vessel of the same type, B-eight. Though actually I'm not certain”.

“But I am!” the princess snapped. “Here on the port side are two black lines. That's the upper part of the royal Air Forces Sign”.

“That may be. But it's equally possible that it's the symbol of the Southern Community's merchant fleet”.

“Aien!” Ahaa addressed him with irritated reproach. “You're the only one among all the High Ones who dares to contradict me at every turn, but sometimes you blatantly abuse my habit of tolerating your impudent antics! I see the royal sign on this ship with my own eyes! What is there to argue about?”

“Even in a nightmare I wouldn't even think of arguing with Your Highness!” the Advisor bowed quite low but spoke loudly and confidently. “I'm ready to agree with any of your words without hesitation, but allow me to remind you of my role as Senior Scientific Advisor. According to the Palace Schedule, point twenty, paragraph two, the Senior Advisor is meant to doubt and, through doubts and questions, lead discussions to the truth. The Schedule doesn't mention any limits on my doubts. This means I have the right to question even Your wisest assumptions, Your Highness. That is my official position and my state duty”.

“Fine”. The princess sighed wearily. “Doubt everything if it pleases you. But nowhere in the Schedule does it say that you must impose” – she emphasized the word – “impose your doubts on me. Keep them to yourself until I order you to express them. Does that, I hope, align with your foolish schedule?”

“Yes, my Gracious Lady, it does”.

“Then let's stop this pointless bickering and finally go to the edge of the map”.

But they didn't reach the edge. Aien lingered over a small blue symbol and suddenly announced:

“I've found it. This is the entrance”.

Rina's heart skipped a beat. With her feet becoming heavy, she approached the Advisor, afraid to even look at the paper.

“Here it is, your long-sought, precious entrance”, Aien said, without any pomp. “And interestingly, you see, it lies along our course, just... um... about four degrees south”.

Ahaa came over, barefoot, holding her right shoe in hand. The Advisor skillfully suppressed his mocking grin.

“Show me!” she demanded, frowning at Aien.

He silently took out his pointer and indicated the symbol. Rina saw that in the center of a dark, irregular polygon – most likely representing a mountainous island – there was a small golden circle, neatly outlined with a thin red line. Around the rim, two dozen black letters were scattered in complete disorder, by their forms vaguely resembling Hindi, with little sticks on each top.

“What does it say?” Ahaa asked.

“Nothing special, Your Highness”, the Advisor replied. “As far as I am able to translate, it says, ‘This door was created by… by the slave, I suppose, slave Naon… in the three hundred and first year… by the will of… yes, will of Nnov the White'“.

“Who was Nnov the White?”

“I can't recall precisely, my Gracious Lady, but if necessary, I can find him in the Great Encyclopedia of Worlds. Evidently, the door is ancient, and the names pointed out here are historical”.

“Is this the language of the Fifth World?”

“Yes, Your Highness”.

“But why are the words written in such a jumble?”

“Not just the words, but all the letters too, Your Highness. In those times, the Fifth World didn't use linear writing, they wrote in triangles”.

“In triangles?” Rina asked in surprise.

“Yes, my lady, precisely in triangles”, he turned to her and now spoke as if solely to Rina. “The first letter in a sentence is the apex of the triangle. Then, you rotate this apex to the left by a quarter of the side's length, keeping in mind that each time, the triangle grows by the same amount with each full rotation, so the letters and symbols are arranged along a broken spiral, stretched to the left”.

“It's impossible to read this way”, declared the princess.

“But that's exactly how they wrote in that World, Your Highness”.

“And the island, is it far from us?” Rina asked.

“Probably yes, it's still quite a distance”, the Advisor replied thoughtfully. “First, we have to fly past the twin islands of Aala-Lao, then we'll spend four days in the capital. From there, we'll head to the Great Blue Wastes, and only then – towards the final destination of our journey, to the North – to the Aam Archipelago. The islands, if I'm not mistaken, are over there”. He gestured with his hand. “Provided Her Highness wishes it, the ship can deviate slightly from its course, and a day after the Wastes, we'll have the opportunity to dock at this exact spot. It's not labeled on the map, and unfortunately, I don't know its name – this wonderful island is too small”.

“If the island has no name, I'll call it Aoa-U-Aau. The Island of the Yellow Door”, proclaimed the princess.

“That would be an excellent decision, Your Highness”.

Standing over the professionally drawn island, Ahaa said to Rina:

“That's it, in just seven or eight days, you'll be here and leave me forever. All I'll have left is to remember you. It's strange, but I've never had true companions. Somehow, I feel that if you had lived with us for a year, we would have become real friends”.

“But I can't stay for a year, I need to...” Rina began.

“I know”, Ahaa interrupted. “Of course, you can't stay, you have no right to linger – not for a year, not even a day. They're waiting for you and counting on you... If only someone waited for me like that and counted on me”. At that, Aien was about to object, but Ahaa turned sharply to him, and he shut his mouth instantly. “I would also, without hesitation, fly to other worlds, I would do everything to help those who need me so much. But no one needs me”.

“You are needed by your nation!” the Advisor exclaimed, unable to contain himself.

“Silence!” the princess hissed through her teeth. “I don't see what nation you're talking about. Where have you actually met that nation? In the Palace?” She turned back to Rina. “Your path is difficult and dangerous, but you ask for nothing. At first, I thought you were cunning, thought you were slick and calculating. But later I realized I was wrong. Everyone who approaches me asks for something, even such rich, fat nobles”. She nodded towards the Advisor, who immediately bowed. “They have everything – they're well-born, wealthy, many of them are famous – but they keep asking endlessly, they want more! And you have nothing. You're alone, you're in a complicated situation. Yet you haven't asked me for anything”.

The princess paused.

“I know how much you need my help. I'll give you my officers!” The Advisor reached out to the princess, but she sharply raised her hand, and he froze with his mouth open. “I'll give you ten of my best officers”, Ahaa continued, “they're educated, brave, and will be ready to die for you at any moment. Please don't say anything, you can thank me later... I don't want you to perish. I will never see you again, but that's not the point. It's more important for me to know that you're okay”.

The princess fell silent, Rina and the Advisor didn't dare break the stillness. Ahaa thought for a moment and added in a strong, commanding tone:

“Promise me one thing – when you have only one officer left, don't send him to certain death. Tell him to go home. He'll make it, my officers are strong, hardy, and smart. If you have only one last officer left, it means things are very bad, and you'll most likely fail. Being alone, he won't be able to save you anyway, but I need to know everything. Let him return and tell me what happened there.

“If you lose, I'll order a monument to be created on the Island of the Golden Door – the largest and most beautiful statue in the entire Kingdom. If you survive and win, I'll record you in the Honorary Sun Book as the only friend and close associate of Ahaa, the Great Princess of Air, and you will live in people's memory for centuries”.

Rina looked the princess in the eye, a tight lump rose in her throat, but she held on with all her might.

“We still have seven days”, Rina finally said with difficulty. “We'll be true friends for a whole week”.

“Yes, a whole week...” Ahaa echoed and suddenly said loudly and imperiously, “Aien, enough! Let's get out of here! I'm tired of wandering through this paper state, I want back to the real world.”

“At your command, Your Highness!” the Advisor exclaimed and, with a curt nod, headed back to the center of the map with an unexpectedly quick, strong, wide stride. Now he walked without pretending to be slow, he marched precisely and evenly, like a lieutenant on a parade ground. Approaching the table, he abruptly bent down and grabbed the magnifier.
