He stood, twirling the cold glass in his fingers, looking at the girls hurrying towards him. “What nonsense all this is”, he said to himself and shoved the lens into his pocket.

At that very instant, a crackling hiss was heard – like a large drop of water falling into hot oil. The map began the process of self-compression. The walls obediently followed it. Rina and the princess saw giant marble planes moving towards them and ran to the Advisor at full speed. After some time, everything ended – the map returned to its original scale, as did the Advisor's cabin. He picked up the paper sheet from the floor, carefully folded it, and handed it to Rina with a bow. She said “Thank you” and slipped the map into her pocket, snapping the button.

“The magic is over!” Ahaa announced, like at that circus after an illusionist's act. “Let's return to our mundane affairs. You must attend the Evening Review”.

“Certainly, my Gracious Lady”. The Advisor bowed.

“And don't forget to prepare the response to Her Majesty's Second Secretary”.

“The response is already prepared, my Gracious Lady”.

“Good”. Ahaa smiled at Rina. “Now we know everything, don't we? I mean... you understand”.

“Yes, now we know everything, I really appreciate your help”, Rina thanked her.

“Oh, high heavens! Don't say it that way. There's no reason to thank me, I had nothing to do with it”, Ahaa replied. “Thank Mr. Aien instead”.

“Oh no, no, no, not at all!” The Advisor shook his head like a parrot. “No need for the slightest gratitude! I experienced true pleasure and immense satisfaction assisting the esteemed Lady, the Personal Guest of Your Highness. You can count on my modest capabilities, I am always at your service anytime, anywhere, under any circumstances, and for any conceivable requirement you might encounter –” etcetera, etcetera...

Finally, the girls managed to get rid of Aien and leave him. They descended his risky staircase, crossed the passage, then climbed to the Second Deck, and entered the White Hall. They walked silently all the way, reflecting on everything that had just happened.

“What do you think of him?” Ahaa asked when they stopped in the Hall. “Quite an unpleasant type, right?”

“I don't know”, Rina shrugged. “Probably yes, a bit unpleasant, but he helped a lot”.

“That's true, he did, he helped us. He helped you – that is what is shocking me. I don't know what to make of that. Aien suddenly started helping someone who wasn't part of the elite. And mind you, he could have just said he had no idea how to solve the problem, but he helped and did it completely selflessly, with no profit at all. Is it even possible?” Ahaa pondered for a second. “Nonetheless, my advice – be careful with him”.

“I'll try”. Rina nodded.

“When you've rested, come to the First Upper Deck. I'll have a fitting there, they're sewing me a High Dark Dress for the Autumn Changes Festival. Will you come?”

“Of course, I'll come!” Rina smiled and added, “Your Highness”.

“No, don't”. The princess shook her head. “When we're alone, call me by name. To you I'm Ahaa, deal?”

“Deal”.

The princess turned and went toward the staircase, her bare feet slapping against the gleaming, arctic-white marble. Rina watched her go.

“How sad it is”, she thought and headed to her room.

24 – THE CITY OF AO
part one

Rina hadn't slept well, even though no one had disturbed her – simply because Herx appeared – or rather, not so much appeared as sounded.

“How are things?” he asked lightly, as if merely passing by.

She was resting in thick April grass, she looked around, but saw no one – only bees and butterflies. In this dream, she wasn't at the beach like before. Instead, she had spent half the morning strolling aimlessly through the large clearing – a meadow or perhaps a hayfield. Everywhere she looked – young, lush grass with tiny fragrant flowers.

And a grasshopper. Nearby. The tireless little violinist was chirring persistently, hopping from blade to blade, teasing her and defiantly clicking its wings. Rina searched for him for some reason, and at last she found him. She stood up carefully, took a small step forward, but the wise insect was watching her closely and jumped far ahead. She didn't even know why she wanted him – she just felt like gently placing him in her palm and looking at him, nothing more. But Herx's voice ruined all her plans.

“Where are you?” she asked, thinking with slight annoyance, “Well, that's that – no grasshoppers now”.

“Doesn't matter where – I'm not too far”, Herx replied.

“Not too far? Are you somewhere here? You're invisible?”

“No, not exactly, but still the distance is not astronomical yet. Anyway, I can hear you, and that's already good. And I've noticed you hear me pretty well too, so we can say we've got something like a phone connection. Without a phone”.

“I was waiting for you”, Rina said after a short pause, with a touch of reproach. “But you hadn't come”.

“Can't be helped. The interaction of the Near Worlds is complicated now. Especially after certain events. It's not always easy to break into your dreams through all that noise, believe me”.

“I believe. But I need advice. I think I've found the entrance to the Fifth World. But I'm kind of unsure”.

“It's great that you're unsure. You are exactly in the situation that worries me the most!” exclaimed the unseen Herx. “In fact, you're in danger”.

“In danger? What do you mean?”

“I mean, Aien is deceiving you”.

“But why?” Rina was shocked.

“I haven't quite understood yet. He has, you see, too many contradictory plans. But it's quite possible the advisor simply wants to get rid of you. You're in his way somehow. I told you about him before, warned you, I said – never trust Aien. Don't you remember?”

“No”, she admitted, “I don't”.

“Ah... yiv... mo...” Herx muttered.

“What did you say?”

“It's not for you – I'm scolding myself. My fault! I didn't bother to boost your nighttime memory, was too lazy to fiddle with the dezminator, and here's the result – you forgot my extremely important messages!”

“But nothing's happened yet”, Rina tried to calm him. “I haven't even reached that Island, the Island of the Golden Door. And besides...”

“And besides, forget it if you value your own safety”. Herx cut her off. “I'm sure there is no such island there”.

“What do you mean, no island?”

“That's what I mean! Aien is a type who plans everything – he's a trickster. And if he helps you, that means he has included you in one of his knotty schemes. Maybe he acts against the princess this way, that's normal, the Palace people are all like that, but it's possible he wants to use you as a trap or, worse, as a weapon. Looks like you're right in the middle of his game, that is the danger. Anyway, I think the island wasn't real”.

Rina's mouth dropped open.

“But how can that be? I walked on that map,” she said in confusion, “and saw the island with my own eyes. And the princess was there near me too, we saw it together. How's that even possible?”

“It's Aien, you don't know him”, Herx replied calmly. “He can do anything to get closer to his goal. And nothing magical about it – the fake island could be drawn on the map by one of his assistants. That's simple”.

“No, the map was always in my pocket”. Rina shook her head.

“Always? And what about sleeping? It takes just five minutes for all. I'm totally sure they did it last night”.

Rina said nothing in reply, she was scared, though mostly frustrated.

“Well, don't be upset. Since we know the truth, there is nothing to worry about, he is powerless against the truth, he's now a puppet in our hands”, the old man giggled. “And by the way, I solved the problem long ago. I know where the real entrance to the Next World is located – it's in the Palace”.

“The Queen's Palace?”

“Precisely! Well, not in the palace itself – under it. You need to get into the catacombs, if that's the right word for something in the cloud. There you'll meet Aoao”.

“Aoao! I remember! That's the man Aunissa spoke about”.

“Yes, probably. What did she say?”

“Just the usual. Aunissa asked me to say hello and thank him on behalf of the ants for his help, that's all, in general”.

“Well then, thank him and say hello. I suppose he'll be happy to get a greeting from an old friend, especially from another planet... Anyway, he knows the path to one of the nearest entrances. There are three such mechanisms in the kingdom. He once told me how he worked on restoring an ancient tunnel and found a hidden door there. And just behind the door was an underground road, and a big hall with the entrance inside... In short, he knows the ins and outs and will help you – he promised. That's enough for us. And I hope you remember my second important warning”.

Rina strained her memory, but no matter how hard she tried, she couldn't recall anything significant. She shook her head.

“Amazing!” growled the wizard. “Then tell me, why do you think I struggle to appear to you in your dreams and tell you all these things?”

Rina sighed guiltily.

“Well, relax”, Herx softened his tone after a pause. “Anyway, I'll have to remind you. I warned you about the flower”.

“Ae! Ae the Flower!” Rina even gave a little hop.

“Thank heavens”, said Herx with relief. “Of course it is Ae! A very useful but awfully wild creature. It's not alive though – it was made from a special material, but it acts and looks completely real. If, of course, you don't count its size. The greatest risk is in how it reacts to touch. It's capable of generating powerful electrical discharges, sometimes fatal”.

“Yes, I remember – I mustn't touch the flower”.

“Please do try to convince yourself not to touch it”, Herx asked playfully. “Don't run to hug it. Better yet, don't even get close. Just listen to what Aoao says and do everything he advises”.

Rina fell silent in thought.

“Something's wrong?” Herx asked after waiting.

“Probably”, Rina confirmed.

“What is it?”

“The basement. I mean, the palace underground. Still, how am I supposed to get there? I don't know what the Queen will say, how she'll receive me, or where I will and won't be allowed to go”.

“Don't trouble yourself with that. The Queen loves new people – she collects and stores them in her personal albums”, he chuckled. “Especially foreigners. At least for the first weeks, you'll be allowed to go anywhere and do anything you want. I guarantee it – she'll even ask you, quite seriously, to sit on her throne”.

“Her throne? What for?”

“For fun. The Queen of Air is a fun person. You people would call her ‘extravagant'. Hilarious word, huh? She's strange, in short. Many think she's a kind woman – but don't forget, she's the queen of a large kingdom, she controls the government, the army, and the police – everything! That means you should keep as far from her as you can, you understand? My people say she's exceptionally insecure, they consider her an unpredictable beast... Okay, soon you'll see her yourself and figure her out. But be diplomatic when you meet her, don't widen your eyes too much – no need to offend Her Majesty like that”, Herx laughed. “And what's more important: don't listen to her too closely, try not to fall for her tricks”.

“Tricks? You mean, the Queen deceives people too? Like Aien?” Rina asked, deeply surprised.

Hard to believe, but she still naively assumed that real, not fairy-tale kings and queens had no reason to trick. Why would they? Kings have fame, power, and are fabulously rich. What would be the point of lying? Ministers, advisors, and courtiers – yes, they lied all the time, it was their job – to manipulate everyone. But they are a different matter – they are under orders...

“Her deceptions are complex”, Herx explained. “Not as flat and primitive as Aien's fake drawings. A queen is a queen, and her pranks are royal quality. But never mind – just look her in the eye, bow, smile when required, stay calm, and be utterly polite. And never agree to anything right away! First think and analyze. Before you answer any of her questions, pause and listen to your mind. Do you understand what I'm saying?”

“Yes, I do. I'll try to analyze”.

“Wonderful! Now about the underground tunnels. Do this: when you get to the Ice Fountain – they'll definitely take you there to show off, they take everyone there, every single guest despite their habit to spin fairy tales about how only the specially chosen are allowed near the fountain according to some secret list. Ridiculous! Everyone's allowed! ... So, once you see the fountain, pretend you're interested in the blue light flowing out of it, ask them to be left alone for a minute. Got it? And when everyone leaves, shout ‘wueot'. Remember that. ‘Wu-e-ot'! An ancient phrase in Emmei language. It's forgotten now – a powerful tongue, I would say, but you need to know the rules to use it the right way. Well, ‘wueot' means ‘I'm here', although not exactly, but that doesn't matter. Repeat it now”.

Rina repeated.

“Okay, you'll shout ‘wueot' just once and stand still. Don't go anywhere, don't do anything. And within minutes, all will happen as it should. Remember, nothing threatens you there. Even in the worst-case scenario, there won't be a prison. The Palace uses different methods – humane ones, so to speak. Besides, they're too slow. The moment the courtiers figure things out, talk it over, and work up the courage to go looking for you in the tunnels, you'll already be forty miles away. Aoao will take care of everything, just don't argue with him – he doesn't like that. Am I explaining clearly?”

“Everything's clear”, Rina smiled.

“Very good, I'm glad... And did you notice? You and I are speaking old Yulret, and I haven't stumbled over a single word – not the slightest mistake! Neither have you. Just listen to how naturally we're speaking now! It's my favorite language, you know. Once you're back on Earth and start using your native language again, you'll realize how much more convenient Yulret was... All right, I'm getting off track. What were we talking about?”

“The tunnels”.

“Oh, it's all clear with the tunnels, it's going to be easy. Aoao will explain the details, you do what he says, and you are safe. Are there any other questions?”

“Sir, no, sir!” Rina laughed.

“Well, terrific then, as they say nowadays! I mean, they did in the fifties. Then I'll go. That is, I'll float off. Goodbye”.

“Goodbye”, Rina replied, and added, “Thank you very much!”

But Herx didn't hear her anymore. The brief session of phoneless interworld communication had ended…

She wandered the fragrant meadow all day long, until sunset – musing, pondering, whispering to herself, arguing with someone in her imagination, even singing, and still looking for the grasshopper. But the green fellow kept silent, it was impossible to find him.

Many times Rina tried to get back to her cabin – in vain, the meadow did not let her go. Rina was pretty upset in the end and the moment the sun touched the dark horizon, she woke up irritated, tired, and rather frustrated. How long had she slept actually? A day, an hour, a minute? And was it already morning, or still evening? She had no idea. The only thing she understood – she was exhausted. As if she hadn't gone to bed at all.

“Next time I'll ask Herx to appear in some other way. And without locking me in the dream”, she said to the glowing spheres. They blinked and stopped rotating as if confirming she was right...

Soon Rina heard a strange commotion outside the walls: knocks, rustling, hurried footsteps, someone's exasperated voice issuing long commands in the consonant-less language. Rina ran to the window and saw a massive cloud island approaching. Taking up half the sky, it was floating toward the ship. In less than five minutes, the island occupied the entire window and was so close that Rina could see dozens of blue-uniform people along the edge, probably port workers. Some of them were waving striped purple rods, and others had small white flashlights on their chests.

Long shadows, watercolor-tinted in rainbow hues, stretched across the giant square, the zeppelin was slowly going down. The people ran through the shifting layers of not yet dissolved night fog and tore it into thick strips with their bodies. It was a chill early morning – ten minutes before dawn. Rina, in her sleepy thoughtfulness, watched her giant ship turned slightly left, then slightly right, preparing to perform a gentle landing right on the square.

“A gentle clouding”, she corrected herself, and thought cheerfully, “If this is the capital, then right now Ahaa is busy changing her scarf, dress, shoes, and instructions, she won't come to get me – she'll send the servants, and twenty of them will help me put on my knapsack”.

She hadn't even finished this long, but still comical idea – which lifted her dark mood just a little – when a breathless boy of about eleven suddenly burst into the cabin right through the back wall. Struggling to catch his breath, as if after a wild half-mile dash, and mercilessly mispronouncing the alien words, he said:

“Rrin... sessa... wai... tin... fo... u... up... stair... z...”

“Where exactly upstairs?” Rina tried to clarify.

“Ae o ieo?” the boy opened his huge eyes even wider in deep astonishment.

“Wher...er...up...stair...raz?” she repeated slowly, syllable by syllable, but the servant only smiled in reply and backed away fox-like until he vanished through the wall.

“Great! Thank you for more problems”, Rina said to the disappeared boy with much annoyance. And yes, she had every right to be upset – the ship had two Third Decks, three Upper Decks at the opposite ends, and two Open Decks up the frame.

But there was no choice – she would have to go find the princess.

In the White Hall, everything had changed beyond recognition: the panoramic window had dissolved into thin air, and in its place stood a dark gray wall. The entire Hall was dimly lit by a bluish glow coming from a long slit in the ceiling – no lamps, no globes, everything was disassembled. Busy people were rushing about, passing with worried faces, dragging boxes of all shapes and never noticing anyone else.

Rina headed toward the grand White Staircase, but it was gone completely. Now there was a scary drop with sheer walls. She cautiously looked down and saw five or six sailors arranging flat black tiles at the very bottom.

“And how am I supposed to get through now?”

“Madam may exit through the right corridor”, a silky voice advised from behind – a tall white-haired man in the lilac livery of the Royal Servants.

“Thank you”, she said, and walked back across the Hall.

Luckily, the corridor hadn't transformed into anything yet – it still bent right and sloped upward, just as it did before. To Rina's surprise, she remained light – her sneakers sank into the marble no deeper than two or three millimeters, and as always, the footprints closed up, fading out almost immediately. Same with the walls – just for fun, she scratched her name with her finger, but the letters smoothed over and evaporated in a few moments. Only the letter “a” lingered a little longer than the rest.

“No wonder”. Rina smirked. “That's the one they put the stress on!”

Climbing up to the middle levels, she entered the Second Deck, where she suddenly found herself in total silence and eerie emptiness. The always-noisy, crowded Second Deck – used for walks, meetings, conversations, games, and merry activities – was now deserted, dark, and even seemed smaller. Rina crossed the echoing hollowness and entered a niche that looked like an open three-walled elevator. In fact, it was a special “waiting door”, as Ahaa childishly called it. If you stepped into such an opening and waited for about ten seconds, all three walls would dissipate on their own and reveal the entrances to other corridors. Rina went along the right-hand passageway, leading to the First Upper Deck – to the stern of the ship.

24 – THE CITY OF AO
part two

And she was lucky: entering the pre-deck chamber, she immediately spotted the princess, dressed entirely in ultramarine – very formal and strict – from a distance she looked just like a manager at a large bank back on Earth. Ahaa was standing with her back to Rina, fiddling with a golden curtain on the porthole and discussing something with a handsome young man in long dark clothes that resembled a judge's robe.

The man nodded constantly, had a serious expression, and listened to the princess attentively. Rina, afraid of interrupting an important conversation, tiptoed a bit closer. But the man noticed her, apparently guessed who she was, and gave a formal bow. Ahaa turned, a trace of surprise passing over her face.

“Oh, you're already here. Good thing you found me on this deck. I was just about to leave for the office and we would've missed each other. Meet my old friend, Professor Alion. Be careful – he's a subtle psychologist. Say three words and he'll learn every secret in your heart”.

“Your Highness, as always, is kindly overestimating my abilities”, said the professor jokingly, with a bow. “Three words are not enough – I need four. Professor Aeas Alion, at your service”. He raised his hand and greeted Rina the way Earth boys do when they spot each other from afar. Rina waved back in the same friendly manner.

“Pleased to meet you, I'm Rina”, she presented herself with a smile.

“I'll be even more pleased if you agree to visit our university”, said Alion, making a complicated gesture with both hands – Rina didn't understand it. “I've heard from Her Serene Highness” – he made a long nod to the Princess – “that on Earth, you hold a completed seventh degree of magistracy and now you are in the eighth. Believe me, it would be a great honor for us to welcome such a versatile and extensively advanced person from a non-adjacent world. I will introduce you to our education system, and show you our university, which I believe to be the best institution of its kind”.

Rina was surprised by the lofty academic degree attributed to her, but diplomatically didn't let it show. The day before, the princess had been asking about certain unofficial aspects of life on Earth, and obviously had misunderstood something about the schooling and teaching in Kyiv.

“I'm afraid I have to disappoint you, Professor, but we have plans”, Ahaa interrupted. “Our guest will first visit the Pink Palace – there's a meeting scheduled with the Queen – then I'd like to show her... uh... a few exclusive sights, which I think will take about two days, and only after that will you be able to steal her away from me and take her to your famous university – if, of course, she agrees”.

“I agree, I do – I'd be glad to! I'll definitely go with you”, Rina readily confirmed.

The professor bowed even more gracefully, but soon, citing an extra-critical – his word – obligation, went down a side staircase. The princess silently headed in the opposite direction.

“Thanks to the summer tailwinds, we're way ahead of the flight schedule”, she said as Rina caught up to her. “It's still only the fourth hour of dawn by island time. Of course, people like Alion are already up, but the rest are sound asleep. It's a good thing we caught the night port service – otherwise we wouldn't even have been able to dock properly. But it's too early for the Palace. We don't have any strict formalities – we're not pedants, after all – but still, it is the main palace of the kingdom. So, formally, and on festive days like today, we wait for the morning door opening, meaning the beginning of the ninth of dawn. But we're not going to stand at the threshold like ceremonial dolls, so I decided it would be reasonable to go to the Lesser Residence, and we'll spend some time there. Believe me, it won't be boring – there's plenty to see at the Residence. And as always I've got some urgent errands there too – a couple of local issues to handle and a few orders to give. Besides, it's not far from the port, the road won't tire you”. The princess paused expectantly.

“Of course I'll go”. Rina nodded. “I'll just run back and grab my things, and in five minutes...”

“Oh, don't be concerned. Your things will be delivered long before we get there. I've got a funny little mix-up with the captain – he's been stuck in the office since the second dawn, poor guy”, she smirked. “If you don't mind, wait for me at the Grand Entrance – I'll be down very soon”.

“Yes, definitely, I'll wait. I'm just not sure I'll be able to find the way”, Rina shrugged uncertainly. “The ship's changed so much – many of the staircases are already gone”.

“Oh, right – you don't know our Main Landing Procedure”, Ahaa remembered.

She turned and said loudly into the empty air a long, sing-song phrase, from which Rina only caught “aoe” meaning quickly or immediately, and “euo” which could be translated as go or arrive. Just about twelve seconds later – whether by magic or duty – a little boy dressed entirely in white appeared from the wall, exactly like the one who had come to the cabin with the invitation to the Upper Deck, except a bit younger. He bowed deeply but then, to Rina's big surprise, he straightened up, looked the princess right in the eyes with a mocking expression, and waited for orders. This didn't bother Her Highness in the least – on the contrary, she calmly raised her right hand and made an amazingly complicated gesture with her palm and fingers. The boy said no word, just nodded.

“The servant will escort you”, the princess said a bit too formally (though perfectly in line with her strict banker-style outfit), and, not wasting a moment, she left without adding another word.

Rina was puzzled by the servant's odd behavior and the princess's suddenly shifted mood, but the next moment she remembered that today she would meet Aoao and leave this world forever. Still, a faint unpleasant aftertaste remained...

Having followed the silent boy through the entire ship along a confusing, unmemorable path, Rina stepped out onto the First Lower Deck, dismissed the servant – he ran off without even a bow – but she chose not to notice anyone's oddities on her last day in the airy expanses of the Cloud World. She simply stood on the top step of the Grand Entrance, leaned against the doorway, and looked out at the island, staring into the distant bluish hilltops as if photographing them with a long exposure, and waited for the princess, relaxed and thinking of nothing in particular.

The zeppelin had landed long ago. She hadn't even noticed when it happened – there had been no jolt, no sound. It had quietly settled onto the firm cloud, and docking workers had immediately secured it at the bow and stern with long transparent beams.

Though Ahaa had said it was still only the fourth dawn, the sun was already shining, casting unnaturally dark, sharp shadows – no longer water-colored – on the snow-piqué tiles of the port square. A fresh breeze freely flew into the Grand Entrance in cool gusts, pleasantly ruffling Rina's hair.

“Aii o eue?” asked a young worker running by in his cyan uniform. Rina silently spread her arms – as if to say, “I'm not local, I don't understand”.

“Eue”, the worker smiled and ran on...

The queen's Lesser Residence was a relatively small, high-spired palace, gleaming with faceted, round icy-glass windows and crystalline-looking seven-pointed stars on its needles. They reached the Residence in a wheelless striped cart that comically resembled half a Kherson watermelon, although a four-seater. However, with equal accuracy, the cart could be called a boat, or a launch, or even a plane – it could both fly and float. Ahaa, Rina, and Anei, the princess's young chambermaid, walked to a blue rectangular platform and descended the shiny steps to the watermelon-shaped boat waiting in the air channel. The boat was piloted by a boy of about nine – or maybe younger – cheerful, wide-eyed, chubby-cheeked, with his bluish curls fluttering in the morning wind.

The princess masterfully climbed aboard without waiting for help or giving any instructions to the driver. Apparently, the boy-boatman was perfectly familiar with Her Highness's manners and the purpose of the trip. Holding the boat in place with a long, thin pole, he paused until everyone was seated comfortably, and then stepped onto the stern. The craft, with a soft bounce, slid along an invisible river with cloudy shores. A minute later, after passing gray buildings and strange heavy mechanisms of the port, the boat entered the city.

At first, Rina could see just rows of cubic buildings without decorations or windows – perhaps warehouses or hangars – but soon, on both sides of the canal, appeared fairytale palaces and villas of astonishing beauty – mostly snow-white, sparkling like frost on a cold January morning, framed with colonnades, twined with elaborate carvings, pierced by ornamental arches. High into the sky rose sharp spires and twisted domes of luxurious estates, especially striking in the slanting rays of the young sun – an unforgettable sight! It seemed that no one had built this city – no masons, no painters, nobody at all – it had simply grown from the pearly whiteness of pure light and steam. It was impossible to believe that the celestial splendor, surrounding Rina from all sides and overwhelming her imagination, had been created by ordinary human hands.

Rina was stunned. The maid Anei didn't notice the foreigner's amazement – she was watching her mistress only. But the princess was openly pleased, clearly enjoying Rina's wide, shocked eyes. Even the curly-haired boy felt the foreign girl's mood and winked at her frantically. But Rina saw only the palaces, fountains, columns, and glass gardens of the wondrous heavenly city.

At last, the boat-cart, carried by a steady air current and deftly steered without any effort or fuss by the little captain, slowed down and gently docked its starboard side against a rectangular platform of greenish marble. The passengers disembarked. The princess leaned down and, smiling, whispered a couple of words to the boy. He nodded importantly, but then turned around and shouted at his pudgy vessel as if it were a living horse. “Aye! Aye!” he yelled, and, with unexpected speed, sent it racing forward along the glassy canal.

“The Queen's Doormaster is waiting for us”, Ahaa announced. “He has some urgent message from Mother”.

The right corner of the square in front of the palace was tightly packed with statues, vaguely resembling prehistoric beasts or sea creatures. Rina assumed they were rough blanks – unfinished bases for future sculptures – but it turned out the statues were the result of a recent competition among contemporary masters of abstract fog-carving.

“The ones I showed you in the Pavilion”, the princess explained, weaving her way through the jumble of seal-like stone giants, “those were documents, realistic forms. But everything you see here – this is artistic. Tell me, what feelings do you find in this form?” Ahaa waved her hand toward a three-meter shapeless mass, which at first glance looked like clumsily mashed-together chunks of dirty plasticine.

“I think...” Rina squinted, examining the artwork like an old expert. “I think there's a feeling of... of peace here. I'd say these are fruits. And if you lay it like this, it looks like two sleeping bear cubs. Here are the heads, and over there – the paws”.

Ahaa scrutinized the “paws” carefully and shrugged.

“You're completely right”. Ahaa paused and looked up, as if trying to recall somebody's phrase. “A poorly trained artist always ends up with a resemblance. Creations of a talented intellect never copy anything”. She smiled. “Do you agree?”

“I agree, yes”, Rina replied rather confidently, though of course, until that moment she had never pondered talented creations.

On the iridescent steps of an immeasurably wide staircase waited the Doormaster Ailon, nicknamed Lioe, which meant ‘long-nosed'. Yet his nose was round and tiny, like a kitten's. Ailon's nosiness was metaphorical, Lioe knew all about everyone, and it was impossible to hide even the slightest detail from him. The only salvation from the doormaster's prying nature was friendship with him or at least good relations. Ailon was a born spy, which allowed him to reach considerable palace heights in his early youth, and at the first opportunity, he was appointed by the Queen herself as her most talented private informer.

The princess quickly spoke with him and, gesturing to Rina, explained the reason for the change in plans:

“Mother is occupied with affairs on the other end of the island and will be able to receive us only tomorrow evening. So the entire Pink Palace is at our disposal for a day and a half, and there's no point in lingering at the Lesser Residence. If you don't mind, we'll leave here right after the Time of Ascension”.

Time of Ascension, by air standards, is approximately eight o'clock in the morning. Naturally, Rina did not object. She was pleased to have the opportunity to see the Residence before going to the Palace. The solemn splendor of refined architectural pomp, enveloping her in a dense veil, was so abundant and heavy that normal communication in such an environment required special training, a particular habit, or at least some experience. However, despite all her expectations and preparations, the Residence simply swept her off her feet...

There are rare dreams where we envision celestial palaces and temples, shining domes and golden towers, chevaliers in emerald garments, bouquets of ruby flowers in every window, white horses with long silky manes, delightfully lovely ladies in incredible pearl dresses. We remember these dreams for many years, they are like reflections of either a lost or a promised paradise, but even in sleep, we doubt the reality of these night visions. Visualize how surprised we would be to find ourselves in the very epicenter of our utopian illusions, yet knowing that everything around is indeed real and solid, not just deceiving smoke of the imagination.

Rina looked at the world with huge eyes, but her mind refused to believe it, and her soul did not accept it at all.

“Do people really just live here?” she thought in complete confusion. “Just live, and that's it?”

It was unthinkable to suppose that someone sleeps at night in these palaces, puts on a robe in the morning, has breakfast, reads the newspaper, talks with their spouse about all sorts of nonsense, walks through the halls without noticing anything, and then runs off to some office for petty, boring paperwork. That can't be! A person spending their time in this enchanting magical beauty, in this improbable splendor, must fly like a bird, dress like an angel, speak like a spring breeze! A person must not – has no right to – remain just a man or a woman in such a city, in such a palace! But, however funny it may be, here, in this magic, lived the most ordinary people, all engaged in their banal concerns – commonplace humans, unpretentious, unassuming, and none of them felt anything special. Well, a palace? So what? A palace like a palace, look how many of them have been built recently – nothing special...

An hour and a half in the Lesser Residence passed for Rina like a dream. Ahaa tirelessly moved through halls, rooms, corridors, and staircases, from time to time, servants in various uniforms would run up to her with bows and say something, report, or inform. Ahaa listened attentively, gave orders, and inquired about details of minor domestic events. Rina politely smiled whenever she managed to realize that the phrase was about her, she even said some words, but didn't hear them at all.

“How I would love to show this Residence and the whole city to Mom, Nina, and everyone on Earth!” she thought, full of emotions, as she walked through another inner garden, peering at the sparkling crystal eucalyptus grove with beryl bridges over a sapphire stream...

However, time relentlessly marched on, and the moment came when the princess had already met everyone she wanted to see, found out everything necessary, and spoke with all the people needed for business.

“Let's go!” she exclaimed, turning to Rina, who lingered near a massive transparent globe filled with a mystical, unceasing whirling of pearlescent liquid. “We'll return to the Residence a day before departure, and you'll see all this again if you wish”.

At that moment, a young, long-haired page of about twelve approached them – his cake-like outfit all garnished with blue ornaments – and bowing at a right angle, extended a small black tray on which stood a toy house.

“Oh! This is for you! A gift from the staff!” Ahaa's face instantly brightened, and now she smiled, shedding the tension of her morning concerns and suddenly recalling the rainbow images of her distant childhood that surfaced from memory. “This is a model of the Beige House where I was born. It stood right on this spot until my father ordered it replaced with the Residence palace. And the old servant Aein made a hundred toys, such funny glassy copies, and now the palace staff gives the Beige House to visitors and guests, but only to those they really like”.

“Th... thank you”, Rina hardly managed to say, blushing deeply in embarrassment...

The purplish-glinting, steep-sided boat was steered by no one. Yet it moved confidently and briskly, cheekily lifting its sharp, upturned nose on the waves, gaining speed on stretches, and slightly sinking into the swaying mist, slowing down on turns.

24 – THE CITY OF AO
part three

Rina sat in a small chair at the back, listening to the princess's story about how the Second Royal Residence, colloquially known as the Pink Palace, was not easy to build, and how the angry king ordered disliked structures to be demolished and blown away by the wind six times, and the old architect Ain Ou, famous across three kingdoms, was unjustly exiled to the distant Flat Islands, where he remained until his death, all because the roofs of the latest version seemed too narrow to the king (here Ahaa added that later the King-Father regretted his emotional outburst, and upon learning of the unfortunate Ain Ou's passing, he ordered all accusations dropped and the Ou family returned to the capital, where he personally apologized in the presence of witnesses, and of course he took care of the payout and made sure the family had everything they needed).

Eventually, frightened by the king's displeasure, many of the architects began to avoid this construction by all possible means, some even left the country, others resigned, and the entire project was heading toward a complete halt.

But one sunny day, a pretty young woman with blue hair appeared at the Court, her name was Aaonna, and she presented the king with a drawing by her six-year-old son – a pink house falling from the sky. The drawing made its way through all the high officials, and that very evening, the king assembled five world-famous artists who wished to create a painting, based on the child's drawing, a huge picture right on the tiles of the square.

Forty people, who volunteered to help the artists, worked on the unusual canvas all night. This episode caused an uproar in the capital, equivalent in impact to a sort of revolution. The people organized a procession and a real celebration, the rumor of the astonishing news spread throughout the kingdom, and, inspired by the new idea, runaway architects began returning one after another, and in those same days, a creative union spontaneously formed. Soon they presented the king with the first sketches of the new palace. The king was delighted, and just seven months later, the palace opened its doors and was officially declared the Second Great Royal Residence.

“And what happened to the boy?” Rina asked.

The princess smiled, remembering the scenes from her distant past.

“A bit later, I think at ten years old, he was made a Knight of the White Order, and my father presented him with the Smaller Sign of the Great A, the first ruler of the kingdom. Then, when he grew up and came of age – by the way, his name was Aev, I saw him but remember little, I was just five, I guess – he moved to the Sharp Archipelago, a decent place in the southern provinces, quite wealthy and popular. There he lives, and according to my secretary, he has a beautiful house, one of the best in the city, a good family, many children, and works as a designer of festive lakes”.

Meanwhile, winding through narrow canals along streets increasingly filled with passersby dressed in loose morning clothes of white, pearl, and blue, the self-guided boat was already approaching the Palace. The maid, sitting in the middle, said something quietly to the princess, she nodded silently and looked up. In the distance, high above the city, hung a steely cloud of strict, proper ship shape, glistening with its mirrored underside.

“That's my father's former cruiser”, Ahaa explained. “He jokingly called it the Pocket Empire. Actually, the ship really resembles an empire, it has everything one could imagine, a whole miniature country. Now it's used for visits to other kingdoms, and even then, very rarely. My mechanic Aisaon says the cruiser is nearing the end of its existence. Of course, if my Mother just wished it, everything on the ship could be repaired, renewed, and restored, but no one sees the point. I don't know, maybe someday I'll take it for myself...”

A minute later, she suddenly exclaimed:

“Oh! Look! The Sixteen-Sided Pyramid is already completed! When did they manage that? It's impossible!” Ahaa turned and asked the maid a question, the girl replied, but Rina understood from her short words that the servant didn't know all the details.

“We'll spend four days in the city, you'll be shown the most interesting things, but you absolutely must visit the Pyramid, it contains the entire history of my lineage, all sixteen generations!” said Ahaa, beaming enthusiastically.

The boat smoothly slowed down, turned right, and entered a wide circular tunnel, at the exits of which pink marble stairs descended toward the air current. The boat stopped, moored, the princess gestured broadly, inviting Rina to disembark. However, there was no palace nearby. Ahaa sensed her friend's confusion and explained:

“We'd have had to walk across the entire White Square from the Lower Canal to reach the main entrance. But here, on the South Side...” she glanced around at the dock and the surrounding area, “...here's one of the back courtyards. Not luxurious, sure, but you know, I'm really not a fan of squeezing through that ever-bowing, sweet-faced crowd of fawning courtiers”.

Rina nodded in understanding, and all three of them climbed a small set of steps and entered a rather cool underground passageway. Ahaa was in a hurry, her heels clicking loudly as she strode toward the bright light ahead...

The Palace interior was crafted in the harsh, semi-military knightly style of the King-Father: stark contrast, austerity, masculinity, severity – everything was to show the main features of the King – determination and strength. Epic open spaces of enormous halls rudely interrupted by complex intersections of sharply-angled, unrounded passages.

But the Queen had managed to introduce a number of feminine, so to speak, upgrades there. Chief among them, spheres. Every so often, the eye would catch heaps of white, plaster-like globes of all sizes, from “apple” to “beach ball”. What this extravagant sphere-fest was meant to signify was known only to Her Majesty – who had secretly considered herself a brilliant couturier, architect, virtuoso sculptor, and visionary painter, of course, and therefore avoided conversations about art or fashion. Naturally, all the courtiers and even servants and low-level staff fully understood the importance – and actual danger – of their Grand Lady's whims, so they did their best to indulge her delusions, casually slipping in admiring compliments about her various “creations”.

And thus, half a year ago, after stringing up all sorts of ugly clusters of cloudy balls in every room and corner, the Queen had since been keenly attuned to every rustle and whisper, trying to catch at least a trace of awe or enthusiasm in the idle chatter of nobles and gatekeepers about her latest decorative revelation...

From Rina's point of view, the Princess was greeted a bit coldly – too officially, too ordinarily perhaps. But the reason was simple, the courtiers left behind in the Queen's absence were just third- or fourth-tier staff, not even familiar with the Princess by sight, and utterly preoccupied with Her Majesty's sudden decrees, which she was now revoking hourly. According to the most reliable reports, the Monarch was expected to arrive by lunchtime – that news catastrophically upended the plans of chefs, stewards, coordinators, administrators, secretaries, planners, managers, and at least fifty other palace staff. So, many of them simply had no time for the Princess.

Still, rushed or not, the ceremonies proceeded as usual. Eventually, Ahaa entered a tight, ice-colored chamber – more precisely, a stone cubicle without a roof, built right in a large hall decorated with polygonal columns – and while Rina waited nearby on a bench, the Princess, assisted by four specially trained handmaidens, was changing into her traditional attire.

When Rina saw the unwieldy piles of silver brocade, stacks of white silks, and masses of snowy lace the ladies carried into that tiny structure, she was honestly puzzled how they would even move in there at all. But clearly, the staff was long accustomed to such rituals. Within half an hour, Ahaa was ready.

Rina barely recognized her.

The young, slim, nice-looking girl had been turned into a stout, totally unattractive, indecently plump matron of indeterminate age. The excessively voluminous and apparently heavy white garments made her look like a homemade rag snowman from a children's masquerade. Only her big blue eyes – still somehow visible through the shawls and capes – gave away the true owner of the absurd clownish costume.

“Now let's go to my side”, Ahaa said simply, seemingly unaware of Rina's stunned expression. “I've got the eastern half here, this way – I urgently need to show you something important”.

The Princess shuffled off, rustling with thirty skirts and clunking with her weighty heels, heading toward a pair of thin, pink columns between which Rina saw a carved white-and-blue door.

Even from afar, Rina felt something was wrong. When she looked closer at the door, she stumbled and froze, mouth agape. Ahaa walked a little ahead, but then she turned to say something and noticed the weird expression on Rina's face. She stopped in confusion.

“What's the matter?” she asked, alarmed.

“Nothing...” Rina tried to smile, but it came out rather crooked and frightened.

“It'll pass – it's the change of climate, it happens to me too”, Ahaa said, peering into Rina's pale face.

“It'll pass...” Rina echoed, but stayed where she was and pointed her finger at the door. The Princess looked and saw nothing unusual. For a moment she thought Rina might've seen one of the servants or staff who startled her by their appearance or something else – but the hall was completely empty.

“The door”, Rina whispered.

“The door?” Ahaa raised her brows.

“It's the door from the pea field. I told you. The entrance to the Desert”.

Ahaa felt her thoughts break loose, spin wildly, and swirl into a hurricane. A chill prickled down her spine. On the one hand, it was insane to expect a miracle from an ordinary passage door. Besides, it was quite new – installed just a few months ago. Standard design, bluish pattern. Ahaa passed this corridor a hundred times a year. She'd seen with her own eyes the workers remove the old door and put this one in.

But at the same time, the Earth-girl couldn't be wrong – the Princess had often noted her new friend's attention to detail. However, Ahaa wanted to figure it out, and so, pulling herself together, she cautiously approached the ominous door and, with a deep breath, gingerly touched it with the very tip of her fingernail.

“Don't!” Rina suddenly screamed. “Don't go in there! It's a horrible place, there's hot sand everywhere and not a drop of water! Without Herx, you'll die!”

Ahaa froze. An unbearable, soul-piercing fear seized her whole body. She yanked her hand back, tore off her veils in one movement, spun around – huge, terrified eyes, her face white as death. She blinked strangely, shook her head, and asked in a thin, childlike voice:

“But... how will I get to my side now?”

“I don't know”. Rina took a few steps back from the door.

“But what if you're mistaken?” Ahaa whispered with a shade of hope.

“I'm not”, Rina replied just as quietly, her eyes full of fright.

At that moment, a page swung the door open. The girls squealed in great panic, and shouting, blinded by fear, bolted madly in different directions. The poor page, stunned and utterly confused, dropped right to the floor, furiously cycling through every possible and impossible reason for such a reaction, imagining every possible and impossible punishment he might now face...

But the dreadful scene ended up being, as they say in palaces, an unjustified concern. Thankfully, it all was harmless. Perhaps the door could be magically technological, but it had shown no sign of it so far. Upon closer inspection, Rina noticed that instead of a round copper handle, it had a small, ornate steel-colored hook. Obviously that difference from the original was decisive – the unusual forces had no effect on the scurrying palace boys, except for a dozen torn sleeves belonging to overly eager pages and footmen who kept catching on the wretched hook...

The Princess's “side” was a vast and seemingly endless tangle of uncountable rooms, corridors, halls, and all sorts of staircases. Plus there were interior backyards, exterior courtyards, and roof yards, twelve in total, and some of them had two or even three levels. And in the most private section of the Palace the Princess had five bedrooms to herself, four guest rooms, and eight contemplation chambers.

As Ahaa explained, a contemplation chamber was an essential part of any aerial dwelling. Everyone tried to have at least three of them – one for each primary human mood, sadness, joy, and confidence. According to ancient philosophy, all other moods were merely combinations of the three. Ahaa had one room for sadness, two for joy, and five for confidence.

But in Rina's opinion the truly astonishing curiosity of Her Highness's entire “side” was the “Well of Cold”. Officially, the well's diameter was said to be twelve child-sized steps – this exact measurement was recorded in the Palace Registry – but it looked more like a small pool, though bottomless. Rina hesitantly approached and, with the utmost caution, peered down: pure, endless blue as far as the eye could see. Not a single cloud.

Ahaa told the story of the unique event that happened on the island five hundred years ago. Back then, a certain a-it arrived in the region (old names were written in lowercase and always hyphenated). a-it was a boat repairman and settled near the aerial river Aean. One night, the craftsman woke to a strange sound, looked out his window, and saw something mystical: a thick pink fog streamed into the sky, rising straight from the cloud-solid ground.

It turned out that beneath the stone slabs lay an ancient, forgotten well, once used in antiquity to extract a special kind of morning air. Over time, the source had dried up, and only the keeper of the island knew of its existence from his archive.

And then suddenly, the well came to life again – only now it had not just exotic air, but something far more precious, Pink Mist. The slabs were removed, the well restored, and as expected, people began settling around the source of the Pink. Before long, a colonnade was built around it, together with the first crystal tree in the kingdom. The well was at the very center of this beautified space.

Years later, when the capital moved to White Island, the Royal Palace was built directly beside the place. The first version of the Palace was modest in both luxury and size, but over time, it expanded and eventually swallowed the entire square together with the colonnade. And soon after that, the well, enclosed within the Palace, fell silent. The pink mist appeared less and less until finally, it stopped altogether.

That same year, the old King – Ahaa's great-grandfather, that cheerful, carefree Aaot who loved carving taaoi – decided to construct a new palace wing at that very site. Following the advice of his architects, he preserved the well in its original state, ordering a separate yard built around it. And after a series of further reorganizations, the yard finally passed into the Princess's possession.

Even though the well had gone dormant and no one expected it to produce either relict mist or morning air again, just as in centuries past, there were never any clouds beneath it. Even at depths of hundreds of miles, ordinary clouds, driven by winds, would suddenly shift their direction and veer away from the invisible axis that began in the Princess's little yard and disappeared into the abyss of skybound infinity.

Ahaa had barely finished her story when a half-bowed, blue-liveried footman appeared, and, in clear Old-Yulret, obviously for Rina's understanding, announced that breakfast would be served in the Right Corner Dining Room, and that the Minister of Defense, Mr. Ayoen An, most humbly requested five moments of Her Highness's precious time. Ahaa gave a careless wave of her hand, and the footman slipped behind the columns and dissipated soundlessly.

“Ha! Breakfasts in the Pink Palace”, said Ahaa with a smirk, “are the least appetizing feasts you could possibly imagine”.

“Why?” Rina asked, surprised.

“Because along with the delicacies, they serve bald ministers. For dessert”. And the Princess let out a merry, ringing laugh.

25 – THE MORNING EVENING
part one

But instead of breakfast with the minister, Rina ended up at supper with someone far more significant – Her All-Illustrious Majesty the Great Queen Auna Aada Aoeina the Third. And naturally, with the crowd of the Highest Circle.

The Great Queen Aoeina, scarcely setting foot on the Palace grounds, declared morning to be evening and the sun to be the moon, which didn't trouble the servants in the slightest – quite the opposite, it delighted them greatly. Of course, such a royal proclamation inevitably meant changing into evening suits and gowns, and speaking in the florid, wordy cadences of the Eastern North, whose elaborate, overlong language perfectly suited lunar rituals. But in return, the servants received unexpected time off and extra sleep – a rare treat in royal palaces.

As for the table and the dishes, however, no changes were expected. The air people could afford many indulgences. They might be accused of an excessive fondness for luxury, rank, and ceremony, but no one in their right mind would ever call them gluttons. There was always the same dish: a cloudy mass, with the only variation being that the elite and powerful persons were prescribed the pink version, while the less influential ones received the white.

On the way to the Right Corner Dining Hall, Rina and Ahaa encountered a number of high-ranking courtiers who had outpaced the Queen and were hurrying to prepare for the important supper. In mere minutes, the princess picked up all the key, trivial, and even microscopic updates about her Most Illustrious Mother's words, moods, and thoughts. The main takeaway from the squires' over-tangled chatter was simple: the Queen was in good health and excellent spirits. Good. For the evening – or technically, of course, morning – nothing more was needed.

Upon entering the surprisingly narrow dining hall, Rina thought there wasn't a single resemblance between Ocean and Air – except for food. The pink cloud slices were hardly different from the pink coral pieces of vura-vura, except that the clouds didn't have the sea smell, and the vura-vura was far tastier. One could only regret the complete absence of culinary art here in the Air Kingdom – that is, of everything that Earth's humankind calls haute cuisine, or simply, professional cooking...

Yet the ravenous appetite sparked by fresh air and dashing up and down halls made Rina come to terms with the gastronomic flaws. The edible cloud slices, this time filigreed and arranged in ornate pyramids on large, thin plates, boasted a delicate, shimmering texture and irresistibly tempted the hungry.

“We're not waiting!” Ahaa declared resolutely.

Rina couldn't believe her ears. What? Not wait for the Queen? Was that even possible? Somewhere in medieval Europe, such a stunt would have gotten your head chopped off easily!

But the princess wasn't joking – she walked up, dropped into the first chair, and without hesitation grabbed a good stack of pink slices from the nearest dish and began eating like a tired reaper having lunch in the field he had just harvested – Her Highness was stuffing her mouth with handfuls of food, paying absolutely no attention to the bustling footmen or the courtiers deeply involved in sorting out their places at the table by rank.

Once again, Rina was stunned by the princess's upside-down manners, but seeing the indifferent faces of the other diners, she figured there was no use waiting for a formal invitation. She pulled up a chair and sat down beside Ahaa, on the left, as etiquette required. The princess gave an approving nod, mumbled something with her overfilled mouth, and reached for the huge platter again.

Rina took a few slices from the opposite dish. The palace clouds turned out to be much better than the flavorless wet pieces on the ship – texture-wise, they resembled both apple marshmallows and mallomars, though without sugar or crackers inside, but with pleasing ingredients, where the main one, naturally, was mist.

She worried she might look like an uncouth hillbilly (“Like a cavegirl”, she thought), but she was in a hurry – swallowing the ‘marshmallies' one after another, barely chewing them, sharply aware that such feasting wasn't entirely within the bounds of the palace rules and might be cut short at any moment. The supper really did stretch the norms – not because the princess and her guest were stuffing their faces without waiting for the Queen, but simply because Ahaa, the kingdom's Second Most Important figure, hadn't bothered to change. She was still wearing that same awful, heavy brocade getup which, frankly, had shed much of its bulk during her wanderings through the Palace.

The trick was that Ahaa kept untying this ribbon, unbuttoning that button, dropping one blouse after another – there were at least a dozen of them. Then off went a collar, and then a pleated skirt – those you couldn't even count. Every “lost” garment was immediately picked up by the ever-watchful maids, who tracked the princess from afar and passed the duty from hand to hand like a relay baton. That's why by mealtime, Ahaa looked almost normal – not as ridiculous as earlier in the day. Still, her remaining outfit wasn't of the right cut and, more importantly, the prescribed evening color. The princess herself was considering sneaking off to her wardrobe room, stripping off every last layer, and tossing on a light pale-pink robe.

But then the Queen appeared.

Everyone jumped up and bowed so impossibly low it was a wonder to watch how these odd folk managed to keep their balance and not face-plant onto the marble floor.

“My girl!” exclaimed the Great Queen Aoeina the Third.

Rina stopped dead mid-bite, a half-chewed hunk in her mouth.

The Queen was Black. As a raven. She was a large, tall, broad-shouldered, round-faced black woman with long arms and a busty frame. Dressed in a curious raiment – a knee-length frock made up of dozens of faintly glowing fabric triangles in the color of falling sakura petals. And just above her waist – three crumpled pale-lilac orbs, resembling clumsily made snowballs.

On her feet, massive wedge boots reminiscent of last century's trends – thin on top, but as if forged from cast iron below. And on her head, beneath the huge round hat three feet in diameter – sharp thorns of bent black wire. Yes, wire! Somehow crafted from royal hair by unknown means. On her hands – white pearlescent gloves clashing starkly with her charcoal, absolutely black skin. And to top off the whole weird picture – a bunch of green and red ribbons trailing to the floor down her back, like those worn by folk dancers in Southeastern Europe.

“My girl! How lovely that you made it back in time for the Oe-Oata Festival!” the Queen cried joyfully from the doorway.

“But Mama”. The princess raised her brows, “Oe-Oata is a fall holiday. It will be in a month and a half!”

“Really?” the Queen looked genuinely surprised. “Ah, they're misreporting again”, she muttered indignantly, pulling off her gloves, letting them fall to the floor, then retrieving a pink handkerchief from her belt pocket, and vigorously wiping her hands as if she'd touched something utterly sticky and filthy. “You know”, she continued, approaching the table and immediately sitting next to Rina, “I had to postpone my meeting with Aioel for twice as long – at least until the next Southern Winds”.

“Why, Mother?” Ahaa asked politely.

“Oh! If I had my way, I'd put off not just the meeting but Aioel himself – and not just until the Winds but... uh... well, you know”. The Queen deftly pulled a slender white stick from her sleeve and, with a swift flick, turned it into the finest ornate fan.

The princess didn't comment on the outrageous, handsome womanizer Aioel, who had already dominated every dinner conversation in the realm. Meanwhile, Aoeina the Third had joined the meal and was now, like a hungry little wolf cub unfed for three days, cramming her mouth with cloud slices in such a rush that she was nearly choking. Rina immediately lost all her appetite watching the poor famished Majesty.

Truth be told, this predatory eating style was just a lifelong habit, an unshakable rule of good manners learned in childhood, and though the Queen didn't mind a snack at the moment, she certainly wasn't actually hungry.

Incidentally, Rina wasn't even offended this time that neither Her Majesty nor Her Highness paid her any more attention than they would a spare chair. In fact, that was exactly the reception she expected – because in the eyes of the air people, anyone unannounced or unacquainted simply doesn't exist.

The Queen and the Princess, defying all laws of physics, managed to chatter animatedly through mouths full to bursting – and over the head of the completely unnoticed Rina. Watching them talk this way, she sadly realized her desire to have lunch was gone for good. She wouldn't be able to swallow another crumb, no matter how rude and peasant-like that might seem to their royal eyes.

At last, she was officially introduced to the Great Queen. Though it wasn't Ahaa or a courtier or a footman who did it. No, it was a cute miniature boy, maybe six years old. Or even younger. How early they get into the Palace – that's what's astonishing!

The little boy darted out of a side door, briskly toddled up to the princess, bowed deeply, properly bending right at the waist, and stood there waiting. Ahaa whispered something to him, he straightened up and took a bluish ice-like cube that appeared on her palm quite unexpectedly. The boy turned to the Queen, dramatically lowered his eyelids, threw back his head adorned with a thick mop of curly gray-blue hair, and, in a shrill, piercing falsetto, cried out with inspired fervor:

“Ayo eo! Aoa Rina!

Ae aa ao! Ae e!

Rina au eae!”

The Queen looked straight at Rina for a minute, eye-to-eye, and gave a slow, courteous nod. Rina responded with a bow – also slowly and ceremoniously enough – at least deeper than the Queen's nod, but somewhat higher than the bows of the staff. The boy remained standing there, stiff at attention, his doll-like face glowing with rapturous delight, beaming across the entire dining hall. He stared expressively at Rina. It was clear, the newly introduced person was expected to say something appropriate.

“Thank you. Your Majesty. I'm very pleased”, Rina mumbled awkwardly and quite out of place, blushing red under the Queen's probing gaze.

“Pleased too”, replied the Queen shortly – also completely off-protocol – and all of a sudden she asked, “Have you already tried to play aaoo?”

“No, we didn't have time”, the princess answered for Rina. “The Captain ordered not to stop at Ayeon and to skip hours at the island of Avaal because of a possible solar storm. Avaal has a decent field – very convenient to play and big enough”.

“Nonsense!” the Queen exclaimed, her voice resounding in the chamber. “Who needs your Avaal to play? We can play aaoo anywhere – even in this very room”.

“N-no, it's too cramped here”, Ahaa disagreed, eyeing the hall with doubt.

“Cramped?!” The Queen looked so astounded, as if she had just discovered the Main Secret of the Universe. “Nowhere is cramped for aaoo! I'll prove it to you right now. We'll play here! On the table!”

25 – THE MORNING EVENING
part two

What happened next defied everything Rina thought she knew about monarchs, palaces, and royal life. The Queen hurled a dish with unfinished cloud-mass into the corner and suddenly leaped onto the table. Just like that – from her chair, onto the table in one jump! Her daughter followed.

Once there, both majestic crowned rulers began – with professional precision and snapping force – to kick the dishes in every direction with their heavy shoes. Vases, bowls, plates – everything flew to the floor, shattering across the white marble in a spray of icy shards, carpeting the hall in glittering wreckage.

Rina, horrified, backed away to the far wall where the courtiers had already retreated, and watched the regal family's barbaric revelry unfold. Suddenly, the Queen began to sing – a pirate song, it seemed to Rina – and with each mighty kick she shouted lines from a wild chorus:

Aye ai! Aye ai!

Ae eu! Ao ai!

From her meager knowledge of the Air language, Rina knew that “aye” was the word sailors yelled – it meant something like “go!” or “come on!”, only way more excited and aggressive.

Fortunately, the players quickly ran out of dishes. And the next moment half a squad of servants rushed in from out of nowhere and instantly wiped, swept, and cleaned away every bit of glass and debris. Rina noted how indifferent and accustomed the palace staff were to such outbursts – always ready for impromptu emergency cleanups.

“The aaoo field is ready!” the Queen proclaimed.

She kicked off both her shoes one after the other, tore a pink fluff ball from her dress in one yank, and hurled it into the air.

The downy button turned out to be feather-light. It floated gently downward. The Queen wound up and kicked it with such incredibly monstrous power it could have demolished a stone wall. The fluff shot forward – though being so light, it only flew four feet before drifting to a stop. The Queen's ferocious energy was instantly dispersed by its tiny mass.

Naturally, the Princess had already joined the game at the other end of the table. Barefoot now too, she returned the blow with equal might and precision.

And then the nightmare began. The impenetrably black Queen and the young Princess, flushed pink with enthusiasm and agitation, ruthlessly pummeled the poor fluff, volleying it between each other with massive, brutal kicks. But after all, it was just a little fuzz glob from the dress, a downy button – it couldn't survive such insane punishment for too long. Two dozen savage bashes later, it exploded into tiny flossy bits and disappeared.

“That's it!” the Queen cried. “My fet!”

“Why yours?” Ahaa protested.

“I struck last!”

“So what? The ball broke – that's a technical situation!”

“Nooo! It's a game situation!” the Queen insisted. “All by the rules – first fet is mine!”

“I knew it!” the Princess sulked.

“Of course!” Aoeina jumped down from the table onto the dazzlingly clean white floor. “Of course you knew. Who's the best player in my league? And if you say they only give me awards because I'm the Queen, I won't agree. I will never object – but I won't agree! Lately I've been playing without any royal insignia at all – did you notice?”

Ahaa sighed and climbed down from the table too.

“Fine, we'll count the first fet...”

“The first full fet!” the Queen corrected.

“The first full fet”, Ahaa confirmed reluctantly. “We'll count the first full fet as yours. But only until morning”.

“Oh!” the Queen exclaimed, even clapping her hands. “I completely forgot! I didn't give the order for Island Time. Those blockheads will never think to switch the schedule!”

“Them? Never!” the Princess smiled.

At that moment, the Queen pointed with her nail toward the fan lying on the marble floor. Swift like a tennis ball, an old servant – dressed in yellow, but pale as a ghost – shot out from the wall, snatched up the fallen royal accessory, and in the next second, with a deep one-sided bow, was offering the Queen an identical, brand-new one. She took it, unfolded it, fanned her face a few times, and said:

“In that case, I'll leave you until dawn”. She pronounced “dawn” with a double or even triple “o” sound somehow, like “daooown”, then spun on her heels to leave, but after a dozen steps she changed her mind, nodded slightly to Rina, who was still stuck to the wall, and asked:

“Do you have a moment? I'd like to exchange a few words”.

Rina bowed and thought, “I know, she'll be asking about Earth”.

But Rina was wrong. Aoeina the Third wasn't the least bit interested in Earth, or the Ocean, or any other kingdom or world. Over the past few weeks, Her Majesty had been deeply preoccupied with redesigning and modernizing the most important element of her entire life – the Throne Room. She had already heard the opinions of her highest courtiers, throne-side advisors, ministers, and even the speakers of both parliamentary chambers, but at that moment, the view of a random foreigner intrigued her far more.

As was customary in the Air Realm, the Queen moved forward without waiting for the guest's consent. Rina had no choice but to hurry after her.

“You'll be doing me an invaluable favor”, the Queen said, not looking back and picking up her pace. “And it'll cost you nothing. In return, I'll gift you a double-decker launch. What are your thoughts?”

“Thank you”, Rina replied, finally catching up with the swift-footed sovereign. “But I don't need a launch”.

“You don't?”

“No”.

“Then what d'ya wanna get?” Aoeina, by her royal habit, had shifted to a rough, shantytown jargon.

“I don't want to get anything, Your Majesty, but if possible, I would like to see your famous Blue Ice Fountain”.

“Fountain?!” The Queen stopped so abruptly it was as if someone had glued her in place with an instant binder.

She stared at Rina, her eyes huge, her mouth open. She couldn't seem to draw a breath, a spasm flickered across her face. Rina began to worry for the monarch's health, but in the next moment the Queen melted like warm ice cream and broke into a benevolent smile.

“I can't believe it! The Earth knows about my Fountain! But how, from where? Do they really teach about the Pink Palace in your schools?”

“Not yet, but one man told me about the Fountain, my good friend”.

“Your good friend? Do I know him?”

“I don't think so, Your Majesty”. Rina shook her head.

“What's his name?”

First, out of caution, Rina considered lying – saying whatever name came into her head – but Queen Aoeina was looking at her with such sincere, childlike curiosity that it felt awkward.

“Am I a spy?” Rina thought. “And Herx, is he a criminal? Why should we hide our names?”

“His name is Herx, Your Majesty. It means The Hand Holding the Sword”.

“The sword?” the Queen's eyes swelled even more. “He's a knight?”

Rina nodded. Aoeina grew thoughtful.

“Unfortunately, Knight Herx has never been introduced to me”, she said slyly, this time squinting. “And what does he know about our Ice Fountain? And the Palace? And the Kingdom?”

“It's probably hard to believe, but I'm sure, Your Majesty, he knows everything about everything”, Rina admitted honestly.

“In that case, I shall order his home address found today in the Universo Book of Faces and personally write him an invitation. We need people who know things! If he truly knows everything, I'll appoint him Minister of Knowledge. Tomorrow morning we'll establish a new ministry, and your friend shall oversee all knowledge throughout my realm. What do you think? Will he be pleased by such an appointment?”

“Definitely”, Rina answered that same second, though with a hint of uncertainty.

By that time, they had already entered the Throne Hall.

As dazzling and breathtaking as the luxurious decor of the Lesser Residence was, it still couldn't compare to the blinding splendor of the Throne Hall of Her Excellency, the Great Queen of Air. It seemed the hall had been woven from cosmic strands of winter morning lights and the pure dreams of little children. If one were to place earthly and airy grandeur side by side, the gilded malachite rooms of Earth's finest palaces would seem no more than musty closets for rags.

The incomparable, marvelous Hall was filled with astounding items, indescribable masterpieces created by fantastically talented aerial artisans, but in the very center of this decorative rapture stood a rather long, plain and low platform about one foot tall, covered with a carpet of sparkling blue frost. On the platform sat a peculiar ivory-colored cube, twenty by twenty inches or slightly larger.

The Queen slowed her step, but Rina was so enchanted – so captivated by the stained-glass windows, amethyst statues, and the topaz bridges – that she didn't notice when she accidentally climbed up the carpeted rise. Realizing what she had done, she glanced around in confusion and found herself right next to the cube.

“I see you like it here”, the Queen said with a smile.

Rina blushed and wanted to jump down, but Aoeina stopped her with a gesture.

“No, no, that's exactly why I invited you. Sit on the Throne and turn your head to the left – I want to take a look. You can tell me later how it feels”.

“The Throne?” Rina thought. “This silly plastic cube is the Royal Throne?”

Indeed, the small, primitive, even downright unimpressive object – haphazardly put on an elegant pedestal in such a charmingly refined hall – seemed completely out of place. It bore no resemblance to the ornate golden thrones in countless movies and antique paintings. Rina touched the cube with her finger.

“So cold... Why is it so cold? Do they keep it in a freezer?”

But she had no choice – the Queen was waiting. Rina sat down and nearly cried out: the cube was much icier than it had first felt. A fleeting thought (and not for the first time): “Was Her Majesty playing a silly prank on a naïve, inexperienced foreigner?” But the Queen paced along the platform with a focused expression, observing the effect produced by the Throne.

The minute Rina felt she was about to freeze solid and literally turn into an icicle, the Queen asked:

“And what are your impressions? What do you think of my Air Throne? Speak honestly – like a friend to a friend”.

Rina eagerly leaped off the frosty cube and ran over to Aoeina.

“Everything's wonderful”, she said with a smile, barely stopping, “but you, Your Majesty, will need to use a cushion”.

“Use what?” The Queen was so stunned she couldn't get her eyebrows back to their usual position. “Coo schenn?! Why coo schenn?!”

“Well, not necessarily a cushion, you could put down a rug or something instead. Or just dress warmer”, Rina mused aloud. “Preferably pants. Yes, pants would be the best. Otherwise, you'll get the flu or severe sinusitis”.

For once, the Queen didn't even have time to take a breath. So she just gawked with her inky eyes, mouth agape like an aquarium Molly fish. If her extraordinary black skin had been capable of reflecting even a little light, she would have turned chalk-white. But her unbreachable, petroleum-like blackness masked all of Her Majesty's true feelings and inner turmoil.

To skip extraneous details, we can say that the initially excellent Cold Throne idea didn't work out as fine as planned. Every one of Rina's statements, literally every phrase, sent the poor Queen into a deep shock. Rina tried to explain more simply, more safely, thinking through not just the words but even the letters. All in vain. Every time, the Great Sovereign froze with her mouth open, not breathing for a while, and Rina could only hope these paralysis episodes wouldn't end too tragically.

“She's got some weak nerves... Not that you'd guess from her shape”, Rina pondered in confusion.

Who knows how long this agonizing ordeal around the throne would have continued, if not for the photographer. Just after Auna Aada Aoeina the Third had finally managed – with great effort – to return to her senses after another Rina's explanation (this time, Rina was trying – as gently as possible – to explain the difference between cotton batting and synthetic padding), a large, burly, wedge-bearded man in a striped suit burst into the Hall. With both his hands he was carrying an enormous and apparently hefty multi-sided suitcase.

25 – THE MORNING EVENING
part three

“Asaol!” the Queen shouted as if calling someone across a packed stadium. “My friend! You've already come from Aaniya! How wonderful!”

“I am deeply honored to behold my Most Glorious Lady in fine health and complete prosperity”. The large man bowed as deeply as his elephantine frame would allow. “Upon hearing of Our Splendid Queenliness's return to the Palace, I took the first opportunity to visit Your Excellent Majesty, knowing and admiring how kind you are to us poor, weak, and wretched slaves of true art and science”.

“All right, all right, better tell me what it was like there! How's Aaniya? How are the islands? Come on, spill it!” the Queen urged impatiently and plopped down onto the mirrored floor.

The fat man immediately followed her lead, not forgetting to bow a couple of times first. Rina stood three paces away but, thinking it over, decided not to go against palace etiquette and also sat down on the polished marble, which, fortunately, happened to be warm, maybe even heated.

“Total chaos on the islands, Your Majesty”, began Asaol, round as a cupcake, his blue eyes sparkling and his smile impossibly wide. “Some were flying this way, others that way! There's just no order, no system whatsoever to the natives' behavior, that's what I'll say with your permission. But on the other hand, the Eastern Winds are absolutely exquisite – simply and purely delightful! Winds like those deserve poems, no, better yet, operas! We were all awestruck to the core, and if not for certain well-known constraints, I'd have surely tried that thing we spoke of... remember, Your Majesty? – but of course, Your Majesty, you know how firm I am on that matter”.

“They reported to me from the Geographic Commission that some new mountains have appeared in the South”.

“Yes! Mountains!” Asaol threw up his hands. “Unimaginable! Just picture it: at the foot of one of those newborn peaks, we found fourteen whole veins of lilac haze – fourteen! And each one was like this!” He held up his palm and pointedly showed his pinky. “Half a finger thick!”

“You don't say!” gasped the astonished Queen. “I can't believe it! That's the discovery of the century!”
