“But I'm looking for the entrance to the next near world”, Rina insisted.

“|There's nothing to look for. The Sixth World is inside you, it's your imagination.|”

“She's mixing everything up again”, Rina thought. “If the Butterfly World is my imagination, and the Ice World is my imagination, then what's real?”

“|The unimagined Ice World remains imagined as long as you imagine you're in an imagined world,|” the fairy replied.

And though Rina didn't understand what was imagined and what was not, she eventually agreed to fly with Dinissa to her Meadows, because sitting in a damp forest wouldn't get her any answers, not even to imaginary questions.

The flight to the Blue Meadows took half a second. The fairy did nothing, she just wished to go there, and that was enough. In the very same instant, Rina found herself in the pouring rain. Huge cold drops fell from the sky, drumming incessantly on the leaves, her head, and shoulders.

But shockingly, the butterflies were flying in the rain. Hard to believe, there were light arguses, purple emperors, skippers, cabbage whites, and blue swallowtails fluttering calmly among millions of flickering raindrops.

“|This is a butterfly rain,|” Dinissa explained. “|It's been falling for twenty-one years and three weeks, and the Blue Meadows have been getting bluer and bluer ever since.|”

Within two minutes, Rina was soaked to the skin and started sneezing. Dinissa flitted beside her, and at each sneeze jumped away in fright.

“But this isn't... isn't... Ah-choo!!! It isn't normal”, Rina said at last. “You need to make a hideout, or I don't know... a shel... a shel... Achoo!!! At least a shelter. You'll all catch co... co... co... Ah-choo!!! Cold!”

“|We don't get cold, the butterfly rain doesn't wet our wings.|”

“Still”, Rina said, thinking the fairy wasn't all that kind – dragging her guest into a puddle while she herself stayed dry.

“|But I absolutely must show you our relic!|” Dinissa whispered excitedly, her voice now loud enough to hear from a few steps away.

“And this relic of yours... it's not far, is it?” Rina asked hopefully, scared to imagine wading through drenched grass with rainwater sloshing in her sopping sneakers.

“|It's right here!|” the fairy said and landed in a clearing.

At the very center of a tiny glade, someone had gathered a pile of stones, into which half of a green bottle was stuck upside down – just an ordinary soda or beer bottle with no label and its neck completely broken off. The bottle, wedged bottom-up between the three rocks, stood there glistening with drops of water on all sides. Dinissa darted onto one of the rocks, dashed into this strange glass container, spun around for a second or two, flew out, and cried joyfully:

“|The Relic of the Blue Meadows! The Magic Palace of Green Light!|”

If Rina weren't so cold and wet, she might have doubled over laughing – so much excitement over a broken bottle! But she only sighed and looked around for a big tree or something like that to hide from the rain. Unfortunately, the meadow held only a few skinny birches with barely any branches on them, and some prickly shrubs. Dinissa, completely caught up in her bottle, didn't hear Rina's grumbling thoughts and continued her proclamations in an ecstatic, triumphant tone. Rina couldn't help but listen:

“|For seven years now, the butterflies of the Blue Meadows have been flying here to admire the splendor of the Green Palace! The heavens, the great heavens have adorned our lives with this unrivaled wonder! The Green Palace is unique! For in the Universe, there is nothing transparent except water and air.|”

Hearing such a ridiculous claim, Rina even forgot about the rain for a minute.

“How's that, nothing is transparent?” she thought, surprised. “Then what about crystal, quartz, mica, and all kinds of natural glass, too? And the windows in houses, ha, aren't they transparent? And plastic can be transparent like glass, even better than glass, and diamonds, sapphires, and other precious stones. And ice! What about ice? And jelly? Many things!”

But Dinissa didn't hear her. In a frenzy of inspiration, the fairy flitted back and forth, her operatic voice swelling as she rhapsodized over the divine gift of the gods – a broken bottle. Then Rina took off her backpack, rummaged around in the bottom, and pulled out a souvenir she got from Princess Ahaa's servants – a toy made according to the Law of Resemblance, a transparent model of the Beige House where the princess was born.

“Here's another ‘solid and transparent'!” Rina announced, holding the little house out on her open palm.

Dinissa fell silent mid-sentence, zipped over to the toy, hovered above it for a few moments, and suddenly collapsed into the grass as if dead. The fairy lay there on a dandelion leaf, her antennae twitching nervously. Rina reached out a pinky to touch her, but Dinissa suddenly fluttered up and shot into the sky, nearly brushing Rina's nose.

“|A great new miracle!|” she cried from above for all the meadow to hear. “|The Magical Translucent Palace!|”

“Everything here is a miracle and everything's a palace”, Rina muttered bitterly, watching as hundreds of curious butterflies swarmed from every side at Dinissa's enthusiastic yells.

After standing nearby and getting her fill of the over-the-top slogans of this silly celebration – and growing a bit irritated with the butterflies – Rina waved her hand in exasperation, set the precious Beige House toy down beside the bottle, then turned around, and walked off in no particular direction.

But Dinissa soon caught up with her.

“|Wait! Wait, please! Don't go!|” she sang anxiously.

Rina stopped.

“|You can't just leave like this! First I must reward you on behalf of all the butterflies of our planet!|”

“I'd feel truly rewarded if you helped me find the entrance to the next world, the Sixth One. But I know you cannot do that”, Rina said, wringing the water out of what was left of her hairdo.

“|I can't,|” Dinissa agreed, “|but no one can – except you.|”

“I don't understand”, Rina sighed hopelessly.

“|It will all happen on its own,|” the fairy tried to reassure her. “|Ditilia lasts forty-eight minutes – two times twenty-four, and only in rare cases three times thirty-three. So you shouldn't worry. You'll reach the next World very soon – much sooner than it seems. And now, to somehow thank you for the great gift you've bestowed, I ask you to accept my invitation to a formal festive flower breakfast.|”

And really, what else could she do? If Dinissa was right – and Rina was almost sure she was – then there was no reason to worry. Whether it was two times twenty-four or even three times thirty-three, it wouldn't be that long to wait, and her journey through the worlds would continue all by itself. That would be the simplest, best, and most reliable solution to everything. And besides, she'd long since run out of food – even the last piece of the edible fog had vanished somewhere – and her empty stomach was clearly hinting that an extra breakfast wouldn't hurt.

Rina followed the butterfly. Soon they both crossed the clearing and descended into a shallow ravine covered in a dense carpet of short, tough grass, like on a soccer field. To Rina's undisguised relief, the festive breakfasts in the Blue Meadows took place under a large fallen tree, which, though beginning to rot, still formed something like a leaky but passable roof. The old birch had once stood on the edge of the slope but fell victim to a hurricane six years ago and collapsed across the wide gully, forming a kind of shelter. It was, of course, damp and soggy underneath, but most of the raindrops slid down the slanted trunk, giving Rina a brief reprieve from the relentless downpour.

She also noticed that near a protrusion in the slope, about five steps from the birch, there grew a strange flower that looked like a medium-sized rubber playground ball with the top sliced off. It was full of rainwater and swayed precariously on its stem. When everyone had gathered – Rina, now surrounded by about thirty big colorful butterflies, settled somewhat comfortably on a thick branch beneath the overhanging trunk – Dinissa darted toward the flower and struck it hard with all her might. The flower swayed, bent over, and suddenly poured its water out near Rina's feet.

“|Now we wait,|” said the fairy mysteriously, and many of the invited butterflies rustled their wings softly, as if in anticipation.

They didn't have to wait long, as soon as new raindrops began to fall into the now-empty flower, it let out a long, resonant bronze hum, like a small church bell struck with a strong fist. The sound filled the ravine, cascading into their ears. And in that same instant, Rina felt a sweet taste on the tip of her tongue. A second later, she sensed something bizarre, as if a scoop of exquisite ice cream were melting in her mouth. Grand-Prix or Mix-Deluxe from the Cherry-Berry shop – hard to tell. The more raindrops hit the flower's walls, the deeper it hummed – the faster her mouth filled with invisible, heavenly-tasting sherbet.

Utterly bewildered, intrigued, and shocked, Rina instinctively chewed the delicate dessert and eventually swallowed it. And immediately, she felt a small cube of chocolate, or more precisely, a Fauchon truffle, gently placed on her tongue.

“With hazelnut”, flashed through her mind.

Now, without a doubt, she joyfully chewed various confections, halva, and even (you won't believe this) ‘rahat lokum' – the Turkish delight! Then something she couldn't even name, but it was unbelievably delicious, with a scent of coffee and mint at once.

Within fifteen or twenty minutes, she was full. No, Rina had never had a sweet tooth, and none of her friends had ever said that – unlike, for example, Olya Gurnovo, who was impossible to picture without candy in her mouth. But after having just swallowed a whole mouthful of honey, Rina thought she'd had enough sweets to last a lifetime, and that all these ultra-caloric treats couldn't be good in the long run. She wanted to at least ask when breakfast would end, but with her mouth overloaded, how could she speak?

Finally, after she somehow managed to chew through a sticky but fantastically tasty caramel, and just as her mouth was instantly rewarded with a custard-filled éclair, Rina couldn't take it anymore. She groaned and waved her arms. Luckily, Dinissa noticed her protests, despite being entirely absorbed in slurping down a steady stream of cornflower nectar through her proboscis.

“|The Great Giver of the Wonderful Palace requests the floor!|” the fairy cried with excitement.

But Rina only blinked and chewed, now struggling with a hefty portion of tiramisu.

“|The Great Giver expresses her satisfaction with the honor shown her!|” Dinissa sang with pomp.

“‘Satisfaction' isn't the word”, Rina thought grimly and downed the remainder of the detestable – in truth, divine – delicacy.

Suddenly, the feast ended, the raindrops had filled the flower to the brim. It gave one last hum and went silent. Chewing fiercely, Rina polished off a handful of celestial watermelon taffies that had appeared in place of the cake, and exhaled helplessly.

A few minutes later, after struggling to catch her breath, Rina climbed out of the ravine and stopped. The fairy flew up, settled on Rina's shoulder, and whispered barely audibly:

“|Just don't tell anyone.|”

“Don't tell about the singing flower?” Rina asked, confused.

“|No, about the Black Bellberry.|”

“What bellberry?”

“|I'm not supposed to, but I will!|” Dinissa declared firmly, flew off, and beckoned Rina to follow.

A few minutes later, the fairy landed on a thin, knotted branch of one of those scraggly birches Rina had noticed earlier. The slippery branch was studded with tiny black nuts.

“|They insisted on a non-floral breakfast, but I refused. I don't care what they say. I do what I believe is right,|” Dinissa mumbled, scurrying along the branch, touching each fruit with her antennae. “|This one!|” she said, stopping by one of the nuts. “|It's ripe! Pick it.|”

Rina managed to pry it off with her fingernails. The Black Bellberry fruits actually resembled gooseberries – just as hard and rough – but smaller and truly pitch-black, like rounded bits of coal.

“|Inside is a drop of Blue Water,|” explained Dinissa. “|This drop can extinguish any fire. I tested it myself once. But blue drops keep well only in bellberries. Well, it's true, the water doesn't spoil in the cups of azure lilies either, but lilies are soft and inconvenient to carry. Take it – soon you might need it. In case you ever find yourself walking through fire and flame.|”

Rina thought, “Why am I even surprised? The fairy hears everything in my mind and knows my secrets”.

“|Yes, I do, I know many things,|” Dinissa confirmed. “|The World of Fire is a harsh place, its rules are cruel – just one drop of blue water could get you branded a state criminal. But that single drop can also save the life of anyone caught in the flames.|”

Rina thanked her for the extremely useful gift and asked:

“How many minutes are left in my ditilia?”

“|Your ditilia ended twelve minutes ago,|” the fairy said. “|I'm only keeping you in the Blue Meadows until we finish our conversation. But now, it seems, the time has come. Though I wouldn't mind if you could stay with us for five or nine years longer, two times four plus one.|”

Rina tried to protest, but Dinissa cut her off delicately and continued:

“|I understand everything and won't insist. You must go. And unfortunately, I have nothing more to give you as a reward for your generosity.|”

Rina held out her palm, and Dinissa immediately flew over and with a familiar maneuver perched on Rina's pinky. Rina said:

“Thank you for everything you've done for me. At least I've eaten enough to last two weeks”. She smiled. “And most importantly – without you I would be worried and anxious, running around these forests and meadows looking for a way out that doesn't even exist. And your Bellberry too, of course, is a valuable thing”.

Dinissa said nothing. She lifted off and began circling above Rina's head, rising higher and higher.

“|Valuable, but too small! Too small a thing!|” came her voice from above. “|I feel I should – no, I'm obliged – I must reveal to you the secret of the Blue Dreams.|”

Dinissa folded her wings and dropped, but just before hitting the grass, she glided neatly onto a slender reed.

“|Blue Dreams saved five sorcerers and eight fairies, including my grandmother, the Great Fairy Illinissa,|” she said. “|This is my clan's secret – I will demand a terrible vow from you. Promise that you will never tell anyone my hidden truth. And swear on the Heavenly Sacred Translucent Palace!|”

Rina looked at the butterfly, and said with a deep breath, trying to be as precise and solemn as she could:

“I will never tell anyone your family's hidden truth, I swear by the Translucent Palace, the Green Bottle, and the Black Bellberry”.

Apparently, her calm and firm tone was enough to convince the fairy. Dinissa looked around, paused, and then declared in a voice of extreme importance:

“|Blue Dreams will instantly envelop anyone to whom you say these words:

Kaes haes mio:

Kiu biu nio|”

Rina smiled instinctively.

“|You think those lines sound silly and childish?|” asked Dinissa. “|You're right. In fact, they're not even a spell, but an old counting rhyme for little ones in the Levetin language. But one day, the lyric was turned into a magical formula, and it gained great power. It cannot be written down, and no one is supposed to know what the words mean. You can only memorize it. Have you memorized it? Repeat it.|”

“Kaes maes laaes”, Rina began.

“|Wrong! There's no maes there!|” the fairy exclaimed, offended. “|Listen!|”

She sang the rhyme from the very beginning, then again, and four more times after that, but Rina, too nervous and eager for the next transition into the near world, kept mixing up the words and sounds. Finally, after a dozen tedious tries, she got it right without a hitch.

“|That's all. Your time has come. It's getting hard for me to hold you here,|” Dinissa said with a sigh. “|Soon you'll be in the ice and snow. But just in case, repeat the spell before sleep so you don't forget it. Use it wisely. As soon as you speak it aloud, everyone around you will fall into Blue Sleep for forty-eight minutes and two seconds – two times twenty-four plus four minus two. But remember, in every kingdom there is one person the spell won't work on. And now, farewell. You could simply wait and everything would happen on its own, but for the ditilia to end properly and precisely, hold your breath as long as you can.|”

Rina puffed out her cheeks, pressed her lips tight, and stood there, staring at the butterfly, eyes wide and unblinking. But she couldn't last more than half a minute before exhaling sharply.

“|Wrong!|” Dinissa shouted. “|You have to hold your breath to its absolute limit!|”

“Easy for you to say ‘to the limit'“, Rina thought in frustration and clamped her mouth shut again.

This time, she held on until her chest ached and purple spots danced in her eyes. Any longer and she would have fainted. She swayed, stumbled sideways, and... in the very next second, she was inside the spinning tunnel. Gulping air, steadying her breath, and calming herself, she scanned around – nothing new, just the same multicolored carousel, the strong warm wind lifting her upward along an endless dappled passage.

“At least I'll have time to dry off before the freeze hits”, she said with a smile, turning and attempting once more to get comfortable on the taut, unruly currents. But the cunning wind kept pushing her back, trying to flip her over to the ‘right position'...

WORLD SIX – SNOW
28 – PURPLE JOKERS
part one

The pipe vanished. Sharp glass needles flashed before her eyes, and in the next instant, Rina found herself buried in a huge, spongy snowdrift. It all happened in less than a second. No long jump, no free fall. She just shot down a vertical shaft about five meters deep – maybe even deeper – and came to a relatively soft stop, without hitting anything or even managing to scream.

She lay still for half a minute, ‘steeling-healing her nerves', as Grandma-2 says, and listened. Nothing. Complete silence. Out of caution, she waited a little longer – what if someone had seen her plunge and was now trying to get to her, maybe to ask who she was, why and where she came from.

But waiting was tiresome. Eventually, once her emotions settled back into place, she decided to climb out.

Though the usual methods didn't work properly – the snow wouldn't pack down. It was too dry and kept collapsing and sliding. She had to dig a horizontal tunnel, forcing her way forward headfirst. Rina scooped the snowy mass with both hands, pulled white clumps under her belly, and awkwardly shoved forward on her knees, inching through the loose, unstable layers. Luckily, the snow was fresh and yielded easily.

“Wait a minute!” She stopped short and looked at her palms in surprise. Light barely filtered through the half-collapsed opening above, but it was enough to see the snowflakes. “This isn't snow! It's warm!”

She took a handful and brought it closer to her eyes – the snow looked just like its ordinary counterpart, say, somewhere like Canada. But then why was it so warm?

“Or am I cold?”

She touched her cheeks with the backs of her hands – no, quite the opposite, they were hot. Steam puffed from her mouth, the way it does in the bitter cold. Yet Rina wasn't freezing. After staring at the snow and failing to unravel the mystery, she waved the question off.

“All right. Not today – we'll figure it out later”.

Indeed, the main thing now was to get out of the drift. She returned to digging. Getting into the rhythm, she became so engrossed in the work that she screamed in fright when a bright light flared suddenly through the newly opened exit. The sun blinded her for a second. Shielding her eyes, she peeked out and knelt on the edge. The fragile snowy shelf gave way, and... Rina tumbled down.

When she got up, she looked more like a snowman than a tourist from Earth. The snow of the Ice World really was different, not exactly like in Kyiv – sticky and clingy, almost like poplar fluff. Falling on it meant spending a long time brushing herself off – the snowflakes clung to her clothes with long, flexible spines and refused to shake off easily.

Well, having cleaned herself up the best she could, she looked around. The snowdrift turned out to be a hill on the shore of a frozen lake. All the way to the horizon – just ice and snow, and nothing but ice and snow.

After wandering a bit across the lake, she found a round patch of clean, blue ice, stepped onto the spot, and sat right down on the sparkling surface. The ice and snow were equally warm. That was good – at least there would be no winter eczema.

She took out her map. Before unfolding it fully, she made a wish: if the image had a lot of confusing labels and symbols, she'd have to stay in the Snow World longer than expected, but if everything was clear at first glance and the names were readable in any ordinary language, the journey would be short and not too complicated.

But the picture exceeded all her expectations, the map was white from corner to corner, and had nothing but a bright red square in the very center. And the word “ENTRANCE” printed beside it in large, clear letters. Just like on a tram door. And below the scarlet square, two centimeters away, was the drawing of a familiar stick figure in a skirt. Nothing else.

Rina even stood up, trying to spot this ENTRANCE, since it was supposed to be so close. But around her was just a white, undulating plain, covered in thick, light snow, and a dull gray sky above, through whose rare gaps a playful lemon-yellow sun tried to shine. No red in sight – not a drop of carmine or burgundy.

“Hee-hee-hee!” giggled a childish voice behind her.

“Oh!” Rina flinched. “Who's there?”

But there was no one around.

“It's me!” came a reply from below.

And then he appeared. A bear! That was the first impression. Rina's heart stopped. Bears! Help!

But very soon her heart started again, beating peacefully and evenly. The bear – or rather, the bear cub – was purple, like a forest hyacinth, fairly small, about the size of a well-fed cat, and with a wide, ear-to-ear grin. He stuck his upper half out of a snow-dusted ice hole five steps away and asked:

“Taking a break?”

“A break”. She nodded.

“Picnic?”

“Picnic”.

“And where's the water?” The bear sniffed the air.

“There isn't any”. Rina shook her head and gave a small sniff herself.

“Then it's not a picnic”, the bear stated matter-of-factly, and with a grunt, climbed out of the hole. Standing on his hind legs, he looked a bit like a tailless raccoon who had put on quite a bit of weight. He came up to Rina's waist or an inch lower.

“If you want, I can help – I know how. Want me to?”

“I do”, Rina said, deciding not to contradict the bear for now.

“Because what kind of picnic is it without water?” the bear murmured, scooping snow with his paws and building a neat little pyramid. He turned to Rina and said, “Don't just sit there – work! We'll rest after, once we get the water”.

“Okay. What should I do?”

“Here, collect a bunch of snow and take it to that little hill, see?” He pointed at a snow bump about fifteen meters away.

“I see”. She nodded, ladling up an armful of snow, and headed toward the bump.

But she didn't make it, after a few steps, she suddenly fell knee-deep into an icy pit, dusted over with light powder. It hurt badly, and she nearly twisted her ankle.

“Hee-hee! Hee-hee! Hee-hee-hee!” the bear cub squealed with happiness.

Rina climbed out of the hole, walked over to him, and looked him in the eye.

“You did that? You knew there was a pit there?”

“Yes! I kneuuu!” the bear shouted through his laughter. “Wasn't. It. Funny?”

“Hilarious”, Rina said, circling him and inspecting him closely. Then she asked, “Listen, maybe you're sick?”

The bear stiffened. His grin dropped. His whiskers – black needles – bristled.

“You noticed it too?” he whispered in fear.

“Noticed what?”

“I'm sick. I'm dying. I've got four and a half days left to live. I'm horribly ill. No cure”, the bear moaned, and as if truly weakened by his terrible condition, he collapsed onto the ice with sighs and groans.

“What's wrong with you?” she asked, alarmed.

“Freeezing”, the poor bear whined feebly, in a thin voice. “Heaaelp me”.

But how could she help a freezing creature on a distant planet, where she herself had arrived just fifteen minutes ago? She pulled off her backpack, took out a denim jacket. The bear watched her actions attentively but he didn't seem to understand the purpose.

“Raaan”, he moaned, sobbing.

“What?” Rina didn't catch the word.

“Raaan erraaaund me, pleeez”, the bear whimpered even more plaintively.

“Why?”

The cub sat up swiftly, his face stern and serious, and asked:

“I'm freezing – don't you see?!”

Rina replied, “Well, okay”, and began jogging in place.

“Faster! Faster! I'm freezing!” the bear commanded briskly.

Rina ran faster. But soon she felt the chill herself, her cheeks stung with frost, her fingers burned with the icy wind. She stopped and, with numb hands, put on her jacket.

“What's wrong with you?” the bear asked impatiently.

“It's really cold”, she said, trying not to shiver.

“And you think I'm not cold? I'm cold and sick! Help an invalid!”

Rina looked at him and said:

“You know what? I think you're feeling better already. And if you really want to warm up – go run around yourself!”

“Ohhh, so that's how best friends act these days!” the bear huffed. He rose to his feet, put his paws behind his back, and began pacing. “So that's what the new generation has been taught! Look where we've ended up – you won't even help an old, unhealthy man!”

“Man?” Rina asked, surprised.

“That's how it is – you live, you work, but the moment you're unwell, everyone around forgets about you for good. They won't even run for you a little. I'm too old to treat myself at my age, you see? I'm already eleven!”

“Eleven?”

“Yes! You heard me. E-le-ven!” the bear repeated, sounding it out.

Rina stayed silent.

“Freezing!” the bear declared grumpily.

“Suit yourself, but I'm not running anymore”, she said. “And if you tell me how to find the Entrance to the Seventh World, I'll lend you my jacket. Just for now, so you don't freeze”. She took off her jacket and laid it on the ice like bait.

The bear glanced at the jacket and wrinkled his nose.

“No, thanks. Better make me a nice snowball, I'm kind of hungry today”.

Rina looked intently at the bear, but didn't argue.

“A snowball's fine, that's okay”, she thought, and reached out to scoop up some snow from the ice.

“What are you doing?!” the bear howled. “Do you really think I'm going to eat that dirty, trampled snow? Who do you take me for?”

“Sorry”, Rina stammered. “I... I didn't know”.

“She didn't know! What do you know, anyway? Where were you raised?”

Rina didn't reply.

“Go get some that's cleaner”, the bear cub said gruffly, though as if calming down. “There's a path over there, see? My tracks. I always get snow on the left, remember, on! the! left!”

Rina nodded and set off down the narrow path trodden by the bear's hairy paws.

“Farther, farther!” the bear kept barking.

“What a pain!” she thought irritably, took another step and... fell waist-deep into a big hole filled with fluffy snow.

“Hee-he-he-he-he! Hee...eeh! Hee...eeh!” the bear's laughter broke into wheezes of sheer joy.

Of course, he'd hoped that sooner or later the stranger would fall into his second pit, but he'd never imagined that it would be this delightful. A wonderful sight!

Rina, too, felt clearly that it was wonderful. At first she was sure she'd broken her ankle – a raging pain shot through her whole body.

But once she managed to climb out of the trap, she realized her bones were intact, though her leg was badly scraped by a sharp ledge of ice.

“That's it!” she screamed. “Now you're in for it! You're gonna get it!”

“He! Hee-he-he!” the bear laughed, sidling closer to his den.

Rina quickly brushed off her sundress and, limping, ran towards the bear. But he was experienced and had everything planned out exactly, and after waiting just long enough to giggle at her limp, he dived into his icy hole and disappeared.

Rina ran up to the den and shouted into the darkness:

“If I see you again, you'll get it! You hear?”

“Hee-he! Hee-he-he!” the bear cub's laughter echoed from somewhere down there. Obviously the lake was frozen solid to the bottom, and the burrow could be very deep. It seemed impossible to reach the offender, anyway.

Rina sighed, returned to her backpack and immediately discovered that her jacket was missing. She searched the snow, even cleared the spot where the bear had just lain, but found no trace of her denim.

“He stole it!” she said in complete astonishment.

She approached the hole, leaned in and yelled:

“Thief! You little jerk! Give me back my clothes!”

No reply – absolute silence. Not even an echo from the deep, as if it had hidden away with its owner.

“What a creep!” Rina fumed, peering into the blackness. “First he nearly breaks my leg, then he robs me! What a terrible country! Where have I ended up?”

Not really aware of what she was doing, Rina took out the map again, unfolded it, and saw a neatly drawn gray circle under the ENTRANCE, with the little figure in a skirt shifted about five centimeters lower.

“The map's scale is changing”, she realized, and folding the paper, she concluded that she'd probably never get the jacket back. She sighed, fretted for a while, but finally talked herself into accepting the loss.

Then she put on her backpack, and walked away from the icy lake – away from that mean, nasty, sly little bear cub. And in the snow in front of his den she wrote five big, square letters with her finger – T H I E F – with a big period below:

●

28 – PURPLE JOKERS
part two

The sun was covered by heavy snow-laden clouds. Everywhere she looked – a flat lowland with a few stumpy hills. But which way to go?

“Doesn't matter for now”, she thought. “It's not cold, the weather's good. Well, tolerable in any case. I'll meet someone on the way and ask him. Simple. The only L is the snow's too deep”.

After walking about five hundred meters, she stopped and pulled out the map again. This time, nothing had changed.

“Good, but what does this gray circle mean?” she wondered. “A well? Or maybe a mountain? Or perhaps a village. I should have asked the bear about the local villages. Or towns. Or cities”.

“Bear City”. Rina was surprised by her own idea, and at once pictured rows of white hills dotted with dens and holes, and in the entrances – smiling purple bears.

“And I'm still going to snitch on that little thief. Let them send a cop, let them deal with it officially. You can't just let a criminal get away with it”, she said aloud, picking her way between the mounds.

“Hee-he-he!” a giggle rang out from somewhere very close.

“Ah, there you go!” exclaimed Rina, spotting a purple bear peeking out from behind a little snowdrift.

“Me? Go? Where am I going?” The bear looked around, totally puzzled.

Rina noticed that his ears were way too long, like they'd been stretched in opposite directions for a day or two.

“That's not him”, she realized. “This one's already had his ears yanked”.

“Yes, you! Who gave you permission to let out a giggle?” she asked menacingly, advancing on the bear.

He looked at his paws in utter astonishment.

“I let out? Out of what? When?”

“Oh, you're so dumb, my friend”. Rina waved her hand at him hopelessly and sat down on an icy block. “Listen, just tell me this – is there a town or at least a village anywhere around here? Or do you all live alone, like gophers?”

“Gou furs?!” The bear's eyes nearly popping out. “What gou furs?”

“Never mind. So, is there a town or not? Spit it out!” Rina didn't mince words anymore – who knows what these purple ones were capable of, maybe they were just crooks by nature, rotten to the core, like Zinaida Abramovna says.

“The town's that way”. His muzzle still blank with surprise, the bear extended his arm to the left. “If you go along the road, it's half a day's walk”.

“Along the road?” Rina brightened. “And where's the road?”

The bear's eyes bulged even more and he pointed at the ground with his nose.

“Here it is! Just a little covered by snow”.

“A little covered?! It's completely buried!” Rina was upset.

“And what do you need the town for?” the bear asked. “Don't you know what happened there yesterday?”

“What happened yesterday?”

“Oh my! Really?!” The bear raised both paws in horror. “You really don't know what disaster happened yesterday morning?”

“No, I don't”.

“Poor, wretched, miserable you! Woe to you! Woe to me! Woe to all of us! It's a tragedy! We're all doomed!”

“Stop the drama! Just tell me what's going on”.

“How can I stop it? You're here and you know nothing! Oh, I can't take it, I can't live through this, I'm going to die very soon, this is too much to bear! ... I've got four and a half days left, no more”, he added seriously.

“How many days?” Rina inquired.

“Four and a half... what?”

“Nothing. And now you're upset and I'm supposed to make you a clean, fresh snowball so you can feel better, right?”

“Raa... right”, the bear forced out, stunned. “And... and you, e-excuse me... h-how do you know... all this and...”

“Oh, we know!” Rina cut him off. “We know what you're up to, don't we?”

The bear cast a wary glance at his den and began a slow, cautious retreat. Rina noticed his maneuver, smirked and asked:

“So are you all such liars in your country, or only the ones living on this lake?”

“Why liars?” the bear asked in a worried tone, desperately measuring the distance to his burrow and cursing himself for daring to wander so far from home. “What liars?”

“The real kind!”

“We're not liars”.

“Then what are you?” Rina shook her head.

“Not liars. We're jokers”.

“Unbelievable! Jokers! I literally almost broke both my legs!”

“Your legs?” the bear stopped and looked at Rina's legs with interest. “Did you break them? Show me!”

“I said almost! Almost broke them! In your pit!”

“In mine?” the bear perked up.

“How many pits do you have?”

“Four. And I'm going to dig another one for New Year's! Or two!”

“Are you actually insane?” Rina twirled her finger at her temple. The bear tried to twirl his right paw near his ear too, but it turned out clumsily.

“Why insane? I'm not insane. I'm a joker, I said. Look, you fell into someone's pit, right? And then somebody else might fall in that same pit too – can you imagine? It's a celebration! Real fun!”

“Have you ever fallen into a pit yourself?”

“Me?!” The bear beamed with a wide smile, his paws up. “Of course I have! A hundred of hundreds of times!”

“And did it hurt?”

“You bet! Very much! Terribly! I fall in and tell my neighbors, and they tell everyone else. My neighbor back there – he eavesdrops – he fell into six pits in one morning a week ago! First five, then the sixth one. In one single morning! Can you imagine? No, you can't! It's amazing! I've never been that lucky”.

“They're all nuts!” Rina thought, indignantly.

If it weren't for the pain in her shin and the stolen jacket, she might have taken the pits more lightly. But jokes aside, the shin hurts, and the jacket is gone!

“All right”, she said tiredly, wanting to get rid of the silly pit-digger as quickly as possible. “Still, how do I get to the nearest city?”

“Straight ahead, just straight and nothing but straight! Basically, don't turn anywhere”, the now fully emboldened bear replied cheerfully.

Rina raised her hand to her forehead, peering into the distance.

“But there's nothing there, just emptiness”.

“Why emptiness?” the bear looked bewildered. “That's not emptiness! That's the Gray Snows! The capital of the Far-East Region!”

“Gray?”

“Gray as a cloud!” The happy purple bear even bounced a bit.

“Now I understand”, Rina said quietly.

“Understand what? About the pits?” he asked anxiously.

“Sure thing, especially about your pits, right. Well, now you go straight ahead and don't turn anywhere”.

“Don't turn anywhere?” the bear clarified.

“Don't turn anywhere. Until you fall into your own trap”.

“Oh no, no, let me explain, madam!” Assuming a ridiculously grave expression, the bear tried speaking in a formal tone. “All my personal excavations are over there, madam, you see? To the left of the road, over there farther away. It's an obligation. Nobody digs anything on the road. No, no, that's not allowed! You get punished for that. And severely, madam”.

A few minutes later, Rina continued on her way. She never managed to find out from the bear how he distinguished the road from the surrounding wasteland.

“By smell, probably”, she decided.

Walking became harder, not only was the road covered with snow, but now there was the added fear of accidentally stepping off the path and falling into a deep trap with sharp edges. Moreover, the faster she walked, the more she was freezing – she had to constantly look for a suitable slab of ice or at least a hard snowdrift to sit on and stay still for a minute to warm up somehow. She had long realized that staying motionless made her warmer, and snow in her hands, if pressed well, heated up by itself and after a while became almost hot.

Fortunately, there were no bears on the road.

“Of course! They're not interested here”, Rina thought with a grin. “Digging pits is not allowed”.

Soon she approached a large gray spot on the snow. The blot was about twelve yards in diameter and looked as if someone had carefully sprinkled coal dust all over the area through a sieve. Picking up some snow, she saw that it was indeed dirty, but not with coal – it seemed to be mixed with some sort of cinder. Or ash. Such dusty spots gradually became more frequent until they finally merged into one endless surface – the entire snowy valley was smeared with this dreary grayness stretching far and wide to the very edge of the earth...

A noisy crowd of purple bears was walking towards her on the road. They were all eagerly discussing something very amusing, probably someone's “wonderful” fall into a deep pit. Rina stopped. The bears passed by her, barely glancing.

“Very suspicious! Why aren't they surprised?” She couldn't fully grasp the scene. “I'm a human, there are no people here, only bears. If a talking bear suddenly appeared in our yard, probably half of Kyiv would come to see it. All the TV stations would be there, and all the newspapers, and most popular bloggers too. But here – no one! I don't understand. A never-before-seen something, an overseas monster – that's me – approaches them, and they pass by as if it's normal. I don't get it!”

However, she wasn't in a hurry to figure out the strange behavior of the locals. On one hand, in distant worlds she was used to mysteries revealing themselves automatically, as she said, and at the same time, she didn't forget the main thing – at night, in her dream, Herx would appear and explain everything, putting it all in order. More than the bears, she was interested in breakfast. She thought – or rather, she was already completely sure – that all those sweets and fairy-tale delicacies she was so generously treated to in the Butterfly World were a mirage, the same ephemeral illusion as their fake butterfly universe.

In short, she desperately wanted to chew on something.

“Anything edible”.

At least, that was how she put it to herself. She had high hopes that upon reaching the bear capital, she could trade for food there – let it be anything local, you know, national, something unusual, not necessarily delicious, doesn't matter, let it be just vaguely resembling human nourishment – she wanted food, some food, anything at all.

A couple more hours passed, and her hunger intensified so much that she was ready to part with almost any of her treasures for a small cheese sandwich – for example, her tassel pen or an empty plastic bag. Maybe even the iron dollar! Judging by what she had already seen in the Snow World, the cheerful purples had nothing, absolutely no personal property, except their silly pits and icy burrows, so alien things from another planet could be of significant value to them.

But although Rina had been trudging through the trampled dirty snow for almost half the morning, she hadn't noticed any signs of what people back home would call ‘a city'. And if there was no city, then let it be at least a settlement, even the smallest one – a village, or a farmstead, or even just a solitary hut!

Ahead and to the sides – dirty snow. That's all. Partly comforting was the fact that the road had finally become visible because of thousands of bear paw prints – you couldn't fall into a pit by the roadside anymore, and that was something.

The farther she walked, the more bears she encountered. And they all moved in crowds, groups of ten, twenty, sometimes even forty or fifty bears. Never alone! But no matter how many bears there were, she remained outside their interest. The bears would occasionally glance at her but their simple faces would immediately go blank and they would return to their endless conversation, constantly interrupted by peals of laughter.

Rina looked at the laughing bears and suddenly remembered how her friend Zlata Sorochenko once said that a cheerful person couldn't be unkind.

“Oh, they sure can!” she thought. “Look how much fun they're having! Exploding with happiness! And who digs the pits? Who stole the jacket? They did! They dig, they steal!”

Soon she approached a group resting on the roadside. The bears lay on clumps of dark snow, just snorting and staring at the sky. Rina slowed her pace.

“Yeah, these ones, I'm sure, they don't know anything about the Entrance”, she thought with some tired disdain. “Their muzzles are too dull”.

Anyway, she had no choice – no one to ask but the bears.

“Better find out something about the city...”

“Excuse me”, Rina addressed a fat, big-nosed, dark-purple bear, who, with his empty glassy eyes, was watching a cloud hanging right above him in the sky. “Could you tell me, is the city far from here?”

“Whaaat city?” the bear drawled lazily, not taking his eyes off the plump cloud.

“The capital of the gray region”.

The bear started to blink rapidly and then reluctantly turned onto his side. Groaning and moaning, but without looking at Rina, he grumbled like an old man and, with a show of annoyance, got on all fours, then looked into the distance along the road with his half-closed, foggy eyes.

“If you go that way”, he waved his paw sluggishly, “you'll come back and arrive by evening”, he mumbled with a hopeless sigh.

“I don't understand”, Rina admitted. “Why go away and then come back?”

“She doesn't understand!” the bear suddenly exclaimed.

The others seemed to have been waiting for this phrase all day. The bears jumped to their feet and began playfully nudging each other with their shoulders. Everyone shouted, clamored, the noise was incredible.

“Is it already a celebration? Is it a celebration or not yet?” screamed the nearest bear, bouncing like an overinflated ball.

Rina waited for a long time, but the excitement didn't die down. There was nothing she could do, so she walked away from them with brisk steps.

“Woof! Woof! Woof!” the bears barked in chorus.

Rina turned around, surprised, all the bears were hopping in unison, barking almost like dogs, their voices hoarse with effort:

“Woof! Woof! Woof!”

But just a minute later, they suddenly collapsed in the snow and, as if nothing had happened, resumed their intense observation of the cloud, completely forgetting about Rina and her silly question about the city...

All the bears she met looked alike – if not like twins, then at least like brothers. She also noticed that there weren't any real cubs around, not a single young bear. She no longer tried asking about the capital – it was useless, they'd only reply with some nonsense anyway. Besides, many of the bears were singing, or rather howling out the sound “u” or “yu”, chanting it over four notes. The whole scene looked as if the bears were returning from an important football match where both teams had played to a draw (Rina once had witnessed such an “incident” in Kyiv with her own eyes and was left with a very vivid impression).

After walking another mile, she again found herself in a wasteland. This time, the ground was covered with almost white snow, lightly dusted with powder, and only five or six lonely chains of tracks stretched along the road.

“How can this be?” she stammered in confusion, stopping in considerable dismay. “Where did they all go?”

A little later, after a quarter hour of uncertain walking, it became clear – the Gray Capital was already behind her. The road had completely disappeared, dissolving into endless snowy expanses without the slightest landmark. She sat down on a warm hummock of ice, rested a bit, but came up with nothing. So Rina decided to turn back and thoroughly question the first bear she came across.

“But let him just try to giggle or bark!” she sternly grumbled, threatening her hypothetical conversation partner. “I'll shake the whole truth out of him! And don't let him even think about lying to me about anything!”

29 – NYUPPY PLAYERS
part one

And then a ‘proper interlocutor' appeared. The beast's final lands are the hunter's hands. A dark-lilac bear emerged from the ground twenty meters away, paying no attention to the stranger, and leisurely waddled off toward the “valley of dirty snow”, as Rina had dubbed that place for the sake of ‘easy navigation'. But the bear didn't even take thirty steps. Rina caught up to him, grabbed him by the shoulder, and with one sharp jerk spun him around face to face. That is, muzzle to face.

The bear looked as if he had spent half his life dreaming of such rough handling. Not a single muscle twitched on his broad snout. The lilac joker was imperturbably cool-headed. He stood, gazing off somewhere into the distance with half-closed eyes, clearly bored.

“Excuse me”, she said through her teeth, trying to control her temper. “Would you happen to know, purely by chance, yes, exactly, by chance, how I can get to the city, to the capital of the Gray Region?”

“No idea”, the bear yawned, lifting his apathetic mug and staring at the empty sky, splotchy with dark chubby clouds that were moving in from the southern edge.

“But you've heard...? Do you even know...? Does such a capital exist or not?”

“Exists”. The bear gaped even wider, raised a paw, and began studying his small, sharp claws very intently.

“And where is it located, this capital of yours?”

“Here”. The bear was practically falling asleep with boredom.

Rina froze, stunned by such unheard-of impudence – first he said he didn't know, and now he declared that it was here!

“Where here?! Right here?! On the road itself?!”

“This is no road”, the bear corrected, lowering his eyelids into a bottomless half-sleep. “This is the Straight Boulevaaa…” He could no longer muster the strength to pronounce the ‘rd' sound.

And then Rina finally understood everything: the valley of dirty snow was the Gray Capital! The bears lived in burrows, in icy dens on either side of the road, so there were no buildings at all – only the Boulevard and dozens of trampled trails running from both flanks, and that was it. Such a capital!

The light-violet bear stood for a minute, eyes still closed, then carefully walked around Rina and shuffled off about his business. She watched him go and thought. And there was plenty to think about.

After mulling it over, Rina steeled herself with determination and resolve, firmly deciding to return to the land of dirty snows and happy bears. But there was a problem, her hunger was growing inexorably, and dreams of food took over her mind, driving out all fears, emotions, and even her worries about the as-yet-unknown direction to the longed-for Exit.

The way back always feels shorter than the route forward. Apparently, this rule applies in all worlds. Rina didn't even notice how she was once again among the lively huddles. The happy bears were strolling along the Boulevard, amusing themselves with chatter and never-ending jokes. This time she didn't hurry, she sat on a chunk of ice at the roadside – fatigue was winning out – and watched the behavior of different bears in their groups.

It was obvious that many of the purple jokesters had been wandering and loitering along the street since early morning, long since having discussed everything interesting or at least remotely funny, and now, silently smiling, they wandered back and forth, giggling from time to time at someone's remembered escapade. Not far from Rina, a large crowd of bears broke up into several clusters – it looked very much like the thoroughly tired friends were heading home.

As Rina expected, the weary violet ones left the road and trudged barefoot along the slippery trails, lightly dusted with thin snow and gray powder. The tracks ran deep into the valley.

She stood up and walked down one of these paths, not knowing where she was going. Like a scouting mission, perhaps. But after moving several hundred feet away from the Boulevard, she was suddenly caught in a wild snow barrage, from all sides, a hail of snowballs and clumps of dirty snow came flying at her, she barely had time to shield her face. At first, she was frightened, but very soon she became furious and, like a wounded tigress, she pounced on the three nearest cheerful bears, knocking them to the ground with one swipe.

“What are you doing?!” she shouted at the top of her voice.

All three bears cowered in terror, pressing themselves to the ice, their ears trembling. The others stopped, motionless like plush toys, instantly forgetting the snowballs – it was time to run for it.

“We... we're playing... that's all...” managed the bravest of them, crawling out of his hiding spot.

“Playing?!” Rina turned on him so sharply that he immediately plopped down on the snow. “Playing, huh?! And did I say I wanted to play with you? Did you ask me? Did I ever tell anyone I wanted to play or something? Look at what you've done to my sundress – it's covered in spots!”

The bears blinked their eyelashes guiltily, though they clearly didn't understand what spots she was talking about.

“At least if the snow were clean”, she said, softening a little, “but how can you throw such filth?”

“The clean snow is outside the city, far away, and first we have to practice and get ready”, another brave bear answered.

“Get ready for what?” Still irritated, Rina didn't get it.

“For the game! We're playing with the Muals!”

“Muals! Muals!” the bears cheered.

“Come with us”, offered a young-looking bear who was sitting on the ice next to the trail. “It'll be fun. We'll win. And then we'll have a festive lunch”.

“Lunch?” Rina repeated quietly, feeling a horrible wave of nauseating weakness rolling from somewhere in her knees up to her throat...

To get to the “stadium”, that is, to the large flat spot where the bears usually played the game they called nyuppi with Muals – their version of snowball wars – they had to trudge a good three miles. Luckily, Rina didn't once fall into any pit, though many careless bears tumbled into deep traps up to their ears, causing their companions to erupt in boisterous laughter, so every time she had to wait until the bears had laughed themselves out.

The purple beasts treated Rina as if she were their old friend. No one asked her what country she had flown in from, where she was going, what she did, or even who she actually was. It seemed they were only interested in pranks, chatter about pranks, all sorts of pits, of course, and also snowballs. The violet brotherhood advanced swiftly into the valley in a blustery, noisy gang, shoving, guffawing, and constantly either starting a skirmish, using ice and dirty snow, or falling into playful, innocent tussles, but overall they moved pretty fast.

Thinking of the food, Rina kept urging them on, though honestly, she could barely keep up. She was in a great hurry and marveled at herself, where was she getting the strength for such a pace?

Clearly, if not for the invitation to have lunch after the game, she wouldn't have managed even half the distance, but the promised table full of all sorts of treats, like a huge magnet, pulled her forward ever more strongly with every step. Of course, she justified her risky adventure with the bears by telling herself that at the stadium, amid the throng of fans, she'd easily find some clever big-nosed fellow who knew the local geography well enough. But in reality, it was all about the lunch. It's amazing how unrecognizably a person's behavior, thoughts, and even intimate beliefs can change when he is just hungry.

At the clearing, the bears had long been expected. It turned out that many of the bears in the group Rina came with were privileged fans – without them, such matches weren't held, and if they hadn't shown up after a while, according to stadium rules, the game would have been canceled, and their team would be recorded as having lost. So it happened that no one else but Rina, who had been continuously hurrying the bears along, had ensured the game would occur.

At the stadium, she settled down on a more or less clean rise and looked around for the opposing team, hoping to see the Muals, mysterious and maybe even alien creatures, but apart from the violet bears, there was no one else on the field.

“And where are the Muals?” she asked the group lounging next to her.

“Over there!” said the nearest bear, pointing his paw to the left edge of the field. There, two dozen ordinary bears were milling around.

Rina looked at them, completely at a loss.

“And where's our team?”

“On the right”, the bear answered shortly, sizing up the foolish foreigner with his gaze and turning back to his buddies.

Spectators were approaching from all sides, silently settling themselves on the snowdrifts as comfortably as they could. The bear “stadium” was a rather strange construction, a huge crowd of quiet bears encircling a dirty patch in an uneven ring about thirty meters in diameter. On the patch, four dozen violet bears swarmed around in pointless anticipation.

Rina thought that maybe they were all waiting for some important person, an ambassador from the neighboring Mual country, for instance. In a way, she was right – the public and both the teams really were waiting, though not for an ambassador, but for the delivery of sporting equipment.

A murmur of excitement rose up, the crowd of fans parted, and in rolled what looked at first glance like a big sled. In fact, twenty burly bears were pushing a heavy, flat block of ice on which rose an imposing pyramid of hundreds of gray snowballs. The pushers shoved the ice slab into the center of the field and quickly retreated and merged back into the masses of onlookers, and at that very moment, the game finally began!

Both teams lunged for snowballs, and having barely grabbed a few crudely molded, ice-hardened spheres, the players immediately began hurling them with all their might, aiming for the opponents' heads. However, since the bears had neither uniforms nor signs, symbols, or anything, each player tried to smack everyone else – they just pelted each other. Who won and who lost could be sorted out after the match. For now, the players were interested solely in hitting bears in the face – no matter whose.

The crowd erupted in wild screams, the bears leaped into the air to considerable heights and mercilessly shoved each other, also trying to step on the neighbors' paws or “accidentally” scratch those nearby with their claws.

Rina got her share too, but she was nearly three times taller and six times stronger, so her kicks sent the bears scattering like bowling pins, and soon she stood in the center of a small empty circle. The bears rubbed their bruised sides, glanced fearfully at the stranger, and now tried to keep their distance from her. However, after a couple of moments, they became completely oblivious to the upsetting minor mishaps and, screaming at the top of their lungs, shouted even louder, supporting their team with mad patriotic roars.

The players themselves had warmed up so much that – feverishly – they attacked the rows of spectators too, brutally smashing stone-hard snowballs into their ecstatic muzzles. The spectators kept falling, but their enthusiasm remained undiminished.

Suddenly, everything paused as if on cue. It became apparent that the referee (surprisingly, there was a referee!) had suddenly declared an unscheduled break after receiving a double snowball to the eye from one of the side midfielders. The bears, both players and fans, simultaneously collapsed into the trampled snowdrifts. Worse still, many of the fans were diligently piling on each other, not caring at all about those who ended up at the very bottom. And those lucky enough to be on top, having turned onto their backs, stared blissfully at the sky, exhaling thick clouds of steam from their brown rubbery nostrils.

After a few minutes of the annoying interruption, the game's entire chaos resumed with triple strength. However, very soon, the last surviving snowball flew into someone's forehead, and without the referee's whistle, the game ended automatically. What's more, no one waited for the announcement of results, the crowd immediately started dispersing in all directions, still “accidentally” stepping on the furry paws of their fellow sports fans. Rina realized that everyone already knew (everyone except her, of course) who had won and who had lost today. And so it was – the Muals were the winners.

At that moment, a plump bear cub ran up to Rina and cheerfully shouted:

“No worries! The day after tomorrow, we'll beat them into the ground! They'll remember it for the whole year! We've got a new tactic! Everyone says so! Right?”

“Right”, Rina agreed, just in case.

“So, what did you decide? Are you coming with us?” the bear cub asked, his little eyes sparkling. The surrounding bears stopped and were listening. Rina noticed this and clarified in a whisper:

“Where are we going?”

“What do you mean, where?” the bear whispered back, looking around. “To lunch!”

“Yes! Yes! Lunch! I'm coming! Sure, I'm coming!” Rina shouted and clapped her hands. The bears froze with their mouths open.

The familiar noisy company was all present. Rina was at its very heart. Somehow, without any prior agreement, the bears decided to postpone discussing the lost match until a more appropriate time and instead focused on their jester (as Rina thought of him), that is, the bear, significantly larger than the others, with long flapping ears. But it wasn't his ears that amused his companions – it was his paws: the bear was extremely crooked-footed, constantly tripping over protrusions and bumps on the path, barely maintaining balance, and swaying desperately.

In moments of particularly funny wobbling, one of the nearby bears would jump up and push the poor fellow in the side with all his strength. Even if the “clown” managed to stay on his feet, the push itself and the subsequent flailing of his paws caused his companions to burst into uncontrollable laughter, so much so that some simply fell onto the ice, unable to cope with the hilarity, which only further incited the laughing crowd...

Eventually, the bears led the exhausted, totally worn-out Rina to the foot of a hill. There was no path visible ahead, the hill was generously covered with pristine, dazzling snow.

“Oh, I'm so hungry!” loudly declared a skinny bear cub who had been leading the procession for the past few minutes.

His words acted like a detonator – the bears dashed off, yelling and whooping, squealing as they rushed up the hill. Rina followed them. Upon reaching the slopes, the bears dropped onto the snow, and greedily scooping it up with both paws, began to eat it, smacking, chomping, and clicking their toothy jaws.

Rina was stunned by this sight to the point of delirium. Like a museum statue, she stood immovable amidst the feasting animals, unable to believe her eyes. The bears were gorging themselves... on snow!

At first, there was hope that the local snow was not entirely regular and that it might possess at least some slight nutritional properties. But after grabbing a handful and tasting it, Rina confirmed – it was just snow, plain water, the simplest water frozen high in the sky and turned into utterly useless, worthless, completely calorie-free snowflakes.

All day long Rina awaited real food. With all her heart she longed for fragrant hot pieces of something and steaming aromatic slices of something. She dreamed of food so much for so many hours that she existed solely because of this sacred faith. Her body listened to her mind and agreed to wait. It tapped into all its last conceivable and inconceivable emergency reserves. But now the poor body was deceived in the most disgusting, most treacherous way.

Finally, her deeply offended, disappointed flesh refused to obey its owner.

That's it.

Out!

Complete kaput!

Rina's head spun, darkness clouded her eyes, a drumming thunder filled her ears as if mallets were pounding on empty barrels. She took a deep breath, tried to grab onto anything for support, blacked out, and... collapsed.

Naturally, the bears noticed nothing. They had no time for her. They were blissfully rubbing their tummies on the ice, savoring the utterly delicious lunch – chewing empty snow and gradually reaching the highest satisfaction. Twelve minutes later, their movements began to slow, eyelids grew heavier, and soon all the purple jokers, one by one, started to fall into a wonderful, indescribably pleasant full-belly sleep.

Rina lay in a coma at the spot where she had dropped on the snow – face down, hardly breathing. The closest bear – he hadn't fully dozed off yet – looked at her sleepily, mumbled something, but receiving no response, he waved it off and turned away. Ten minutes later, the sky above the hill and the valley around filled with the snores, sighs, whistles, and mutterings of peacefully sleeping, infinitely satisfied purple easy-goers...

29 – NYUPPY PLAYERS
part two

Rina opened her eyes. The black haze that had covered her thoughts now surrounded her from all sides. Emptiness and silence. If not for the aching knees and the stifling hot air, she would have been sure that her body was gone. But no, the body hadn't disappeared, on the contrary, it persistently demanded oxygen. Which had vanished somehow. Had it flown into space? Rina tried to take a deep breath but coughed, instead of air, snowflakes streamed into her throat. She wanted to lift her hand. Couldn't. A leaden weight pressed down on her like a concrete wall.

“It's snow”, she guessed. “I've been buried by snow”.

Her obliging memory instantly painted Norway, the town of Trysil – images from her distant childhood floated before her eyes, snow everywhere, Dad at the top, next to him the little rosy-cheeked Nina in an orange helmet waving her arms and shouting something from above. Suddenly, a rumble, the ground shakes, Rina looks in horror at the distant slope and sees a whole sea of snow collapsing and rushing into the abyss at terrifying speed, crashing into rocks and ledges.

“An avalanche...”

But do avalanches happen on plains? Avalanches on hills? Yes, it's a hill, but only about two dozen meters high, if not less. Nonsense, it's not an avalanche. Then what?

Meanwhile, there wasn't enough air for normal breathing. Rina gasped for it with her mouth, but what filled her lungs didn't satisfy them at all. Large drops of sweat appeared on her forehead, burning waves ran down her body. Fear was growing, the instinct of self-preservation took control with its tough, sinewy hands. Rina pulled herself up and, with a strong movement, yanked her numb leg from under the snow's pressure.

Fortunately, the snow was dry, crumbly, easy to dig through. However, she had to stop often – lack of air made her face numb, a loud ringing filled her ears, and her heart pounded wildly, threatening to leap out through her mouth. Nevertheless, after each pause, Rina managed to crawl at least two feet forward.

The snow wall was endless, and when the tension had reached its limit, when the air, devoid of oxygen, became a sheer illusion, she suddenly broke free! She stuck her head out and inhaled the clean air so forcefully that her shoulders ached.

A starless, quiet night hung over the World of Ice. Massive clouds sealed the sky, and only from a distant edge, somewhere from the very corner, timidly emerged the first golden ray of the yet invisible moon. Perfect, untouched, untrodden matte snow lay in a thick fluffy blanket from horizon to horizon.

“But what has happened here?” Rina asked herself, quickly regaining her senses and finally having breathed enough. “Was it really such a terrible snowfall? No, there was much more snow on me than around. It almost crushed my back! And where are all the bears? Why did they abandon me?”

Rina was puzzled, but her questions were surprisingly close to the correct answers. It was indeed about the unusual snowfall, but mostly about the bears.

The picture, in general, was simple and easily explainable in full accordance with the reckless customs of this country, the bears had been lounging for a long time, sleeping off their hearty lunch, and when they woke up, they were wildly delighted that fresh snow was falling from the sky.

Truly, they went mad with boundless happiness! In rapturous frenzy, the bears dashed around the neighborhood, catching the large, sweet (from their point of view) snowflakes on their tongues. After running and jumping in the new fragrant snowdrifts and indulging in their bearish gastronomic amusements, the purple ones finally noticed the foreign girl lying on the ground, and the same “wonderful” idea was born in their empty heads – to prank her!

The bears, without much thought – never thinking at all – dropped to all fours and, like little bulldozers, puffing and giggling, began to shovel snow from the entire hill directly onto Rina. The higher the pile, the funnier the joke! Under such an unspoken slogan, the work progressed easily, so that very soon a pack of joyful blockheads constructed a huge pyramid over the poor girl. They even tamped it down as best they could. Wonderful! Could there be a better prank?

Then they left. Needless to say, none of them cared in the slightest about what would happen to the foreigner or whether she would survive. Each imagined her awakening – the moment she opens her eyes, crawls out in deep shock, and laughs cheerfully, admiring the brilliant sense of humor of the local population.

But Rina was not in the mood for laughter – a little later she discovered that she was left without her backpack. At first, she couldn't believe it, running around the hill.

“It can't be! It can't be!” she repeated in panic, digging through every snow pile.

Then a reassuring thought occurred to her: most likely, the backpack had simply fallen off her shoulders, just slipped off somehow, apparently when she was working inside the pyramid, trying to break free. She had to climb back in. And there, in complete darkness, she carefully felt the surface of the hill inch by inch, but found nothing for a long time until she stumbled upon something crunchy and slippery. It was a plastic bag!

She intensified her search, and thanks to her persistence and extraordinary luck, she came upon the strap of the backpack, and crawled out with the happy find in her hands. Imagine her indignation when she saw that the backpack was torn in one place, the Herx map mercilessly crumpled, only three cellophane bags remained, counting the one she accidentally dug up in the snow, and most importantly – the flashlight and the brush pen were gone. Rina had been robbed again. More than that – not just brazenly robbed, but cruelly plundered bandit-style!

Be that as it may, she was lucky. First, although heavily wrinkled, the map was still there. Second, the bears (the thieves, as Rina preferred to call them from that moment), yes, the thieves had not figured out the “secret” of zippers, so the whole contents of both inner compartments and the large outer pocket remained untouched – the glass of tortoise Oluvila, the Blue Camellia from Triazis, the pebble-nut from the Belmons' prison, the yellow citrine, dollars, pen, compass, the ball with the light of the Ice Fountain, the apparently forever stopped watch, and some other smaller items – everything was in order.

At the bottom of the backpack, she found a microscopic, half-inch piece of vanilla cookie. Rina inhaled its magical aroma, placed the crumb on her tongue, and held it there mournfully, not daring to swallow, until this fragment of a distant paradise melted gradually and dissolved, leaving a painfully exquisite aftertaste in her mouth...

Yet, despite hunger, extreme fatigue, and a terrible mood, regardless of the pain in her soul and the burning resentment in her heart, Rina abandoned her initial idea of resting until morning and decided not to linger even a minute among the vile, hateful bears, and, catching the first one she encountered, to squeeze the whole truth out of him by any means necessary – down to the last drop of it. This determination inspired her greatly.

Somehow tying the straps chewed by the nasty pranksters, she put on the backpack. And changed her mind – she strapped it to her front, the way dads carry babies in those ingenious slings with a soft seat. Carrying the backpack in such an unusual way was not entirely convenient.

“Let's see them try to steal from me now!” she said loudly into the darkness.

The downy snow scattered to the sides like weightless foam, walking wasn't difficult. Though not only the road but also the bumps had become invisible, so Rina walked, constantly stumbling and sliding in her sneakers as if on butter. But perseverance is a great thing! Rina moved forward confidently and strongly, searching for a sacrificial bear like a lynx stalking a hare.

And again, the little beast came running into the hunter's arms, as it should be by rights – a bear appeared about twenty minutes later. He didn't try to hide, wasn't frightened, never even glanced at the rapidly approaching stranger.

He didn't care. The purple joker simply crawled out of his cozy den to snack on fresh snow for the seventh time that evening, and most importantly – to gaze at the folds of clouds, by which he tried to calculate the answer to the most important question of the era – whether the tasty snowfall would continue tomorrow morning or not.

As to those wandering nearby – they interested him only to the extent that three deep dugouts had been artfully covered with fluffy snow between his den and the Boulevard. However, he had long since stopped hoping for luck.

Nevertheless, the bear turned out to be an amazingly fortunate one! Rina dropped straight into the very first trap at full speed, almost losing both her kneecaps!

The bear gazed around in shock, not believing his goggling eyes and suspecting a complex trick from the neighbors, as a result of which he himself would end up looking foolish. But he was about to be convinced of the unthinkable – the old pits, known to the entire district, hastily and carelessly scratched out, suddenly worked! He even squealed in the unexpected celebration.

Which was immediately spoiled by the very source of the joy – Rina – who jumped out of the hole annoyingly quickly and pounced on the disappointed bear with such fierce rage that the poor cub himself was ready to fall into anything, anywhere, anyhow, even headfirst. Dazed by pain, Rina clutched the bear, squeezed him like a sponge in her fingers, and shouted right into his muzzle:

“Speeeak!”

The bear vibrated, gurgled something. Rina shook him with such anger that the purple head barely stayed on his shoulders.

“Speak right now! Thief! You little creep!”

“I... I... don... don... der...” the bear stammered desperately.

“What did you don-der?” Rina couldn't make sense of his babbling.

“N-n-n... n-no! I say... don't... don't... have”.

“What don't you have?” Rina shook him again, not as hard, though the wretched purple one bit the tip of his tongue pretty painfully.

“I don't have any! Don't have anything!” the bear blurted out, gathering his courage.

Rina looked into his cowardly darting eyes and pushed him away with disgust. The cub immediately began his cautious backpedaling toward his burrow.

“No, you stay right there! I'm not letting you go until you answer my questions!”

“I'll answer, I'll answer”, the bear prattled, nervously glancing around, searching for the neighbors to help him. “Anything you want! I'll answer everything! Every thing!”

Rina sat down on a high, thick pile of snow, exactly midway between the bear and his shelter, and said, almost calmly:

“All right then, listen and remember. First, you're going to explain to me where the entrance to the Seventh World is...”

“The seventh, the ninth, whatever you want!” the bear readily agreed and broke into a servile smile.

“What do you mean, the ninth?” Rina flared up. “Either you tell me the truth, or you're coming with me all the way to the Entrance! We will go together. Got it? And you will enter with me. Inside!”

The bear sank down onto the ice, shaking his head.

“N-no, no! I'm not going! I'm not entering! I can't! Please! Please, don't make me!” Real terror lit up in his blueberry-ish eyes.

“Oh, so that's how it is!” Rina got to her feet and stepped right up to the purple one. “You're scared to go. You are frightened to enter. You don't want to. That means you know everything. You all know everything! You're just pretending, you play dumb!”

“W-we know... We p-play...” the cub confirmed, still trembling.

Rina looked at him and waved her hand hopelessly.

“I don't want to talk to you anymore. I'm sick of you. Just tell me, where's the road to the Exit?”

“B-beyond the Straight B-boulevard”.

“There?” Rina pointed ahead, the bear nodded.

“There! Beyond the Boulevard. The Boulevard ends. The road begins. There are no other roads. Just one. We call it Straight”.

“Everything's straight here”, Rina remarked with a sigh.

“Straight, straight, everything's straight”, the bear agreed willingly. Rina thought for a moment, tapped her foot on the ice, and asked:

“Why isn't your snow cold?”

“That I don't know. Everything's cold, but the snow isn't. I don't know”.

“All right. Then tell me why the whole valley is gray and dirty”.

“From the cloud”, the bear pointed a finger at the sky. “It flies in and dirties the city. Ruins the taste!”

“Where does it fly in from? There? From the direction of the Entrance?”

The bear nodded three times. Rina adjusted her backpack and thought:

“Pathetic, all of them. Empty-headed. Nothing's inside. And they steal just because of that emptiness... My flashlight, by the way, could have been really useful. What am I going to do without the flashlight?”

“You guys are exhausting”, she told the bear. He just blinked in response. “Tell me, what else do you eat besides snow?”

“Snowballs”.

“And ice?”

“No, ice is too hard, you have to melt it first. Snow is much better”.

“Well, I know your kind – what you guys are like, you hear me? And I have seen your Muals. Now tell me, is there anyone else here on the plains? Or somebody who lives nearby? I mean birds, animals, fish, I don't know, anyone at all”.

The cub pursed his black lips and thought for some time. After a minute, he shook his head and said:

“No, no one lives here. They do, but they live in another country”.

“Who? What country?”

“The Muals live there. They, with the Muals...”

“No! I've already mentioned the Muals”, Rina cut him off.

“Really? It's nice...” The bear smiled and immediately lost all interest in the conversation. He lifted his snout straight up and, with his mouth slightly open, stared at a gray swirl drifting quickly beneath the belly of a massive black thundercloud. The bear was completely absorbed by this captivating sight and had already forgotten all about Rina. Well, perhaps some faint, blurry memories.

Rina coughed, sounding a bit hoarse (the purple one flinched), looked around, and strode off.

“That's all they know, whether you shake them or not”, she thought with weary irritation. “The Straight Boulevard, the Straight Road. Period. But what's beyond that, how far it is, how it's set up – they know nothing. They fear, but know nothing...”

The appearance of the “dirty valley” was brightening by the minute, the clouds were dispersing, and the young, tangerine-colored moon grew bolder, no longer ashamed of her nakedness, and started peeking out more and more coquettishly from behind the hazy curtains until she finally emerged in all her radiant splendor. The snow gleamed and sparkled, sharp golden needles dancing across the snowy waves.

Charm, beauty, and freshness – that's how an ancient Düsseldorf poet might have phrased it (why the city of Düsseldorf popped into her head, Rina couldn't really answer).

But never mind poetry. Let's just say – the weather was good. In any case, Rina walked pretty briskly now, even her legs stopped hurting, her mood lifted, and if not for the gnawing hunger, she might truly have rejoiced in the white expanses – the high sky, and the juicy, clear moon. Here and there along the road were patches of pure snow, free of dust and soot. And though there wasn't a single bear track to be seen, the road embankment rose slightly above the valley, making it impossible to stray from the path.

“Just don't want to run into any of those...” she thought.

The bears – so cute and funny on the outside and so disgustingly foul inside – stirred up the most unpleasant feelings.

“I wonder”, the question flitted by, “are real bears – I mean our earthly ones – just as shameless and thieving?”

She remembered the Kyiv Zoo and the sweet roll she and Nina had split in two and thrown into the brown bear cub's enclosure, only to have his half-ton mother snatch up both pieces and swallow them in no time, while the poor cub was left to sniff the air, blinking at the girls with his pleading eyes.

“No, it's not fair to judge her for that. A cage means captivity, a prison”, she said aloud. “People in cells do not act very human, either. But in the wild, in the forest, would a mother bear take food from her cubs? I don't think so. These purple ones – they're another breed, a bad, wrong kind of greedy, nasty, thieving bears. Greed-Thief-Bears – GTB. That's their scientific name from now on!”

The moon seemed to have been plugged into another socket – this cheerful companion shone so brightly now that the whole world turned white, clear as day for miles around. Rina took off her backpack and decided to use it the right way – on her back. The leather straps had rubbed her neck raw, wearing a backpack on her front was really uncomfortable. She untied the straps, took out the map, and smoothed it as best she could, spreading it out right on the snow. To her great relief, the gray circle had disappeared.

“It's clear – I've passed the ‘Dirty Valley'“, she concluded with satisfaction. “And now I'm going the right way along the right road”.

The thought that the entrance to the last world might be just a few kilometers away lifted her spirits and filled her with hope – only to drop her into the dark of anxiety.

The World of Fire!
