She had seen it so many times in her dreams and musings, imagined herself wandering its blazing streets and suddenly finding her sister – chained, or locked in an iron cell with rusty rivets, or tied to a post in the middle of scorching stones. In Rina's visions, Nina appeared as a slave. But sometimes, in those fantasies, her sister was a princess or a noblewoman, seated in a large gilded hall on brocade cushions and thick furs, surrounded by courtiers and bowing attendants...

Though right now, what really troubled the tired, weary Rina was just one thing – how to get to the Entrance as quickly as possible.

“The task is to find it, and then I'll figure it out”, she said to the kindly smiling moon. Folding up the map, she shouldered her backpack and kept walking.

Soon, her strength gave out again. The moon, too, quickly grew older and duller, wrapping itself in gray clouds, and although the road was still clearly distinguishable, resting seemed like the only sensible choice. With effort, Rina stepped away from the embankment onto the deep, newly packed snow, stood still for a minute or two, scanning the area, listening to the night's tiniest rustlings, and, finding nothing even remotely suspicious, began the long climb up the nearest hill.

She had climbed quite high, found a more or less level patch of ground, kicked away the excess snow with her sneaker, and lay down with pleasure. It was nice and pretty comfortable. When she closed her eyes, trying to ignore the New Year's scent of snowflakes, it became easy to imagine herself lying on May grass somewhere deep in the forest, never thinking about anything and just blissing out. Then the moon winked again from behind the lacy haze, and Rina couldn't resist – she sat up, unzipped her backpack, and took out the map. Hooray, the red rectangle had doubled in size!

“I'm getting closer, the entrance is already near!” she declared straight up to the sky, and started counting out loud. “Two hours at five kilometers per hour, that's ten kilometers... Well, let's say eight. Eight kilometers. In eight kilometers, the Entrance became twice as close. So that means... How much is left?”

She lay back down, staring up at the moon, but the arithmetic wouldn't come together – the closest cloud kept changing shape, distracting her. She had to focus. She lowered her eyelids. The numbers flickered, the digits in the first row lost their color, and then lined up into a beautiful oval. Rina tried to concentrate, but the digits instantly turned into green rabbits, round and three-eared—they chased each other, spun their triple ears, and laughed silently.

Rina was slowly falling into a deep sleep of hungry exhaustion. Such dreams are heavy and murky, but this time, while never taking her eyes off the crazy rabbits, Rina still remembered that it was just a dream.

“Fifteen and a half kilometers”, she murmured weakly to the most emerald rabbit and finally fell fully asleep.

30 – GARLIC WIND
part one

It was hard to get up. Reeeally hard. The warming smoke of lazy morning dreams pleasantly hovered right above Rina's forehead. A gentle snowy feather-bed coaxed her to stay for a while, it didn't let her go. Even the hunger had stopped tormenting her, wisely replaced by a ticklish itch deep in her stomach. A sluggish, mercury heaviness spread through her entire body. The low-hanging sun, with its piercing rays and youthful February appearance, was busy stretching out a flock of stubborn orange shadows from one snowdrift to another.

“It's so peaceful here”, whispered the first thought, gazing into the valley.

“Yes, true, a nice place”, agreed the second thought with a yawn.

The worries and fears had retreated, obediently slipping down into the bottomless chasm of silence, which now seemed tangible, even a little palpable. Harmony and stillness. Quietness of the eternal winter – as that poet from... well, you remember.

However, the silence was deceptive – just in that moment of relaxation and slothful euphoria, a vague rustling reached Rina's ears from afar, as if several people were tossing boulders into a large pile of dry leaves somewhere in the distance. She propped herself up and cautiously peeked out of her little nook: there, along the Straight Road stomping towards the Dirty Valley, an impressive crowd of bears was moving slowly and, for some reason, wordlessly.

Rina ducked back down, but the sharp-eyed purple ones had already spotted her. Without delaying the fun, three dozen of them hurried toward the hill.

She jumped up, grabbed her backpack, and ran, counting on losing them in just a few leaps – it didn't look like the bears were the swiftest runners on earth. But astonishingly, they overtook her in seconds. Snorting and puffing, they were surrounding her like a pack of wolves tightening a circle around a hapless fawn. Fear gripped her. The nearest bear yelled:

“Shulala Bulala!”

And the whole throng lunged at her. Rina stumbled, flew into the snow, and immediately found herself inside the mass of furry bandits. Luckily, by nature they were as light and helpless as kittens. After a minute of frantic struggle, Rina managed, with thirty hard, determined blows, to shake off the particularly clingy pests and stand up to her full height. She was in a wild rage, ready for a mortal fight.

But the bears had no such intention. Real beatdowns and brawls, genuine ferocity and aggression – these were foreign to them. To frighten someone? With pleasure! To chase, to knock down and start a playful dogpile? With triple pleasure! But beyond that? Only the wish to play the fun game again and again.

“What do you want?” Rina shouted.

The bears, looking hurt, dispersed in all directions, rubbing their unmercifully bruised sides and napes.

“We're heading to the Low Hills!” one of them replied loudly.

“So what?” Rina protested. “March to your high ones for all I care – even the sky-high ones! What does it have to do with me?”

The bears frowned but said nothing. Rina shook her head and went back to the road. Most of the bears followed her.

“What now?” she turned around and glared at them. The purples stopped in their tracks. “For real. You're so dumb, guys!”

She suddenly thought it was very funny – she had been running from them. From whom? Who was she scared of? These pitiable weaklings? They were scared of everything themselves!

Reaching the Straight Road, Rina carefully brushed off the snow, adjusted her sundress and her hair, tightened the backpack straps, and only then did she look over the bunch of silly bears and smirk.

“Well then. Arrivederci, babies! Give my regards to the edible hills and all your pits”, and she set off towards the Entrance.

The bears trailed after her in a tight huddle, as if she were their leader, the Mama Duck. For a minute of two Rina was surprised and rather frustrated, then she decided to try shooing them away, like village boys chasing off a pesky mutt – she bent down and made a motion with her hand as if picking up a stone. The bears watched wide-eyed, sincerely fascinated by the imaginary stone. But they knew nothing of the fear of stray dogs.

“Oh well, let them tag alone if they've got nothing better to do”, she reasoned, and picked up her pace.

The bears, giggling in anticipation of a new funny game, resumed their flanking maneuvers – small purple groups spread out along both sides of the road. Rina stopped. So, what to do with them? They didn't seem to pose much of a danger, but their shameless persistence was crossing every line. Fight them? Pointless – just look how many of them there behind her! Besides, what reason did she have to fight and hit them? Perhaps, she could try to negotiate with them? Ha, good luck convincing such notorious dummies!

And then a marvelous trick popped into her head. A first-class hoax, you might say.

“Oh, how sick, how tired I am of this disgusting sweet snow!” she proclaimed loudly so even those in the very back rows would hear. “I'm really fed up with it. I can't even stand it anymore! My teeth ache from all this sweetness. No, I want snow apples – that's the best deal, that's the flavor, that's the smell! Simply paradise!”

The purple ones perked up their ears and opened their mouths in wonder.

“Such fragrant little apples, like my grandmother makes, you won't find anywhere in the world”, Rina continued with the flair of a child reciting poems at a holiday show. The audience was transfixed, not missing a single syllable. “Oh, smooth and round, they just melt in your mouth. Lovely! Yummy! Wonderful!”

“Wonderful!” chorused the bears, repeating their treasured word.

Meanwhile, Rina threw herself into the role completely.

“Grandma gathers half a pile of night snow, half a pile of day snow, sprinkles it with morning snowflakes and stirs it, stirs it, stirs it”, Rina demonstrated dramatically with her hands just how Grandma stirred the snow. Hypnotized by the performance, the bears stared without blinking. “Then my dear Grandma makes little balls, each with a tiny groove for extra flavor. You have to dry them out well under the midday sun, then you polish them carefully with ice crystals. Done! Delicious, marvelous treats are ready!”

The bears' minds were reeling.

“And how could I forget such a heap of Grandma's apples?” Rina made a silly face and spread her hands in frustration. “A whole hill of apples! You couldn't even count them! They're sitting there now, getting tastier and ripening even further”.

“Where are they? Tell us!” the big fat bear in the front row blurted out. “Where are they getting tastier?”

“Right over there, not too far”, Rina said as indifferently as she could, waving her hand off into the distance. “Two-hour walk, or half-hour run”.

“Where? Where do we run?” the bears clamored.

“Over there, to that big sharp peak”, Rina pointed her finger at the harshly lit horizon.

“To the Grayish Mountain! To the Grayish Mountain!” shouted the smartest bear. “To the mountain, to the mountain!” the others took up the cry excitedly, and without wasting a second, pushing and mumbling “apples”, “grandma”, “grooves”, the whole gang poured into the depths of the valley. Rina watched the quickly receding purple cloud and couldn't believe that such a primitive childish trick had finally freed her from her unbearably annoying pursuers.

“Now I have to get out of here quickly!”

Though “quickly” was just a pretty word. As soon as the danger passed, her vigor left her, too. She had to sit on a little snow hump and rest for at least several minutes.

But luck was still with her, beyond this point, there had been no recent snowstorm, and the old snow lay in a thin, dense layer that didn't slip at all. Walking felt as easy as strolling down an asphalt path.

If only she had a couple of cheese sandwiches! With mushrooms, of course! Yes, she wouldn't have turned them down! Well, even just one sandwich would do. No need for mushrooms. Or cheese. Even just a piece of bread. It didn't have to be white bread. Let it be a little dry gray slice. A tiny one...

But there was nothing edible anywhere, except for the snow – this meaningless, elegantly frozen distilled water. Rina sighed, chewed some snow, found a few bluish ice pebbles the size of cherries right under her feet, and ate them too, pretending they were menthol candies.

In truth, she sat there for a long time, not three minutes as she planned, but probably more like twenty, though her energy still didn't come back. She clenched her resolve into a tight fist, well, not exactly a whole fist, but anyway there was some will left somewhere, hopefully enough to stand up and, stumbling and swaying as if after a long day of slave labor, she managed to keep moving. She was slow, to put it plainly. Limping along, stopping every few minutes, peering into the distance, listening to the silence – first, to calm her breath, and second, to be sure the bears weren't coming back. She did her best to gain a bit of strength and warmth. And more air too.

With every half mile, the plain grew more deserted. The raised roadbed kept sinking down to meet the level of the surrounding ground, melting into the flatness, and then she had to walk even more carefully and slowly, afraid of hidden pits, but mostly terrified of losing her direction altogether. More than once she thought she had strayed off the path, but the road, now narrowed and barely visible, kept re-emerging.

And so she walked, bit by bit, with long pauses, covering two or three hundred yards at a time, resting on the first lump she came across, dragging herself to her feet with difficulty, and trudging onward. But getting up from those bumps grew much harder. Finally, she decided to rest like a horse – just standing.

A good ten times she pulled out the map, but there were no noticeable changes yet. Time seemed to have shriveled up. Everything had stopped. Only the pop-eyed sun continued its tireless work. Rina watched the sun and was amazed at how quickly it was moving, while she was painfully trudging through another grueling quarter mile, the sun was easily running its arc across the sky by several degrees. Morning had passed, then noon (Rina was afraid even to think the word “lunch”), the sun had crossed its modest zenith and was now rushing headlong towards the sunset.

“How much have I managed to walk? Thirty-three breaks. Five or six hours. Let's say three hundred meters each. Fine, let's count two hundred and fifty. If it's two hundred and fifty, that's... that's... eight kilometers. I think. Only eight kilometers?”

Rina was disappointed, so much effort spent on just eight kilometers. But she would have been even more disheartened if she had known the actual numbers...

For the next two hours she dragged herself through the snow at an average speed... speed of what? A tortoise? No, tortoises walk faster... Once she persuaded herself to try an experiment – lying in a snowdrift to catch her breath and recharge better. But it didn't work. First of all, she couldn't fight off the drowsiness and fell asleep immediately, and second, it took her about ten minutes to haul herself out of that snowdrift. So there was nothing for it – she returned to her old method, three hundred meters of half-crawling, then resting upright in the pose of a sick ostrich.

Finally, in the last rays of the sun, she took out the map again. She didn't even have the strength to feel happy, the red rectangle had grown fourfold and was occupying the entire center of the white cardboard.

“Close”, she whispered. “It's close”.

Walking until midnight – her previous bold plan – was out of the question. Rina decided to settle in for the night earlier and sleep longer, so that in the morning, “with a clear head”, she might be able to slip through the first kilometers as if without noticing them – until the moment her legs would refuse to carry her any farther.

She knew she was fooling herself. But she needed an excuse, a reason to justify her physical weakness, her “inability to achieve her goals successfully”, as TV announcers would say in the days of her father's youth. She felt she had no right to be this helpless.

Besides, Rina was genuinely shocked by herself, how could it be that she, healthy, more or less strong – almost athletic – who could run the hundred-meter dash in fourteen point one seconds, had gone three days without eating – and here she was, out of energy and practically hallucinating, seeing stars and shapes in her eyes.

“Of course”, she concluded, trying to suppress her annoyance, “if I'd spent this time resting on a couch or at least sitting in a chair, then sure, I'd still have some energy left. But I've been walking. And not just walking – running from the purple ones... Although no, I lost consciousness before that”.

For some reason, Rina got it into her head to calculate down to the exact hour how much time had passed since her last real meal. That fake feast in the Land of Butterflies didn't count.

“As for the butterflies, everything's a lie there, just hallucinations. And really, they probably don't even exist at all – no butterflies, no Blue Meadows”.

However, the black bell-fruit with a drop of Blue Water inside, not stolen by the bears, was still resting at the very bottom of her backpack somewhere in a hidden corner, silently proving to the world that the Blue Meadows were alive and well. Okay, fine! The Meadows, all the flowers and butterflies were real, but there definitely hadn't been any sweet breakfast! No arguing with that...

In short, exhausted and squeezed dry like a lemon, Rina might have miscounted a bit. In the end it added up to not three, but over four days – one hundred and two hours without a crumb of food! Incredible!

“One hundred and two? Of total starvation?” she shuddered. “If Mom knew, she'd faint too. From the horror...”

Finally, having thought it over and put together a plan as best she could, she moved away from the road toward the long snow dunes. This time she went really far, so even the most sharp-eyed bear wouldn't notice her. By then, the sun had sunk chest-deep into the snow but wasn't red yet – it was browning by the minute, just like a very hot iron ball cooling. Anyway, Rina took up residence in the hills like a queen – on a wide, flat snow ledge.

“A couch, the Mercure Hotel”, she thought with a touch of sadness, remembering her trip to Brazil. But her poorly controlled memories immediately jumped from the hotel lobby to the restaurant with its huge buffet of thirty-nine ingredients. It's too dangerous, remembering such a marvel after four days of hunger. To distract herself from the tempting pictures, she gazed at the sunset. And it worked! With a great effort Rina drove away Brazil that had come to mind out of nowhere, pulled the six-hundred-pound backpack off her shoulders, and – aiming herself right at the center of the “couch” – she collapsed into the tender snowy down like a felled fir-tree.

Her legs and arms went numb so quickly that she was scared they would not come back in the morning. But at that moment, she felt a stabbing pain in her knees and was reassured – they would. She fell asleep on the count of three. And immediately ran into Herx. The dream was weird beyond belief…

A dusty, battered old room cluttered with broken household items, what some call junk, with all the windows crudely boarded up from the outside. Through the narrow, crooked cracks, sharp rays of sunlight pierced, lighting up hundreds of dust motes drifting in the stale air. Everything was in decay. It was the kind of place slated for demolition, when a house tortured by time had aged and rotted so much it was no longer fit to live in. The people had moved to new apartments, leaving behind things no one needed anymore. The house was about to be wiped off the face of the earth to make way for a neat apartment building or, say, a big store.

But something had gone wrong – some papers hadn't been approved, and the house wasn't being torn down. So it stood there, forlorn, its doors nailed shut, crumbling slowly, going moldy, gathering dust. Month after month, the house was forgotten and abandoned. No, it would be demolished eventually, as soon as everything was signed, but in the meantime, waiting for its sentence to be carried out, the old house was even sadder, gloomier, and more bitter.

And in her new dream Rina found herself in a house like that.

Across the room, raising little clouds of red dust, Herx was striding energetically from corner to corner, limping on his right leg, constantly bumping his knee on his heavy sword. Some paces to the side, on a sagging, ragged armchair, staring at his every movement sat the ugliest old woman. From a hole in the ceiling above her white light streamed, illuminating her almost bald, wrinkled head. The terrible skull glowed like a hellish lamp lighting up the triangle of the rotting walls in the corner.

Herx stopped, sharply turned to the old crone, the floor creaked.

“Alit an di un dalemai”.

The old woman didn't hear him.

Rina somehow knew the old woman was completely deaf. And it was obvious that Herx knew it too. But the old woman understood everything by reading his lips and gave him a nasty, toothless smile.

It seemed like Herx had been pacing for a long time. He was arguing and proving something that was clear to him but incomprehensible to the crone. He asked:

“Estikel, nid alev va lot?”

Suddenly, to her total surprise, Rina translated the question: “Estikel, are you completely blind?”

Herx continued, “Sati ans kerivi lo akei”.

“Your daughter would do it too”, Rina translated.

“Kui alev andi taar de on, Estikel?”

“Or do you not care a damn about her, Estikel?” Rina heard in her thoughts.

The old woman shook her head, closed her eyes, and slowly turned away, putting her brown, knobby face into the sliding beam of bright light.

Rina was so absorbed by the scene she entirely forgot her own problems. She didn't even try to call out to Herx or get his attention somehow. All she wanted at that moment was for the old woman to finally understand the deal and agree with Herx's arguments, and give him the coin at last.

“What coin?” she wondered, but the dream was already slipping away.

The room with Herx and the old woman flattened, the picture shrank, diminishing before her eyes, and at the same time transforming into something indistinct and fuzzy – until it suddenly turned into a brightly patterned, harlequin colored cardboard box.

Unexpectedly, someone's strong foot in a black cleat kicked the box very hard. Rina saw an unfamiliar boy of about eleven with a buzz cut. He was wearing a white-and-blue Dynamo Kyiv soccer jersey with a big “D” and two little stars.

“Leezik! Leezik! Drop that cat right now! Get home, quickly!” a clear woman's voice shouted from somewhere above.

But the boy had no intention of stopping, he kept driving the colorful box forward.

“Leezik! Stop it! Leave that cat alone! Now!” the invisible woman demanded.

“He doesn't have any cat!” Rina replied loudly with barely concealed annoyance and... woke up.

30 – GARLIC WIND
part two

The dream vanished. But not entirely. Not without a trace.

A multicolored box lay on the snow, half a meter from her sneaker. True, the box was about eight times larger than the one the Dynamo kid had been kicking in her dream. Rina recognized it instantly and understood everything.

“A package from Herx!”

Surprisingly, that was her first thought.

A package? Why not? If audio communication works through dreams between worlds – and not only sound, but even real three-dimensional video with augmented reality, as they call it now – then why not allow for a simple postal service? Some delivery. Although, of course, even through a real phone, you can't send a package.

“What if the bears are playing a prank?”

Rina stood up, quickly climbed to the top, and looked around. As far as the eye could see, from edge to edge, an endless sparkling plain of fluffy snow, all in humped hillocks and fine ripples left by the blizzard's drifts. No bears, not a single microscopic dark spot.

Calmed, she came back, cautiously approached the box, bent down, and lightly touched the lacquered cardboard with her fingertip.

“Funny!” she said aloud. “What am I afraid of? A bomb, or something?” And decisively took the box in her hands.

But first, she examined it from all sides for any inscriptions. A return address, for example. No, nothing was written. The slippery cardboard was entirely covered with blue and red triangles. A harlequin pattern, for some reason. The box was somewhat heavy, weighing about a kilogram and a half or a bit more, and something inside was tapping – a hard little ball.

Rina reverently placed the package back on the snow and suddenly, come what may, with a swift motion, tore off the lid. Inside were more than a dozen small things, tightly and neatly wrapped in white foil. All different, of various shapes – round, oval, angular. When she unwrapped the first one, she nearly collapsed in excitement, carefully swaddled in a sheet of thin aluminum foil was... a piece of Dutch cheese! In some inhuman (to avoid saying beastly) delight, she clawed the foil off the bundles and, like treasures, solemnly laid their contents on the “featherbed”:

cheese – a generously heavy triangular chunk.

poppy seed buns – small ones, but three of them!

a sponge cake – substantial slice all in glaze.

a red apple – that was what had been rolling inside.

yogurt – a stout pink cup.

chocolate bars – four medium, different, but all creamy ones.

mushrooms and potatoes pie – a hefty slice.

three bumpy dill pickles – carefully packed in cellophane

a big plump Truffle candy – in a golden wrapper

and two Turbo bubble gums – directly from Kyiv!

In short, Rina flatly refused to believe her eyes! For a moment.

Well, she began with the cheese. Greedily chased it down with pie and apple simultaneously. Then came the cake. After that – yogurt with a chocolate bar. And a roll. And another chocolate bar. A royal feast! An incredible treat! A fairy-tale deliciousness! Rina bit into a pickle and thought that never ever had an ordinary salty cucumber been such a marvelous treat as it had become here, in the World of Snows.

She broke off and popped into her mouth pieces of this, of that, and of the other, chewed slowly, stretching out the pleasure, she ‘reveled in sensations' (Svetka's phrase from an old movie). Finally, having eaten her fill, she lay down on the “sofa”, and, looking at the sky, rolled the Turbo over her tongue, paradised out by the beloved taste and thought of absolutely nothing.

But then she remembered, jumped up, and started running around.

“A note! There must be a note!”

A note, precisely! If Herx had so easily managed to send an entire box of food, it would have been silly not to include a scrap of paper with a few words. Herx understands perfectly that Rina requires not only something to eat – for which, of course, an enormous, immeasurable thank you – though food is food, but now she needs advice, she is in desperate need of information! Because questions have piled up – a trainload of questions! First of all, what to do next?

Naturally, she had to go forward, but had she chosen the right direction? And what does the red rectangle on the map actually mean? And the Entrance itself, what does it look like in reality? But most importantly – how does it work?

Rina once again thoroughly examined the box inside and out, then smoothed out the crumpled pieces of foil, shifted everything that remained of the feast from place to place – no note!

She was about to remove the harlequin covering from the lid when suddenly her attention was drawn to a tiny, shiny ball – the Turbo insert. In her haste, she hadn't even looked at it, just took the gum with a habitual motion and popped it into her mouth. At home, she never collected the inserts, so she had automatically rolled it into a ball and placed it next to the box. There it lay, in a small round snow dimple.

Rina grabbed it, unrolled it. Sure enough! Instead of a little car, the glossy paper showed Herx smiling joyfully through his gray beard, and beneath the photograph, in white angular letters, it read:

FOLLOW THE GARLIC

“What garlic?” she wondered.

She reread the inscription over and over, understanding nothing. Of the entire magical package, only the potato pie, half of which had already been eaten, had a slight garlicky flavor. At the same time, Herx couldn't have made a slip of the pen, despite often confusing similar languages. No, it's impossible to make a mistake in three words!

“If flowers of Ae grow on clouds”, she finally reasoned, “then in the ice-world, a snow garlic could very well grow too”.

The only thing that bothered her – Rina had never seen how not just snow garlic, but even the most ordinary human garlic grows. Of course, Rina's grandmother had a large garden, but for some reason, she never grew garlic, she preferred to trade it with neighbors – such an unfortunate coincidence. Yes, there was garlic at home in the fridge, but exclusively in the form of papery, light gray bulbs.

However, after a couple of minutes of hard thinking, Rina decided that, essentially, it doesn't matter what the plant looks like when you're searching for it in a snowy desert – there's nothing living here at all, unless you count the bears, and therefore any sprout will be noticeable from afar, you won't miss it. Green on white is visible from a great distance!

“And then I'll smell it”, she concluded her deduction with a bold stroke and immediately prepared to set off.

This time, the preparations were exceedingly pleasant: the remaining food supply could well last another day. But most importantly – Herx had found a way to transmit physical objects across dimensions. Rina began to dream and thought:

“If it was possible to send a package with pie and pickles, then in the same way, anything at all can be delivered”.

What specific items were hidden behind the “anything at all”, Rina didn't understand yet, but the realization that a powerful wizard not only sees you and appears in your dreams but even gives you such necessary and timely gifts – this thought calmed her and gave her confidence, offering hope for a quick success.

Rina took out the map: the red rectangle hadn't grown or shrunk, but now it seemed more orange, not as crimson as yesterday. Although the reason could have been the yellow sun, which had risen over the boundless snowy steppe in all its splendor. Rina tucked the map back in place and, within a minute, was cheerfully, almost sportily, striding along the Straight Road in the most glorious spirits.

Fortunately, the road hadn't deteriorated overnight, no new snowflakes had fallen, and the old snow was gradually compacting and becoming a solid layer of hardpack. Rina walked with a strong, honed rhythm, left-right, left-right, marching as if in a parade – will and confidence in her eyes, determination in her body, and resilience in her soul... At least, that's how it seemed to her, imagining herself from the outside. In reality, the picture was, of course, somewhat more modest...

Six short breaks flew by imperceptibly – three minutes each – plus she ate the two remaining chocolate bars without noticing, as if it happened by accident. Now she was terribly thirsty. Slightly annoyed at the local nature, she swirled warm snow in her mouth. It melted reluctantly into a disgusting, chemical-tasting water. Was that a real drink? No! Just a poor imitation. To balance the nasty taste on her tongue, Rina unwrapped the second gum, but on the insert, a useless Yamaha motorcycle gleamed against a checkered background. And that was it. No Herx, no inscriptions, zero.

Thoroughly chewing the gum, Rina felt an unpleasant aftertaste, like a drop of Marinara Sauce had been added there as a joke. She chewed a bit more and grasped the truth – it wasn't the Turbo. The smell of Marinara hung in the air. A side wind blew, and the sauce aroma disappeared completely, but within seconds, it returned, this time much more repulsive.

Rina hadn't yet had time to comprehend this new phenomenon when she suddenly noticed, with horror in her soul, that the Straight Road was no more. That is to say, the embankment had been literally scraped off, as if bulldozed away – the road ended in jagged edges, and ahead, as far as the eye could decipher it, stretched a smooth, hockey-rink-flat white wasteland. Though there was nothing for it, road or not – she had to keep going.

Without stopping, Rina walked forward, doing her best to maintain the direction of the vanished Straight Road. The oncoming gusts of ‘Marinara air' kept growing stronger, beating against her face and lifting little fountains of snow fluff from the lumps.

“And this isn't any marinara!” she exclaimed. “It's garlic, that's what it is!”

But what thing could be producing such a killer stench? Maybe somewhere far ahead there was a plantation of flowering garlic bushes, or a factory grinding up garlic powder? Guessing was pointless – she wouldn't understand anything until she saw it with her own eyes.

On the whole, aside from the smell itself, Rina didn't feel any particular unpleasantness. Yes, of course, the loneliness of this endless snowbound desert weighed on her nerves. Understandably so. When you're alone, when you've been walking for hours and see not a single sign that your journey will ever end, your nerves can definitely get a little over-strung. True, Rina had one such sign in her hands – her map. And though the rectangle on it hadn't grown in a long time, it was definitely changing color, now its yellow-red hue was unmistakable.

“Just a little more”, she thought with a smile. “And it'll turn into a square orange”.

But the farther she ventured into the valley, the more disgusting the smell became. Very soon it completely lost that hint of cooked meat and turned into nothing but the purest, sharpest garlic reek, the kind you'd get if you grated up several cloves of it, say, in a bedroom. Garlic is good in meals, but in bedrooms – no, it's revolting. That was exactly the case – the repulsive, unbearable nastiness.

The garlic hung in the air, present with every step, so at first Rina didn't really understand Herx's words, “Follow the garlic”. But as soon as she involuntarily veered off the proper path by just a few paces, the garlic disappeared without a trace – the waves of fresh wind washed her face with the gentle scent of the first January snowfall. She had to track the garlic with her nose, panicking and sniffing around like a bloodhound, darting about and nosing the air like mad. And she was truly happy when the impossibly foul, suffocating stink once again enveloped her from head to toe.

It became apparent that the noxious stink flowed through the valley like a narrow, invisible channel, maybe just twenty steps wide. A garlic river.

“I just don't get it”, Rina muttered quietly, though rather indignantly. “If they had to mark the road this way – it's okay, do it – but why couldn't they have used French perfume instead, or at least rose oil?”

On her mother's dressing table, amid all sorts of clutter, there was a black cylinder of real Iranian rose oil – very expensive, incredibly fragrant. Just a touch on the cap, and you could be sure your fingers would smell of roses until the next morning.

“They simply have no imagination”, Rina declared with authority, meaning the unknown designers and engineers, the creators of the garlic wind.

Suddenly, the weather changed – it turned colder. During all the time she'd spent in the Land of Snow, she'd gotten so used to the warm winter landscapes that the first breath of truly icy air made her stop in amazement.

“Getting colder – and my jacket was stolen”, she thought with a pang.

The cold and the warm air currents battled stubbornly, one moment winning, the next retreating, but the cold won out – Rina began to shiver, and forgetting about local peculiarities, she tried to warm up with some half-sporty exercises, but the freezing breeze chilled all her hopes within a minute.

To make things worse, the garlic in the air had reached such a concentration that it felt as if someone had poured a giant spoonful of the most repulsive garlic paste straight onto her tongue.

Later, when she accidentally stepped out of the stream again, she found herself, as if by magic, in the same pleasant warmth as before. Clean, tender gusts filled her lungs with healthy freshness. Relieved and warmed, she decided to try walking not inside the garlic river but along its edge – she moved slightly to the left, skirting the bank of the smell brook, and from time to time, every twenty or thirty yards, she took a few steps to the right, where the nauseating mustiness and the freezing draft met her that same instant.

Rina even felt a little proud of how cleverly she'd managed to solve the problem of garlic and cold. But puffed up with pride, she immediately lost the path and spent a desperate minute and a half running back and forth until she stumbled upon the unbearable rotten stench and calmed down. After that, she walked a little slower and visited the garlic river more frequently.

The former red rectangle continued to yellow and fade, and as Rina had rightly guessed, it would soon disappear without a trace.

“But what will I do when it blends into the paper?”

Sure, as long as the garlic wind's pungency remained, there was no reason to worry. At the same time, she understood that the hated fetor could vanish as suddenly as the Straight Road had. Then she'd be stuck, sitting stupidly until nightfall, not daring to budge, hoping Herx would nail it down once and for all...

She trudged on for another mile and noticed that it was getting noticeably colder, not only in the garlic river but all around too. She thought for a moment and tried an experiment, she took off her backpack and set it exactly on the border of the garlic, then turned her back and walked a hundred steps to the side. At first, it really did get warmer by six to eight degrees, but that was it. The true, cozy warmth that had sustained her these past days had disappeared.

“Wintery summer is giving way to summery winter”, a clever but not too encouraging thought flashed in her mind.

Everyone knows – there's no such thing as bad weather. Sometimes you're just dressed wrong. A sundress and sneakers are never good protection against low temperatures. Rina was still mourning the jacket she'd lost for good, though to be fair, the thin denim wouldn't have saved her from the true cold if (she didn't want to believe it) the real frost were to set in.

“What could be worse than winter?” she asked the low clouds passing overhead, and answered herself, “Nothing! Nothing's worse than winter”.

But freezing or not, she had to keep moving. And she did. She even attempted to come up with a universal remedy for the cold. Clearly, her recent inventive success – figuring out how to manage the garlic fumes – had given her a boost of confidence, but no practical solution to the problem of cold entered her head, try as she might.

“I don't even have matches!” she exclaimed. “Lost them somewhere”.

She knew exactly where – on the pea field. But even if she hadn't lost them, there was nothing to build a fire with anyway.

Out of nowhere, a long, snow-covered hill loomed ahead, as if it had grown out of the earth, very tall – probably forty meters high. It was strange that Rina hadn't noticed it before. Maybe she'd just been too lost in thought to look forward. But a great unexpected miracle happened there, the higher she climbed up the slope, the warmer the air became. And at the very top, there was such heat and steam as you might find in an overheated house – or more likely, in a Finnish sauna.

To warm herself up completely, she wandered back and forth along the hot, flat hump of the icy dune and noticed that the garlic odor had faded away without a trace.

“I told you! The garlic's over!”

Luckily, she was wrong, the stinky stream flowed around the hill like a real river would encircle an island. In any case, Rina could stop worrying about garlic for the moment – the main thing was the warmth. Dropping her backpack, she sprawled out on the puffy drifts of untouched snow with great relief. Her legs buzzed like taut wires in the wind, her head as heavy as a bucket full of honey, her thoughts a dense fog, and besides, she was terribly sleepy. She simply needed a nap – to whistle just a tad, as Nina used to say – knowing that the ‘tad' could last easily half a night...

Rina lay there and rested a bit, then devoured the remaining piece of pie and the last pickle, both with a great appetite, and then she molded funny shapes out of snow, pretending they were homemade cookies. Snow cookies – not a very good substitute for Coca-Cola, they barely quenched her thirst. After that, for what felt like the hundredth time that day, she pulled out her map. As expected, the rectangle had vanished into the white background, and only a transparent yellowish border still betrayed its presence...

This time, she decided not to rush. The sun was racing toward the evening, though there was at least an hour of daylight left. Climbing to the very top, Rina peered at the horizon and saw that there were a dozen more of these hills along her path, some quite tall – looking like real mountains in the distance.

“Perfect!” she was pleased. “Now I won't freeze – at least I can sleep in comfort”.

She was sure she'd have to spend another night in this snowy valley. She even thought that if the Entrance to the Seventh World appeared somehow right in front of her this very moment, she still wouldn't enter it today – she'd rather stay and wait for Herx. There was so much to ask him about! And, certainly, she still needed a good night's sleep to gain enough strength and vitality for the final interworld jump.

“Herx is due to come tonight. This night is too important for him not to show up!”

On the other hand, remembering the two previous sessions, she worried whether another glitch in communications had occurred. And what form would Herx take this time – would it even be possible to talk to him? Though remembering the package, she assumed that the connection was working fine, but it might just be that the old man simply wouldn't want to come, so he wouldn't let anything slip that he shouldn't say.

“Like Aunissa”, Rina thought resentfully. “The moment you try to talk about the World of Fire, everyone immediately tries to get by with general phrases”.

One way or another, the plan for this evening was perfectly clear, to keep going until deep night fell, then climb to the next high point and settle in warmth until dawn.

Descending onto the plain, Rina nearly choked on the garlic she'd already become “unaccustomed to” during the rest. She had to quickly run off to the side. Soon she found a more or less comfortable border of the garlic current, and the journey into the Unknown went on.

31 – BEETLE IN KALEIDOSCOPE
part one

Evening had come. Night was approaching. The garlic river led to a stepped hill. From a distance, thin paths seemed to run along the slopes, but a little later she saw that these were only cracks in the snow – the upper layer had melted and was slowly sliding down in huge sheets, forming dark seams and fissures.

The landscape was incomparably different from the Gray Valley. First of all, there were no straight routes any longer. Surrounding her, like dwarf copies of the Himalayas, were chains of sharp-peaked mountains and white ridges that looked like sleeping dragon cubs from a distance – miniature toys, crafted from ice.

Rina rightly assumed that in all these endless lowlands and hills there wasn't a single stone. Everything soft was snow (unless you counted the bears, of course), and everything hard was ice.

Climbing the slope, she quickly found a wide, flat shelf. There she settled in perfectly and had a pleasant meal, using up almost everything she had left. Rina saved just two small foil-wrapped bundles for breakfast. She didn't open them, didn't look at what was inside – she let it be a “surprise”. The wind changed direction by the minute – sometimes blowing from below, bringing an unwanted chill, and with another wave sweeping suddenly down from above, washing over the hill with oven-hot gusts.

“Freezing in the morning, scorching heat at night, but on average warm and comfy”, her mother's joke came to mind.

She didn't feel like sleeping yet. On the contrary, in her restless wakefulness she decided to climb to the summit and closely examine the entire hill country. And if it hadn't been for the most awful pain in her legs and the deep bass hum in her head, she would have done exactly that, but her loudly protesting body cooled her enthusiasm rather effectively.

“Doesn't matter in the least”, she reassured herself, “Tomorrow I'll get up early and see everything in the bright light. That's even better”.

She was waiting for Herx. There was no doubt now, everything pointed to the fact that the coveted, so sharply desired entrance was somewhere here, very close. Her skin felt that closeness, the entrance was burning her cheeks – it was there, beyond the hills. Rina was overflowing with feelings, her hands trembling. She didn't understand what was happening, where the tension and fear were coming from, she saw no reason for such concern, but be that as it may, she was truly scared. The loneliness, the emptiness, the monotonous snow desert, the weak but cynical, brazen, stupid, and therefore dangerous bears – none of these were counterbalanced by anything. She needed a meeting with a friend, with a reliable, close person, and only old Herx could come to her tonight. Even if it was in a dream, even in a strange guise and in some ridiculous place – that didn't matter, so long as he saw her, and heard her, and said something encouraging to her.

Rina sat on the warm snow for a long time, hugging her knees, staring into the dark horizon. Thick heavy clouds were crawling there as if looking for something among the hills. Memories washed over her, and every time they carried her home, Rina, trying not to be weak, would switch her thoughts to her sister. Why worry about the past, when now the only thing that mattered was the future? But memory is not a car – you can't steer it at will, so immediately, without wanting to, she saw the yard and the flowering jasmine bush by the bench – the picture was so sharp that she could barely hold back – her eyes began to well.

“When Dad, Nina and I were in Brazil”, she whispered, calming down a little, “we were far away, very far, but we knew that Mom was somewhere nearby, she was there, just on the other side of the Earth. But now? Is this even Earth? No! And where is the Earth?”

The clouds piled up, mixed together, broke helplessly into oval pieces and just as languidly gathered again, as if an invisible giant kid was constructing fancy figures, like the neighbor's toddler who loves to sit there on the balcony, never noticing anything around him, and mold little elephants from gray play-doh.

Rina fell asleep sitting up, unintentionally, but soon awoke from the uncomfortable position and lay down properly. She even made a little pillow out of snow, and covered her legs with white velvety fluff. She was trying to attune herself, “to set aside to focus”, as her German friend says. So as to see nothing in her dream until the minute Herx appeared, and then in the morning, upon waking, there would be no confusion between what had really happened in the night and the myths her careless mind would inevitably invent while coming out of sleep.

But dreams are much harder to control than memory, dreams are stubborn, they don't obey our wishes – no sooner had she fallen asleep than the will of free, unleashed imagination carried her onto the wet wooden deck of an old, apparently pirate ship.

Why pirate? Well, first of all, there were no flags on the masts, and everywhere was trash, disorder, and unkemptness. It wouldn't be like that on a merchant ship, and certainly not on a military one. Nearby, to the left and right, sailors in mismatched clothes staggered drunkenly from the heavy pitching – the dirty cedar planks creaking beneath them – and smiling, cursed each other in old Swedish. One of them showed Rina his hat with three bullet holes in it and laughed heartily.

Finally, Herx climbed up the soggy ladder, his hair glistening with drops of recent drizzle. He was the captain. True, there were no badges or epaulettes on his worn frock coat, but who ever recognizes a pirate captain by his insignia?

Herx stopped, searching for something with his eyes. A hook-nosed sailor in a leather jacket jumped up and set down a small barrel on the deck – an empty one, but obviously once filled with rum. Herx sat down, grunted, cast a glance at the stars, puffed mirthfully on his short pipe, and looked Rina in the eye.

“So listen here, kid. It's not a hard job, but don't miss anything. To Stone Harbor – well, that's where the dirt ends and there's no more dust – we'll head there southwest”, he declared in a hoarse voice. “You understand, with rigging this ragged”, he nodded at the masts, “you can't go too fast. The clean deal you'll do two dozen cables per clang at best, even counting the current. Damn that cursed Ruby Cape and their long cannons! Well, we are also wiseacres, got carried away playing war, exposed our underbelly. Now we'll have to haul this tub in for a fix, and that means no Norvell ale the entire way to Corniland. Five weeks, damn them, Hades! And don't forget, there won't be water there – not a desert, but you'd better bring something to drink, so you don't dry up”, he grinned.

Rina greedily studied his wrinkled, sunburnt face and couldn't tell if Herx really didn't remember her, or if this was just an act.

“You'll go scouting in my name, like we agreed”, he rasped, blowing out a huge cloud of foul smoke. “When you see the wall – an old brick wall – climb up, then wait for the orange light and jump. The more fire, the softer the landing – remember that! Am I right, you bloody thugs? Did you hear me, chump-heads?”

“Er sanning, herr kapten!” the sailors shouted.

“So here's the main thing – don't go into the darkness!” Herx shook his hand, crimson embers sprinkling from the pipe. “You'll break your neck! The darkness is your enemy! Jump into the fire, straight to hell, don't chicken out! And once you land, sit for a minute with your eyes shut. Just in case. Everything there is bright and red – if you're not used to it, you might scratch your portholes. So sit, think, and don't rush anywhere. Rushing is no good for your health – am I making myself clear, kid?”

“Got it, Captain”, Rina answered and barely stopped herself from biting her tongue – her voice was no more melodious than Herx's, just as soaked in booze and smoke.

“Later you'll get more explanations from one-legged Hubert, curse his crutches! Hubert! Hubert, damn you, where are you stuck?”

The sailors parted, and then two stocky strongmen dragged a rust-eaten anchor onto the deck.

“Hubert”, Herx addressed the cast-iron piece, “you'll go after him”. He nodded at Rina. “Wait a couple of days, then move. In the meantime, we'll give His Majesty's convoy a shake. Enough to fatten up on these warm waves! We've had our fun, true, but that's that! Or don't you want to hear how the chests full of royal coins jingle?”

The sailors cheered approvingly.

“Be smart – don't rush into the fight, but never waste time either”, Herx smiled, showing a tattered row of brown teeth. “If you don't wish to die or rot alive in their filthy White Prison, jump off that wall right into the hellish light! Got it? I'm telling you this as a friend, you know me. And Hubert, he'll catch up to you soon. Right, Hubert?”

The anchor was silent. Captain Herx waved his hand at it irritably, stood up, barked a long Swedish phrase at the sailors in some hissing dialect, and headed off toward the stern. Rina waited until the sailors had gone about their business, then decided she'd better wake up quickly so she wouldn't forget anything and could calmly think over everything she'd heard.

But unfortunately, waking up quickly didn't work. Another dream came, in which Rina was Aunt Rinaida, a nanny at a Romanian kindergarten, talking to a little blond girl of about four. The girl, whining and nagging, tried hard but couldn't manage her toys while setting them up on a little table – one by one, they fell to the floor.

“Bietul copil (poor child)”, Aunt Rinaida sighed theatrically, looking at little Nina with a tender smile. “Totul ii aluneca dintre degete (everything slips through her fingers.)”

Rina was disturbed by a vague noise. She opened her eyes – moonless night, no hint of the coming dawn. But the sky had changed, something had happened up there, something new in the heights. Looking closely, she noticed that many of the clouds were slightly tinted with burgundy on their right sides, as if with the diluted juice of overripe cherries. Sitting up, she saw vague ruby reflections at the other end of the sky.

“I've got to see it”, she said sleepily, and climbed up the steep slope, stumbling and scraping her knees on the hot ice crystals.

Several minutes later, she stood at the highest point of that hill. The incandescent air blew as if from a forge, and right before her eyes stretched a breathtaking, otherworldly landscape of snowy mountain passes. But far off on the horizon, flames blazed. The fire itself wasn't visible – it was somewhere deep below – though red and yellow flashes lit up the very edge of the sky, casting long, frightening shadows on the nearby cloud fields.

What could be burning among the snow? Or perhaps, in that direction, there was no snow at all? Could it be a forest fire?

A sense of unease crept into her soul. One of her strongest childhood memories was the fire at their old country house. She was only five, just five, but Rina remembered the entire catastrophe as if it had happened a week ago – that mad night, the raging towering bonfire, the wail of sirens, the panic, women screaming, firemen running. She was shocked seeing such a beautiful, such a beloved little house turning into a blinding pillar of flames right before her eyes. And she couldn't do anything – she was a child, they wouldn't even let her near, she stood and watched all the horror through streaming tears. But even more frightening was the morning, when little Rina wandered with her father and sister through the still smoking ruins. The sharp, cutting smell of wet, charred planks stayed with her forever...

Fortunately, Rina hadn't forgotten her dream. She clearly recalled Herx the pirate captain. She understood that most likely this wasn't a fire, but that very same fiery light the old man had spoken about.

“There should be a brick wall there”, she said, peering into the distant play of crimson, scarlet, and garnet stripes. “And from that wall, I'll need to jump somewhere. Right into the blaze”.

In a few moments, the strange dream surfaced from memory in all its finest details. Yes, illogical, absurd, and confused. But the main point was as simple as two plus two – she must jump into the light.

“I'd just like to know, is it high there or not so much? And who was Hubert? Could he really be an anchor?”

But having pondered a bit, she concluded that the anchor was more likely a symbol, an image of some yet unknown thing. Or perhaps a real person. An anchor is always massive and immobile. And what in the world is immobile and heavy? Illness? Sleep? Death? Prison? But maybe the answer is much simpler – it's a man who stops you, who hinders your movement, or in other words – your enemy. True, for now, Hubert is an obstacle solely in the realm of dreams, and here, in the Snowy Land's reality, there are no anchors whatsoever...

Speaking of dreams. Even without a watch, it was evident – morning was too far off yet. Rina couldn't stand there like a post until dawn on this stuffy, unbearably hot summit. Having gazed enough at the mysterious glow, she descended along her own tracks to the chosen ledge. And a few minutes later, she fell into a peaceful sleep again.

But she didn't have any dreams. Well, most likely, something did appear in her nocturnal fantasies, though in the morning, upon waking for the second time, she couldn't recall anything coherent – she remembered just those Swedish pirates with their whistling accent and Hubert the rusty anchor. As to the Romanian nurse and little Nina – the vision had faded away for good.

31 – BEETLE IN KALEIDOSCOPE
part two

Under the unrestrained rays of the newborn sun, Rina felt fresh, strong, and a bit combative. Gradually, her mood had risen almost to festive levels. She washed herself remarkably clean with warm fluffy snow, stretched, even tried to jump a bit like in a gym, and carefully tidied her hair – thank high heavens, the comb hadn't been stolen, although it was precisely the essential item the bears had lacked.

Then, from her famous backpack, she extracted the very last remnants of the package – the two yesterday's “surprise” wrappers. Inside were a three-inch piece of cheese, all bitten, of course, and half of the bun, already quite stale. For a drink – snow with more snow, and shards of ice for dessert. After breakfast, she climbed the ridge again but saw nothing new, only the whiteness – a chalky desolation and endless sloping hills. Descending to the flats, she noted the drop in temperature with a shiver – it was barely twelve degrees, maybe less. Well, it would be hard to last long in this.

She had to invent an improved way to move farther – sprint dashes from hill to hill. Each time, she ran three to four hundred yards through the dreadful cold, then, frantically trembling, she rushed up the slope, and basked there, resting in the warmth of furry snowdrifts. It's impossible to explain, but the upper layers of air heated by the sun hung literally overhead and didn't mix at all with the lower cold streams.

After a couple of hours of such spurts, she noticed crimson reflections on the clouds – the glow became visible even in sunlight. And as if to spite her, the closer she got to the source of this redness, the colder the air became. Soon it got so wintry that even on the tops of large hills, there was a chill.

“Well, how will I go farther?” she wondered. “I'll freeze to death there”.

Climbing another pass, Rina saw the boundary of the white area for the first time. Approximately two miles ahead, the snow ended, and beyond, occupying the space to the edge, was a black lowland, sparkling either with ice or puddles.

She no longer searched for the Garlic River. Firstly, you can't run inside the horribly stinking air – you'd literally suffocate, and secondly, it wasn't about garlic anymore. The only thought was how to traverse the cold space from ridge to ridge quickly enough. The only way of salvation – to sit on the top for a while, gathering strength for the next dash. Moreover, the red reflections cut across half the sky like a real sea beacon, she couldn't stray from the path now.

To an onlooker, such a sprint could be taken for a new game of “who's faster” – either the cold would freeze Rina to death, or Rina would manage to reach the safety of the slope alive. The game turned out to be not funny at all and mercilessly exhausting.

Only an hour and a half later did she finally reach the border of the black land.

And even earlier, having climbed the last hill and examined the panorama revealed before her eyes, she was convinced that the snow had been replaced not so much by earth as by mud. This discovery didn't bring joy.

But a pleasant surprise was waiting for her, a big one – the mud was warm. When Rina approached the black frontier and, shivering all over, her teeth chattering, stepped from the sharp snow tongue onto the soft, wet ground, it was as if someone had flipped a switch on nature around her – the sun flared brighter, the wind died, and the air turned instantly spring-tender, thick with aromas of a forest thawed after a long winter.

Therefore, the only difficulty was, in fact, the mud, which covered the entire valley floor with just a two-inch-thick, utterly repugnant, sticky, slimy layer. Her sneakers soaked through in twenty steps. Splashing in this endless mud puddle with squelching shoes was miserable. Especially since at first, Rina could barely stay on her feet – the soles slid over the bumps as if greased with massage oil.

After some time, patches of dry land began to appear. Rina jumped into the first such spot and forcefully knocked the mud off her unfortunate, now unrecognizable Pumas. True, she had to step back into the slushy mire, but the dry islands became more frequent, and within an hour, Rina emerged onto a plateau completely dry, even somewhat dusty. Hooray! The mud was over! Finally! Normal ground underfoot. She was giddy with relief.

A bit later, the most annoying nuisance of this land was precisely the dust. The finest, incredibly light powder lay like a plush blanket on all surfaces, ready to rise into the air at the slightest breeze. And the winds were working as if on command, with dense, dusty blasts flurrying into her eyes, seemingly intent on hurling as much of that tawny flour at her face as possible. Dust crunched between her teeth, dust streamed down from her hair in little rivulets, dust lay on her shoulders like admiral's epaulettes – dust was everywhere.

Fortunately, by and large, the universe is fair. In due course, the dust turned out not to be the undisputed queen of the dry steppe. After overcoming yet another long, sloping rise, she stepped onto a new plateau and found herself on a smooth stone surface polished by millennia of crosswinds. “The Ironing Board”, she had dubbed this place in her trail musings. And indeed, everything was so flat, as if someone had paved it over. Though not without flaws – here and there stood occasional, big, but rather cute-looking pink boulders. She even sat on one for a bit and got a decent rest, for a change.

But the best part was the narrow stream cutting across the plain. The little brook generously offered the purest spring water to parched travelers. It was so cold it made her teeth ache. On the monolithic bank, there wasn't a single grain of sand, not a speck of debris – perfectly hewn gray slate! Rina even thought that perhaps it wasn't just a stream, but an ancient aqueduct. However, she had no time to dwell on that idea.

She was as delighted by the fresh water as she had been in the Desert. To her shame, she had forgotten to bring any snow from the World of Ice, and a wicked thirst had been gnawing at her.

“Four days of nothing but snow all around, water in frozen form on every bump”, she muttered, trying to justify the oversight.

But here it was, an ideal little flow, and the problem of thirst resolved itself... uh... automatically (yes, the silly word was stuck in her head again) – Rina drank beyond all her limits, as much as she could stand, to the point of nausea, and then filled the three remaining plastic bags to the brim, mindful of Herx's warning, “don't forget, there won't be any water there”.

A few minutes later, she sat for a while on the bank of the amiable brook, savoring the melodious murmur of the precious liquid, relaxing as much as she could. Then she washed her hands and face, and even managed to scrub her sneakers so again they looked new (well, almost). She spent about twenty minutes on her water rituals, maybe more, but in the end, she got herself into a decent, human-like state.

After cleaning herself up so impeccably (again, almost), she felt a surge of confidence – or better yet, it was more like self-respect – she was a teen already, a young woman, very young, but not a little girl after all! She was happy to look nice. Rina stood, scanned the peaceful dale, and commanded herself in her best coach's voice:

“All right, team! Enough lazing about – wake up, get movin'!”

And set off over...

The wall promised by Herx in the dream rose up in her path almost instantly, as soon as she climbed a little higher onto a new flat tableland in the slate valley. Rina approached and stopped nearby.

At first glance the wall looked ancient. A huge, oval structure with no roof. Rina had never seen anything like it, but some features made it seem like the perimeter of a large circus or stadium.

“Amphitheater”, the right term came to mind from the history textbook.

One of the Universal laws of our world says, “A small cylinder makes people want to roll it, a large one makes them walk around it”. Looks like it was ingrained in human nature long ago. So, Rina walked in a spiral, getting a bit closer to the wall with each step.

She didn't expect to find an entrance arch or a door – somehow she was sure there wouldn't even be a notch. But it was simply impossible not to go around and see the entire object with her own eyes. Finally, she came right up to the wall, stretched out her hand, and touched the brick. Rough, weathered, cool, colder than the air.

Because of centuries of erosion – or maybe due to ancient wars – the wall had ragged, uneven edges, and in one place it had crumbled so much that if Rina stood on tiptoes and stretched, she could reach the upper margin with the tip of her middle finger.

Though there was no ladder or anything like that nearby, unless you counted the squat, almost globular boulder lying about forty yards from the wall.

“Fine”, she sighed. “I get it, I'll have to roll the rock over”.

If the boulder hadn't been so melon-shaped, Rina wouldn't have been able to budge it for all the diamonds in the world. She managed to shift it an inch, but despite its supposedly convenient form, the stone weighed over two hundred pounds. Well, maybe not exactly two hundred, but at least one seventy at a minimum! There was no other choice, and no help, either. Luck, as always, came to the rescue – the ground from the boulder to the wall was smooth, slightly sloped, and as flat as a desktop.

But a stone is a stone, so only after about fifty minutes of desperate efforts and many breaks did that dreadful granite finally thump against the bricks and settle into place.

The one thing she knew she must not do in this situation was to rush. In any case, there's no point in clambering up the wall right away. Because that's what they taught her – safety first! You have to look around, ‘sense the sublevels' (Grandpa's words), and think as hard as you can. And sure, there was plenty to ponder.

First of all, the fiery glow in the sky had died out completely. No matter how she stared, she couldn't see any red, or orange, or other reflections of flame on the clouds. When exactly that redness had disappeared, at what moment? She had no idea, she hadn't noticed the trick at all.

Second, was this even the right wall? Herx had mentioned a wall, but here everything was round, an amphitheater, no better word for it. As she circled the strange structure for the second time, she found rectangular pits someone had hacked into the slate surface five yards from the wall. She decided there had probably been a wooden house at that site.

Wooden, because if it had been brick, there would have been something left of it, but a wooden house might have burned, for example. Though if people had lived here, why would they have needed to build a monumental round fence – especially one with no entrances or exits?

“For defense”, the answer came.

Yes, it is quite possible that the amphitheater was the remains of a fortress.

“But then Herx would have said so – a fortress. Not just a wall”.

What if Rina had gone off track? By accident somehow. Especially after the brook, or when the glow in the sky went away?

“No, that's stupid!” she firmly declared to the empty surroundings after a minute's thought. “There can't be another wall here!”

In any case, there was nothing left to do but climb to the top and at least see what was inside. Strangely enough, the climb wasn't all that hard. Rina hadn't expected it – she felt as if she were being pushed from below, she even looked down a couple of times. But be that as it may, she hopped nimbly onto the rounded boulder, which leaned snugly against the bricks, and with just a few quick pulls, she scrambled up onto the wall, as if she had been climbing there every day since childhood.

The wall surprised her very much – it was hard to call it a wall, actually, it was seven or eight meters wide – practically a circular road around the pit.

But you couldn't really walk along it – jagged, broken spurs jutted out like the vertebrae of a dragon's skeleton dusted over with a thin layer of powdery dirt. Almost no room to step, two-foot spikes everywhere.

Somehow Rina picked her way to the inner edge and, just as she had expected, she saw a well. From wall to wall, it gaped open. A black, tarry abyss, a chasm of hell. Quite a scary scene.

Yet if you stripped away the emotion and philosophy, the whole edifice looked more like some abandoned subway station, dug out vertically for some reason. Below – a bottomless, yawning void, and on the walls – no ladders, no steps, no footholds. So getting down, as she'd managed in the Desert, was impossible. The only option – to jump...

Rina stood at the edge, her legs slightly trembling, her eyes wide open, staring into the depths.

And then – white flash!

Blazing, blinding, searing! A light burst of astonishing density exploded right in front of her eyes, swept across her body in a molten wave, throwing her backward, slamming her hard against the sharp corners of the bricks.

Stunned, deafened, terrified, she lay there for a long time too scared to move. Blue spots danced in her vision, she couldn't see a thing. Only after some minutes did the world begin to regain its colors and shapes, though turquoise shadows still hadn't entirely vanished. And the first thing Rina's recovering sight noticed was the red gleam on the clouds. The deep glow of the abyss had become visible again. Then, three minutes later, along the opposite inner wall of the well, orange flares began to flicker, as if from a great fire.

Finally, Rina stood up and, swaying, once again approached the very edge. She had to concentrate all her remaining determination for that. But she didn't dare to look down. By the brightness of the red and yellow shapes, it was clear when the next volley would occur. As for the clouds, their swollen sides gradually turned bloody, but only in those minutes when the colored stains fade away from the wall.

“Here everything is wrong!” Rina said in wonder, having almost recovered from the impact. “When there's fire in the well, the clouds darken, and the moment the fire goes out, the clouds light up”.

But if so, when exactly should she jump? What was the light she was supposed to count on, the one that Herx had spoken about in his pirate guise?

“‘You'll break your neck..'“. Rina recalled.

Not the most promising words. However, thinking it over, she came to the conclusion that the clouds didn't count at all. Who knows what kind of wonders happen overhead! Herx had talked about the wall, he had never said anything about the clouds. And what if not only the clouds, but also the stars colored themselves somehow or, on the contrary, dimmed above the well? Then what? No, the main thing here is the wall and the void inside.

“I'll jump into the orange light”, Rina declared categorically, but then drooped and grew sad. “It's so deep there!”

Indeed, the well looked bottomless, but there's nothing truly infinite in the universe, everything has an end. Which means, no matter how long you fall, no matter how deep you drop, you'll still have to crash eventually. It's one thing to jump in the World of Clouds, where there's no ground at all, only air and mist, but here, standing on the jagged, broken bricks, Rina felt far less sure of herself.

31 – BEETLE IN KALEIDOSCOPE
part three

It was clear, the jump was inevitable, no other options were offered. Apparently, that terrible way was the only method to get to the World of Fire. The creators left no choice.

“But why? Tell me! Why can't they just set up the exact same door as in the pea field?” Rina exclaimed, addressing the clouds with crimson sides. The clouds didn't respond. Calming down, she thought:

“If I do something wrong, if I mess up even a little bit, then it'll be the last jump of my life. Either I'll burn up down there like a napkin in an oven, or I'll shatter into shards”.

Rina imagined herself as a Chinese porcelain doll, flying apart in splinters in all directions. And, strangely, it made her feel better.

“Well then!” a brave thought rang out loudly and fearlessly. “If I crash, then I crash! Although, probably, everything will go just as it should...”

Settled? Settled!

Ready? Ready!

It was true, she was ready.

She took a deep breath, inched carefully to the very brim of the black abyss, so that the tips of her sneakers hung over the emptiness. She exhaled and froze. It seemed like everything was okay, but she had to hurry, before boldness turned to weakness.

Right then, shadows began to flicker on the opposite concave wall – long thin stripes racing – scarlet, crimson, cherry, red. The reflections grew stronger, the invisible, soundless fire flared far below somewhere in the darkness at unthinkable depths, and soon the whole wall was engulfed first in orange, then in a furious, yellow-white blaze.

“Banzaaaai!”

She jumped.

She forgot to close her eyes, but there was no flash. A mighty flurry of hot wind struck her from below, and a moment later the well, the colors, the wind – all rushed away. And again – the darkness. As total as it could be. Her eyes had nothing to cling to, blindness was absolute.

But despite this obscurity, Rina felt she was flying, gliding in an airy space, and in that same instant, all the tension, fears, and troubles of the past hours turned into a pleasant, carefree relaxation.

“Ha! Scared for nothing”, the young lightheaded thought said with a sly grin, “No fire at all. Just an ordinary transition”.

“Everything's ordinary for you”, the wise second thought grumbled with annoyance, “And what if there had been rocks? Or cliffs? Or lava? What then?”

The young thought considered it and... decided to stay silent.

The flight was not too long. In a few minutes, the gliding stopped gradually – if there was any movement left, it was slow and almost imperceptible. Though still there was something else – it wasn't speed, it wasn't space – something which no dictionary had yet named, and Rina felt it.

Then, without warning, the darkness shivered and parted like curtains in a village theater, unveiling an amazing hawk's-eye view of the World of Snow. Rina lay on an invisible, rough, coarse, solid plane that was sliding over the Gray Valley. All the lowlands below were thickly splattered with numerous blurry dirty spots, through which shiny tracks scattered in all directions. And here and there flocks of dark dots stood out – crowds of purple bears.

“Why did they bring me back?” she asked the concealed inter-world pilot. But he didn't answer.

Strangely, the altitude no longer scared her, on the contrary, the flight was delightful – it even made her want to play around, to zoom right over the heads of the silly bears and spook them a little. And, for no apparent reason, the wish was immediately granted – the flying device she was lying on unexpectedly tilted quite dangerously and whistled downward. Rina let out a startled cry, but the air platform was pretty skillful – gently braking thirty yards above the surface, it began floating along the Straight Boulevard.

As expected, the purple pranksters noticed nothing. In large groups, the bears, having feasted on the frozen distillate, leisurely walked around the dirty snow, bursting into loud, hearty laughter now and then.

Without speeding up, having flown for about a mile, the platform lifted its nose and abruptly shot skyward like an unbridled lightning bolt, so that Rina barely managed to hold on to its surface. And in the next second the world was plunged back into darkness. Of course, Rina guessed what would happen next. And she was right: somewhere at a great depth below, a colorful landscape was silently unfolding and brightening – the flower meadows of the fairy Dinissa.

Rina didn't want to see the butterflies. It flickered through her mind that from now on and forever she would have a so-called ‘negative attitude' to those light-winged, motley creatures, but the platform – that completely transparent support which she decided to call ‘the platform' for simplicity's sake – was descending toward the grass, and a minute later, swarms of joyful butterflies were all around. The boldest of them (only the blue ones) were persistently trying to land right on Rina's shoulders.

Nothing had changed, the meadows were just as before – the same big bright flowers, the same pine forest at the edge. Though the rain was no more, and Dinissa the fairy was nowhere to be seen.

“It would be nice to take my glass house back! That would cause a great tragedy for them”, Rina thought with a sort of malicious chuckle. But immediately felt ashamed of such ideas and was very glad she hadn't spoken them aloud...

The flowers, by the way, didn't smell. Perhaps, because of the dense clouds in the sky, who knows. Rina looked at this beauty and was a little surprised that despite the days that had passed and the countless adventures and dangers, she still felt a prickly resentment over the butterflies' deceit, especially over the fake sweets and treats.

“But what if they aren't to blame?” she suddenly thought, watching the carefree play of goldtails, monarchs, skippers, and heliconians. “Most likely you actually can eat your fill of the local delicacies, the food was real, but nourishing only here, in the butterfly world. While on other planets these ice creams and cakes vanish at the moment of transition – they just evaporate from the body. And Dinissa and her friends, they simply don't know that...”

Maybe Rina was right, but she didn't have time to ponder her lawyerly, defensive theory of justification, as the platform once again sped skyward, this time in a vertical line like an open high-speed elevator. And again it flew into the night, though the darkness lasted no more than three heartbeats.

Rina expected to see the city of Ao and the palace of the Air Queen, but below, across the whole width of the space, stretched the Desert in its cruel magnificence, cut in half by a long, winding gorge. A hundred meters short of the sand, the platform glided over the Blue Ravine. There was no doubt – up ahead, on a small oval platform, lay the sleeping giant tortoise Oluvila.

“Yes, except now I've got nothing to tickle her with”, Rina sighed regretfully. “The poor bears stole my favorite pen-brush. How stupid! What will they do with the pen? They don't even have paper!”

That's exactly what she thought – “poor bears”. Even though they were mischievous, thievish, and treacherous, in their own way they were still pitiful, lost, and unfortunate, so yes, they ended up being “poor”. At least that was the mixed impression that remained in Rina's mind after thinking about the purple folk...

The platform lowered and now hovered ten feet above the tortoise's head. Oluvila looked a bit larger than she had before. Rina wondered if her friend had grown over the last weeks. But probably it was just an optical illusion, a view from above. The tortoise apparently felt something, she got restless, twitched her nose, but didn't open her eyes, only snuffled a little louder, and a minute later dozed off again as peacefully and heedlessly as she had slept in this desert for all these hundreds of years, waking just for half an hour a day, and even then, only if there was something new around.

Rina remembered that Triazis was about five miles away, she wanted to see Gri-Gri, to say nice encouraging words to him, but it was unlikely that the platform could be controlled to such an extent – she had to obey the laws of local mechanics.

After hanging in the hot air for a couple more minutes, as if giving the passenger a rest, the platform shivered like a living thing and drifted off, gathering speed with each moment, until it flew full tilt into an impenetrable starless night.

She wondered what world they would show her in the next episode, but she couldn't guess. She watched. And almost fell over: right below her they switched on a bright crisp picture of her own yard in Kyiv.
