Down there, only a hundred meters away, boys were running around, adults were walking dogs, cars were arriving and departing. With no sound, like a silent movie, but Rina didn't notice that right away.

Instantly she found her own balcony and... went numb – there stood her mother, all in red, and next to her Aunt Vera, all in black. Both of them were looking at the tennis court, where little kids were trying to play. Mother and Aunt were watching the children.

The platform was descending in an tricky, winding curve that passed in front of the windows. In half a minute, Rina was standing – yes, she couldn't help herself, she stood up to her full height – and now she was only six steps from her mother, looking right at her face, unable to move even a finger. Mom looked up, Rina flinched. The Aunt said something and walked back inside.

“Mom! Mom!!!” Rina shouted.

But her mother went after Vera, moved the curtain aside, closed the balcony door behind her, and the curtain fell.

“Mom”, said Rina quietly.

She stood there waiting, but no one came out.

“Aunt Vera didn't leave and she's in a black dress”, the thought whispered.

The fact that her aunt hadn't left, that she had stayed with Mom in this difficult time – it was a relief. But why in black? Rina pondered, and without taking her eyes off the balcony, cautiously sat down on the platform.

And the platform seemed to be waiting for that – it moved to the right side, then tilted slightly and floated sideways over the yard, but not reaching the opposite building – Svetka's house, number nineteen – it once again soared into the sky. Rina didn't notice anything, her memory was fixed on the picture of the little green balcony and the narrow door with the curtains...

The searing glare of the sun snapped her to her senses, brought her back to reality. This was the Air World. She recognized it instantly. Such a pure violet sky couldn't be confused with anything else. True, there were no cities or islands below or above, only empty cirrus clouds and white, misty horizons. The platform was flying at zeppelin speed, but not finding anything interesting, two minutes later it gained speed and once again pierced into a black wall of darkness.

Only this time the starless night turned to a sunny day almost instantly – the yellow endless Great Desert burst open with a gust. Rina was puzzled: what was the point of repeating the just-seen episode? But the new film proved to be very short, it hardly had time to flare up before the desert darkened and soon, having reached the perfectly black inkiness, it gave way to the Blue Meadows, which in turn also didn't last long, filling up with dark blue paint at the count of three, and out of it, without a pause, emerged the snowy Gray Valley.

After the Snow Planet, Kyiv appeared twice in a row – the same yard but from different angles. Then again the Desert and three times the land of Dinissa. Rina began to feel lost and confused with these rapidly changing scenes – the images of worlds were blinking out just as they emerged.

But the next minute, Rina's yard, familiar down to the tiniest pebble, arose out of the blue in the middle of the sands. It looked like a mirage with blurred edges, though Rina clearly saw how the doors of the apartment tower number seventeen swung open and a big, happy crowd of festively dressed people with a bride in a long white dress tumbled out. Rina easily recognized the bride as Polyna Mazur, the younger sister of their new math teacher – all the girls in Rina's class had been talking about the upcoming wedding.

Though even such absurdity – a wedding in the desert – was not yet the peak of the craziness: fish began to float over the dunes! And not just fish, either. Octopuses, squids, crabs, even sea snakes, all of them the size of clouds. And then – a fluffy snowfall! Rina reflexively caught a large snowflake and let it go – it was too cold.

As soon as darkness returned again, Rina took a deep breath and thought it would be better to tune into these visions as an abstract movies and not look too closely at whatever they might be showing to her. She was right: this time, all the worlds appeared at once. Only the Ocean had gone somewhere, although half a dozen fish still fluttered around the Desert sun. But all other universes intertwined, mixed together, and overlapped – boys on bicycles were racing across Triazis, lilac bears were chasing red butterflies, and the tortoise Oluvila was rubbing her lacquered purple shell against a yellow Kyiv bus frozen in the ice at the stop, full of dancing people.

When the new momentary night fell and immediately dissolved, Rina was no longer on the platform, but in the mass of a multi-galactic cyclone with triangular, square, and oval images of worlds flying like dry leaves in the wind – dozens of carefully cut pictures from the most diverse corners of our Splendid Universe – they spun under her feet and above her head. She looked around and immediately remembered the little bug in the kaleidoscope.

Long, long ago, before the first grade, Rina was ‘vacationing' at their summer house with her parents. Thanks to her innate foresight, little Rina had brought a whole mountain of toys from home and now played with them in the thick grass under the apple tree. Among other things, there was a kaleidoscope – a thick plastic tube with strips of mirrors and pea-sized shreds of multi-colored glass inside. If you looked through the tiny lens, you could watch how snowflakes and fairy-tale patterns were formed from the colorful bits – instantly changing from triangles and hexagons to squares and stars, and then back again if you turned the toy even slightly.

And then, right into these very patterns, in an inconceivable way, a little bug had crawled: a small, red, pearlescent beetle. Rina looked and saw an unprecedented picture – eight bugs running along the motley shapes, merging, spinning, and darting right through each other.

Using his makeshift tools, Daddy managed to get the imprudent insect out somehow, and let it go free into the grass. Rina remembered this event so clearly, as if it had happened yesterday. And now, swaying on the unsteady waves of a warm whirlwind, looking at the polished, shimmering engravings of seven worlds orbiting around, she suddenly felt like that very bug in the kaleidoscope.

“But who will get me out of here?”

Yet the universal merry-go-round ended abruptly, as if a worker at a galactic amusement park had come, pressed a big red button, and shut down this whole carousel in one go. The last rainbow polygon fell into infinity, the airy wave stopped in mid-breath, everything went quiet – no movement, no stirring. Ahead – a heavy, lifeless night. Just in case, Rina got on her knees and pressed her hands against the invisible smoothness.

And immediately pulled her palms back, as if from hot metal – her fingers sank into dry sand. The darkness rippled. A large, powerful wave ran across the entire space. Somewhere at an unimaginable depth, a reddish spot began glowing faintly. High overhead, black-and-gray swirls took on a pinkish hue. Dawn was approaching. The new morning quickly gathered its force and potency, it stretched, shook its head, and spreading its mighty wings over the giant expanses of the Fiery Kingdom, glanced at the world.

Rina was kneeling on the prickly sand. Long, still-dark mounds overgrown with brown grass were crowding around her. And a hundred paces ahead – a half-ruined wall, almost like that brick well in the World of Snow, only flat, not rounded. Rina stood up, brushed the sand from her palms.

“That's it... You may congratulate me – I've arrived. Here's the Kingdom, and it's just the beginning”, she said, and without looking back walked toward the wall.

the end of book one
